Estuary — Working Draft

Estuary

Part I — The household left behind

Chapter 1 — The Duke’s Morning

The hawk had put a foot through Ouen’s letter.

It was his father’s letter, strictly speaking, but it had been addressed to him and nobody had told him anything about the state of the northern roads that could not have waited until the bird was settled. She stood on his glove with the torn corner caught beneath one talon. When he tried to draw it free she tightened her grip.

“Leave it,” he told the man beside the desk.

Halden left it. He had not been touching the letter.

Ouen worked the paper loose with his other hand. The hawk turned her hooded head towards the sound. She had cast well that morning and stood lightly on the glove; he could feel the small adjustments of her feet as he moved. It would be a good day. There was very little wind.

“His Grace asks for forty horses this month,” Halden said.

“I can read.”

“Yes, my lord.”

Ouen flattened the letter against the desk. His father had written three lines himself, beneath the secretary’s much longer account. The personal lines concerned the household and his grandson. None asked how Ouen thought the war should be conducted.

“And fodder for a hundred,” Halden said.

Ouen looked up. “He has other horses.”

“He does. The instruction is to hold the fodder here.”

“Then hold it.”

“In which store, my lord?”

“The stores.”

Halden laid another sheet beside the torn letter. He had drawn the three buildings in outline and written their contents inside them. Ouen could have understood it from the door. There was something especially disagreeable about that.

“The lower granary will have to be cleared. The hay cannot go into the upper one until the roof is finished.”

“Why isn’t it finished?”

“The slates arrived yesterday.”

“They were ordered last month.”

“Yes, my lord. The carrier came back by a different road.”

Everyone came back by a different road now. They told you which one, and why, and whom they had paid to use it, until an explanation of a roof required knowing the affairs of three cantons. His father appeared to enjoy this. Ouen had seen him keep a carter standing beside the supper table for half an hour, asking questions while the man twisted his cap into a rope.

“Get another roofer.”

“The men are working already. I need authority to move the grain from the lower fields into the stone granary beside the mill.”

“Then why are we discussing the roof?”

“To explain why we need the other building.”

The hawk shifted. Ouen raised his glove a little, giving her the perch she wanted. She settled at once.

“There,” he said, though neither man had asked about her.

Halden waited. His grandmother had left the man with him as though a household officer were a coat Ouen might grow into.

Ouen had once asked to see the household correspondence, certain that Halden was reporting to his grandmother. He had been given the keys and spent a miserable afternoon finding requests for lamp oil, a dispute over a damaged boat, and letters to his father saying things Halden had already said to him. There had been no secret account of Ouen’s shortcomings. There had not even been a private complaint about the falconers’ expenses.

He could not forgive the man for making him spend the afternoon that way.

“My father left me in charge here.”

“He did, my lord.”

“Then I shall decide when the grain moves.”

“Certainly.”

He heard himself. He heard the shape of the sentence, which was the shape of a boy arguing with his tutor, and he hated it, and he could not stop.

“I don’t need every cart brought to me.”

“No. Only the use of the mill granary. It has been reserved for His Grace’s rents.”

“You’ve said that.”

Halden drew the plan back from the bird’s reach.

“The teams are harnessed. They can begin when you return.”

Ouen had been going to say he had not decided whether to go out. His cloak was over his shoulder. Two horses waited in the court, with a third for the falconer, and the bird was on his fist.

“See that they are ready,” he said.

His son was on the steps with his nurse, wearing one of Ouen’s old gloves. It reached almost to the child’s elbow. He held his arm out rigidly, his face solemn with the effort.

“Not yet,” Ouen said.

“I can.”

“She would hurt you.”

“I won’t mind.”

“You would mind very much.”

He bent and let the boy touch the smooth leather of the hood. The nurse drew closer. Ouen kept the hawk turned away from the small bare hand; he could feel her attention change even beneath the hood.

“Gently.”

The boy touched it with one finger, then looked up at him.

“She can’t see.”

“No.”

“Take it off.”

“Out in the field.”

“I’ll come.”

“Another day.”

The boy’s face crumpled. Ouen wanted to promise tomorrow, but his wife had asked him not to promise days he would forget. He told the nurse to have a glove made that fitted. The boy might carry a wooden bird until he was older. He could paint it himself.

“Like yours?” the child asked.

“Better. Mine bites.”

That was accepted. Ouen kissed the top of his head and went down to the horses feeling that he had handled something well.

Beyond the lower fields, the ditches were full. The men waiting with the grain carts watched him pass. One took off his cap. Ouen returned the greeting with his free hand and turned towards the meadow where the falconer had seen quarry that morning.

The bird flew beautifully.

He knew she would before she left the glove. He had watched the way she held herself in the last few yards, felt her feet gather, waited until the turn of her head gave him the line she wanted. Released, she rose above the willows and made a broad circle in the clear air.

The falconer said something about the light. Ouen did not answer. He was watching the hawk.

She took the quarry beyond the ditch, on ground too soft for the horses. He dismounted before the groom reached him and went across on foot, choosing the firmer tufts. Mud filled one shoe. He scarcely felt it. The hawk crouched over her kill with her wings spread and watched him approach.

He came slowly for the last few paces. There was no point hurrying a bird into defending what she had caught. He spoke to her, offered the food he had brought, and let her exchange one thing for another when she was ready.

When she stepped onto the glove, the falconer nodded.

“Well taken, my lord.”

Ouen looked back across the ditch. The horses stood against the pale sky, the men beside them watching. For a moment he wished his father had been among them. Valdais would have known what it had taken to recover her without a struggle. He would have said so, too. Then he would have asked why the ditch had not been cleared, and by supper Ouen would have been answering questions about the mill.

His father could be attentive to women in the same exhausting way, remembering a small wish and sending halfway across Adria to satisfy it. He could do it for Taliya and for the woman she had asked him to give up, in the same week. Ouen had once suggested that the presents might be sent by different servants. Valdais had told him to mind his own household. He had said it in front of people.

Ouen turned his attention to the bird.

They stayed out longer than he had intended. On the way home he remembered the wooden hawk and told the groom to speak to the carpenter. Then he remembered his wet shoe. The discomfort became considerable as soon as he began attending to it.

The grain carts were still beside the lower fields.

At the house, Halden waited under the arch. He had moved a chair into the sun and was examining a torn harness strap with the carter who had brought it. When Ouen rode in, both men stood.

“Good sport, my lord?”

“Very.”

The falconer came to receive the bird. Ouen transferred her carefully, easing the jesses clear of his fingers. There was blood on the glove and a small feather caught against its seam. He picked the feather off before turning towards the door.

“My lord. The grain from the lower fields.”

“Move it.”

“Very good, my lord.”

Chapter 2 — The Useful Crossing

The clerk had drawn a line through Cedric’s expenses before Cedric sat down.

He could see it upside down across the desk. Three entries, the last of them the cost of the ferry, and one straight stroke that took all three together. The clerk shook sand onto the ink. He gave the paper a moment, then tipped it carefully over a dish.

“I was required to attend,” Cedric said.

“Yes, sir.”

“And to get here.”

“Yes.”

The clerk set the dish aside.

Cedric had chosen his better coat. It pulled a little across the shoulders when he sat, and the high collar touched the place beneath his jaw where the cold sometimes made the scar ache. The man across from him wore a brown gown with an ink stain near the wrist. He had no business making Cedric feel that he had dressed for the wrong occasion.

“The notice says my account is to be settled.”

“The account of the restitution.”

“The expense of answering a notice belongs to the account.”

“Not to the people being repaid.”

Cedric looked at the line again. His ferry fare was small. The very smallness of it made the refusal insulting. The clerk could have passed it without anyone remarking on it, but he had evidently decided that the queen’s affairs would collapse if a gentleman went across a river at somebody else’s expense.

“My father’s officer allowed it.”

“Your father’s officer entered it. He asked us to determine where it should fall.”

“Where is that written?”

The clerk turned a page and pointed.

Edran’s hand. Cedric knew it before reading the words. His brother had put the matter in the middle of a paragraph about two claimants who had failed to attend the autumn hearing. Even here, in a room where he had never been, Edran had managed to arrange a small embarrassment for him.

“There are still people claiming money for which they have no proof,” Cedric said.

“They have witnesses.”

“They have each other.”

“That is what witnesses often are.”

The clerk said it without looking pleased. Cedric had been preparing an answer to a sneer and found himself with none to give to the plain sentence.

Outside the half-open door somebody was explaining why a man due to leave at noon had already left. A woman’s voice asked where the message had been put. The answer was inaudible. The woman said, “Then fetch the pouch.”

The clerk slid another sheet across.

“Your acknowledgement, here.”

Cedric left his hands on his knees.

“It isn’t an admission of the disputed claims,” the clerk said. “Those are copied on the second page. It acknowledges that you have seen what has already been paid.”

“I should like the words changed.”

“Which words?”

Cedric read it. The statement was annoyingly precise. It allowed him to object to everything he meant to object to and made him acknowledge the money that had indisputably come out of his allowance. At the bottom, the space for his signature had been ruled wide enough for a man with a much longer name.

He signed.

The clerk blotted the paper and stood.

“Master Fayd would like a word before you leave.”

Cedric kept hold of the pen.

“About this?”

“He did not say.”

“Has my father written to him?”

“I couldn’t tell you.”

The clerk held out his hand for the pen. Cedric gave it to him.

He waited long enough to regret having sent the boy for his horse.

The waiting room held two benches, a shelf of cups, and a brazier giving off more smell than heat. Somebody had left a woollen hood drying on a stick across the top. Cedric moved away from it. The only other man in the room had a raw patch on his heel and had taken his boot off. When he saw Cedric looking, he covered the heel with his stocking.

“Road’s bad,” the man said.

“It usually is.”

“This was inside the boot.”

Cedric chose the other bench.

He knew who Fayd was. Men at Harwick had been discussing the appointment for days. A man who had talked to rebels was now to be paid to talk to them for the queen, which seemed to Cedric a distinction made chiefly for the benefit of the man being paid. His father had listened to the discussion and asked whether anybody knew the extent of the warrant. Nobody had. Edran had said he would obtain a copy.

Cedric had left the table before it arrived.

The man with the sore heel was called first. He carried a narrow packet under his arm and walked with the deliberate care of someone trying to keep a limp from being noticed. He came out in less than a quarter of an hour, with the packet gone and a piece of bread in his hand.

Then the clerk appeared.

“This way, sir.”

Fayd’s room was smaller than Cedric expected. It had a second door, shut, and a window overlooking a yard in which a boy was scraping mud off stirrups. Fayd stood by the window, reading a strip of paper. He wore no chain of office.

“Master Aeryl.”

Cedric inclined his head. Fayd indicated a chair and finished reading. Cedric used the interval to put his gloves down where the stitching could be seen.

“Your journey was tolerable?”

“As far as the ferry.”

“Which one?”

Cedric named it.

“Still using the small boat?”

“The larger one’s laid up.”

“Both banks, or only the eastern landing?”

“Both. The chain on the other boat broke.”

Fayd wrote something at the foot of the strip.

“I’ll have your return fare paid here,” he said. “You have stayed on my account.”

It was a better beginning than Cedric had expected. He settled more comfortably in the chair.

“I understand you know the approaches to Tarlington Ford.”

“I’ve ridden them.”

“On the Viterran side.”

“Yes.”

Fayd took a folded plan from beneath the papers. He opened it between them and turned it so that Cedric could read the place-names.

“How would you bring thirty mounted men to this point without going through the settlement?”

Cedric leaned forward. The ink had been put on good parchment by someone with a patient hand. Every road was given the same width.

“You wouldn’t use that,” he said, touching the upper line.

“Why?”

“It’s a drove-way.”

“It is marked as one.”

“Then why have you put the bridge there?”

Fayd looked at the mark.

“The bridge exists.”

“Over the ditch. The drove-way turns before it. Whoever drew this has joined the road to the wrong end.”

“Could the men get across?”

“Yes. To a field.”

“And out of the field?”

“There is a gate.”

“Then we have a route.”

“Into the owner’s yard. He’d have to take his fence down to get you out towards the ford. Or you’d have to take it down for him.”

Fayd rested a finger on the little bridge. Cedric had imagined some admission of the error. Instead the man asked how long the fence was.

He told him. Fayd asked what it enclosed, whether there was stock in it through winter, and who held the neighbouring pasture. Cedric found himself drawing with the blunt end of a pen. He knew the place. He had once lost half a morning there because a gate had been chained by a man who did not believe him when he gave his father’s name.

“The widow still keeps the key herself?” Fayd asked.

Cedric stopped.

“You know the place.”

“I know about the gate.”

“You could have asked somebody else.”

“I did.”

Fayd waited while Cedric put down the pen.

“Their account made the field sound passable,” he said. “Yours tells me what would be necessary to pass it. Have you eaten?”

The question came so ordinarily that Cedric answered it. Fayd called for bread and something to drink. He did not have the plan removed.

Cedric looked again at the roads. It was better work than he had thought at first. The wrong turning remained wrong, but the elevations along the approach had been marked, and somebody had put the landing places in with a different ink.

“You should move these men farther back,” he said.

“Which men?”

“The thirty. If they’re waiting here, anyone coming down from the mill will see them.”

Fayd turned the plan towards himself.

The food arrived on a board: a heel of bread, cheese with the cloth still attached to one side, and a pear so soft that Fayd cut it over a bowl. He offered half to Cedric. Cedric declined, then regretted it when the bread proved dry.

Cedric put his bread down.

“Is this the queen’s work or the Vigil’s?”

Fayd finished cutting the pear.

“The queen requires information about an army. I have people who can obtain it.”

“My father used to hear men speak of the Vigil. They disagreed about which dead emperor had been betrayed.”

“Some still do. The emperors used it to watch governors, carry private instructions, discover which commander meant to stop obeying. The empire broke. Its servants found other employers and kept the name.”

“And you want it restored.”

“I want the roads and the armies to answer to something that survives a quarrel between neighbouring lords. Men here have spent generations calling that restoration while refusing to obey anyone living.”

“Would they obey the queen?”

“Some will. Others must be persuaded.”

Cedric looked at the map. The fields he knew occupied a small corner of it.

“Are you asking me to join them?”

“I am asking you to do the work I describe, for the pay we agree. Do not mistake an employment for initiation into everything your employer believes.”

Fayd pushed the bread towards him again.

“Dame Ysabeau de Corvaunt employs men who know those approaches,” he said.

“She employs a great many people.”

“Does she?”

“She has the horse that came out of Hollin.”

“Some of it. What else do you know of her?”

Cedric knew she rode for Valdais and that people who had seen her companies spoke well of their equipment. He knew a cousin of a man who had served under her. For a moment he considered making the connection closer.

“I have never met her.”

“I haven’t asked whether you have.”

“No.”

Fayd scraped a soft part of the pear into his bowl.

“She needs people who can move between the river houses without asking for a guide at each turn. She needs information about the households supplying them. And she has reason to listen to a young man badly used by the other party.”

Cedric reached for his cup. He was suddenly too warm in his coat.

“Is that what you call it?”

“What do you call it?”

There was an answer he had given often enough to know how it would sound. The toll had been a small matter. His father had a right to protect the road. A stranger had attacked him. The duke’s son had arrived already determined to find his family at fault. He could tell the whole thing without once using the word allowance.

Fayd had the restitution papers in the next room.

“My brother brought outsiders into a family matter.”

“Your brother wrote because the road was closed.”

“He could have spoken to my father.”

“He had.”

Cedric set the cup down carefully.

“Then you have your account already.”

“I have several. I need to know which part you still believe when you are angry.”

The room went quiet. From the yard came the sharp sound of iron dropped on stone and a boy’s immediate denial that anything had broken.

Cedric stood.

“If you asked me here to rehearse that affair—”

“I asked you to stay because you know a road I need understood. Sit down or leave, as you prefer. I will have the fare paid either way.”

Fayd did not rise. Cedric had his gloves in one hand. The fingers hung crookedly, and he found that he wanted them properly folded before he could decide what to do with them.

“What is the work?” he asked.

“To go where Dame Ysabeau can find a use for you. To give me accounts of the river houses she deals with. Who furnishes what they promised. Who delays. Who asks after another protection.”

“And when she asks why I left home?”

“You will have an answer.”

“You mean to manufacture one.”

“Would it be difficult?”

Cedric sat down.

He had imagined service with an army. A place in a company, a horse better than the one Edran would let him take, a commander to whom he could be useful without having everything weighed against a morning at Whiterun. He had imagined people learning his name for something he had done since.

He had not imagined writing to Fayd from another man’s camp.

“What happens to the accounts?”

“They are read.”

“By whom?”

“People who have work to do with them.”

“And if she finds out?”

Fayd drew the map closer and folded back the northern section.

“We arrange how you leave before you arrive.”

“You can promise that?”

“I can arrange it. If I promised that every person between you and this room would do exactly what we intended, you would be foolish to believe me.”

Cedric looked at the folded edge. A route disappeared beneath it.

“I would need money.”

“Yes.”

“A horse.”

“A serviceable one. Your father will notice if you suddenly own the best horse in his stable.”

“My father notices horses.”

“So I understand.”

Cedric found himself smiling. He stopped when he realized Fayd had not.

They spoke of the report. Fayd wanted names attached to observations and a distinction between what Cedric had heard and what he had seen. A message saying that the cantons were disaffected would be worthless. A particular captain refusing a summons, and the reason he had given, could be checked. If Cedric could not write safely, there would be a person to whom he could speak. That person would know a question agreed between them. Cedric was to remember the answer, not carry it.

It took longer than he expected. The bread had gone hard at the cut edge by the time Fayd drew a small sheet towards him and asked what he would need for the first stage.

“What about the money I still owe?” Cedric said.

“It remains owed.”

“If I am in the queen’s service—”

“The claimants remain out of pocket.”

“Part of my pay, then.”

“If that is how you wish to meet it.”

Cedric calculated. The first sum Fayd named would leave him with less ready money than he had expected. The second, to be paid after an accepted report, was better. He asked what made a report acceptable.

Fayd told him. They argued about travelling expenses. This time the expense belonged to work the man across the table wanted done, and Cedric could see which point he would concede.

When the terms had been copied, Fayd read them aloud. Cedric corrected an allowance for a groom: there would be no groom. Fayd crossed it out and put the money towards another stage of the journey.

“You need not decide before you leave the room,” he said.

Cedric thought of arriving at Harwick with a signed acknowledgement and the ferry fare in his pocket. Edran would ask whether the disputed claims had been resolved. His father would ask why it had taken so long. Both questions would have answers. He would spend supper giving them.

“I have decided.”

Fayd handed him the pen.

The clerk in the brown gown met him outside. He had put the return fare in a small folded paper, with the amount written across it.

“Your horse has been brought round, sir.”

Cedric took the money.

“Do you know the road to Tarlington?”

“I’ve travelled part of it.”

“The ford?”

“No, sir.”

“There is an error in your plan.”

The clerk waited.

“In Master Fayd’s plan. The drove-way and the bridge. He’ll want it corrected.”

“I’ll ask him.”

Cedric heard himself giving the explanation again and stopped. The man did not need it from him. Fayd would have somebody redraw the lines, and the next person to look at the map would find the route where Cedric knew it to be.

Outside, the boy had cleaned his stirrups. Cedric gave him a coin before mounting. It was more than he usually gave for work he had not asked to have done.

The boy thanked him and moved out of the way. Cedric rode towards the ferry, holding his left shoulder a little higher to ease the pull of his collar.

Chapter 3 — The Teams Waiting

By the time Ouen had changed his shoes, the first cart was stuck at the mill.

He learned this from a kitchen girl carrying his boots away. She said it as an explanation of the mud on the back stair, and was halfway through the door before he asked which cart she meant.

“The first one, my lord.”

“Whose?”

She did not know. She knew one of the horses had sat down, which seemed to her the more important part. Ouen went to the window. From there he could see the roofs above the mill yard and the top of a man standing on a load, waving his arms. The windows faced the wrong way for anything useful.

“Find Halden.”

“He’s down there.”

“Then have my horse brought round.”

The girl looked at the clean shoes he had just put on. He looked at them too.

“Other boots first.”

He had intended to be generous about the day’s delays. The bird had flown well and his son had accepted the wooden hawk in advance of its existence. He had even thought of telling Halden that the chair under the arch was a sensible improvement. Now he would have to go down to the mill and be shown why something apparently easy had turned out to require everybody’s opinion.

He chose the old brown boots himself.

At the gate a cart came towards him empty. Its driver was walking, leading the pair by the cheekpiece of the nearer horse. A cloth bundle hung from the cart rail.

“Where are you going?” Ouen asked.

“Home, my lord.”

“We’ve scarcely begun.”

The man stopped. He had a narrow, chapped face and the mark of a cap across his forehead. Ouen had seen him earlier, beside Halden and the broken strap.

“I was hired till evening.”

“It isn’t evening.”

The driver glanced towards the western roof. The sun had gone behind it, leaving the yard pale and cold.

“Your officer said I could go.”

“Which officer?”

“Master Halden.”

Ouen looked past him towards the mill. Three carts waited on the incline. Nobody appeared to be trying to move them.

“Wait here.”

“My lord, my wife—”

“I said wait. I shall speak to Halden.”

The driver lowered his hand from the horse’s head. Ouen went on before there could be another explanation.

At the mill the first cart stood diagonally across the gate. One wheel had sunk into a soft patch beside the stone sill. The near horse was back on its feet, with a rubbed place above the breast strap. Halden held a lantern, though there was still enough light to see. A man with his sleeves rolled up was digging beside the wheel.

“What happened?” Ouen said.

“The turn was too tight,” Halden answered.

“Then why bring it this way?”

“The other gate opens onto the mill race.”

“I know where the mill race is.”

“Yes, my lord.”

The man digging stopped to listen. Ouen disliked the shape of them all waiting together, as though he had come to give a demonstration of something.

“Take some of the load off.”

“We are doing so.”

There were six sacks on a cloth beneath the wall. Ouen had ridden past them.

He dismounted and gave the reins to a boy. The ground smelled of wet meal. Somewhere inside the mill, water struck steadily against a wooden obstruction, a hollow knocking beneath the voices.

“The driver up the road says you’ve sent him home.”

“Ernet. Yes.”

“We need the carts.”

“We need his load moved. I’ve put it on the second pair.”

“Then we are paying for an empty cart.”

“We paid for him to be here today.”

Halden lifted the lantern so that the man digging could see beneath the axle. The light showed a split in one of the braces. Ouen stepped closer. The horse tossed its head at the movement, and he put a hand against its neck.

“We should have used the upper store,” he said.

“The roof is open.”

“I know.”

Halden waited. The digging man kept at his work.

“What is wrong with his wife?” Ouen asked.

“She has a fever. Her sister was able to stay today. The sister has children of her own.”

“Everybody has something.”

“Yes.”

It had sounded different in his head. Ouen kept his hand on the horse and felt the animal lean a little against it. There was a swollen place under the mane where the collar had rubbed. He loosened a buckle without asking.

“Fetch the man,” he said.

Ernet came down on foot. He had left his team outside the gate and tied the cloth bundle to his belt.

“You’ve been paid?” Ouen asked.

“Not yet, my lord.”

“Why not?”

Halden took a purse from his coat. “I was coming back up.”

“Then pay him.”

The officer counted the coins into the driver’s hand. Ernet looked at them and left his hand open.

“That’s the day,” he said.

“Yes.”

“The extra load was to be another penny.”

“You have not taken the extra load.”

“I was waiting to.”

Ouen looked from one to the other. Halden had not closed the purse.

“The load is going on another cart,” Ouen said.

“It is now.”

“You expect payment for work another man will do?”

Ernet rubbed a thumb over the edge of one coin.

“I turned down the miller’s barley because I was to do yours. He found another man.”

The horse shifted behind Ouen, moving the traces with a dry clatter. The man digging glanced up, then bent low over the shovel again.

“How much barley?” Ouen asked.

“One load.”

“And he would have paid a penny?”

“Two. His road’s worse.”

Ouen almost smiled. The man had said it as though the difference might now be added to the account.

“Give him the penny.”

Halden did.

Ernet put the money away. “Thank you, my lord.”

“See that the harness is mended before you’re hired again.”

“It is. Your man did it while we waited.”

Ouen let that pass. He watched Ernet go, then asked what was needed to get the stuck cart clear.

“A sound brace and less weight,” Halden said. “We have both coming.”

Ouen looked at the sacks under the wall. The cloth had begun to soak through at one edge. If the grain got wet he would hear about that next.

He pulled the nearest sack farther onto the dry part. It was heavier than it looked, and he had to get both arms under it. A string caught on his sleeve. The labourer came to help and Ouen said he had it, rather too sharply.

Between them they moved the remaining sacks. By the time the replacement timber arrived his hands were dusty and his sleeve smelled of grain. Halden held the lantern while the brace was fitted. Ouen stayed by the horse, which was quieter when someone stood with a hand against its neck.

Getting the wheel up took three attempts. On the second, the men shouted at once and the horse threw its head hard enough to strike Ouen’s shoulder. He stepped back and swore. On the third they had the board beneath the wheel before the team pulled, and the cart came out with such sudden ease that the men holding the spokes had to jump aside.

The driver stopped it in the yard. Somebody laughed. Ouen found himself laughing too, partly from relief and partly because Halden, who had stepped carefully out of everyone’s way, had put his foot in the one hole the cart had left.

The officer looked down at his wet stocking.

“The grain will be in by tomorrow,” he said.

“Tonight.”

“The first loads tonight. We will need daylight to bring the rest.”

Ouen looked at the narrow entrance. The lantern’s circle no longer reached the men at the other end of the cart.

“Very well.”

He wanted to say that he had meant the first loads. It would have required another sentence, and they were moving again.

His wife had sent a bowl of hot water to their room. It stood beside a clean towel, with the nurse’s smaller bowl on the floor for their son to wash in. The child had washed one hand and put a wooden spoon into the other bowl.

“He is measuring,” Iselde said.

“What?”

“I haven’t been told.”

Ouen took off his coat. Grain dust fell from the sleeve onto the rug. Iselde looked at it.

“The cart stuck.”

“So I heard.”

“I was helping.”

“I can see.”

She took the spoon out of the water and gave it back to the boy, who immediately tried to put it in again. Ouen caught his wrist gently.

“That is for my hands.”

“Mine too.”

“You have your own.”

“This one’s warm.”

Ouen tested the smaller bowl. It was cold. He poured some of his water into it and let the boy put the spoon where he liked.

Iselde sat by the fire with a piece of torn linen across her knee. She had been taking out a seam. There were tiny white threads clinging to her dark skirt.

“You could ask somebody to do that,” he said.

“I want the cloth for his glove.”

“The glove is leather.”

“The lining isn’t.”

He washed his hands. There was a red patch at the base of one finger where the sack had caught it.

“The carpenter will make the bird,” he said.

“He came to ask which wood.”

“Does he need me for that as well?”

“He needed to know whether you meant one to stand on a perch or one Hew could carry. I told him to make it light.”

Their son looked up at his name and held out the wet spoon. Ouen took it.

“I was going to tell him.”

“I know.”

Iselde bent to the seam again. He knew she did not mean it as Halden might have meant it, but he found himself listening for the same patience and became irritated with her for making him look for it.

“The men couldn’t get the cart through,” he said.

“Did you?”

“We did.”

“Good.”

She had given him the answer he wanted. It was unsatisfactory chiefly because she returned to the linen before he had described the wheel.

A servant knocked with word that a rider had come from the army. There was a horse to be looked at and a packet for the steward. Ouen dried his hands on the towel and asked where they had put the animal.

“The lower stable, my lord.”

He went before anyone could suggest sending for the horse-master instead.

The mare stood with one hind leg resting on the toe. She was a sound-looking chestnut, though her coat had dried badly after the last stage and there was a sour smell beneath the stable’s usual warmth. The rider held her head while an old groom cut away a crust of dirt near the girth.

“Stop,” Ouen said.

The groom lowered the knife.

“Wet it first.”

“We have water coming.”

“Then wait for it.”

He laid a hand on the mare’s shoulder and moved slowly along her side. She turned her head, showing the white of an eye. He stopped short of the wound.

“How far today?”

The rider named two places and a bridge. Ouen knew the distance between the first two. It was enough without the rest.

“Why did you put a saddle over this?”

“I had a pad under it.”

“That’s what has stuck.”

“I changed it.”

“When?”

“At the bridge.”

Ouen looked at him. The man was young, with the dry skin about the eyes of someone who had spent several days moving in cold weather. His coat had a tear sewn up with dark cord. He kept looking towards the mare’s flank as though the wound might have improved while they spoke.

“The instruction says saddle gall,” the groom said.

He handed Ouen the folded slip. There was a list of animals, three names crossed out, and a short statement about the chestnut. Serviceable after rest.

Ouen gave it back.

“Bring more light.”

With warm water they got the pad away. The sore extended beyond the place he had first felt, raw beneath a line of dried discharge. The mare’s skin trembled when the groom touched it. Ouen told him to stop again, then sent for the man who treated their own horses.

The rider had taken his cap off. Ouen asked his name.

“Joss, my lord.”

“Were you at Hollin?”

“Yes.”

“On her?”

“For the last part.”

“What happened to yours?”

Joss looked at the straw.

“Lost him at the brook.”

“You couldn’t recover him?”

“No.”

Ouen waited. He had intended to ask which side of the brook, but Joss began speaking before he could.

“He went over with me in the water. I got my foot out. The others were coming back and I couldn’t get round his head. There was a man pulling at the rein. I don’t know whether he was ours.”

He stopped. The mare moved her foot and set it down, then lifted it again.

“This one was loose,” Joss said. “Somebody caught her. I took a man up behind me.”

“All the way?”

“To where the road rose. He couldn’t hold on after that.”

Ouen looked at the sore beneath the girth. He could see where the weight of two men would have shifted a saddle already fitted to somebody else.

“What happened to him?”

“They had a cart.”

“You put him on it?”

“Yes.”

“What was his name?”

“I didn’t ask.”

It was a stupid omission. Ouen nearly said so. The young man was watching the mare again, and Ouen remembered the account of a bridge where somebody had been cut off and his father’s assurance that most of the horse had come away in good order. He had read that passage twice, trying to decide what it required him to tell the men at home.

“Did you see my father?”

“On the road, afterwards.”

“How did he look?”

Joss considered the question with care.

“He was riding.”

“Yes, I assumed—”

“He spoke to us. Told us which way. Men were going into the wrong field because the hedge was down.”

“Was he hurt?”

“I don’t think so, my lord.”

The horse-master arrived with a basket. Ouen gave him room and told Joss to find the kitchen when the mare was settled. He would need a fresh horse for any onward journey. Nobody was to put a saddle on this one until the wound had closed.

“We have the order for her return,” the groom said.

“Show it to me tomorrow.”

The rider thanked him. Ouen left while the warm cloth was being held against the mare’s side.

Upstairs, Hew had fallen asleep across Iselde’s lap. The unfinished lining lay on the table. Ouen sat at the small desk and asked for paper.

He began with the horses. Forty would be difficult without taking animals already promised elsewhere. The fodder was being put under cover. He described the chestnut’s condition and asked that future returning horses be examined before they were sent on.

Then he wrote: How many men came back on horses that were not theirs?

He read the sentence and almost took another sheet. His father might think it an impertinence. He might ask why Ouen wanted to know, and Ouen would have to explain the mare and the rider and the man on the cart whose name nobody had asked.

He folded it with the question still there.

Chapter 4 — Leaving Harwick

Cedric found his brother in the room above the wharf, eating with the man who had refused him credit for a saddle.

The saddler rose when he came in. Edran did not. There was a plate between them with the rind of a cheese laid along its edge, and a little heap of metal fittings on a cloth. The window had been propped open despite the cold. Below it, somebody was calling numbers to men unloading a boat.

“I need to speak to you,” Cedric said.

“About what?”

“In private.”

Edran looked at the saddler.

“Give us a moment, if you please.”

The man gathered the fittings into the cloth. Cedric waited for him to close the door before taking the chair. Its seat was still warm.

“Are you settling his bill?”

“Some of it.”

“He sent mine back.”

“Yours is for a new saddle.”

“Mine is split.”

“The seat needs repairing. He has offered to do it.”

Cedric put his gloves on the table. He had folded them on the stairs, where he could not be watched doing it.

“I’ll need the grey.”

Edran stopped cutting the bread.

“For how long?”

“Through winter.”

“What for?”

“I’m taking service.”

“With whom?”

The room had seemed too small from the moment he entered. Now Edran’s questions appeared to be reducing it further, each one closing a possible way through the conversation. Cedric could remember coming here when the room had been used for fishing nets, before his brother had moved a desk and a locked cupboard into it. There had been somewhere to sit then without being made to account for it.

“Do you require a name before you’ll let me use a horse?”

“I require a name before I send Father’s horse away for a season.”

“It was bought for me.”

“It was bought for the household. You were permitted to ride it.”

“By my father.”

“Yes.”

“Then I shall ask him.”

Edran put down the knife.

“Cedric.”

He had got halfway out of the chair. He stayed there, one hand on the table, waiting for the voice Edran used when he wanted to sound like the older brother rather than merely be him.

“If there is a position, let us hear what it is. You might need more than a horse.”

“You would like to choose it.”

“I would like to know whether the pay exists.”

“Some people will pay me.”

“I didn’t say otherwise.”

“You have said it often enough.”

Edran stood and closed the window. Below them the counting continued. He pushed the latch home before turning back.

“Where are you going?”

“To the south.”

“That covers several choices.”

“Then you can write to all of them.”

The moment after saying it, Cedric wanted the words back in order to say them better. Edran had gone still. The insult was plain and clumsy, too close to the accusation he had made before the duke’s men arrived and found the whole house waiting to explain him.

His brother took the cloth napkin off the back of the chair and folded it beside the plate.

“I won’t send the grey,” he said. “He is needed here.”

“Needed by you.”

“By Father, when he can sit him again.”

They both knew the answer to that. Their father had needed two men to get him down after the last attempt. He had said the saddle was wrong. The same saddle had fitted him for four years.

Cedric could have left the room. The quarrel was already enough. A dissatisfied younger son denied a horse, his pay restricted, his prospects examined by an elder brother who had obtained a prince’s intervention against him: he could have told that account without adding anything.

“He isn’t going to sit him again,” he said.

Edran looked towards the door, although it was shut.

“You can take the bay gelding. Ask the stable to show you which.”

“I know which. The old one.”

“He’s sound.”

“He’s fourteen.”

“And sound. Leave the grey.”

Cedric picked up his gloves and caught one of the fingers beneath the plate. The cheese rind fell onto the floor. Neither man bent for it.

His father was having his boots pulled on.

The servant knelt with one knee on a cushion and supported the heel while Lorran pushed. The baron gripped the arms of his chair. His face had gone red with the effort. Cedric stood by the door and looked at a hunting scene on the wall until the boot was on.

“The other one,” Lorran said.

The servant changed sides.

“I need a horse,” Cedric began.

“Have you lost yours?”

“No.”

“Then you don’t need a horse.”

“For service.”

His father looked up. “Whose service?”

“Nothing is settled.”

“Then settle it. A gentleman doesn’t arrive at another man’s gate and ask to be given something to do.”

Cedric felt his collar against the scar. He moved a finger beneath the edge.

“There are men taking service in the cantons.”

“There are men taking anybody who brings a horse.”

“I know the roads.”

“You know some roads.”

The servant eased the second boot over Lorran’s heel. His father let out a breath and leaned back. The room smelled of the ointment they put on his knees. On the table stood a cup gone cold beneath a skin of fat; somebody had been trying to make him drink broth between meals.

“Sit down,” Lorran said.

Cedric remained standing.

“I’ve asked Edran for the grey.”

“And he said?”

“He has decided that I should take the old bay.”

His father considered this.

“The bay will travel.”

“The grey was bought—”

“The grey will fret on a long road. You will spend your first week’s pay replacing shoes.”

“He has never thrown a shoe with me.”

“Because you come home at night.”

The servant took away the cushion. Lorran watched him leave, then moved the broth farther from his elbow.

“Who has asked you?”

“Nobody has asked.”

“Then what has put this into your head?”

Cedric could give him none of the answer he wanted to give. There was a man who had listened while he explained a crossing. There was a plan being redrawn because Cedric knew something the draughtsman did not. There would be money for the use of that knowledge. Fayd had made the matter sound ordinary enough to undertake, and now Cedric stood in his father’s room appearing to have conceived an aimless journey because he could not bear being at home.

“Is there anything for me here?” he asked.

“You have duties.”

“What duties?”

“You know them.”

“I carry the papers to the hearing. I sign where they tell me. Edran takes the rents. You meet the tenants. Berent has gone to a prince’s household, and I am asked whether the ferry fare may be taken from my allowance.”

“Berent did the work given him.”

“So you sent him away.”

“He was offered a place.”

“With the man who came to dictate terms to you.”

Lorran’s hand tightened on the arm of the chair.

“I heard the terms,” he said. “I agreed to them.”

“You had very little choice.”

“Enough.”

Cedric stopped. His father had used the word quietly, which was worse. The window behind him gave a view of the upper yard, and a groom passed leading the grey towards the far stable. The horse moved as though it had all the time in the world.

“Ask for a place properly,” Lorran said. “If the commander is a man I know, I will write.”

“And if you do not know him?”

“Then I will find somebody who does.”

It was an offer. Cedric could see that, even while the prospect of being sent anywhere under another letter about his conduct made his chest feel tight.

“I mean to arrange it myself.”

His father looked at him for a long moment.

“You can take the bay,” he said at last. “Have the feet seen to. The off fore has been giving trouble.”

“Edran says he’s sound.”

“He is sound. He needs the right shoe.”

Cedric went out with permission and nothing that felt like having won.

Packing took most of the following morning.

He had not known how many of the things in his room belonged to the household until he tried to take them with him. The good cloak was his. The second blanket was marked for guests. The little lamp had been carried up from a cupboard and could be replaced, but the housekeeper objected to losing the oil flask that fitted it. A saddlebag had a rotten strap. His sword belt needed another hole if he wore it over the coat he had chosen.

He sent for the young groom who sometimes rode with him. The boy listened to the plan with the intent expression of someone trying to determine which part was a request.

“I was to stay for the lambing,” he said.

“You work in the stable.”

“My father doesn’t. I help when I go home.”

“There will be pay.”

“How much?”

Cedric named a sum smaller than he had thought of offering. The boy did not seem to notice the generosity it represented.

“For how long, sir?”

“I don’t know yet.”

“I’d have to tell him.”

“You can tell him you have a better place.”

The boy glanced at the open bags on the bed.

“May I speak to him first?”

Cedric almost said yes. Then he thought of the agreed contact, the question he was to remember, and the report he must make without anybody in his service becoming curious about his letters.

“Leave it,” he said. “I’ll take somebody on the road.”

The boy looked relieved and tried to conceal it by asking whether the bay was to be saddled now. Cedric told him to wait until after dinner.

He ate alone. Edran had gone down to the wharf, and his father had sent word that he was resting. The room had been laid for several people, with the cups already out. Cedric could hear somebody moving plates in the kitchen and wished that whoever it was would come in and ask him a question.

No one did. He cut meat, found that he was hungry, and ate more than he had intended.

When he rose, the housekeeper came with the smaller flask.

“This one will fit the bag better,” she said.

He took it.

“Thank you.”

“The stopper’s loose. Keep it upright.”

She went back to the kitchen before he could ask whether his father wanted him to come up.

Edran was in the stable yard when the bay was brought out.

The saddle had been repaired. The new stitching was darker than the old leather, and Cedric resented how sound it looked. His brother stood on the far side of the horse, holding a folded cloth.

“Spare stockings,” he said.

“I have stockings.”

“Dry ones.”

“I know what spare stockings are.”

Edran laid them over the saddle. Cedric put them into a bag. The groom checked the girth, making an unnecessary business of passing the strap back through the keeper.

“How far today?” Edran asked.

“To the lower road.”

“Which lodging?”

“I haven’t chosen.”

“Then avoid the first house after the turning. The roof over the stable fell in. They are still taking money for it.”

“I can look at a roof.”

“Yes.”

Cedric put his foot in the stirrup and mounted. From above, his brother looked tired. There was a cut at the side of his thumb where the nail had split. It was wrapped in a strip of cloth already dirty at the edge.

He should have said something about the stockings.

Instead he said, “You can tell Father that I left peaceably.”

Edran stepped away from the horse.

“You can write to him yourself.”

The groom opened the gate. Cedric rode out without turning his head, though he listened for a call until the noise of the wharf covered anything that might have come from the yard.

He stopped at the lower turning and tightened the saddlebag straps. The bay stood patiently. It was a better travelling horse than he had admitted. He rubbed the mane once, then took the road south.

The contact kept a shop that sold cord, lamp wicks, and the sort of wooden bowl that cracked if left too close to a fire.

Cedric arrived two days after leaving Harwick, with wet stockings rolled in a cloth behind the saddle. He had not taken the first lodging. The second had charged him more and given him a room next to a child with a cough. By morning he had been angry with Edran again, though he could not have said how his brother was responsible.

The woman in the shop had a box of buttons open on the counter. She made him wait while she counted six into another woman’s palm and received two coins in return. Then she asked what he wanted.

“Cord.”

“What thickness?”

He looked at the coils hanging from the beam. Fayd had said to ask about cord. There had been no thickness in the instructions.

“For a baggage strap.”

“You want leather.”

“Cord will do.”

She cut him a length and named the price. He paid, then gave the question he had been told to use, in a voice which sounded too low to him.

The woman pushed the cord across.

“My nephew will show you the horse trough.”

“I have watered him.”

“Then he won’t need to drink.”

A boy came from the back carrying an empty bucket. Cedric followed him into a yard where a brown hen was trying to get onto a pile of split wood. There was no trough, only a basin beneath a spout.

The boy put the bucket down and went indoors. The woman came out by a second door a little later, with a cloth over her shoulders.

“You’re early,” she said.

“The road was clear.”

“It won’t be farther on.”

She told him where the water had risen and which ferry was still working. He asked whether she knew the people receiving him.

“I know where you’re to go.”

“And if I have to come back?”

“Come back.”

“To here?”

“To the shop.”

It was disappointingly ordinary. He had expected some assurance, a person with a horse waiting, proof that the route Fayd had described extended beyond the next stage. The woman looked towards the back door.

“I have customers.”

“How will you know it’s me if the shop is shut?”

“I have seen you.”

“And if you aren’t here?”

“My sister has your description.”

Cedric touched the side of his face before he could stop himself. Her eyes followed the movement.

“The bay gelding,” she said. “The coat. Your name. I can describe a man without being rude about him.”

He lowered his hand.

She gave him a short account of the next lodging, its owner, and the fee already paid for one night. He repeated the name back. This appeared to satisfy her more than his earlier questions had.

At the gate she stopped him.

“Use the cord. If somebody asks why you came in, you’ll have bought something you needed.”

He tied the wet stockings more securely to the bag before he left. The hen had got onto the woodpile and was standing there, looking down at the yard.

Chapter 5 — What His Sister Asks

Vaelrie had drawn a bird beside the date.

It was a poor bird. The head was too large, the feet belonged to something that scratched for grain, and a little crown had been placed between its ears. Ouen looked at it for some time before deciding that the things on the head were meant to be feathers.

To my brother, who has lately become too occupied to tell me whether his son has learned to spit farther than his own shoes.

If you have taught him, you must tell Iselde that it was your idea. I will not have another letter saying that the habit began with something I wrote. I merely asked.

Ouen looked across the room. Iselde was fitting the glove to Hew’s hand. The boy had spread his fingers as wide as he could and was refusing to close them.

“Paget asks after him,” Ouen said.

“Does she?”

“About the spitting.”

“Tell her he is improving.”

“Is he?”

“He hit my shoe yesterday.”

Hew looked pleased. Ouen read on.

I have been told about your new hawk by somebody who has never seen her, which seems a long way round when I know a man who spends most of his time holding her. Does she still pull the glove when you put the hood on? Tell me something about her besides the price. The price has already been admired sufficiently.

Braymond says Father promised him a horse. Father tells me this was a promise to consider a horse, which appears to satisfy Father. It has not satisfied Braymond. Cullan has made a list of the horses he thinks unsuitable, beginning with every horse Braymond likes. Taliya says that if we are to spend the winter arguing about an animal which does not exist, she would prefer us to choose a quieter one.

He laughed. Iselde looked up from the glove.

“Read it.”

He did, giving Cullan’s imagined objections more gravity than they deserved. Hew listened solemnly until the absent horse failed to do anything interesting, then began pulling at the new glove’s fastening with his teeth.

“Don’t,” Iselde said, and took his hand.

There was more about the household. Vaelrie had remembered a game in which Ouen persuaded her to deliver a letter to their father without telling her it was a demand for the return of something he had confiscated. She had been rewarded for the delivery, and he had had to watch her spend the reward. She still considered this an example of sound judgement on her part.

Then she asked about Taliya’s stores.

I have copied the numbers at the bottom because you will otherwise tell me that the figure looked like another figure. Six lengths of the coarse wool. Two of the closer-woven cloth if they can be spared. Taliya has women still lodging with her whose things were sent by a different road and have not arrived. One of them has begun borrowing from another who was borrowing already, and they are both pretending to find this amusing.

She asked about the cloth before Father asked about the horses. Please do not explain the horses to her as though she has never seen one.

Ouen lowered the letter.

“She makes everything a joke.”

“Who needs cloth?” Iselde asked.

“Taliya’s people.”

“Which people?”

“It doesn’t say.”

It named one of them on the next line. He read it silently. A woman who had been with Taliya for years, whose husband had been sent with one of the household consignments and had not returned. There was no suggestion that he was dead. The consignment had been delayed, and everyone apparently knew where except the woman waiting for him.

Send it with someone who will remain until it is received. Last time the carrier left the bales in a shed because a man at the gate said the shed belonged to the household. It did. It was also the wrong household. Taliya got most of them back.

At the bottom of the page, the figures were set out very clearly.

“She writes as though I am likely to keep it for myself,” he said.

Iselde held the glove away from Hew while she tied off a thread.

“Will you?”

“No.”

“Then that part should be easy.”

He turned the page.

There were two lengths of the close-woven cloth in the upper cupboard and four in the lower store. Halden knew this without going to look, which made the question of why none had already been sent more trying than it should have been.

“The four below are allotted,” he said.

“To whom?”

“The infirmary at the eastern lodge.”

“Taliya asked first.”

“The order for the lodge came before her second message.”

“There was a first message.”

“There was. It asked what we had.”

“She would hardly ask what we had unless she wanted some.”

Halden put the inventory on the table. Beside the entry for the cloth he had written a date. Ouen considered reading it and decided it would merely give Halden something else to explain.

“Send the two upstairs, then.”

“Those belong to your wife’s household.”

Ouen looked at Iselde, who had come into the room with the child because Hew wanted to watch the fire being made. She had not taken a chair. The boy stood beside her with his new glove on and a wooden spoon balanced across it, pretending it was a bird.

“You can spare them.”

“What is being sent?” she asked.

“The cloth Paget mentioned.”

“All of it?”

“The better cloth. There is coarse wool below.”

“I meant whether we are sending the rest of what Taliya asked for.”

“The six lengths, yes.”

“And a person to hand it over.”

Ouen remembered the sentence about the carrier. Halden had looked up.

“I will have it delivered,” Ouen said.

“By whom?”

“Whoever goes that way.”

The spoon fell from Hew’s glove. The boy picked it up and began again. Ouen wished somebody would take him outside.

“If we wait for a carrier already going,” Halden said, “I can send it at the end of the week. His next destination is beyond their lodging. He won’t stay.”

“Why must he stay?”

“To obtain an acknowledgement from Taliya’s officer.”

“Get one from the man who receives it.”

“Last time—”

“I’ve heard about last time.”

Iselde put out a hand to steady the spoon.

“Is there nobody we can send?” she asked.

“There is Perrin,” Halden said. “But his wife is due. He asked to remain within reach.”

“Then another man,” Ouen said.

“Joss could go once his horse is fit.”

“His horse won’t be fit for weeks.”

“I understood you had offered a fresh one for his onward journey.”

Ouen had. He remembered standing beside the mare and issuing the instruction with the satisfaction of knowing exactly what was necessary. Now it appeared that he had also been assigning animals to errands he had not heard of.

“He has come from the army. Give him a few days.”

“Yes, my lord.”

“And send the cloth with the carrier.”

Halden made a mark.

Iselde waited until he had left before speaking.

“You didn’t tell him what she asked.”

“He knows what she asked.”

“You told him the number of lengths.”

“Must I read every letter aloud?”

“No. You could have handed it to him.”

He picked the letter up, then put it down again. The drawing looked at him from the corner. He had begun to see a resemblance to the way he held his head when someone was taking too long with an explanation, which was probably what his sister had intended.

“I’ll write to Taliya,” he said.

“Good.”

“Tell her it is coming.”

“Tell her how.”

Hew managed three steps with the spoon balanced on the glove. Both parents watched. On the fourth he lifted his arm to show them, and the spoon struck the hearthstone.

“It flew,” he said.

The household cloth was in a chest whose key Iselde kept with her other keys, on a ring too large to fit comfortably in her pocket. Ouen followed her upstairs because he had decided to look at what they were sending. She opened the chest and stood aside.

The top length was pale brown, folded about a piece of smooth wood. Beneath it lay darker cloth, finer to the touch than he had expected. He pinched an edge between finger and thumb.

“This is good.”

“It is.”

“What were you going to use it for?”

“A coat for Hew. A lining for mine.”

He let go of the edge.

“There must be something else.”

“You have allotted the other cloth.”

“For the sick.”

“Yes.”

“You make it sound as though I have been careless.”

Iselde knelt beside the chest and pulled the pale length free. It caught beneath the darker one. She had to put a hand under both and lift them together.

“Hold this.”

He took the wood with the cloth folded over it. It was light compared with the grain sacks. She unrolled enough to examine a flaw at the edge, then folded it back.

“This length is sound,” she said. “The other has a thin place. Taliya should be told before she cuts it.”

“Do you want me to recall the order?”

“No.”

“Then I don’t see why—”

“Because I was going to cut them tomorrow. Now I need to find something else. If you ask me first, I can have done that before the cloth goes.”

He stood holding the pale wool while she found wrapping cloth. Through the window he could hear Hew in the courtyard asking somebody whether a bird could fly without feathers. The answer was too low to hear.

“You can use the fur from my old coat,” Ouen said.

“For his?”

“Yes.”

“It is very heavy.”

“Part of it.”

She considered this.

“Part might do.”

He felt better immediately and knew enough about her expression not to ask whether that settled it.

They wrapped the lengths. Iselde cut a strip from the damaged edge and fastened it outside the package so that whoever opened it would look for the flaw. Ouen carried the bundle downstairs himself. Halden had already obtained six lengths of coarse wool and a carrier willing to take them at the end of the week.

“Put these with them,” Ouen said.

“Does your wife want an acknowledgement?”

“Yes.”

“From the carrier, or the receiving household?”

Ouen looked at the wrapped cloth in his arms.

“From Taliya’s officer. Have Joss take them when he’s rested.”

“He will need the fresh horse.”

“Give him one that can come back.”

Halden took the bundle. “They can all come back, my lord.”

“Then make certain he knows it.”

He wrote to Vaelrie after supper.

The drawing tempted him to begin with an insult to her knowledge of birds. He made it halfway through the sentence before realizing that she would enjoy this too much, and crossed it out. The paper was good; he did not take another sheet.

He told her Hew had a glove, that the wooden hawk was being made, and that his new bird no longer pulled against the hood when she knew his hand. This last detail occupied more space than he had intended. He described the flight beyond the willows, including the distance he had had to walk through the wet ground. He left out the carts.

He had not read the last part of her letter properly. She had changed ink and squeezed a line into the margin.

There is a woman here called Maelis who serves Mab and says she has been invited to stay. When she asked whether that included being paid for the winter entertainments, Jaennis laughed and asked her to sit down. I could not hear the rest because somebody wanted the chair I was standing behind. Maelis has a cricket. I thought at first that it was in the wall, and complained about it. She asked whether I always began an acquaintance by discussing the removal of the other party’s lodging.

I like her. I should like her better if she had let me finish the complaint.

Ouen smiled and read the passage to Iselde. She asked whether the cricket had a name.

“Apparently she didn’t ask.”

“Then ask her. Hew will want to know.”

His sister wrote of the company as though he knew the rooms in which the women met. He could imagine her standing behind a chair to hear a conversation nobody had forbidden her to hear, growing indignant when the chair was needed. At home she had done it at their father’s table. He wondered whether Jaennis had noticed the same habit and whether she liked it.

He added a question about the cricket. Below it he wrote that Taliya’s cloth was arranged and a rider would remain until it was properly received. The statement was true. It did not mention Iselde or the several stages by which he had arrived at the arrangement.

Before folding the sheet he looked at the crossed-out opening.

Your bird has ears, he wrote beneath it. I assume this was intentional.

He put the letter aside to dry and went upstairs to ask Iselde where she had put his old coat.

Chapter 6 — The Men Without Horses

The guard at the yard gate took Cedric’s sword and told him to wait beside the empty trough.

“Dame Ysabeau is expecting me.”

“She is expecting several people.”

“I have a letter.”

“The man carrying it has gone inside.”

The guard hung the sword under the roof of his little shelter. There were three others there, two with plain grips and one with a red tassel that had become tangled in the buckle of its belt. Cedric watched him put his among them.

“Be careful of the scabbard.”

The guard adjusted it by half an inch.

Cedric led the bay to the trough. There was a skin of ice in the bottom and no water beneath it. The horse nosed the rim, then looked towards a bucket being carried across the yard. Cedric called to the boy carrying it.

“Where do I water him?”

“Other side, sir.”

“Of what?”

The boy pointed with his chin towards a stable, keeping both hands on the bucket. Cedric moved that way, then stopped because the guard had told him to wait. By the time he decided it could hardly matter which trough he stood beside, the boy had disappeared into a doorway.

A woman came out carrying his letter. She wore a dark coat with one cuff turned back and a narrow leather belt from which a knife and a small key hung together. Her hair was held close to her head. Cedric had seen more elaborate preparations for a morning inspecting tenants.

“Master Aeryl?”

He straightened. “Dame Ysabeau.”

“Your horse needs water.”

“I was told to wait here.”

She looked at the guard. “Give the lad his rein. We can speak inside.”

Cedric surrendered the bay to a boy who had appeared from nowhere with a bucket that was apparently permitted to go where it was needed. He followed Ysabeau across the yard.

A large horse in the open stable leaned its head out as they passed. She put a hand against its nose without slowing.

“Later, Bastian.”

The horse reached after her sleeve. She moved her arm out of range and led Cedric through a low door into a room where four men were bent over a table. One of them had a bandaged hand laid flat beside a cup. He looked at Cedric’s face, then at the letter Ysabeau was carrying.

She sent the men out with two short instructions and asked Cedric to shut the door.

“You have left Harwick.”

“Yes.”

“With your father’s permission?”

“He knows where I am going.”

“Does he know you are here?”

Cedric hesitated.

“I will write.”

“Do so. I have no use for a dispute over whether I have abducted a baron’s son.”

“He won’t claim that.”

“I would prefer to know.”

She sat at the end of the table. The chair opposite had papers stacked on it. Cedric waited until she moved them.

“The man who recommended that you come has known your family some years,” she said. “He speaks well of your father. He has very little to say about you.”

“I have not had the opportunity—”

“He says you ride well and know the Viterran approaches. Both could be useful.”

Cedric sat down. The room had been a kitchen once. He could see where a shelf had been taken off the wall and the place above the old hearth where pots had left a dark band.

“What happened at Whiterun?” she asked.

He had prepared for this. He gave an account sufficiently close to the record that a man checking it could have found disagreements of emphasis rather than fact. His father’s rights had been disputed. There had been violence. The duke’s son had intervened. He had accepted satisfaction under terms witnessed by both households.

“You omit the toll,” she said.

“I was coming to it.”

“Come to it.”

He described the levy. She asked who had authorized it, how much had been collected, and whether the restitution was complete. He felt himself growing hot in the coat and wished he had taken it off before sitting.

“My allowance is assigned to it.”

“So you need pay.”

“Yes.”

“I can pay you for work. I will not undertake your old debts.”

“I haven’t asked you to.”

“No. You have asked to serve.”

She put the letter on top of the papers from the chair.

“Your brother has not written for you.”

“My brother is well occupied with his own affairs.”

“I expect he is.”

Ysabeau pushed a cup towards him and poured from a jug. It was water, warmer than he wanted but clean. He drank enough to give himself time before she asked another question.

Instead she took a scrap of paper from beneath the jug.

“Where would you turn off the road above Tarlington Ford if you were going to the eastern grazing?”

He told her.

“And with wagons?”

He gave the longer route.

“Why?”

“The ground between the two banks won’t bear a loaded axle after rain.”

She nodded. He could not tell whether she had known the answer or written down somebody else’s.

“You may remain for a week. Captain Rovel will give me an account of your work. If you find the work beneath you, tell him before you abandon it.”

“I don’t abandon duties.”

“Then he will have nothing to report under that heading.”

She rose, and he rose with her. The interview had ended before he had asked about rank.

At the door she said, “Write to your father before supper. Have you eaten?”

“Yes.”

It was nearly true. He had eaten at dawn.

“Good. Rovel has been waiting since then.”

Captain Rovel’s party had eleven men, seven saddles, and three horses.

The figures took some time to establish because Cedric initially counted a mule tied at the end of the line, and one of the saddles belonged to a man who had been sent elsewhere with somebody else’s horse. Rovel let him finish counting before explaining this.

“We are to have two more men at the lower lodging,” he said. “If you prefer a larger problem.”

Cedric looked at the men. They had made themselves comfortable under the eaves of a barn, with cloaks laid on straw and a little fire in an iron basket that appeared to have been borrowed from a kitchen. One man was shaving another with a knife. The man being shaved had his eyes shut and held a cloth beneath his chin.

“They’re ready to move?”

“They’ve been ready since yesterday.”

“Then we’ll get the rest of the horses.”

“From where?”

“The order says mounted escort.”

“I read it.”

Rovel was broad through the shoulders, with a grey patch in his beard and a thumbnail missing from one hand. He stood as though one boot was less comfortable than the other. Cedric tried to determine whether this was insolence or an old injury and decided both were possible.

“Who has the animals?”

“The household troop that passed yesterday. Their officer carried a prior claim.”

“Then why were these men promised them?”

Rovel looked towards the barn.

“Perhaps you could ask the man who promised.”

“I have the order to take you to the next quarters.”

“And we are waiting to go.”

Cedric approached the men under the eaves.

“Pack your things.”

The shaving stopped. The seated man opened one eye.

“How far, sir?” somebody asked.

“To the lower lodging.”

“On foot?”

“Until we have the horses.”

One of the men got up and began folding his cloak. Another made no movement. He had a leg stretched out in front of him, wrapped from ankle to knee in an old piece of blanket. Cedric looked at it, then back towards Rovel.

“Can he ride?”

“He can sit.”

“Put him on one of the three.”

“What do you propose for Hannet?”

Rovel indicated a smaller man sitting beyond the fire. His right arm was bound across his chest beneath the coat. Cedric had taken the position for an attempt to keep warm.

“Another horse.”

“And the third goes to the man who owns it,” Rovel said. “He was hired with it. If he walks, he still expects the mounted rate.”

“He can carry something.”

“What?”

“Whatever needs carrying.”

Rovel’s face changed a little. It was the first expression Cedric could have called amusement.

“Then we are making progress.”

They had not left by noon.

There were seven saddles to be carried, grain for animals they no longer possessed, two locked boxes belonging to the quartermaster, and three men’s equipment left in the barn while the men were elsewhere. The barn’s owner wanted the space cleared because a load of hay was expected. The mule belonged to his sister.

Cedric sent one man to find the quartermaster and another to ask about a cart. The first returned to say that the quartermaster had moved with the household troop. The second came back with a price and a requirement that the cart be returned by morning.

“We’ll pay it,” Cedric said.

Rovel took a piece of paper from his coat.

“Write it down.”

“The price?”

“And who pays.”

“The army.”

“Which officer’s account?”

Cedric looked at the paper. He had been given authority to escort men between lodgings. There was nothing on the order about hiring carts.

“Dame Ysabeau’s.”

“Then ask her.”

“She is occupied.”

“So is the carter.”

The man who had fetched the price stood waiting. Cedric could see that he would be amused by a further delay, and the knowledge made him give the next instruction too quickly.

“I’ll answer for it.”

“Put that down, then.”

Cedric took the pen. Before he touched it to the paper, the man with the bound arm spoke from behind him.

“Does it have boards?”

“What?”

“The cart. Is it boarded or just the frame?”

“I didn’t ask,” said the messenger.

Hannet turned his head away. The movement pulled his injured shoulder and he took a breath through his teeth.

“Ask,” Cedric said.

The man went out again. Rovel watched him go.

“Have somebody finish shaving him,” Cedric said, pointing towards the man who still had half a beard.

Rovel looked at him in surprise. Then he called to the man with the knife.

It was the first instruction Cedric had given that anybody appeared glad to carry out.

Ysabeau found him at the yard gate, trying to persuade the barn’s owner that two boxes could remain in a corner until somebody collected them.

The owner had explained three times that he had been paid for the use of the entire barn and expected it returned in the same form. Cedric had explained, with increasing emphasis, that two boxes did not occupy the entire barn. The man had now stopped explaining and was simply shaking his head.

“What are we waiting for?” Ysabeau asked.

“The horses were taken,” Cedric said.

“I know.”

“I was given a mounted escort.”

“You were given men to take to their quarters.”

“The order calls them mounted.”

She held out her hand for it. He gave it to her.

She read it, looked towards the barn, and folded it once along the existing crease.

“The men who wrote this expected the animals to be here. They are gone. What have you arranged?”

He told her about the cart, the three horses, the boxes, and the absent quartermaster. Halfway through, she asked for Rovel. He came out with a saddle on one shoulder and set it down when she called him.

“Can the two hurt men make the journey?”

“Hannet on a horse if someone leads it. The other in a cart with straw. He won’t bear a saddle for that long.”

“Why haven’t you gone?” she asked Cedric.

“The cart has no boards.”

“Can boards be fitted?”

“The owner wants to know who will pay for them.”

“Will he take them back as part of the cart afterwards?”

Cedric had not asked.

Ysabeau looked at the barn’s owner.

“Are these quartermaster’s boxes yours to move?”

“No, my lady.”

“Will you store them for three days if I pay for the corner?”

“Yes.”

Cedric stared at him. The man had been refusing the identical arrangement for a quarter of an hour.

Ysabeau looked at Cedric.

“Had you offered to pay?”

“He has already been paid for the barn.”

“Until today.”

The owner nodded. Cedric wanted to take him by the collar.

Ysabeau gave him back the order.

“Agree the storage. Have the boards fitted if the hire can be settled. Bring me the cost before you commit it. Rovel will stay with you until the men are lodged.”

“I understood I was to be in command.”

“You are to get them there.”

She turned to Rovel and asked about his foot. He said it was much the same.

“Let someone else lead Hannet’s horse,” she said.

Then she went back across the yard, leaving Cedric with the owner, the boxes, and an instruction sufficiently plain that he could find nothing in it to argue about.

They left an hour later.

The carter was prepared to keep the boards if they were cut to the right length, but he wanted somebody to cut them. Cedric paid the carpenter from the travelling money in his purse and obtained a written acknowledgement. Ysabeau approved the total after striking out the proposed hire of a second mule; the remaining saddles could be divided between the cart and the animals they had.

Cedric gave his bay to Hannet and walked beside it for the first mile, holding the leading rein. The injured man kept apologizing whenever the horse stepped awkwardly.

“It’s a horse,” Cedric told him. “You aren’t making it stumble.”

“I know, sir.”

The bay turned its head to investigate his sleeve. Cedric pushed the nose away.

“Does the coat hurt?”

“A little.”

“Why wear it?”

“Cold.”

He could have worked that out. He took a different hold on the rein and watched the road.

Behind them, the cart creaked beneath the saddles and the man whose leg had been wrapped in blanket. Rovel walked with the rest, limping more openly now. Their conversation had resumed. One man told a story Cedric had heard at Harwick, with a different lord and a less believable animal. The others laughed before the end because they had heard it too.

At the rise the horse stopped. Cedric looked back. Hannet had gone very pale.

“Do you need to get down?”

“If we could wait.”

Rovel came up and took the rein while Cedric fetched water. The man drank only a little. They loosened the fastening of his coat without disturbing the bound arm. Cedric found that the lining had twisted beneath his shoulder and straightened it as carefully as he could.

“That better?”

“Yes.”

Hannet’s eyes were shut. They stood until he opened them.

Rovel came close enough to speak without the others hearing.

“You’ll need to tell the lodging he needs a low bed. Last place put him in a loft.”

“Why didn’t they move him?”

“He didn’t ask until the stairs got him.”

Cedric looked at Hannet. The man was about his own age, perhaps younger, and determined to sit the horse without appearing to require it.

He led the bay more slowly after that.

At the lodging, the woman receiving them counted thirteen men on her paper and twelve standing in the yard.

“There are eleven here,” Cedric said. “Two to join us.”

“And you.”

“I am returning.”

“After supper?”

“When they are settled.”

“Then you’re here for supper. Twelve.”

He accepted this. Rovel had gone to look at the sleeping places. The woman wanted the saddles out of her passage before the men came inside, and a boy wanted to know who would see that the cart was returned.

Cedric dealt with one question at a time. The saddles went into a locked shed. The carter would sleep in the kitchen and take his vehicle back at first light; the woman named the extra charge for feeding him, and Cedric added it to the paper rather than pretending it had been included before. Hannet was given a pallet in the lower room, close enough to the fire to be warm and far enough away that passing feet would not catch his injured side.

The woman called him back before he could go to the stable.

“Who are the other two?”

He looked at the order. There were names, but one had been crossed out and replaced in a hand he could not read.

“Ask Rovel,” he said.

“I’m asking you.”

Cedric felt the familiar heat rise into his face. He almost said that Captain Rovel would be standing beside her shortly and could give an answer without either of them having to fetch him.

Instead he fetched him.

Rovel read the name. The woman knew the man and said he had arrived that morning, eaten, and gone to find his brother in the village. She sent a boy for him. The other was still expected from a neighbouring quarter.

Cedric wrote both circumstances on his order. He had not been told to do that. It seemed preferable to having the question asked again when he returned.
