He ate before leaving. The soup had a thick skin at the edge of the bowl and tasted strongly of onions. Hannet was asleep by the time he finished it.

At the gate Rovel handed him a folded acknowledgement.

“The men lodged,” he said. “And the saddles accounted for.”

Cedric put it inside his coat.

“You could have told me about the cart at the beginning.”

“I told you we had three horses.”

“You knew what we needed.”

“I knew what I wanted. Somebody had to agree to pay for it.”

Cedric mounted. The bay, relieved of the injured rider and most of the baggage, started willingly towards the road home.

Ysabeau received the papers beside a lamp whose wick needed trimming.

She read the lodging account first, then the cost of the cart. At the carpenter’s payment she looked up.

“You advanced this?”

“Yes.”

“Have it returned from the travelling account tomorrow.”

Cedric nodded. He had been preparing to ask.

“What happened to your father’s letter?”

“I haven’t written it.”

“Then do it now.”

He stood by the table while she turned to another paper. At last she noticed that he had not gone.

“Was there something else?”

“Rovel says he is to report on my work.”

“He is.”

“Some of the delay was caused before I arrived.”

“I know when you arrived.”

She took the lamp’s small iron pick and lifted the blackened end of the wick. The flame steadied.

“Tomorrow you can help him account for the equipment those men left behind. He knows where some of it ought to be.”

Cedric bowed and went out.

In the room he had been given, he wrote to his father that he had arrived safely and found employment with Dame Ysabeau de Corvaunt. He named the horse and said the shoe had held. He considered mentioning that he had walked most of the afternoon so an injured man could ride it, but could find no way of putting this that did not invite his father’s opinion of whether he should have done so.

The second account took longer. He wrote down the house whose troop had taken the horses, the officer who had produced the prior claim, and the time the men had consequently spent waiting. He could distinguish what he had seen from what Rovel had told him. Fayd had insisted on that.

He folded the report into a smaller sheet and put it beneath his spare stockings until the agreed day.

Chapter 7 — The Household Account

His father’s answer came folded inside an account of fodder, and Ouen nearly gave both to Halden.

He saw his own name as the papers parted. Valdais had written it himself. The outer account went to the officer, and Ouen took the smaller sheet to the window where the light was better.

My dear boy,

I am glad you have looked at the mare. Have her treated and kept until she is fit. If her owner is among the dead, the claim must still be entered. Somebody will inherit it.

You ask how many men came away on other men’s horses. We are still finding that out. Some men have named the horses more readily than the people who rode them before. I have required each company to give its account, and will not improve the figure by guessing at the remainder.

Ouen read the paragraph twice. He had expected either a number or a reprimand for asking. His father had managed to give him work instead.

Joss should report to my officer when he has rested. Give him a mount for the journey, with its description. The chestnut must have hers written separately. Tell your horse-master that I will hear what he finds.

Beneath this, in a less crowded hand, Valdais asked whether the new hawk had flown as well as the man who sold her claimed. Someone had evidently told him the price. He asked whether Hew still used both hands to lift his cup and cautioned Ouen against letting the child carry even a small live bird before he could hold his arm without watching it.

Ouen looked up. Across the yard, his son was standing very still beside the carpenter. There was something wrapped in cloth on the bench between them.

“He asks after Hew,” he told Halden.

The officer was reading the fodder account.

“Good, my lord.”

“About the bird.”

“Yes.”

Ouen waited for him to look up, then continued with the letter.

The horse requirement had changed. Twenty would do if they could be sent promptly; the remaining animals would be needed later. This seemed a concession until he reached the next paragraph, where his father asked for a consignment from the delta to be received, stored under cover, and made available to an officer carrying the proper authorization. Until it was called for, some of it could be used to meet household needs. An account was to be kept.

“What has he bought?” Ouen asked.

Halden looked through his papers.

“Cloth. Oil. Salt fish. Some worked leather.”

“Then we can give the cloth to Taliya and keep ours.”

“Ours has gone.”

“I meant next time.”

“Yes, my lord.”

The cloth Iselde had surrendered was already several days down the road. Joss had taken it, with instructions to obtain the acknowledgement before riding on to Valdais’s officer. The letter before Ouen was too late to change that. He disliked the number of things which became impossible to alter while somebody else was carrying a piece of paper.

“Where will the new goods go?”

“The dry store, if we move the leather already there.”

“Move it.”

“To the west room?”

“Wherever it will be dry.”

“The west room is being used for the harness repairs.”

“Then the men can use the leather while they’re there.”

Halden paused.

“They can,” he said.

Ouen felt an unreasonable satisfaction.

“Arrange it.”

He folded his father’s letter and went down to see what the carpenter had made.

The wooden hawk had one wing slightly larger than the other.

The carpenter showed Ouen before he let the child take it. The wood had split at the place where he intended to narrow the left wing, and he had chosen to keep the extra width rather than weaken it. Ouen turned it over in his hands. The body was hollowed beneath to make it lighter, with a smooth strip left for a thong.

“Will it balance?”

“On a broad perch.”

“His glove.”

“If he keeps the arm level.”

Hew was already holding out the glove. Iselde stood behind him with a pot of paint she had evidently been persuaded to find. The child looked at the bird, then at his father’s face, anxious that something might now be discovered which would prevent him having it.

Ouen set it on his fist.

For a moment it stayed. Hew’s arm began to tremble. The hawk leaned sideways, and Ouen caught it before it fell.

“Keep the elbow in.”

Hew pressed his elbow so tightly to his body that the glove turned inward.

“Like this.”

Ouen knelt beside him and set the child’s arm in position. There was a little leather loop on the glove. He passed the thong through it, leaving enough play for the bird to sit upright.

“Now if she falls, she won’t get away.”

“She can get away.”

“Only as far as your wrist.”

“She can fly.”

Ouen looked towards Iselde. She lifted the pot a little, showing him that its contents were blue.

“She will be a remarkable bird,” he said.

They took it inside to paint. He had meant to leave them to it, but the carpenter had cut a plausible head and the bill would be improved by a touch of darker colour. He sent for another brush. Hew objected whenever he worked on a part of the bird that had already been declared finished, but allowed the eye after Ouen made a black dot on the back of his own hand to demonstrate its size.

The right wing became almost entirely blue. The left was brown, because Hew grew tired of blue halfway through. Iselde painted the perch while they quarrelled over the feet.

“Birds have yellow feet,” Ouen said.

“This one has red.”

“It will look ill.”

“This one has red.”

Ouen gave him the smaller brush.

He was holding the bird above a sheet of old paper while the feet dried when Halden came to say that the first wagon had arrived.

“Have it unloaded.”

“There is a question about the account.”

“There always is.”

“Yes, my lord. The carrier says he is to be paid here.”

Ouen handed the hawk to Iselde, taking care that her fingers touched only the dry wood. Hew objected to the interruption. Ouen told him he would be back before the feet were dry, and saw his wife’s expression change a little.

“I’ll come back,” he amended.

The carrier had been paid as far as the river landing. His paper said nothing about the road from the landing to the house.

“He cannot expect to leave it on the bank,” Ouen said.

The man stood beside the wagon, with one foot on the lower spoke. He wore a felt hat whose brim had been stitched to keep a tear from widening. He took it off when Ouen approached and put it on again when a cold drop fell from the eaves onto his head.

“I expected the receiving officer to send teams.”

“Then why didn’t you ask for them?”

“I did. Your man said bring it on.”

Halden turned to a servant by the stable door. The servant looked alarmed.

“He asked if this was where it was coming,” the servant said. “I said yes.”

“And to bring it up?” Halden asked.

“I said the gate was open.”

Ouen looked at the carrier. The man met his eyes with the expression of somebody who had found several meanings in an open gate and preferred the one that had got his wagon off the riverbank.

“How much?”

The sum was modest. Ouen was prepared to pay it until Halden asked whether it was for one wagon or all three. The man said one.

“There are three?” Ouen asked.

“The other two behind. My brother’s bringing them.”

Ouen looked down the road. A pair of horses appeared beyond the turn, drawing a wagon piled above the height he would have chosen. The covering had slipped, and one of the bales was resting against the rope rather than the load beneath it.

“Get that down before somebody is hurt,” he said.

The carrier climbed onto the wheel. Two of Ouen’s men went to meet the arriving wagon. Halden began speaking to the first driver about the payment while Ouen watched them lower the loose bale. It came down heavily and split its outer wrapping at the corner.

Dark red cloth showed through the tear.

Ouen touched it. The weave was close and smooth. Even damp from the journey, it was better than the rough lengths he had expected to receive for coats and bedding.

“What is this for?”

The carrier, back on the ground, said he did not know. He had taken it as it was given to him.

“There should be marks.”

Halden turned the bale. A black anchor had been stamped on the wrapper, with three dots beneath its crossbar. The same mark appeared on two smaller packages behind it. Beside the anchor, someone had brushed a broad white stripe across the cloth.

“Whose is that?” Ouen asked.

“The owner it came from,” the carrier said.

“And who is that?”

“I couldn’t tell you. The receiving note gives the store.”

Halden held out his hand. The carrier went to fetch another paper from under the seat.

Men were gathering around the wagon, waiting for instructions about where to put things. The red cloth had attracted attention. Ouen could hear a woman by the kitchen door saying there was enough for a hanging if the rest of the bale was like the corner. He put the torn wrapper back over it.

“The dry store,” he said.

“All of it?” Halden asked.

“The cloth. Put the fish wherever it will do least harm.”

One of the men laughed. Ouen smiled back. He was tired of the arrival already, and a sensible instruction delivered without a second question was pleasant to hear himself give.

The carrier returned with the paper. Halden read it and asked whether he had collected the load at the named store.

“No. It was on the wharf.”

“In Fairport?”

“It had come from there. I took it farther up.”

Ouen glanced at the black mark again.

His father’s letter authorized household use. The goods had been allotted to their account. There must have been an officer at the place where they were collected, somebody responsible for determining what belonged in which wagon. Ouen had no way of resolving that in his own yard with the cold coming down and three teams waiting to be unharnessed.

“Enter the mark as well as the quantity,” he said.

Halden nodded.

The carrier waited until Ouen looked at him.

“The road money, my lord.”

“For all three,” Ouen said, naming the smaller total Halden had proposed. “And you remain until they’re unloaded.”

The man considered the distance back to the landing, then agreed.

By the time the last wagon was empty, it was too late to send the carrier down the road before dark.

Halden asked whether the men could sleep in the outbuilding beside the lower stable. Ouen agreed, with the stipulation that no fire be lit there. It seemed simple enough until the housekeeper asked what they were to eat, and the carrier asked where the horses would stand, and the man repairing harness objected to having his work moved twice in one day.

Ouen dealt with the food and left Halden to arrange the horses. He went into the store to look at what had come.

The room had changed. Bales stood in rows along the wall where old harness had hung that morning. Two casks smelled of oil, and the salt fish had been taken elsewhere at his insistence. On top of a small chest lay a roll of worked leather, smooth and dark, with the edge protected by a strip of coarse cloth.

He unrolled a little. It would make an excellent pair of gloves.

“Is this allotted too?” he asked.

Halden looked at the list.

“To the household provision, my lord.”

“Then send some up to my wife. For the boy.”

“How much?”

Ouen held his own hand over the leather and measured generously around it.

“That much twice.”

The officer marked the list. Ouen watched him do it.

“Must you write down every scrap?”

“His Grace asked for an account of anything used.”

“Write down the boy’s gloves, then. He asked about him as well.”

Halden made the entry. Ouen wondered whether the line would amuse his father, and whether he could arrange for it to be near the top where it would certainly be read.

There were twenty-one horses available without recalling an animal already sent out. Halden had the list ready when Ouen asked. Five were promised to households whose own teams had been used through harvest. Three belonged to men under hire, and could be taken only if their hire went with them. Four needed shoeing. One was too old for hard work but might do for a quiet rider.

“How many have we without all this?” Ouen asked.

“Eight.”

“My father has reduced the number by half.”

“He has brought the date forward.”

Ouen ran a finger down the names. He knew some of the horses better than the men listed beside them. He stopped at one.

“This isn’t fit for the army.”

“He could carry a messenger.”

“He shies at water.”

“Yes, my lord.”

“Then take him off.”

Halden struck the name through.

Twenty would have been less embarrassing than eight. Ouen had begun the morning pleased with his father’s letter. He could hear the questions that would follow an answer saying he had accepted the goods, cut leather for the child, and found eight horses for a request of twenty.

“We’ll send the hired men with theirs,” he said.

“I will ask them.”

“And have the four shod.”

“Yes.”

“The promised five can wait a few days.”

“The people, or the horses?”

“The people. Until we have others.”

Halden left his pen above the page.

“They were given a date.”

“Then give them another.”

“Which?”

Ouen looked towards the open door. The yard had emptied. Somebody was sweeping straw towards a heap, working slowly in the last light.

“Tell them I will hear the difficulty,” he said.

“They will bring it.”

“Very well.”

Halden wrote it down. Ouen was almost at the door when he stopped.

“Don’t send anything meant for Taliya.”

“The cloth is already on its way.”

“I mean anything else. Put hers aside first.”

“Yes, my lord.”

It was the second sensible thing he had said in the store. He would include it in his letter to Paget.

Hew’s bird was dry.

The child had stayed awake to show him. When Ouen came upstairs, he stood in his nightshirt beside the bed with the hawk on the glove and the thong wrapped twice around his wrist. His elbow was tucked in just as he had been taught.

“Look.”

“I am looking.”

“Both feet.”

They were very red. A red fingerprint had also appeared on the underside of one wing. Ouen inspected it without comment.

“She’s standing well.”

“Hold her.”

He knelt and offered his bare hand. Hew moved the wooden bird solemnly from the glove to his father’s fingers, with a care Ouen recognized. The child’s tongue protruded slightly at one corner of his mouth.

“Don’t let her go.”

“I won’t.”

“She’s strong.”

“I can feel it.”

Hew watched him for a moment, then touched the bird’s head with one finger. The paint beneath the beak had remained tacky. He looked at the little stain on his finger and wiped it on Ouen’s sleeve.

Iselde made a sound from the other side of the room. Ouen looked up. She had covered her mouth with her hand.

“It will wash,” he said.

“Of course.”

He handed the bird back and watched the child settle it on the bed beside him. Iselde went to remove the glove. Hew insisted on keeping it. They negotiated the glove onto the chair within reach and left the hawk where it was.

At the desk, Ouen took out his father’s letter again. He wrote an acknowledgement, described the consignment, and said he expected to send the first horses shortly. He had no fixed number from the hired men yet. It seemed better to let that be settled before committing it to paper.

His letter to Vaelrie was easier. He told her he had attended to Taliya’s provision personally, which he had. He described the hawk’s red feet and said Hew expected a proper reply to the question about the cricket. He added that their father had asked sensible questions about the bird and less sensible ones about what could be found in the stables at short notice.

Iselde read that part over his shoulder.

“Will he get the horses?” she asked.

“I am arranging it.”

She rested a hand briefly on the back of his neck. Her fingers were cold. He leaned his head against them before she drew away.

“Come to bed when you’ve finished.”

He folded Vaelrie’s letter first. Beneath it lay the household account Halden had left for him, with a clean space where he was to confirm the arrival of the goods. The black anchor had been copied carefully in the margin, the three dots placed beneath it.

Ouen signed above the mark and put the paper with the others for the morning post.

Part II — People who keep their word

Chapter 8 — The Order Carried Back

At the broken bridge Cedric could hear the men on the other bank arguing about a ladder.

The river had taken the middle span during the night. The near end remained where it had always been, with a handrail leaning over the gap and a length of rope tied to its last post. Beyond it, muddy water turned under the broken beams. On the far bank a man in a red cap had laid a ladder flat on the ground and was standing over it as though he expected somebody to steal it.

Cedric rode as close as the bank allowed.

“Is there another crossing?” he called.

The man looked up. He shouted something lost beneath the water.

“Another bridge.”

The man cupped a hand to his ear.

Cedric pointed upstream. The man shook his head and pointed downstream. Then another man came forward, pointed upstream instead, and the argument about the ladder resumed.

He looked at the order again, though he had known what it said before taking it out. The party at Meret was to withdraw to the old press-house before nightfall. They were to bring their equipment and the two men held there pending exchange. Cedric was to deliver the instruction and return with the officer’s acknowledgement.

The press-house lay on his side of the river. The man who had written the order had assumed the bridge would be standing.

He tried to attract the men’s attention once more. The one in the red cap came to the broken end of the bridge and gestured for him to stop moving forward. A strip of bank fell away beneath the near post while Cedric was looking at it. He reined the bay back.

There was no prospect of getting across there. He rode along the bank until the ground became too wet to trust, then turned towards the road. The nearest houses had smoke coming from their chimneys. Someone might know a ford. He looked at the sky, calculated how much of the afternoon he would have left after finding one, and turned the horse back towards quarters.

Ysabeau was at the stable when he returned, holding Bastian while a groom worked at a shoe.

“The bridge is down,” he said.

“Which span?”

“The middle.”

“Since when?”

“Last night, I think.”

“Did you speak to the officer?”

“I couldn’t get across.”

The groom set down the horse’s foot. Bastian shifted his weight and leaned towards the open stable door. Ysabeau shortened the rein without looking at him.

“Who told you it fell last night?”

“There were men working on the far side.”

“You spoke to them?”

“They couldn’t hear. The water was high.”

She handed Bastian’s rein to the groom.

“Keep your horse saddled.”

Cedric had been about to ask whether she wanted an amended order. Instead he stood beside the bay while she called for her cloak.

Rovel came out with two men and a pack animal. Cedric watched a coil of rope being fastened to the pack. Ysabeau took the order from him, read it, and put it into her own case.

“You will show me where the bank gave way.”

He followed her out of the yard.

By the time they reached the bridge, the red cap had disappeared and the ladder was lying half in the water.

The men had tried to reach a beam from the far bank. One of them was sitting with a cloth pressed to his forehead. Another was shouting at him. Ysabeau stopped where Cedric indicated, dismounted, and stood for a little while looking at the water.

“How many houses did you pass?” she asked.

“Four.”

“Rovel. Ask at the first.”

The captain took one of the men and went back up the track. Cedric looked at the broken beam. The river had risen since he was last there. A branch caught against the handrail and turned over, showing its pale torn end.

“I couldn’t have crossed,” he said.

“I can see that.”

“The order required an acknowledgement.”

“It required those men moved.”

He looked at her. She had not raised her voice. She was watching the opposite bank, where the man with the cloth had stood and was attempting to wring blood out of it with one hand.

“I would have sent you on a different road if I had known this one would fail,” she said. “You found out. I was still waiting.”

Cedric felt the wet in his boots. He had walked the bay along the bank earlier to keep its feet out of the worst ground, and cold water had come over the leather. He had expected some notice of the effort.

Rovel returned with a girl carrying a basket of chopped roots. She was barefoot despite the weather and had tucked her skirt up above her knees. She took them along a path through the willows to a place where a small boat lay upside down beneath a thatched shelter.

“My uncle’s,” she said.

“Where is he?” Ysabeau asked.

“Over there.”

She pointed back towards the men on the other bank.

“Can we use it?”

The girl looked at the river. “He’ll want paying.”

“I will pay him. Is it sound?”

“He gets angry if we put stones in it.”

Rovel and the two men turned it over. There was a repaired seam along one side and water in the bottom, but the wood beneath the shelter was sound. They carried it to the narrow inlet where it was usually launched. The current outside was fast enough to make Cedric’s stomach tighten.

Ysabeau looked at the men.

“Who knows a boat?”

One stepped forward. The other shook his head.

“Rovel, stay here. Master Aeryl, you can hold a rope.”

Cedric took the coil.

They sent the boat across below the broken bridge, with the rope paying out from the bank and the man in it working an oar against the current. He landed considerably farther down than he intended. The red cap appeared from behind the trees, waving both arms at him. The boatman held up the little purse Ysabeau had given him. That quieted the owner enough for them to speak.

The order went over on the next crossing. Cedric held the near rope until his palms burned. He could see why taking a horse would have been impossible. He could also see the four chimneys he had ridden past without asking.

The officer from Meret reached them before dark, with nine men, two prisoners, and far more baggage than the boat could carry in one trip.

He was a narrow man with a wet cloak and a habitual squint. His name was Derrit. He had expected a wagon at noon. When it failed to come, he had sent a man to inquire, who found the broken bridge and returned to say the order could not arrive either. Derrit had waited because departing without instruction would leave the place unguarded.

Ysabeau listened while the first prisoner was helped out of the boat. The man had a bandaged hand and a patch of grey skin beneath one eye. He was shaking too hard to stand unaided. Cedric took his other arm before the escort could call to somebody else.

“Fire,” Rovel said, and pointed.

The girl with the roots had brought her grandmother. Between them they had made a little fire well above the wet bank. The old woman objected when the men tried to pile damp wood on it and took charge of the additions herself.

Derrit needed his equipment across. The girl needed the roots cooked before the family came home. The boat’s owner wanted to know whether the payment covered each journey or the entire use of the vessel. Cedric moved between the questions until Ysabeau told him to stay with the men coming ashore and count their bundles against Derrit’s list.

One bundle failed to appear.

“Whose?” Cedric asked.

The man beside him did not answer at once. He was watching the other bank, where the final load was being lowered into the boat.

“Mine, sir.”

“What’s in it?”

“My things.”

“I assumed that.”

The man looked at him.

Cedric took a breath. “Describe the wrapping.”

“Blue cloth. White cord.”

The boat landed. There was no blue bundle in it. Cedric checked beneath the others, then called the boatman back before he could start arguing with the owner again.

“One still there.”

“Then fetch it in the morning.”

“The man goes to the press-house tonight.”

“He can go without his bundle.”

Cedric looked at the soldier, who was rubbing his bare hands together. He had the wet clothes he stood in and a belt with no knife attached to it.

“I’ll come,” Cedric said.

Rovel caught his sleeve.

“You don’t know the boat. Give him a light and ask the men still on the bank to look.”

Cedric did. The bundle was found beneath a cloak somebody had thrown over it. When it came ashore, the owner opened it and took out a dry cap. He put it on before thanking anyone.

Ysabeau waited until they had started towards the press-house before returning the order to Cedric.

“His acknowledgement,” she said.

Derrit had signed beneath a record of the time the message reached him. Cedric folded it carefully.

Four days later, Rovel asked him where a company could cross a flooded meadow without waiting for the downstream ferry.

Cedric thought the question a joke. He looked up from the bootlace he was replacing. Rovel held a map and was waiting for an answer.

“Which meadow?”

The captain showed him. It lay on one of the last approaches Cedric had ridden on his journey south. The drawn road ended at a watercourse, resumed on the other side, and contained no suggestion that the missing length mattered.

“Upstream,” Cedric said. “Where the ground narrows by the old willows.”

“How far?”

He told him. Rovel put the map on the table and made him show the route with his finger.

“Will a cart take it?”

“An empty one. Loaded, it will need to go round.”

“These are horses.”

“Then yes, if the water is where it was last week.”

“If.”

“I’ll look first.”

Rovel nodded. “Take someone.”

Cedric chose a man who knew how to test ground beneath shallow water. He had seen him do it on the way from Meret. They left before the company, crossed the meadow on foot with the horses behind them, and found the old ford a little deeper but otherwise passable. The far bank had softened. They cut brush to mark the sound approach and sent word back.

The leading officer arrived displeased. A shorter route had been promised, and he did not see why his men should get their boots wet when there was said to be a bridge.

“The bridge puts you on the lower road,” Cedric said.

“Which goes to the same place.”

“After the turn by the mill.”

“I know where the mill is.”

“Then you know the distance.”

The officer looked at him. He had laughed once, at the yard table, when another man made a remark about Cedric’s scar. Cedric remembered the sound perfectly. He had wanted an opportunity to make him look foolish and now found that the man was doing it by himself.

“Show me,” the officer said.

Cedric walked into the water, keeping to the brush markers. His stockings were already wet. He crossed to the other bank, waited, and came back.

“Ride through in pairs. Keep to the upstream side of the brush. The bottom falls away beyond it.”

The officer rode in alone to test the advice. Halfway across, his horse moved towards the downstream marker and put a foot into deeper water. He pulled it back too hard. Cedric caught the near rein before the animal could turn across the current, held it a moment, and let go when the horse found its footing.

The officer reached the far side with mud to his knee.

“Pairs,” he called back. “Keep to the markers.”

Cedric stood in the cold water while the first six went past.

Ysabeau heard about the crossing at supper.

Rovel gave the account while the officer sat within earshot. He named Cedric as the man who had known the ground and said how long the company had saved. The officer contributed that the crossing needed marking for future use.

“It is marked,” Cedric said.

“With something that will remain,” the officer answered.

Ysabeau looked at Cedric.

“Can the owner be asked to keep the approach clear?”

“Yes.”

“Do that tomorrow. And ask what it costs him to have men passing there. I would rather hear it from him before he puts an obstruction across it.”

She passed him the dish that had been set beside her. He took a piece of meat before realizing that he was being served first. The officer who had laughed at his scar had to wait while Cedric found a place for the dish among the cups.

He enjoyed the meal more than the food deserved.

Later, in his report, he described the company, its destination as given to him, and the delay avoided by using the crossing. He left out the officer’s horse stepping into the deeper water. It had no bearing on what Fayd wanted to know, and he had already told it twice to men who appreciated the detail.

His wet stockings hung beside the fire. He turned them over before going to bed.

Chapter 9 — The Mark on the Bales

The safe-conduct entitled Ouen to bring six men across the ferry, and the clerk at the landing counted the horses instead.

“There are seven,” he said.

“One is carrying bags.”

“Seven horses.”

“Six men.”

The clerk read the instrument again. Ouen could see him deciding whether a horse with nobody on it represented an attempt to deceive the crown. Behind them, the ferryman held the empty boat against the landing with a pole and waited for somebody to stop discussing things that would be obvious once they boarded.

At last the clerk handed back the paper.

“You’ll be expected at the customs house.”

“By whom?”

“The people who arranged the meeting.”

Ouen put the document into its case. He had been travelling long enough to know how many men a piece of paper would permit to be unhelpful without quite being rude.

The ferry moved awkwardly off the bank. Its boards had a slope which encouraged the packhorse to press against his own. He held both heads apart until the ferryman told one of the grooms to bring the pack animal forward. From the water, the customs house looked smaller than the account of it Halden had supplied. A strip of canvas had been stretched over one end of the roof. Smoke rose from a chimney which leaned enough to make Ouen wonder where the hearth stood beneath it.

He was going on to Valora after the conference. That had required another instrument, covering another set of roads, with an allowance for servants and a list of the places where he might stop. Vaelrie had sent a letter about his room. The letter had been much easier to understand than the safe-conduct and would have been no use at all to the clerk on the landing.

Inside the customs house, the room set aside for them smelled of damp plaster. Constano Fenwood sat beside the fire with one foot on a low stool. His silver-capped cane leaned against his chair. He had arranged himself so that he could see the door without turning.

“My lord,” he said.

Ouen inclined his head. “Lord Constano.”

There was a moment in which he could have asked after the foot or the journey and found that either question would expose how little he knew of what had happened. Constano indicated a chair instead.

“Matteo Tireno. You may have met.”

“Once,” Matteo said. “At your father’s table.”

Ouen remembered a man objecting to a toll while his father persuaded him to try another dish. He had not listened to the objection. He hoped the man would not now ask whether he remembered it.

The woman beside Matteo stood when she was introduced.

“Dame Barenne,” Constano said. “She has the account of the goods.”

She was shorter than Ouen expected, perhaps because she had been sitting very straight. Her gown was dark and plain. One cuff had been replaced with cloth that almost matched the sleeve. She held a folded piece of coarse wrapping in both hands.

Ouen took the chair. The clerk Halden had sent with him sat a little behind his right shoulder and began taking papers out of a bag. There were more of them than Ouen remembered packing.

Constano waited until they were ready.

“Your household received three wagons from the delta.”

“Yes.”

“Under your father’s allotment.”

“Yes.”

“Dame Barenne believes some of the contents are hers.”

The woman unfolded the wrapping and laid it on the table. A black anchor showed against the coarse fabric, with three dots beneath the crossbar.

Ouen had seen the mark copied into Halden’s account. The original seemed larger now.

“How do you know these are the same goods?” he asked.

“I don’t know that every piece is,” Dame Barenne said. “I know the marks on the bales entered at your landing.”

“Many merchants use an anchor.”

“Yes. This is mine.”

“And the dots?”

“The warehouse.”

“There could be another.”

She looked at him for a moment, then opened a book.

“These are the lengths received there before the sack. The wrapping number is entered against each. Your receiving officer has entered four of those numbers.”

Ouen turned towards his clerk. The man found the page without needing to be told which one.

“Four, my lord. And two packages with marks too damaged to read.”

“The cloth had been allotted to us.”

“I understand.”

“My father didn’t come ashore at Fairport.”

Constano’s hand moved on the arm of his chair. Ouen looked at it and wished he had finished the sentence differently.

“The corsairs—” he began.

“The cloth is in your store,” Constano said.

He let the statement stand while Dame Barenne put a finger against the first entry.

“The red. Was the wrapping torn?”

“At the corner.”

“Which end?”

Ouen had to think. He could remember touching the cloth, but not which way the bale had been lying.

“It is in the account,” his clerk said. “Left of the mark.”

Dame Barenne nodded.

“I repaired that corner before it went down to the warehouse. The first wrapper was too narrow. There should be two lines of stitching beneath the tear, one darker than the other.”

“You couldn’t know where it would tear.”

“The seam would pull before the wrapper did. I told the man who tied it.”

Matteo drew the book towards him, checked a figure, and turned it back to her. There was no satisfaction in the movement. They appeared to have done this together often enough to know where the relevant entries lay.

“How much are you claiming?” Ouen asked.

“The goods.”

“An amount.”

“I need the goods.”

“You must have valued them.”

“I have buyers for two lengths of the red and one of the undyed. If I can deliver them, I can meet what I owe the man who is repairing my store. If I bring him a promise to value them, he will finish somebody else’s roof.”

There was a loose thread at the edge of her replacement cuff. She rubbed it between finger and thumb while she waited for his answer.

“I can’t send them from here,” Ouen said.

“You can instruct the officer who has them.”

“My father’s officer may call for them.”

“Then instruct yours first.”

The directness of it irritated him. She had correctly identified the cloth, possibly. She had not acquired the right to order his household because her warehouse had been burned.

“I am prepared to hear the claim. That is what I came for.”

Constano leaned forward and took the book before the woman could answer.

“Then we should establish how much of it is in dispute.”

They broke for food when the man tending the fire brought in a tray and found no space on the table.

Ouen was glad of the interruption. His clerk moved the papers while Matteo helped Constano get his foot off the stool. The older man made the movement slowly, with one hand on the cane and the other against the table. Ouen looked away too late to prevent Constano seeing him do it.

“The other chair,” Constano said to the servant. “This one is too low once I am up.”

They changed it. Dame Barenne poured water, and Ouen took the cup she offered because refusing it would be absurd. The bread was fresh enough. He ate standing by the window.

Across the water, men were loading something into a shallow boat. The boat sat almost level with the landing until the last load went aboard, when its near edge sank visibly. One man pushed it away with his foot and another began shouting at him.

“Will your room be ready?” Ouen asked the woman.

“My store?”

“Yes.”

“If the roof is finished.”

“Was all of it burned?”

“The upper room. The wall at the back fell into the yard. We can use the front if it stays dry.”

“You were there?”

She tore a piece from her bread.

“I was at the house when they reached the warehouse. My eldest went down. He brought two men back, and we tried to carry out what we could before the lane filled.”

“Your son came back?”

“Yes.”

Ouen was relieved and hoped he had not shown it too plainly.

“Then he can help you reopen.”

“He is helping.”

A silence followed. Ouen looked at the boat again. The men had settled whatever they were arguing about and were pushing off together.

Dame Barenne put the rest of her bread on the plate.

“He was going to work with his uncle,” she said. “He has written to say he won’t come this winter.”

“Where?”

She named a town Ouen had passed through as a boy. He remembered a fountain in a square and the taste of something sold hot from a pan. He could find nothing useful to say about either.

Constano called them back to the table.

“The four numbered bales are identified,” he said. “Your officer can inspect the seams and marks against this description. Until then, will you undertake that they remain unused?”

Ouen looked at his clerk.

“I can write the instruction, my lord.”

The cloth had seemed a relief when it arrived. Now keeping it required more labour than receiving it had. His father’s army might call for it before an inspection could be made, and somebody would ask who had promised it elsewhere.

“For how long?”

“Until the inspection,” Constano said. “Name the day.”

Ouen calculated the return journey of a messenger. There was enough time if the roads remained open. There was never enough time if one began allowing for every way they might close.

He named a date. Constano repeated it to the clerk, who wrote it down.

“And if the marks are as described?”

“Then the claim is proved.”

“Then will you return the cloth?”

“I would need to consult my father.”

Dame Barenne sat back. The movement was small, but he saw that she had stopped expecting anything immediate from the meeting.

Matteo opened another paper.

“There is an arrangement we can make for the carriage,” he said. “A boat going north for oil can return with the bales. You will have no further transport to provide beyond the landing.”

“Whose oil?” Ouen asked.

“The merchant’s. It is available to buy.”

“I did not ask for oil.”

“Your household received two casks. Your officer has asked whether further supply is available. This would allow a trade to resume without putting another wagon on the river road.”

His clerk nodded reluctantly when Ouen looked at him. There had been a second page to Halden’s instructions which he had intended to read at the lodging.

“I cannot pay for goods my father has already allotted to me.”

“No one has asked you to,” Constano said. “You can return those that have been identified and buy what you need afterwards.”

“With money.”

“Yes.”

Ouen pushed his chair back an inch. He had been brought across the river to agree to impoverish his own store so that a woman could sell cloth from it to somebody else. Stated like that, the proposition sounded ridiculous. He looked at the marks in the book and found that he could not say it aloud in quite those words.

“Only the numbered four,” he said.

Dame Barenne leaned forward again.

“The two damaged—”

“Have not been identified.”

Constano looked at her. She considered, then nodded.

“The four for now,” she said.

“If the inspection agrees.”

“Yes.”

His clerk turned the undertaking towards him. Ouen read it carefully. He changed one phrase which could have been taken to authorize the return of other goods without another hearing. Constano accepted the change. Dame Barenne asked to see the date once more.

He signed.

The separate Adrian request took another hour.

Ouen had authority to seek passage for two household consignments across roads now watched by the queen’s men. Constano could speak to the people maintaining the commercial arrangements, but the royal protection had to come from the office issuing it. The visit to Valora remained necessary. His safe-conduct would get him there; it would not do the business for him.

The distinction annoyed him less now that someone was asking exactly what his own men needed. He described the teams, the people travelling with them, and the need for a return journey. Matteo caught an omission about the empty wagons. Constano asked whether the guards belonged to Ouen’s household or to his father’s field command.

“Does that matter?”

“It will matter at the first barrier.”

They amended the request. Ouen could see that this version was more likely to get his goods through than the one he had brought. He wondered whether he ought to thank somebody and could not decide whom.

At the end, Dame Barenne took back her wrapping and book. She thanked him for the undertaking. He said that it depended on inspection, though they had all just agreed that, and immediately regretted making her thank him twice.

Outside, the ferryman was arguing with the same clerk about a different horse. Ouen waited under the customs-house roof while his own animals were brought round. Constano came out after him, moving carefully over the raised sill.

“Your sister is expecting you,” he said.

“She has written.”

“She will be glad to see you.”

“She has written that too.”

Constano smiled. For the first time that day, Ouen could see how Jaennis might be his daughter.

“Then believe her.”

The clerk gave Ouen a copy of the undertaking to carry on to court. He put it beside the safe-conduct. The black anchor was visible through the thin paper, and he folded the sheet so the mark would be inside.

Chapter 10 — A Place at Supper

Vaelrie came down the stairs too quickly for the woman behind her to finish announcing him.

Ouen had just given his cloak to a servant. He turned at the sound of her feet, and she put both arms round him before he could arrange the expression with which he had intended to greet her. Her cheek was cold from an upper room.

“You are late.”

“The ferry—”

“I have heard about three ferries. This is the first brother.”

She drew back far enough to look at him, still holding his shoulders.

“You’ve had the coat altered.”

“It needed it.”

“At the waist?”

“At the shoulders.”

“Of course.”

He caught her hand before she could poke him in the stomach. She laughed, and he found that he had missed the sound more than he had expected.

“Where are my rooms?”

“Upstairs.”

“I had assumed that.”

“Then you are already halfway settled.”

The servant holding his cloak waited patiently while they spoke. Vaelrie noticed him and took the document case from Ouen’s hand.

“This goes with the bags. He will want to pretend he has read everything in it before the morning.”

“I have read it.”

“All of it?”

“Enough.”

“Put it where he can find it,” she told the servant.

Ouen followed her up. The staircase turned sharply beneath a window, then opened into a passage warmer than the rooms at home. There were flowers in a shallow bowl beside a door, late stems coaxed out of somewhere sheltered. He wondered what they had cost to obtain in winter and was annoyed to have thought of the question in Halden’s voice.

His room had a good bed and a narrow desk beneath the window. Vaelrie showed him the water, the bell, and the cupboard in which his clothes could be hung. He listened to the explanations because she was pleased to give them.

“Iselde sent you this.”

He took the small packet from his bag. Vaelrie opened it at once. Inside lay a length of ribbon and the drawing Hew had made under his mother’s supervision. The bird had acquired seven feet.

“He thinks yours is deficient,” Ouen said.

She sat on the edge of the bed to examine it.

“Does he remember me?”

“Yes.”

“Properly?”

“He asked whether you would bring the little cup again.”

“He broke it.”

“He may be hoping for another.”

She held the drawing a moment longer before putting it carefully beside the ribbon. He told her about the wooden hawk, making its red feet sound more disreputable than they were. She wanted to know whether he had helped paint it. He admitted the eye.

“Only the eye?”

“And the bill.”

“He let you?”

“There was a discussion.”

“I would have liked to hear that.”

For a while they spoke as they had at home, choosing which of their father’s remarks each wished to hear repeated. When she asked about Taliya’s cloth, he told her it was on its way with Joss and that the man would remain until it was received.

“Thank you,” she said.

He had prepared an explanation of the difficulty. The thanks left it looking unnecessary.

Then someone called her name from the passage. Vaelrie turned before the second syllable was finished.

“Later,” she called back.

A woman’s voice said there would be none left later.

“Then keep me one.”

“I make no promises.”

Vaelrie smiled towards the open door. Ouen waited until she looked back.

“What is there to keep?”

“A small cake. With orange.”

“You have been brought a brother.”

“He will last until supper.”

She kissed his cheek and stood.

“Wash. You have mud on the back of your ear.”

The supper room held more women than Ouen had expected and fewer people waiting to be told where to sit.

A chair had been drawn up by the fire with sewing left across it. Two women were arguing over whether the musician had played too quickly at the last gathering, while the musician himself tuned a string and looked as though he would have preferred not to hear their conclusions. A serving girl went through the room with a plate, offered it to one of the disputants, and received a question about her mother before moving on.

Jaennis came to meet him. She gave him both hands.

“My lord. We have been warned to feed you promptly.”

“By my sister?”

“Who else knows enough to warn us?”

Vaelrie stood just behind her, pleased with the greeting. Ouen bowed to Jaennis and said something about the kindness of receiving him. Jaennis accepted it without making the exchange solemn.

“Your business is tomorrow. Tonight you may be as useless as the rest of us.”

He looked around the room. Several women had heard and were making room at the table. He could hardly object to an invitation to be comfortable, but he wished it had been put in a form that gave him something to agree to.

Margery introduced herself, and then Joan and Catrin. He knew Dame Ysolt by reputation and was grateful when she asked a straightforward question about the road. He was answering it when another woman approached with a cup.

“Maelis,” Vaelrie said. “My brother.”

“So I hoped.”

Maelis offered him the wine. She had a full mouth and a small pale mark at the edge of her chin, visible when she turned towards the light. Her gown was a dark green which appeared almost black in the folds. One sleeve had been pinned back above the wrist, and there was a loose thread caught on the pin.

“Have you been hoping to meet me?” Ouen asked.

“I have been hoping we would eat before the fish spoiled. Your arrival makes that more likely.”

Margery laughed. Maelis held the cup steady until he took it.

“A favourable beginning, then,” he said.

“Very. Sit near me. I have questions about the bird.”

That restored the encounter considerably. He took the place she indicated and found Vaelrie opposite him, with Jaennis at the end of the table. A little later another woman pulled her chair between Vaelrie’s and Catrin’s to hear something Catrin was saying. Nobody asked her to move it back.

Ouen described the hawk. Maelis knew less than she had implied, but she asked about the recovery after the flight, which was the part worth describing. He explained the exchange of food and the need to let the bird settle before moving too quickly.

“Would she come to anyone with the food?”

“No.”

“Then how does the falconer take her from you?”

“She knows him.”

“Does that offend you?”

He looked at her. Her expression was so agreeable that he nearly answered yes.

“I employ him to care for her.”

“I know women who employ someone to dress them and resent the dresses fitting.”

“Name them,” Margery said.

“I want another invitation.”

The fish arrived before the question could be pressed. Ouen took the larger portion offered to him and watched Maelis pass her plate to the woman beside her to have a bone removed. It was an ordinary little dependence, accepted without apology.

Vaelrie asked him whether their father had written. He told her about the chestnut mare and the account of the horses. Halfway through, she turned towards the musician, who had begun playing a tune under his breath while he waited to be fed.

“What?” Ouen said.

“Nothing. I was listening.”

“To me?”

She looked back, guilty and amused.

“Start at the mare. Please.”

He did. She stayed with the account until he had finished, then asked whether the young rider had found out the name of the man he helped onto the cart. Ouen had not asked Joss again. He said he would, and meant to.

The cricket began during the second dish.

It was a clear, repeated note from somewhere close to the table. Ouen looked towards the wall. The sound stopped, then came again when Jaennis asked the musician whether he would play after supper.

“It’s hers,” Vaelrie said quietly.

“Where?”

Maelis heard. She lifted a small wicker box from the bench beside her. Its lid was tied with a loop of fine cord. Through the lattice Ouen could see something dark moving against a piece of bark.

“Does it have a name?” he asked.

“It answers when it wishes.”

“My son wants to know.”

“Then tell him it has not given me one I can pronounce.”

The note sounded again. Maelis tapped the edge of the box with one finger.

“Yes, I have asked.”

Ouen waited for something more. The creature moved along the bark, folded itself beneath a leaf, and went quiet.

“You speak to it.”

“Frequently.”

“And it understands?”

“Enough to disagree.”

Vaelrie had warned him that Maelis served Mab. He had expected a jewel, a mask, something by which a man could recognize the distinction before being required to behave as though a cricket at supper were ordinary. He looked at the box again, taking care not to lean too close.

“Should it be on the table?”

“It is beside the table,” Maelis said. “If you prefer it on the table, I can oblige.”

“I am quite content.”

“Good.”

She gave the box a little turn so its occupant faced the musician. Jaennis asked whether it had an opinion of his playing. Maelis said that was between them, and the musician said he would prefer to be consulted before being placed in a dispute with anything that could continue a note all night.

The room laughed. Ouen laughed too. He found the creature easier to accept now that somebody else had admitted it might be troublesome.

After the dishes were taken away, Maelis stood and shook out a silk handkerchief. The cloth had a knot at one corner and another tied into its middle. She passed it over her wrist and asked Margery to name a colour.

“Gold.”

“You always begin expensively.”

“You asked.”

Maelis drew the cloth through her hand. In the lamplight it went pale, then deepened to the warm yellow of the little pears on the sideboard. Ouen could see the knots. They remained where they had been while the colour moved round them.

Vaelrie leaned forward. Catrin named blue, and the silk took it along one edge, spreading slowly until the gold had dwindled to a thread.

“Red,” Joan said.

“One at a time.”

The cricket sounded from its box. The musician began a few quiet notes, testing whether they would interfere, and Maelis let the coloured cloth fall from one wrist to the other while he found the tune he wanted. It was a small entertainment, too close to examine without making a spectacle of one’s own suspicion. Ouen watched her empty hand. He could detect no dye on the fingers.

When the music ended she held the cloth out for Vaelrie to take. It was white again. Vaelrie examined it on both sides and returned it with a disappointed smile.

“You could have left me the blue.”

“I might need it.”

“For what?”

“I haven’t been asked yet.”

The company moved nearer the fire. Someone brought more wine, but Jaennis asked for water and had it set within reach. Vaelrie sat on the low seat beside her chair. Ouen found himself next to Ysolt, who wanted an account of the customs-house meeting.

He gave one that included the faulty conduct at the ferry and omitted his first attempt to dispute the anchor. Ysolt listened, asked which merchant had made the claim, and said she knew someone who had bought cloth from the same warehouse.

“Were the goods sound?” Ouen asked.

“They had not burned them first at that time.”

He looked at her. She reached for her cup.

“The buyer was pleased,” she added. “Will the woman get her cloth back?”

“If the marks agree.”

Ysolt nodded and let him turn the conversation to his onward business. He was grateful, although he suspected she knew it.

Across the room Maelis had begun putting the silk away. There were several handkerchiefs, some plain and some woven with a narrow coloured border, tied into one another in a sequence Ouen could not follow. Jaennis rose and went to speak to her. They were close enough for him to hear when the music paused.

“Leave them there. You can finish tomorrow.”

“May I use this room tomorrow?” Maelis asked.

“Of course.”

“At an hour when nobody is receiving callers?”

Jaennis looked towards the door.

“After breakfast. I can give you the small room beyond it if that is easier.”

“That would be kind. There is also the matter of how often you want me here.”

“As often as you wish.”

“For supper, I might make a scandal of that invitation.”

“I rely on it.”

“And for the performances?”

Jaennis smiled. “We were interrupted before we settled that.”

“We were.”

“Sit down.”

Maelis sat with the silk across her knees. They spoke more quietly about money, rooms, and a winter engagement Maelis had already promised elsewhere. Ouen heard Jaennis ask what date she needed to be away and Maelis answer without offering to abandon it. The answer was entered on a scrap of paper which Jaennis obtained from a servant.

There was something agreeable in seeing a person at court require a precise sum and receive an ordinary response. Ouen had imagined the Runekeeper might ask for a promise nobody understood or a favour to be named on some inconvenient future occasion.

Maelis caught him listening.

“Does the amount surprise you, my lord?”

“I hadn’t heard it.”

“Then I have negotiated very badly. I meant you to be impressed.”

Jaennis laughed and set the paper aside.

“Stay tonight,” she said. “We have finished employing you.”

“For tonight,” Maelis answered.

“For tonight.”

Maelis laid the knotted silk on the side table and moved her chair closer to the others. She took the cup Margery offered and began arguing about a line in the song. Her laugh was lower than her speaking voice. Once she laughed hard enough to catch her breath, and Margery put a hand between her shoulders until she waved it away.

Ouen watched Vaelrie watching them. His sister’s face was open in a way he remembered from childhood, when she was waiting for their father to turn and find her beside him. He had expected to be alarmed by something more readily named. Instead he felt the unreasonable irritation of finding a room in which she had been happy before he entered it.

She saw him looking and came to sit by him.

“You’re tired.”

“I’ve ridden all day.”

“I know.”

“Your letters could have said there was so much company.”

“They do. You complain about the names.”

He could not deny that. She rested her shoulder against his for a moment and asked whether his room was warm enough.

“Yes.”

“Good. I told them which one.”

“Did you?”

“Mine had the cold wall when I arrived. I thought you would make more of it.”

He laughed despite himself. She stayed beside him until the next song began, then turned towards the music without moving away.

He left later than he intended.

In the passage, Maelis was standing beneath a lamp with the silk gathered in her hands. The cricket box rested on the window ledge. She was speaking softly, unwinding one knot while describing the dispute over the song. He stopped, uncertain whether he was interrupting a prayer.

She looked up.

“Good night, my lord.”

“Is that necessary?” He indicated the silk.

“Yes.”

“Every night?”

“Often enough that I cannot pretend I shall remember next week.”

She freed a narrow blue border and laid it across her palm.

“Does it take long?”

“It takes longer when somebody asks how long it takes.”

He smiled. “Then I shall leave you to it.”

“Thank you for telling me about the hawk.”

“You were interested?”

“Yes.”

He had wanted the answer to suggest something beyond that. It was already a pleasant answer, and he found himself able to accept it.

Upstairs, his papers had been laid on the desk. The safe-conduct lay on top, followed by the revised request for household passage and his copy of the cloth undertaking. Beside them stood a little plate with an orange cake on it.

He ate the cake while reading the first page. The crumbs fell into the fold. He brushed them off, turned to the second page he had neglected before the conference, and kept reading until he understood what Halden had sent him to ask.

Chapter 11 — The Requisition

The old woman had put her foot on the first sack.

It was an effective obstruction. Two men had hold of the corners, but neither would lift while her slipper remained in the middle. The slipper was soft and badly worn. Cedric could see the shape of her toes through it.

“Please move your foot,” he said.

“Please put down my grain.”

“The grain has been assessed.”

“Then assess it with your hands off it.”

The men looked at him. He told them to set the sack down. The woman withdrew her foot and smoothed the creased cloth with the sole before stepping away.

Her son stood by the barn door holding a paper which had been unfolded so often that one corner was coming off. The carter waited outside with six empty spaces arranged among the sacks already loaded. There was no rain yet, but the air had acquired the close dampness which usually preceded it. Rovel had advised leaving before noon. Cedric had thought that ought to be easy.

He looked at the paper again.

“This is for hay.”

“Yes,” the son said.

“We are taking grain.”

“Yes.”

“They are separate assessments.”

“They come from the same holding.”

Cedric resisted the answer that came to him. He could hear exactly how the man would repeat it afterwards. He asked instead whether the neighbouring holding had furnished its share.

The son pointed at the cart.

“Theirs is on there.”

“Then you are being asked equally.”

“He has three fields I have the use of only after harvest. They counted those with mine when they took the hay, and with his when they took the grain.”

Cedric studied the paper. He could make no sense of the field names. The writing had been copied from a description older than either man in front of him, and two names were crossed out, with alternatives written above them.

“Who made the assessment?”

“The canton officer.”

“Is he here?”

“If he was here,” the old woman said, “I wouldn’t be standing on the sack.”

Cedric folded the paper carefully at a place which had not yet split and gave it back. He asked the men to leave the last six sacks where they were until Ysabeau had heard the matter. The son began to thank him, which made him uncomfortable because he had done nothing beyond postponing it.

“Remain here,” Cedric said. “I’ll bring her.”

“Will she come?”

He had not considered the possibility that she would refuse.

“I’ll ask.”

Ysabeau came after eating.

Cedric had expected her to come at once when he described the mistake. She asked whether the loaded cart was under cover. He said it was. She asked whether the grain could wait an hour. He said yes, though he had given the people in the yard to understand that an immediate correction was likely.

“Then I will eat while it waits.”

He stood beside the table until she offered him a bowl. He declined because he had eaten, and regretted declining when she continued with her meal instead of hearing the argument again.

Rovel sat opposite her. He wanted to know whether the old woman had moved her foot.

“Yes.”

“Then that part went well.”

Cedric did not answer. Ysabeau put a piece of bread into the remaining broth and asked whether the house description had been compared with the last assessment.

“I brought what they showed me.”

“Have the older one found.”

It took almost the whole hour. The officer responsible for the papers was in another lodging, and the bundle Cedric needed had been put beneath a folded cloak to keep it from dripping water from a leak in the ceiling. He found the entry by recognizing the name of the holding rather than the fields.

When he returned, Ysabeau had finished eating and was fastening her cloak.

“Show me.”

They read it together at the table. She asked him to explain where the man’s claim agreed with the older account. He pointed out the second field. The third had a different tenant now, and the change had not been carried through to both lists.

“How much difference?” she asked.

“Two sacks.”

“Of the six?”

“Yes.”

“Then four remain to be furnished.”

Cedric looked at her.

“He has already given the hay.”

“According to his holding.”

“They said the last collection was difficult.”

“I expect it was.”

He had brought her a mistake and thought, without quite acknowledging it, that correcting it would release him from the whole unhappy business at the barn. She took the paper and sent for her horse.

The old woman had sat down on the sacks while they were gone.

She rose for Ysabeau with less speed than Cedric thought respectful, then gave a correct inclination of her head. Her son explained the fields again. Ysabeau listened to the account without interrupting and asked to see the place where the holding’s boundary turned.

They walked out behind the barn. From there the ground fell gently to a wet meadow, with a line of bare trees marking the edge of the neighbouring pasture. The son showed her which strip he could cut and when the cattle were turned onto it. Cedric had looked at the same ground from the road and seen nothing that would help him distinguish one claim from another.

“The reassessment is wrong,” Ysabeau said. “Two sacks have been charged twice. They remain with you.”

The man let his breath out.

“And the other four?”

“Are due.”

His mother looked away across the meadow.

“Can we pay in another form?” he asked.

“What have you?”

He named a small quantity of beans and the use of a team after the wet weather passed. Ysabeau asked how many days. He offered two. She asked whether the team was his or his neighbour’s. He said his, but his mother made a little sound.

“One horse,” the woman said. “The other is theirs.”

The son kept his eyes on Ysabeau.

“They have agreed.”

“Then bring the agreement,” she said. “Until then, I can credit only what you can furnish yourself.”

He looked towards the barn. Cedric could see him calculating whether to fetch the neighbour, how long it would take, and what the man might ask for changing a friendly arrangement into something written before soldiers.

“The grain,” he said at last.

Ysabeau nodded. “Four sacks. Have the correction entered while we are here.”

Cedric took the paper. The old woman watched him draw the line and write the reduced quantity. His pen caught where the paper had worn thin.

“On the other side,” she said. “It won’t hold there.”

He turned it over. She was right. He copied the correction again where the surface was sound.

They loaded the four sacks. This time nobody put a foot on them. The son helped lift the last and stood beside the cart while Cedric checked the tally. His face remained closed when Ysabeau thanked him for making the error known.

On the road back, Cedric rode beside her for some distance before speaking.

“He won’t think he has been fairly treated.”

“He may not.”

“You corrected the mistake.”

“Yes.”

“Then why—”

“He still has four fewer sacks.”

She drew Bastian aside to let the cart go past a narrow place. Cedric waited with her, hearing the horse breathe and the wooden axle complain under the load.

“The men need grain,” she said when the cart had passed. “They will eat tomorrow because it is on that wagon. He will eat from what remains. If he tells you he dislikes the arrangement, hear him.”

“Would it change the amount?”

“It changed two sacks today.”

She rode on before he had found an answer.

A woman was waiting in the yard when they returned, holding a travelling basket against her hip and looking at Bastian with evident disapproval.

Ysabeau dismounted and gave the rein to Cedric.

“You said two days,” the woman told her.

“I was two days on the road.”

“After the three days before you left.”

“Yes.”

The woman set the basket on the mounting block. Ysabeau leaned in to kiss her. She accepted the kiss, then caught the front of Ysabeau’s cloak between two fingers.

“Who mended this?”

“I did.”

“I can see why you decided to become a soldier.”

Cedric kept his face still. Bastian began to investigate the basket.

“Keep him out,” the woman said. “He has had enough of my good things.”

“Master Aeryl, this is Aude,” Ysabeau said. “Aude, Master Aeryl is helping Rovel discover where we have put everybody’s equipment.”

“A worthy ambition.”

She looked at him directly, without pausing at his scar. Cedric bowed as well as he could while holding two sets of reins.

“My lady.”

“Aude will do. If you call me that in front of my sister, she will expect me to pay for more of the supper.”

Ysabeau took the basket before the horse could reach it. She asked whether Aude had eaten. The answer was that she had been assured of food at the lodging and had found the room empty.

“It is often empty,” Ysabeau said. “People keep sending for me.”

“Then I shall send for you. Apparently that is the way.”

They went inside together. Cedric took the horses to the stable and wondered how long the visit would last. He had assumed the companion mentioned by one of the men was somewhere safely at home. There had been no room in that assumption for a travelling basket, a badly mended cloak, or the particular way Ysabeau’s face had changed when she saw her in the yard.

At supper Aude chose the chair which usually held Ysabeau’s papers.

They were moved. Rovel pretended to find this remarkable, and Aude told him she would lend him the chair when she left if he promised to enforce its proper use. He said he lacked the authority. Ysabeau poured his wine and suggested he apply through the usual channels.

Cedric had been asked to stay because the revised assessment needed to be copied before morning. He had expected to finish the work at a side table while the others ate. Aude asked whether he was being punished, and Ysabeau said he was being paid. A place was made for him.

The basket contained a small glazed dish, a pair of clean stockings, and a jar of something preserved which Ysabeau tasted on the end of her spoon. She shut her eyes briefly.

“Your sister’s?”

“Mine.”

“Then hers has improved.”

Aude took the jar away until Ysabeau asked for it properly.

They spoke of a wall blown down near the house Aude had left, of a servant who had married without telling anyone until she wanted the morning off, and of whether a room upstairs could be warmed before bed. Cedric found himself listening to the ordinary matters with more interest than he expected. Ysabeau remembered the servant’s name. She asked whether the husband could work and was told that he could do almost anything provided someone stood close enough to prevent him beginning something else.

“Send him to us,” Rovel said.

“You have enough.”

“We can always use another man who needs watching.”

Cedric looked up. Rovel was reaching for bread. Aude asked him whether he intended to eat all of it, and the moment in which Cedric might have taken offence went past.

After supper he copied the assessment. Ysabeau checked the reduced amount against the original, then asked him to take the copy back to the household in the morning.

“They have the correction,” he said.

“They have it on paper which is falling apart.”

He thought of the old woman’s slipper and the pen tearing at the fold.

“I’ll take it.”

Aude was sitting by the lamp, undoing the stitches in Ysabeau’s cloak. She had made Ysabeau take it off but had put a blanket around her shoulders before letting her return to the papers. Cedric gathered his things and wished them good night.

In the passage, he paused to fasten his own coat. He could hear Aude asking whether Ysabeau would be away again before she left. Ysabeau said she would know in the morning.

“You said that yesterday in your letter.”

“It was true yesterday.”

“And now?”

“I’ll ask Rovel to go if it is only the lower quarters.”

Aude said something he could not hear. He moved on before the boards could creak beneath his feet and tell them how long he had stood there.

The old woman took the new copy and held it close to the light.

“Will they accept this next time?”

“It is signed.”

“The other one was signed.”

Cedric opened the sheet where the changed field description had been entered beneath the quantity.

“This is the part they must use.”

She read it slowly. Her son came in from the yard, wiping his hands on a cloth. She made him read it too.

He thanked Cedric and offered him a drink before he went. Cedric accepted because he was cold and the man appeared to mean it. The cup had a broken foot and needed to be held level.

Outside, the cart marks from the collection had hardened in the overnight frost. Cedric stepped between them on his way to the horse. He had reported the original error to Fayd as evidence of how the assessment was being made. He would have to add the correction. Otherwise whoever read the account would think Ysabeau had taken all six.

Chapter 12 — In This

Vaelrie found him practising the first sentence of his request.

He had said it twice to the empty room, changing require to seek and back again, when she opened the door. He stopped. She looked at the paper on the desk and then at him.

“Was it going well?”

“I was making certain the wording was clear.”

“Was it?”

“Until you interrupted.”

She came in and closed the door behind her. There was a book under her arm and a folded shawl over the other. She put both on the bed.

“You are wanted after the morning audiences. There will be somebody to fetch you.”

“Who told you?”

“The woman who would otherwise have had to come up here herself.”

“Does Jaennis know what I’m asking?”

“I expect so.”

“Have you told her?”

Vaelrie looked at him for a moment. Then she took the chair by the window.

“Give me your hand,” she said.

“Why?”

“Because there is a loose thread at the cuff and you will pull it all morning if I don’t cut it.”

He held out his wrist. She took a little knife from the desk, caught the thread, and severed it close to the seam.

“I am asking a reasonable question,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Well?”

“I told her that you were coming on business arranged through the conference. I did not tell her what you wrote to me about Father, or Halden, or Iselde’s cloth.”

“That isn’t what I meant.”

“Then what did you mean?”

He withdrew his hand. The cuff looked better. He wanted a way of continuing which would not make her look at him as though he had begun the quarrel deliberately.

“You’re here,” he said. “With her.”

“Yes.”

“And our father is fighting her queen.”

“I have noticed.”

“Have you discussed that?”

Vaelrie set the knife down.

“I was writing to you when she came in. I told her so.”

“What did you say?”

She repeated it without imitating either voice.

“‘I am writing my brother.’ She said, ‘I know.’ I told her, ‘I am telling you this plainly.’ And she thanked me.”

He waited.

“Then I said, ‘You will not use me in this.’”

“And?”

“‘I will not.’”

Ouen looked at the book she had put on the bed. He could not read the title from where he stood.

“You believe her.”

“She does not lie.”

“That leaves a great deal.”

“I know whom I was asking.”

There was a firmness in her face he had seen before, usually when their father believed a discussion had ended and she still had a question. He could remember being amused by it when the question was not addressed to him.

“I am trying to look after you,” he said.

“I would like that. You can begin by telling me if you intend to stop writing.”

“Of course I don’t.”

“Good.”

She stood and picked up the shawl. He felt they had reached an agreement without his having said the part he meant to say, which was that he had missed knowing whom she would sit beside at supper and where to find her when he had something absurd to tell.

“I brought another drawing,” he said.

She stopped at the door.

“From Hew?”

“He insisted. Iselde said the first was enough.”

“Where?”

“In the small bag. It may have been folded.”

She went to find it. By the time the servant came to fetch him, she was trying to determine whether the large shape beside the hawk was meant to be Ouen or a horse.

The audience room had been warmed before the people arrived, which made Ouen aware of how often he had waited in rooms where this had been considered unnecessary.

He stood behind a man disputing the ownership of a team left on an estate during the fighting. The queen was asking who had fed the animals in the interval. The claimant began explaining who had owned them before. She let him finish, then asked about the feed again.

“His tenant, Majesty.”

“Has he been paid?”

“I couldn’t say.”

“Find out. The animals have been eating while you argued.”

A clerk made a note. The man bowed and moved away, holding his papers as though they had become less helpful since he entered.

Ouen’s turn came before he had decided whether the exchange made his own request easier or more difficult.

Veralyn wore a dark gown with a narrow band of embroidery at the neck. The scar on her cheek was faint in the morning light. Shadowsteel stood against the side of her chair, its scabbard close enough that a passing clerk had to turn a little to avoid it. She rested one hand on the table while the request was read.

“Your household goods,” she said.

“Yes, Majesty.”

“Under armed escort.”

“Six men.”

“Your men?”

“The household’s.”

“Whose instructions do they obey?”

“Mine for the journey.”

“And afterwards?”

“My father’s officer may require them.”

She looked up from the paper.

“Then the return matters.”

He explained that they would be returning with empty wagons. She asked whether the men would remain with those wagons until they were back on the Adrian side. He said yes. It was one of the points Constano had made him put into the revised request. He was grateful now that it had an answer.

Fayd stood at the side of the table. Ouen had not noticed him when he entered. The man took the request when Veralyn handed it across and pointed out the route where protection had already been agreed with one of the local captains.

“The other section requires a separate instruction, Majesty.”

“Can it be given?”

“Within the existing warrant, if the return is entered.”

“Enter it.”

Ouen felt his shoulders ease.

“There are commercial matters also,” he said. “The oil and—”

“We have those,” Fayd said. “Your officer’s second page.”

He looked at Ouen without suggesting any opinion of how recently Ouen had read it. The courtesy was welcome and made him more wary than an ordinary sneer would have done.

Veralyn asked which landing the wagons would use. Ouen named it. She corrected his pronunciation of the neighbouring hamlet, then smiled when he repeated the name badly in exactly the same way.

“They will know where you mean.”

“My men had better.”

“Yes.”

The clerk brought the working copy. As he did, a door opened at the far end of the room and someone said there was a message from the marshal. Veralyn turned. For a moment the little audience seemed about to dissolve into more important business.

Then she looked back at Ouen.

“The two loads,” she said. “The return on the same protection. No reinforcements under the household designation.”

“I understand.”

“Have it read before you sign.”

She had resumed at the exact place she left, without asking for the paper again. Ouen had to look down to make certain he had not forgotten something she expected him to remember. The clerk indicated where he was to stand while the instrument was finished.

Fayd passed him on the way to the door.

“Your sister has been looking forward to your visit.”

“So I hear.”

“I hope the rooms are comfortable.”

“They are.”

“Good.”

The man went to receive the marshal’s message. Ouen watched him speak briefly to the messenger and choose which of two packets to take in first. The other remained in the messenger’s hands.

They had moved Ouen’s clerk to a little table beside the passage. He was checking the names on the new instrument against their own copy, reading each aloud under his breath.

“How long?” Ouen asked.

“A moment, my lord.”

The man worked more slowly once he knew he was being watched. Ouen stepped away to let him finish and found himself near the partly open audience door.

A visitor stood inside with his hat in both hands. He wore a travelling coat, plain and damp along the hem. There was nothing in his appearance to explain why the woman admitting him had asked the next petitioners to wait.

“Whose business?” Veralyn asked.

“We have come to Gelfrey’s heir.”

“From whom?”
