“The Mound, Your Grace. Those you may have heard called the Shadow-Court.”

Ouen glanced towards the clerk, who had stopped murmuring over the names.

The visitor said something lower. Ouen heard the word private and the queen’s answer granting it. The woman at the door stepped into the passage and closed it behind her.

“Is your business complete, my lord?” she asked.

“Nearly.”

She waited while the clerk put the papers into their case. There was nothing to object to in her manner. Ouen had heard the end of somebody else’s introduction because he happened to be standing too close to a door. He left with the instrument he had come for.

On the stairs he asked the clerk whether the name had been Gelfrey.

“I believe so.”

“What does it mean?”

“I don’t know, my lord.”

“Whose heir was he asking for?”

“The queen, I assumed.”

“Yes, I gathered that.”

He had expected the man to know more. Clerks generally did, particularly when a thing was obscure enough to be inconvenient.

Vaelrie was in the small room beyond the supper chamber, helping Maelis clear a space near the window.

The Runekeeper had laid her silk on a cloth and asked them to stay away from it until the knots were in order. Ouen stopped at the doorway. Vaelrie came out to speak to him and pulled the door almost shut behind her.

“Did they give it?”

“Yes.”

“All of it?”

“Enough.”

She looked as though she might ask what that meant. He produced the instrument before she could.

“There. Your queen’s seal.”

“She is your cousin.”

“She remembered that less readily than the route.”

“She may think you remember it for both of you.”

He gave her an account of the audience, including the man whose animals had continued to eat during the dispute. She laughed at that and asked whether his officer had the departure date he needed. He said the date had been confirmed.

Then he mentioned the visitor.

“Gelfrey?” she said.

“That was what I heard.”

“What did he want?”

“A private audience. He got it.”

“Then we are unlikely to find out in the passage.”

He looked at her. “You could ask.”

“Yes.”

She did not say she would. From the room behind her came Maelis’s voice, counting softly in a rhythm Ouen could not follow. He waited until it stopped.

“Does she know about such things?”

“Maelis?”

“She serves one of them.”

“One of whom?”

He made a vague movement with the document case. Vaelrie’s expression changed to the one she used when he had asked a question with more confidence than knowledge.

“She serves Mab. You could ask her about Mab.”

“I was only wondering.”

“I know.”

She touched his sleeve, close to the thread she had cut that morning.

“Come downstairs. There is something I want you to see.”

It was a little cup in a cabinet, blue at the rim and broad enough at the base that Hew might not knock it over immediately. She had asked the owner where another could be bought. Ouen inspected it and suggested two, in case the first suffered the fate of its predecessor.

Vaelrie said she had thought of that already. He promised to carry them home.

Chapter 13 — Who Will Protect Us?

Lord Verren had arranged the meeting in a room from which Ysabeau could see his boats.

There were four at the landing. Three had been unloaded; the fourth still had men passing goods up a plank. Every time somebody moved a cask the plank bent beneath him, and the man behind waited until it stopped shaking before taking his own load. Cedric watched for a moment, then looked at the chairs.

Verren had placed one beside the window and another well away from it, where the fire would warm its occupant without requiring him to sit close to the hearth. Ysabeau chose the one near the window. Verren’s smile remained in place as he took the warmer chair himself.

“Wine?”

“After we have finished.”

“As you prefer.”

He nodded to the servant, who moved the jug to a side table and stayed within reach of it. Cedric stood behind Ysabeau’s right shoulder. He had been given no part to play beyond carrying the account and answering questions about the men at the lower quarters. Rovel had made him repeat their number before they left.

Verren began with the boats. Two had been delayed at a landing where Valdais’s men were collecting passage dues under an authority he had understood to exempt his own household. The loss of time had cost him a sale. Ysabeau asked whether he had the exemption. He produced a copy.

She read it and handed it to Cedric to hold.

“The due concerns goods carried for another house.”

“Carried on my vessels.”

“On your vessels, under their marks.”

“My bargain was for passage.”

“Your bargain was for your household’s passage. If you want a commercial exemption as well, His Grace must hear it.”

“He has heard it.”

“What was the answer?”

Verren looked towards the servant. The man poured a cup without waiting for another instruction.

“The answer was that we should consider the whole of our arrangement.”

Ysabeau inclined her head.

“Then that is what we are doing.”

Cedric placed the exemption beside the list he carried. Verren looked at the list and took his cup.

Twenty-four mounted men had been promised. Eleven were at the quarters. Four others had been named in a letter received three days earlier. Of the remaining nine, Verren’s secretary had given two different accounts, one saying that they awaited horses and the other that they were guarding the household’s goods.

“Both are true,” Verren said. “The men guard while the horses are found.”

“Where?”

“At home.”

“Your home here?”

“My family’s holdings are not all within sight of that window.”

Ysabeau waited. He drank before naming the place.

Cedric knew it. They had passed the approach the previous afternoon. Rovel had spoken to an officer there about a saddle and received an answer from the man now being described as absent in search of horses.

He looked at Ysabeau. She was watching Verren and gave no indication that she had noticed him.

“The four named separately,” she said. “When will they arrive?”

“Two are unwell.”

“Then send replacements.”

“I have supplied eleven fit men already.”

“Of twenty-four.”

Verren put his cup down.

“My lady, the figure was agreed before Hollin. I have had losses which the agreement could not anticipate.”

“Which men?”

“Animals, stores, opportunities to replace both. You know what the movement has done to trade.”

“I know what eleven men can hold. His Grace’s officer has been told he can expect twenty-four. If the difference is never to arrive, he must be informed before he puts them on a road.”

Verren looked at Cedric for the first time since greeting him.

“Your young gentleman has a very exact account.”

“He has seen the eleven.”

“And the missing thirteen?”

“Some of them,” Cedric said.

Ysabeau turned her head slightly.

Verren’s expression remained courteous. “Indeed?”

Cedric named the officer Rovel had spoken to and the place where they had found him. He described the six saddled horses standing in the yard and distinguished them from the three animals being shod beside the shed. He had no need to improve the figures. They were enough.

“Household mounts,” Verren said.

“They may be,” Cedric answered.

“Then they are not promised under this instrument.”

“I was asked where the officer was. I saw him there.”

Ysabeau put a hand on the list.

“Can he be sent for?”

Verren looked towards the boats. One of the men on the plank had put his load down to argue with somebody below. The queue behind him had stopped.

“He can.”

“Please do.”

The servant went to the door with the message. Verren sat very still until it closed.

They waited in the gallery while the officer was fetched.

Ysabeau walked to the far end and stood looking at a portrait which had been hung where the light made most of the face difficult to see. Cedric joined her with the papers under his arm.

“Should I have spoken?”

“You were asked.”

“By him.”

“Yes.”

“He was trying to make the account look uncertain.”

“Then it was useful to say what you had seen.”

He looked at the portrait. The painted man wore a chain too elaborate to have sat comfortably over the collar beneath it. His hand rested on a hound’s head. Cedric wondered whether the hound had endured the painter any more patiently than he himself would have.

“What happens if he refuses the men?”

“Someone else must cover the crossing.”

“Who?”

“I have a company within reach.”

“Then he can refuse.”

Ysabeau looked at him.

“The company needs quarters. His store houses are on the approach. He has been allowed to keep them in commercial use because he undertook to supply the guard himself.”

Cedric thought of the boats and the busy plank. He could see what a company billeted along that landing would do to the arrangement Verren had meant them to admire.

“He will say he has been threatened.”

“He has read the same instrument.”

“Has he?”

“I read it with him when he signed.”

At the other end of the gallery a door opened. The servant returned with word that refreshments had been set in a smaller room. Ysabeau said they would wait where they were. Cedric was thirsty, but stayed beside her.

He asked about the man in the portrait. She said he believed it was Verren’s grandfather, although the chain had belonged to somebody else. She had been told the painting was copied after his death from one in which he wore a different coat.

“Why change it?”

“The family preferred this one.”

Cedric looked at the hound again and wondered what else had been improved.

The officer arrived with mud on his boots and no prepared account of being elsewhere.

He confirmed that six horses could be sent immediately. Two of the men were ill; substitutes could be found among those guarding the landing. Verren required that the substitutes remain available for household service if the boats were threatened. Ysabeau asked whether the mounted men at the crossing would be permitted to return at any moment their lord found another use for them.

“Under extraordinary circumstances,” Verren said.

“What circumstances?”

“An attack here.”

“Who gives the order?”

“I do.”

“Then the commander must be informed before they move. He must know what has happened to his guard.”

The officer looked relieved at the word before. Cedric suspected he had once been given incompatible orders and been blamed for choosing either one.

They settled six immediate arrivals and a date for the remainder. Ysabeau accepted the reduction for the two sick men until replacements had been named. Verren required an acknowledgement that he had reported the illness himself. Cedric copied the clause into the margin, leaving room for both parties to initial it.

The possibility of quarters in the store houses was mentioned once. Verren listened, asked how many men the company contained, and ceased arguing over the six horses.

Wine was served when the document had been read. Ysabeau took a little. Cedric accepted more than he intended because he had been thirsty for over an hour. Verren asked him after his father and remembered a gathering Cedric had attended as a child. He named someone who had died since then, and for a moment the conversation became easy.

“You have your father’s shoulders,” he said.

Cedric had been told this before and liked hearing it here.

“I am sorry he could not come himself.”

“He isn’t travelling much now.”

“Give him my respects when you write.”

“I will.”

Verren seemed sincere. Cedric wondered whether he had been too pleased to produce the account of the horses. The agreement lay on the table, and the officer was already waiting at the door to put it into effect.

Ysabeau left Cedric to supervise the departure of the six men. She had another meeting before evening and wanted confirmation that the horses on the paper were the ones sent.

It was the first time she had left him in such a position without Rovel nearby. He watched her mount Bastian, exchange a few words with the groom, and ride away. Verren came to the steps to see her off, then returned indoors without inviting Cedric to follow.

The six men needed feed for the road. Their officer had a store key which opened the wrong lock. While someone fetched the keeper, Cedric went to the side door for water.

He heard voices through the open window above the trough.

“The protection is written,” a man said.

“For their side of the water.” Verren’s voice. “Who will put horse here when she comes?”

“There is provision for mutual assistance.”

“Between houses which have all sent their best men away.”

“The queen has upheld the previous agreements.”

“With her own rebels, on her own roads. I have just signed another undertaking to the woman who knows how long it takes to ride between every landing I use. Give me a name.”

Cedric stopped with the dipper against the bucket’s rim.

“A name?”

“Of the man who will stand here in her way.”

There was a silence. The unseen visitor spoke more quietly, and Cedric could not hear the words. He put the dipper back into the bucket to cover the sound of his own movement, then drank.

Verren said, “When you have that answer, bring the rest again.”

Cedric moved away before the door opened.

The keeper had found the right key. Men were filling nosebags in the yard. Cedric checked the animals against the paper, making a careful business of the markings while he thought about the conversation. He had heard no name for the visitor. The offer could have come from one of several houses now looking towards the queen. He could report the words he knew.

He wanted to see the man leave. To do that he would have to delay the escort, and Ysabeau was expecting the men before dark.

He gave the order to move.

The six arrived together.

Rovel checked them in while Cedric took the acknowledgement to Ysabeau. She was removing a bandage from a small cut at the base of her thumb. Aude held a clean strip ready.

“Verren’s?” she asked when he entered.

“All six.”

“Good.”

“One horse has a sore under the pad. His officer says it began yesterday. I have put it in the account.”

“Have the stable look at it.”

“I have.”

She held out her uninjured hand for the paper. He watched her read it and waited for a question he could answer. None came. She put it with the other returns.

“Thank you,” she said.

Aude caught her wrist and began wrapping the clean strip. Ysabeau let her do it, still looking at the next paper. Cedric left before the bandage was tied.

In his room he wrote Verren’s question exactly as he remembered it: Who will put horse here when she comes? He identified the speaker and the window through which he had heard him, then described the limits of what he knew about the visitor. He included the newly agreed six, the date for the remainder, and the possible use of the store houses as quarters.

He read the report before folding it. It was the most useful account he had yet sent. The confidence with which he could write it pleased him.

Rovel knocked while he was reaching for the wrapper.

Cedric put the acknowledgement over the report and opened the door. The captain stood with one boot unlaced, keeping his weight on the other foot. Behind him waited the man who had ridden the horse with the sore under its pad.

“He says you entered it,” Rovel said.

“I did.”

“His officer’s copy says nothing about a sore. Show me yours.”

Cedric looked at the paper on the table. He had given the original to Ysabeau. The sheet covering his report was the brief acknowledgement of arrival, which named the horse but did not describe its condition. He had meant to copy the fuller particulars for his own use after eating.

“The dame has it.”

“Then fetch it. I want this settled before someone tells me we damaged the animal after accepting it.”

The rider looked relieved. He had been told he would pay if the horse needed time out of service. His relief made Cedric feel suddenly slow and foolish. There was an ordinary duty before him, and beneath the paper Rovel had come to see lay an account of the command intended for its enemies.

“I’ll bring it to you.”

“I’ll wait here. Sit down, lad.”

Rovel indicated the stool to the rider and lowered himself onto the edge of Cedric’s bed. He bent to loosen the boot farther, breathing sharply through his nose. Cedric stood between the table and the door.

“You can eat while I find it.”

“So can he, when we’ve finished.”

The captain glanced up. Cedric had offered too much assistance and received the attention he was trying to avoid.

He took the acknowledgement from the table. Beneath it his own writing lay exposed. The top line named Verren. Rovel could read it from where he sat if he leaned forward.

Cedric turned the sheet over, put the acknowledgement back on top, and went out.

Ysabeau had moved to the next room. Aude was clearing the bandage ends from the chair beside her. Cedric asked for the fuller return and explained Rovel’s request with such care that Ysabeau stopped looking through the pile.

“Has the man been accused?”

“He thinks he will be.”

“Then let him see the account corrected. His officer knew about the sore before he sent him.”

She found the paper. Cedric took it and hurried back, trying to make the hurry belong to the rider’s need for supper.

Rovel had the acknowledgement in his hand.

Cedric stopped in the doorway. The report remained face-down on the table. Its edge had moved clear of the place where the acknowledgement had covered it.

“There is no condition entered here,” Rovel said.

“No. It’s on this one.”

He held out the return. Rovel put the acknowledgement on the report again and read the relevant passage aloud. The rider corrected a word: the sore had begun the previous morning, not the previous evening. Cedric checked the original note in the margin. The man was right.

“Write that properly,” Rovel said. “And give him a copy. If someone stops his pay, he needs something to show.”

Cedric had one clean sheet within reach. He drew it towards him without disturbing the others. Rovel watched him write, dictated the officer’s name when he paused, and required him to read the whole statement back to the rider.

The work took no more than a few minutes. Cedric could feel sweat cooling beneath his collar.

When it was done, Rovel signed and sent the man down to eat. He remained on the bed, tightening his boot again.

“Was Verren difficult?”

“He argued.”

“About the horses?”

“Yes.”

“What else?”

Cedric looked at him. The captain pulled the lace through the upper hole and winced.

“He wanted the men available if the boats were threatened.”

“He generally wants them available for whatever occurs to him next. You did well to bring all six.”

Rovel stood and steadied himself against the bedpost. Cedric offered an arm. This time the captain took it as far as the door.

After he had gone, Cedric remained with his hand on the latch. He listened until the uneven footsteps reached the stair. Then he went back to the table.

The report looked as he had left it. He could not tell whether it had been lifted. Rovel’s questions might have meant nothing beyond an officer asking how a negotiation had gone.

Cedric read the page once more. Verren’s question was exact. The account of the visitor distinguished what he had heard from what he had guessed. He folded it into the agreed packet, put it inside his coat, and took it downstairs with him. At supper Rovel made room on the bench. Cedric sat where he was directed. The rider thanked him for the copy before passing the bread.

Chapter 14 — The Wedding Table

The invitation required an extra four days on Ouen’s safe-conduct.

He was inclined to regard this as somebody else’s problem until the clerk pointed out that he would be travelling under the expired instrument himself. Vaelrie stood beside the desk, waiting for him to agree. She had already decided he was staying.

“Is there a difficulty with extending it?” Ouen asked.

“The issuing office must enter the new date.”

“Then have them enter it.”

“I need your request.”

“This is my request.”

The clerk put a sheet before him. Ouen signed and gave the pen back. Vaelrie thanked the man before he could begin explaining what else might be needed.

“You needn’t stand over me,” Ouen said when they were alone.

“I wanted to be certain you knew it was possible.”

“I had assumed it was possible to attend a wedding.”

“You assumed it was possible to ride home the day after tomorrow.”

“It is.”

“Yes.”

He had wanted to see Elyas, although he would have preferred the meeting to occur without the whole court attending. They had not spent much time together as adults. There was a particular difficulty in being of an age with a man whose standing had improved through actions everybody expected you to know about. Ouen had read the accounts. He had also heard three different descriptions of the woman he meant to marry, of which only his sister’s supplied much information about the woman herself.

Vaelrie took the signed request.

“Come down later. They are arguing about where everyone sits.”

“Who is?”

“People who will be fed wherever they put themselves.”

She went to deliver the paper. Ouen called after her to ask whether he needed another coat. She answered that the one he had would do, provided he did not allow the child who had drawn the hawk to paint it first.

He found Mara in the room where the tables were being laid.

She had a cup in one hand and a piece of paper in the other. A household officer was explaining why three people could not sit together without moving a fourth person to a place beneath her rank. Elyas stood beside the window listening with the expression of a man who had discovered that suggesting another table would merely enlarge the dispute.

“I asked for Gisla beside me,” Mara said.

“Yes, my lady. The difficulty is—”

“I’ve heard the difficulty. I don’t want her put at the far end because she doesn’t have a title you can fit into the list.”

“There are other guests—”

“Who can sit at the other side,” Elyas said.

The officer looked at him.

“If Gisla sits there, Your Highness, Lady—”

“Ask the lady whether she objects,” Mara said. “You have told me twice that she will. Has she?”

The man had not asked. Ouen could tell from the way he began gathering the loose sheets. He was being made to risk giving offence where he had hoped to anticipate it, and would probably be blamed either way.

Elyas saw Ouen in the doorway and came to greet him.

“You’ve arrived safely.”

“With several people knowing the number of my horses.”

“That sounds safe enough.”

They clasped hands. Elyas looked thinner than Ouen remembered and had a small mark near the jaw which might have been a wound or merely a shaving cut. Ouen wanted to ask about the fighting, but Mara had put her papers down and was coming towards them.

“My lord.”

He bowed. “Lady Mara.”

“Vaelrie says you can stay.”

“She is generally the first to know what I shall do.”

Mara smiled. “She has been looking forward to it.”

The household officer returned with word that the lady in question had no objection to moving, provided she could hear the music. He sounded almost disappointed. Mara thanked him and asked him to place her nearer the musicians.

“There,” she said to Elyas. “Now we can eat.”

“On the day.”

“I meant now.”

She raised the cup, which was empty. He took it from her and went to the sideboard for more water. Ouen watched the exchange. It had the ease of a married household before anybody had signed or sworn anything, and he wondered what the objectors expected to change by arranging the chairs differently.

Mara asked after Iselde and Hew. He gave the answer he had grown accustomed to giving and found himself describing the red feet again. She asked whether the paint was safe if the boy put the bird in his mouth.

“He’s past that.”

“Are you sure?”

He thought of the glove fastening.

“I’ll write.”

She nodded, entirely seriously. Elyas brought her the water and offered Ouen wine. They sat at the end of the unlaid table while the officer continued shifting the names elsewhere.

The ceremony was shorter than Ouen’s had been.

He had been required to stand in a heavy coat through enough words to make his feet hurt, with his father watching from a place where he could catch his eye whenever his attention wandered. Iselde had stood so still that Ouen had wondered whether she was frightened, then forgotten to ask her afterwards. Remembering it now, he wished he had asked.

Mara’s sleeve moved against Elyas’s when they turned together. The woman conducting the ceremony had to ask them to face forward again. Someone behind Ouen shifted a chair. A child whispered loudly enough to be heard, was hushed, and asked the same question more quietly.

The promises were spoken clearly. Elyas’s voice carried to the back of the room. Mara’s was softer, but Ouen heard it. When the woman officiating placed their hands together, Mara moved her thumb once against his knuckles.

The witnesses came forward. Ouen stood aside to let Constano pass, then noticed Berent following with the folded cloth meant for the table. The young man had acquired the solemn manner of a person determined that no small failure of his should disturb the occasion. He spread the cloth, adjusted a corner, and retreated only after Mara thanked him by name.

Vaelrie touched Ouen’s elbow. He moved because she wanted to see.

Afterwards there were greetings, and a period in which everyone stood too close to the door while insisting that everybody else go through first. Jaennis resolved it by taking Mara’s arm and walking. The others followed.

Veralyn spoke to Elyas near the entrance to the supper room. Ouen heard her ask whether the house was ready. Elyas said part of it was. She asked which part and laughed at his answer. Whatever had been said about the kitchen made Mara turn her head and insist that he had agreed to the arrangement.

“I did,” Elyas said.

“Then don’t make that face when you tell people.”

The queen laughed again and went in with them.

Ouen was seated beside a man who wanted to discuss the bride’s family.

He began politely enough, asking whether Ouen knew her people. Ouen said he did not. The man remarked that few seemed to, then looked towards the upper table with an expression inviting agreement about what this implied.

“She has been with Elyas some time,” Ouen said.

“So I hear.”

“They seem well acquainted.”

The man paused, unsure whether Ouen had meant to make a joke at their expense. Ouen had meant exactly what he said, but the possibility of having made a better answer than intended encouraged him to go on eating.

At the upper table Gisla was speaking to Mara, who listened with her head turned away from the man approaching to congratulate her. The man waited. When she turned back, she apologized and thanked him. He found nothing to complain of in the words and still looked aggrieved as he went away.

“What can be so urgent?” Ouen’s neighbour murmured.

“I don’t know.”

“One would think the work could wait for a day.”

Ouen looked at the man. “What work?”

“The sick. The stations. Whatever she has undertaken.”

“Then you do know.”

The man fell silent. Ouen returned to the dish before him. He had not intended to defend anybody’s arrangements, but he disliked being asked a question merely to provide room for someone else to answer it.

Later, when Mara came past, the neighbour congratulated her and said she must be relieved to have more settled duties now.

“After today I shall be back with Gisla,” she said.

“Yes, of course, but in time—”

“She is expecting me.”

She smiled and moved on before the sentence could be completed. Ouen watched her stop to speak to a serving woman carrying empty dishes. They appeared to know each other. The exchange made the serving woman laugh, then look quickly towards the table to see who had heard.

Ouen asked his neighbour to pass the bread.

The music began while people were still eating.

Maelis had arranged a short performance for two singers and a man who objected to being asked to play while his supper cooled. She moved his plate to the hearth and told him she would personally prevent anybody stealing it. Margery said this was insufficient protection, since Maelis had already eaten something from it.

“A piece of bread.”

“It was under the meat.”

“A very well-placed piece of bread.”

The musician gave up arguing and began. One of the singers missed his entrance. The other waited for him, smiling so broadly that the room understood the error before the man had recovered. By the second verse they were together. Ouen found himself tapping the side of his cup in time with the refrain.

Maelis joined the company afterwards with her pinned sleeve undone and the silk put away. The cricket’s box had been moved off a table where the servants were clearing dishes. She found it beneath a chair and spent some time explaining to its occupant that she had not chosen the location.

Vaelrie listened, then asked whether the cricket preferred the music to conversation.

“That depends on the conversation.”

“Then it has been well supplied today.”

“We can hope.”

Maelis began an account of an entertainer who had once agreed to play at two weddings on the same afternoon. Margery interrupted to say she knew how it ended: he had gone to the wrong one and been paid twice to leave.

“That is another man,” Maelis said.

“It is the same man.”

“He was my employer.”

“Then you should have asked for more.”

The people listening laughed. Maelis did not.

“I was going to get to the part about the horse.”

“We have heard about horses,” Margery said, glancing at Ouen.

Maelis set down her cup. “Then I shall spare you.”

There was a little silence. Margery saw that she had hurt her and tried to restore the account by asking which horse, but the moment had gone. Maelis rose to see that the musician had recovered his supper.

Vaelrie looked after her. Ouen expected her to follow, but she remained beside him and asked how long the ride home would take. He described the planned stages. She knew the first lodging and told him which room to refuse if it was offered. They argued pleasantly over whether a warm room above a kitchen was preferable to one that smelled less of onions.

Across the room, Margery went to Maelis and spoke to her. Maelis answered without smiling. A little later they were both helping the musician free a string caught beneath a fastening on his case. Margery held the lamp while Maelis worked. When it came loose, Maelis handed it back and accepted something Margery said with a nod.

Ouen lost sight of them when the chairs were moved for dancing.

Veralyn stepped onto the floor with Tylan after he had been persuaded that he could not remain beside the wall through every tune.

He was a large man to make himself inconspicuous. Ouen had noticed him earlier, speaking quietly to an officer who kept pointing towards the door. Now the queen took his arm to step past a chair and retained it until they reached the other dancers. He said something which made her look down at his feet. She laughed, then allowed him to place her for the first figure.

Jaennis stood near Ouen and Vaelrie. Her attention followed them for a moment before she turned to answer a question from Ysolt. Vaelrie had brought her a cup of water and waited while she finished speaking, holding it without trying to enter the conversation. When Jaennis turned, she accepted it and rested her free hand briefly over Vaelrie’s.

“Thank you.”

“You had wine before.”

“I did.”

“You said it would make your head hurt.”

“And you remembered when I didn’t.”

Vaelrie looked pleased. Jaennis drank, then asked whether she had eaten enough. Ouen, who had been asked the same question by three people since arriving, almost intervened to say that his sister had done very well. He found he did not want to disturb her expression.

Elyas and Mara danced only once while Ouen was watching. At the end Mara said something close to her husband’s ear and left the floor with him. They were stopped twice before reaching the door and once more in the passage beyond it. Ouen could see Elyas waiting while Mara answered the last well-wisher, with the same patience he had shown over the seating. When they finally got away, the next tune had already begun.

Vaelrie asked Ouen to dance.

“I have been riding.”

“So you keep telling me.”

“My knee is stiff.”

“Then I will choose something slow.”

She took his hand before he could produce another reason. He went with her, conscious at first of the people watching, then sufficiently occupied in remembering the steps that they ceased to matter. She corrected him once, quietly. He told her she had moved too early. She agreed for the sake of getting them both to the next turn, and he recognized what she had done only afterwards.

When they finished, he was warm and in a much better temper.

“You could have asked sooner,” he said.

“You could have agreed sooner.”

She leaned against his arm while they stood aside for the next pair. He put his hand over hers and let the answer go.

His revised safe-conduct was delivered before breakfast the following morning.

The new date had been entered cleanly and the extension sealed. Ouen checked it himself. He had also received a short acknowledgement from home: the four marked bales had been set aside, and an inspection would be made as agreed. Halden had written that the two wrapping seams were visible. Ouen folded that letter and put it into the case without mentioning it to his sister.

Vaelrie came to see him off with the two blue-rimmed cups wrapped separately. She made him watch while they were put into his bag.

“If one breaks, tell me which.”

“They’re identical.”

“This one has a little mark inside.”

“How will I see it when it’s broken?”

“You will have the pieces.”

He laughed and kissed her forehead. For a moment she held his coat as she had on the stairs when he arrived.

“Write when you are home.”

“I will.”

“And tell Iselde I am writing to her separately.”

“About what?”

“Things I wish to tell Iselde.”

“I see.”

“I doubt it.”

Jaennis came down while his men were bringing the horses into the yard. She wished him a good journey and asked him to send word if the first road officer failed to honour the passage. It was a practical offer. He thanked her for it and for the room.

“You have made my sister comfortable,” he said.

Vaelrie looked at him sharply, perhaps expecting something to follow.

Jaennis met his eyes.

“I am glad she is here.”

“Yes,” he said. “So is she.”

He could give the answer without knowing what else he meant by it. Jaennis let it stand.

At the gate, Maelis appeared carrying the cricket box beneath her cloak. She was going to the morning market with Joan. Ouen asked whether the creature had forgiven the chair.

“It has found another grievance.”

“Then it is settled into the household.”

She laughed. He had meant the remark to amuse her and was pleased that it had.

As he mounted, Vaelrie reached up to touch his glove.

“The cups,” she said.

“I know.”

“And the boy.”

“I’ll tell him.”

He rode out looking back once at the women under the arch. His sister lifted a hand. Then Joan said something to her and she turned to answer, still smiling, while he took the road towards the first barrier.

Part III — An agreement that might hold

Chapter 15 — Both Promises

The man from the lower holdings had brought the harness with him.

It lay over the back of a chair in Ouen’s hall, damp at the buckles, with a clean strip sewn into one trace. Beside it stood a boy holding two halters. The boy kept changing the halters from one hand to the other because the wet rope was cold.

Ouen had expected callers. Halden had told him there would be callers on his first morning home, and he had agreed to see them after breakfast. He had not agreed that they could bring harness into the hall.

“Put that by the door,” he said.

The man lifted it at once. His name was Martin; Ouen remembered him from the autumn, standing in a field with a hand on a horse’s neck while Halden explained when it would be returned. He had lost a front tooth since then. The gap gave his face an expression of permanent disapproval which Ouen tried not to resent.

“You’ve come for the five,” he said.

“Yes, my lord.”

“They may not all be ready today.”

Martin looked at the boy. The boy lowered the halters.

“We were told today.”

“I’ve only just come home.”

“We know. We waited.”

The end of breakfast still stood on the table: a dish of pears, an overturned salt spoon, the cup Hew had used so that his father could see him using it. Ouen moved the cup away from the edge. One of Vaelrie’s two had survived the journey unbroken. The other had acquired a small chip, which he had hoped Iselde would not notice before he could decide how to describe it in his letter.

“What is the immediate need?” he asked.

“The barley ground, my lord. The upper strips have dried enough. If we can begin this week—”

“You have other animals.”

“Two. For three households.”

“You managed before.”

“Before the loan, we had seven.”

Ouen could see that answer arriving and had still given the man the opportunity to deliver it. He looked towards the passage. Halden appeared with a second visitor before he had to ask another question.

The officer wore a travel-stained cloak fastened at one shoulder. He bowed, gave his name as Renaud, and said he had come for the horses promised to His Grace’s command.

“The first party is ready to leave tomorrow,” he said. “I have men enough to lead the remounts.”

Ouen looked at Halden.

“The five in the lower stable,” the officer added.

Martin set the harness down on the floor.

For a little while Ouen believed that the mistake could be made to belong to the way the horses had been described.

The five were not all remounts. Two were household animals used for riding and light work; one was a mare Martin had borrowed from a neighbour in order to furnish the original loan. If Renaud required five serviceable cavalry horses, the lower stable would disappoint him.

The officer listened to this explanation without looking disappointed.

“I have seen them, my lord. They will carry baggage, and two will do for messengers. That was what your letter offered.”

Ouen held out his hand. Renaud produced the letter.

He had written it before leaving for Valora. He remembered wanting the business settled so that he could begin the journey without another question about his father’s requirements. The phrase was quite plain: the five could be collected once the officer had men available to receive them. He had not added that their original owners expected them back first.

Martin had a paper too. He held it by the edges, keeping his dirty thumb off the writing.

“The second date, my lord. After the first was changed.”

Halden laid the two documents side by side. He had left room for Ouen to read them without reaching across anybody.

“Why wasn’t I told?” Ouen asked.

“I brought the revised return date before you left,” Halden said. “You confirmed it.”

“And the army letter?”

“You dictated that afterwards.”

“You might have pointed it out.”

“I asked whether these were the same five.”

Ouen remembered the question. He remembered replying that he was quite capable of recognizing five horses without having each described to him again. The two visitors stood silently while he read his own words.

“We can provide substitutes,” he said.

Renaud nodded. “Provided they can travel.”

Martin said, “Provided they can pull.”

Halden looked at the papers.

“Which have we?” Ouen asked him.

“The horses not already assigned are your grey, the small bay used by the nurse, and the chestnut recovering from the sore.”

“The chestnut can’t go.”

“No, my lord.”

“Nor the nurse’s.”

“No.”

His grey stood in the upper stable, where it had been groomed for the ride Ouen had intended to take after the callers were finished. He was a good horse, steady among the hawks and willing to wait while another animal made itself the centre of attention. Renaud knew which one Halden meant. He had looked towards the yard.

“I need five,” Martin said. “If I get only one, I must decide whose ground stays untilled.”

Ouen had begun considering offering him one. He closed his mouth.

They went to the stable together.

It would have been easier to inspect the horses without both claimants present, but neither had shown any inclination to leave. Martin’s boy came behind them with the halters. Renaud called his groom out of the yard. There was now a small procession following Ouen through his own house, every person able to identify a promise he would fail to keep.

The horses were in good condition. This made matters worse. Martin ran his hand along the mare’s flank and looked at the new shoe on her near fore. Renaud examined the smaller gelding’s back and asked whether it had carried a pack recently. Both spoke to the stablemen as though the animals were about to be handed over to them.

“Stop,” Ouen said.

They looked up.

He went to the grey’s stall. The horse turned towards him, ears forward. Ouen rubbed the smooth place beneath the forelock and felt a wave of resentment towards everybody waiting behind him. His father had twenty horses going to the army already, or nearly twenty, depending on which hired men had finally agreed to go. Five more ought not to determine a campaign.

“When do you need to arrive?” he asked Renaud.

The officer named the day.

“If I send you three now, can two follow?”

“Three of these?”

“No. This one, and two hired.”

“From where?” Halden asked.

“The posting house.”

“They charge for the return as well.”

“I know what they charge.”

He did not, precisely. He knew it would be too much.

Renaud came to look at the grey. He ran a hand down one leg, asked its age, and said he could accept it with two others if the later pair reached him before the company moved again.

“Can you promise that?” he asked.

Ouen looked at Martin’s boy holding the halters.

“No. Halden can ask what is available. I can promise to send what we actually hire.”

Renaud considered the answer. “I must put that in my return.”

“Put it there.”

“And if I cannot obtain the other two?” Halden asked.

“Then tell me before you promise them.”

The words came out too sharply. Halden inclined his head. Ouen heard the unfairness and let it remain because correcting it would require doing so before the others.

Martin still stood beside the mare.

“You take your five,” Ouen said. “All five. Today.”

The boy looked up so quickly that the halters struck his knee.

“Thank you, my lord,” Martin said.

“Send word when the ground is worked.”

“Yes.”

It was an unnecessary instruction. Ouen would learn nothing useful from the message. He wanted the man to leave with something required of him besides gratitude.

Renaud’s groom began asking about the grey’s feed. Ouen stayed to answer, telling him about a slight tenderness beneath one part of the pad and the animal’s habit of reaching for oats before the bowl had been set down. The groom listened well. Ouen disliked him less by the time they finished.

“Don’t have a boy pull at his mouth,” he said. “He will stand if he’s asked.”

The groom promised.

The posting house had four horses available and would hire three.

Its keeper refused to send the fourth because he expected a courier whose authority obliged him to furnish a mount. Halden returned with the terms written out and asked which two Ouen wanted first. The hire cost more than the two later horses would have been worth if they never came back.

“Must they be good enough to breed?” Ouen asked.

“One is a gelding.”

“I was speaking of the price.”

“Yes, my lord.”

He paid it from the money set aside for replacing his own hunting saddle. The saddler would have to wait. The old saddle had another season in it if the stitching was renewed, and he had no immediate use for it on the grey now being prepared for another man’s baggage.

By midday the lower holdings had their horses and Renaud had three with a provision for the remaining two. There had been an hour in which Ouen could have told himself the matter was settled. Then Iselde asked why the payment had come out of the saddle money.

“Because I was short of horses.”

“I thought we had given enough.”

“We had given the same ones twice.”

She looked at him. He corrected himself.

“I had.”

Iselde was standing beside the chest where she kept the household linen. She had a pair of Hew’s stockings in her hand, one turned inside out for mending. She put them down.

“Can we afford the hire?”

“Yes.”

“Then why do you look as though I ought to be pleased?”

“I have sent my own horse.”

“I know. I saw him go.”

“I could have taken the other five.”

“You had told the men they could have them.”

He walked to the window. The grey was still in the yard; one of Renaud’s men was shortening a strap. The animal stood with a hind foot resting, waiting in the manner Ouen had just explained to the groom.

“I’ve put it right,” he said.

Iselde took up the stocking again.

“For today.”

He wanted to object to the way she said it, but she had not said it unkindly. He stayed by the window until the horse was led through the gate.

Hew had been promised the lower field after dinner.

Ouen remembered only when he came down to find the child in his cloak, wearing the glove and waiting on the stair. There were two letters still unanswered on the desk and a man from the posting house requiring the payment to be confirmed before he released the third animal. Halden had told the man to wait because Ouen was eating. Ouen had finished eating half an hour ago.

“Perhaps tomorrow,” he began.

Hew’s arm dropped. The wooden bird swung on its thong and struck the step.

Ouen stopped.

“Pick her up. We must have a look at the wing.”

The boy examined the hawk. A little blue paint had chipped from the tip. He rubbed it with his thumb, then held it out as though the injury required a decision from somebody qualified.

“She can still fly,” Ouen said.

They went down to the yard. The man from the posting house approached with his paper. Ouen told him to bring it to the field if it could not wait. The man looked at Halden. Halden said he would come too.

It was a disagreeable arrangement. Ouen had hoped the walk would take him away from the horses he had promised, the horses he had sent, and the horses he was paying to send. But Hew was beside him, taking very long steps to keep up, and he slowed until the child could walk ordinarily.

In the lower field they stopped near the willows. The ground was dry enough to stand on the rise. Ouen set the wooden hawk on Hew’s glove and showed him how to keep the thong from tangling round its feet. The boy practised presenting it to a fence post.

Halden held the posting-house paper against the back of a book. Ouen signed, checked the amount, and handed it back. The man thanked him and went. Halden remained a little longer, watching the child.

“Anything else?” Ouen asked.

“Nothing that cannot wait until you return.”

“Then let it.”

The officer took the path back towards the house. Hew had managed to make the bird stand on the post and wanted his father to admire it. Ouen bent to inspect the red feet. The chipped wing pointed crookedly towards the river.

“She sees something,” Hew said.

“What?”

“A rabbit.”

“Where?”

The boy pointed to a patch of grass beneath the hedge. There was no rabbit, but Ouen followed the line of his finger and moved a little to improve his view.

“Keep still,” Hew whispered.

Ouen kept still.

Chapter 16 — A Place After the War

Derrit asked whether Cedric wanted the men to arrive together.

Cedric looked at him. “Of course.”

“Then we must leave the rear cart behind.”

The cart stood beneath the eaves of the empty dye-house, with two men sitting on its edge and a third asleep among the folded cloaks. Its right wheel leaned inward. Cedric had noticed the angle without understanding why the team had been left unharnessed.

“Can it be mended?”

“It has been mended. That is the mending.”

Derrit pointed beneath the axle. A piece of new wood had been wedged against the damaged part and bound with cord. It might survive another few miles on a level road. Ahead of them, the track climbed through an orchard before joining the road to their new quarters. Rain had left long ruts beneath the trees.

Cedric had thirty-two men to move, eight of whom could not make the distance on foot. Ysabeau had given him until nightfall. Derrit knew the men and was to go with them, but the responsibility for the movement had been placed in Cedric’s hands.

He had been pleased by that when he took the order.

“What would you use?” he asked.

“The wagon carrying Sir Gauter’s chest. It has a sound axle.”

“And room?”

“If the chest is taken off.”

Cedric looked at the other vehicle. Two men stood beside it in Gauter’s colours. One had his hand on the lid of the chest as though its owner might accuse him of neglect merely for standing near it without touching it.

“What is in it?”

“Money. Clothes. Plate. Depends who asks.”

“Has anyone asked him to move it?”

“He asked us to put it on.”

Cedric walked over. The guard with his hand on the chest watched him come and straightened. He said Sir Gauter was inside the house. Cedric went to find him.

The knight was standing in a room whose curtains had been taken down.

He had used the dye-house as quarters through winter and now disliked the manner of leaving it. His men were packing more slowly than the others. On the mantel stood a cup nobody had claimed. He picked it up while Cedric spoke, inspected the mark beneath it, and called a servant to say it belonged with the rest.

“The wagon must carry the men who cannot walk,” Cedric said.

“They have a cart.”

“The axle is failing.”

“Then have it repaired.”

“There isn’t time to repair it properly.”

Gauter gave the cup to the servant.

“I have paid for that wagon.”

“It is travelling under the movement order.”

“With my property in it.”

“Your property can go on two packhorses.”

“My money can go on two packhorses where every man on the road can see how little protects it.”

Cedric had considered the weight and not the reason for the guard. He glanced towards the door. The men outside could probably hear them.

“What part must be kept locked?” he asked.

“All of it.”

“Which part is small enough to go on a horse without the whole chest?”

Gauter looked at him more closely.

“Are you proposing to divide it?”

“I am proposing to put the men where the wagon can carry them. You may choose what travels beside you.”

“And leave the rest here?”

“The broken cart can take it on the lower road, slowly. With a guard. The wounded cannot sit on it while the axle moves like that.”

“The lower road adds half a day.”

“To the chest.”

Gauter’s mouth tightened.

“Who told you to give orders about my effects?”

“Dame Ysabeau told me to move the company.”

“Then let her answer for the loss.”

Cedric felt an old, unwelcome impulse to make the answer sharper because the man had said it in front of servants. He could win a few words and still have eight men in the wrong cart. He asked Gauter to come outside and look at the axle.

The knight refused at first, then came because Cedric remained at the door waiting for him.

The men had stopped pretending to pack.

Gauter examined the repair, called it sufficient for a little baggage, and appeared to realize too late that he had used exactly the description Cedric needed. He looked towards his own wagon.

“I will choose the guard.”

“Four men,” Cedric said.

“Six.”

“I need the rest on the orchard road.”

“So do I.”

They settled on four and an officer who knew the lower road. Gauter opened the chest himself, turned his body to obscure the contents, and took out two wrapped parcels. The remainder was lifted onto the damaged cart. Cedric ordered extra rope round it and had the owner of the cart shown the additional weight before they left.

The wounded were helped into the wagon. One of them cursed when a helper pulled at his bad leg, then apologized to the wrong man. Cedric laid the cloaks along the rail where a rough edge might catch them. Derrit examined the arrangement and asked for another bundle beneath the man’s knee.

Gauter watched all this from his horse.

“If my property goes missing, the complaint will name you.”

“Then spell it correctly,” Cedric said.

Derrit lowered his head. Cedric knew he had heard. The reply pleased him for perhaps three breaths, until he considered the complaint reaching Ysabeau in that form.

They left the dye-house with the second party already taking the lower track. Cedric rode at the rear until he was satisfied the wagon could manage the first incline. Then he went forward to ask the man who had served longest in the quarters where the road worsened.

“At the upper orchard gate,” the man said. “The hinge post has moved. You can’t swing it clear.”

“Why wasn’t it mended?”

“The owner says we moved it with the last wagon.”

“Did we?”

“Yes.”

Cedric sent two men ahead with tools.

Beyond the orchard they found smoke.

It rose from the shelter where the shepherds kept hurdles, on ground below the track. Cedric stopped and called Derrit forward. A few men were moving near the shelter, too far away to identify. The sheep had scattered into the next field.

Gauter rode up before either man spoke.

“My tenant’s,” he said.

“How many?” Cedric asked.

“Enough that they shouldn’t be burning his shelter.”

He called to his mounted retainers. Three came forward at once. Cedric turned his horse across the road.

“Wait.”

Gauter stared at him.

“There are men below,” Cedric said. “We don’t know whose.”

“I know whose sheep they are.”

“Send someone down to ask what happened. Keep the wagon here until we know.”

“My people are being robbed while you arrange enquiries.”

A man appeared above the shelter, running uphill. He stopped when he saw the horses and shouted something. One of Gauter’s men recognized him. The knight urged his horse forward, and Cedric had to move or be struck.

He let Gauter go with two men and kept the third beside the wagon. Gauter turned in the saddle to object. Cedric pointed at the wounded. The knight looked at them, swore, and rode on.

Derrit dismounted and went up the bank to get a better view. Cedric had the bowmen spread along the hedge, giving them clear sight down the slope. The man remaining from Gauter’s party said the owner of the adjoining land had disputed the grazing for years.

“Could be his men,” he said.

“Would he burn the shelter?”

“He says it shouldn’t be there.”

Downhill, Gauter reached the running man and stopped. The figures near the sheep began withdrawing towards a stand of trees. One carried something over his shoulder. Another turned and loosed an arrow which struck the ground short of the mounted party.

Cedric’s horse flinched. He shortened the rein too sharply and had to release it before the animal backed into the wagon.

“Hold,” Derrit called from the bank.

Their bowmen had begun to draw. The distance was poor, and Gauter’s men were between them and the retreating figures. Cedric repeated the order loudly enough to be heard by the nearest man, who lowered his bow with evident relief.

Gauter pursued as far as the trees. His men stopped him there. Even from the road Cedric could see the argument, the knight pointing beyond the trunks, one of the retainers holding his bridle.

They returned with the tenant and two sheep driven ahead of a horse. The man carrying something had escaped with it. Gauter’s face was white with anger.

“Six more horse and we would have had them,” he told Cedric.

“Six more horse are on the other road with your chest.”

“Four.”

“Four. And two here would still leave us without a guard for the wagon.”

Gauter turned away.

The tenant had a bleeding cut on his scalp. Derrit gave him a cloth. He needed to get the remaining sheep back into the field before dark. His younger son was hiding somewhere beyond the shelter and would not know whether it was safe to come out.

Cedric sent two men to call for the boy. It delayed them longer than the broken gate had. When they returned, the child walked close to his father and refused a place on the wagon. Gauter told them where they could sleep. He gave the instruction in a quieter voice than he had used on the road.

They left the tenant trying to count his animals. Cedric could still see the smoke when they crossed the rise.

Ysabeau heard Gauter’s complaint before Cedric had finished getting the wounded indoors.

The knight followed her into the yard, describing the divided party, the surrendered opportunity, and the dangerous precedent of a young gentleman overriding a landholder on his own road. Cedric stood with straw on his coat and one wet sleeve where a basin had spilled. He had not eaten since morning.

“Was any of your property lost?” Ysabeau asked Gauter.

“The chest has not arrived.”

“Then we don’t know yet.”

“My tenant lost sheep.”

“Master Aeryl was ordered to bring the men. I will hear the complaint about the sheep separately.”

“He prevented pursuit.”

“How many did you see?”

Gauter answered. She asked where they had gone, which men had followed, and how close the trees stood to the track. By the third question his voice had changed. The men standing beside the wagon were listening.

“You had two good men with you,” she said. “They got you back. I am glad they did.”

Gauter looked at her for a long moment before inclining his head.

“I will have the tenant’s losses brought.”

“Please do. With the account of the boundary dispute.”

When he had gone, she turned to Cedric.

“You will amend the count of the rear guard. Four, I am told.”

“Yes.”

“And you will give the carter the additional hire for the slower road.”

“Yes.”

“You need not ask Sir Gauter to spell your name. He knows it.”

Cedric looked down at his sleeve.

“I should not have said that.”

“No.”

She waited until he looked up.

“The wounded are here. You asked Derrit before you loaded them. That was sound work.”

He could feel the exhaustion more strongly after she said it. She sent him to eat.

The chest arrived the following morning, with everything still in it. The axle broke in the yard while the men were taking the last load off.

Aude had gone home for a few days. Ysabeau asked Cedric to sit with her after the evening meal because there was no one else to prevent her drinking the remaining wine while reading bad handwriting.

“Mine?” he asked.

“Among others.”

He sat. She gave him the paper she had been reading and asked whether a village name ended with an n or an r. He knew the place and could answer.

“I need someone to do more of this,” she said. “After the marching is over as well.”

He waited.

“The households will still argue about whose men came and what they brought. The winter quarters must be settled. Officers need to know who will answer a request when I am elsewhere. Rovel is useful in the field, and his foot is giving him trouble. He cannot ride every road because nobody else knows whom to ask.”

“You have clerks.”

“I have men who can copy a letter. I need someone who knows when the letter describes an impossible journey.”

Cedric turned the paper in his hands.

“It would be a permanent place?”

“If we continue to suit each other.”

She named the pay. It would meet his restitution and leave enough to keep his own horse properly. There would be quarters when he was required to remain near her household, money for travel, and authority to answer specified matters without sending them all to her.

“What would you call it?” he asked.

“We can find a title after we establish the work.”

He smiled. She smiled back.

“Write to your father if you wish. He may prefer to know that someone intends to keep you employed.”

“I would.”

“Then tell him what it is before telling him what you hope it will become.”

She poured him another little measure of wine. He stayed to help with the place-names until the lamp needed more oil.

At the cord shop, the woman gave him a letter with an ordinary commercial address on the outside.

He had brought a report under cover of arranging the repair of two saddle straps. The work was real. Her nephew had already taken the straps away. Cedric stood in the back room while she put the letter on a stool.

“Read it here.”

It acknowledged his previous account and told him where to go if he had to leave his position without returning to the shop. A carter would take him as far as a particular ferry. A sleeping place beyond it had been paid for. He recognized the name of the keeper; the man had sold him a meal on his first journey south and asked no questions beyond whether he wanted another egg.

“How long has that been arranged?” Cedric asked.

“Before the last payment.”

“Before my report about Verren?”

“I don’t read your reports.”

He looked at the instructions again. The ferry closed at the evening bell unless someone had obtained prior permission for a crossing. The carter was supposed to bring him before then.

“And if he is late?”

“Stay at the yard. There is a room.”

“Whose yard?”

She pointed to the address he had just read. He had been looking for a hidden difficulty and missed the answer in the line above.

“Does the keeper know whom to send to?” he asked.

“He knows who pays him.”

Cedric folded the letter. She held out her hand for it.

“I was going to ask for another week before the next report,” he said. “The dame has offered me work after the campaign. There are matters to arrange.”

“Put it in your account.”

“I have.”

“Then they will read it.”

Her nephew came back with the straps. Cedric paid for the repair, took them, and returned to quarters. That evening he began a letter to his father about the place Ysabeau had offered. He got as far as the amount of pay before starting again on a clean sheet.

Chapter 17 — An Evening Missed

Vaelrie’s answer began by correcting the date of his complaint.

You wrote this before your previous letter could reasonably have reached me. I have put the dates beside one another so you may enjoy them together.

She had done so. Ouen looked at the two dates and decided the fault belonged partly to the man who had carried the first letter. He had said he was going directly. He had apparently meant directly after several other errands.

I am sorry I did not write sooner. I had intended to tell you about the evening with the singers and found that the description was becoming longer than the evening. Maelis has a grievance against Margery which changes each time they tell me about it. Catrin says this is because they are enjoying the grievance. I was going to ask whether she meant both of them, but she took me to hear a new tune and I forgot.

I have not forgotten you.

The last sentence stood alone. Ouen read it twice and shifted in his chair.

He had not accused her of forgetting him in those exact words. He had said that he understood the court had many entertainments and hoped she might one day find time to answer a letter from home. It had seemed restrained when he wrote it. Beside the two dates, it looked less so.

You can be cross with me for being late without telling me what I must have been thinking. It is a very disagreeable habit in Father and I have no wish to encourage it in you.

There followed a description of Jaennis falling asleep in a chair while Vaelrie tried to read something aloud, waking, and correctly repeating the last sentence while denying that she had slept. Ouen smiled despite himself. His sister had always taken particular satisfaction in catching somebody at a fault which harmed no one and could therefore be enjoyed without requiring a remedy.

He had interrupted his own reading so often that Iselde asked whether the letter was bad news.

“No.”

“Then stop folding the corner. You’ll tear it.”

He flattened it against the desk.

Vaelrie asked after Hew’s cup and said she had obtained the name of the man who made them. She had also found a little book of birds which she thought the child might like when he could read it. Someone had drawn feet with the wrong number of toes on one page. Ouen could have the pleasure of finding it.

I should like to see you all before the roads become impossible again. I do not know when. Please do not turn that into a promise for a particular day and then write to say I have broken it.

He looked towards the yard, where two men were taking a wheel off a cart. He would have liked to be cross about the tone. The sentence about wanting to see them made that more difficult.

At the bottom of the second page she had begun a separate matter.

Maelis asks whether you would be willing to hear a man named Guiran. He plays the rebec and has worked with her. His trunks are being kept at the house of Sire Montrel, who says the fee remains in dispute. He cannot take another engagement without the clothes and instruments. Maelis says he can be late and is excessively proud of one tune, but she has never known him steal. I give you her description because my own would be of a man I have not met.

You may say no. This is her request, which I offered to put in my letter. Nobody has asked me to obtain a royal answer through you. If you prefer her to write separately, tell me and she will.

Ouen read the paragraph aloud to Iselde.

“Now I’m to hear musicians.”

“You have heard less interesting people.”

“I don’t know the man.”

“Neither does she. She says so.”

“Montrel will be difficult.”

“He usually is.”

Ouen leaned back. Montrel held a small property within reach of the household’s authority and disliked every exercise of that authority which did not relieve him of an obligation. He had sent a useful team when asked in autumn, then spent the following month telling people what it had cost him. He would be particularly disagreeable about a man who played tunes for money.

“Tell him to bring proof,” Iselde said.

“He isn’t here.”

“Then tell whoever asks when he comes.”

The request seemed likely to grow larger if left unanswered. Ouen dictated a short note to Montrel asking that the property be preserved until the dispute could be heard. He took care to call it the property in question, so no one could say he had recognized ownership before examining it.

Halden listened to the wording.

“Do you wish to hear the man?”

“If he comes.”

“He has come. He was told to return when you had read the letter.”

Ouen looked at him.

“Who told him?”

“I did.”

“Then you may tell him when.”

“This afternoon?”

Ouen considered the alternatives and could find none less troublesome.

Guiran had a damaged thumbnail and a way of holding his shoulders as though he expected to be struck by low beams.

He removed his cap before entering the room and kept twisting its edge between his fingers. He was older than Ouen, but his attempt to appear properly respectful made him look briefly like a boy brought in for some fault. Ouen recognized the expression and disliked seeing it across his own table.

“Maelis says you have worked with her.”

“Several times, my lord.”

“What work?”

“Music. A little performance with two singers. I make the changes between their parts.”

“Does the cricket join you?”

Guiran smiled before checking whether a smile was permitted.

“When it chooses. Never at rehearsal.”

Ouen indicated the chair. Guiran sat on its edge.

“What does Montrel say you owe?”

“Another evening.”

“Was one promised?”

“Four were spoken of.”

“And you gave?”

“Three.”

Ouen looked at Halden. The officer had begun writing and did not look back.

“Then there is an evening owed.”

“We were sent away.”

“By whom?”

“The lady. Her husband was gone hunting. The child had a fever and she wanted the house quiet. She said the household would settle what remained.”

“Do you have that in writing?”

“No, my lord.”

“Then how do I distinguish it from something you’ve remembered because it suits you?”

Guiran stopped twisting the cap.

“Ask her.”

It was such an obvious answer that Ouen was irritated with the question which had produced it.

“I shall. What is in the trunks?”

He expected a general account. Guiran described each item: the rebec in its case, two pairs of shoes with thin soles for dancing, a painted screen which folded into three parts, costumes belonging to his wife and the other singer, a small drum, and clothes that had nothing to do with the performance. The second trunk belonged to a man named Colas, who was staying at a cheaper lodging while they waited.

“How do you play without the instrument?” Ouen asked.

“I borrow when someone will lend. Not everyone wishes to.”

“Why didn’t you take it when you left?”

“I went to find the cart. When I came back, his steward said nothing was to be removed until the lord returned.”

“Did you try the lady again?”

“She had gone to the child.”

“And afterwards?”

Guiran looked at the cap.

“I was told not to trouble her.”

Ouen could imagine exactly how that instruction had been given. A man at a door, very certain that refusing entrance counted as doing his duty, and a person outside unable to make himself important enough to be admitted.

“Bring me any paper you have about the engagement,” he said. “Even if it doesn’t say everything you would like.”

Guiran drew a folded note from his coat. Ouen had expected to send him away to find one and was mildly annoyed to have more work placed before him at once.

It named the first three evenings. A fourth was to be agreed if the household wished to retain the players. Beneath the fee a later hand had entered an advance for repairing the instrument.

“How much of this have you received?”

Guiran answered. He had not mentioned the repair himself.

“That comes out of the fee,” Ouen said.

“It was agreed as assistance.”

“The word here is advance.”

Guiran looked at the writing. “His steward put that in.”

“Did you accept the money?”

“Yes.”

“Then we shall hear that part as well.”

The man looked less hopeful when he left. Ouen felt the interview had been conducted reasonably and was pleased that Halden had witnessed it.

Montrel sent his steward the next morning with an objection to the whole proceeding.

His lord had made no complaint to Ouen and had not invited interference in the conduct of his household. The trunks were secure. The musician had left before completing the engagement. There would be a settlement when Montrel returned from visiting his sister, at which time the amount of inconvenience could also be considered.

“When is he returning?” Ouen asked.

The steward did not know.

“Where is his sister?”

He named a place which could be reached in two days.

“Then write to him.”

“I am not authorized to propose a settlement.”

“You are authorized to carry a question.”

Halden shifted a paper on the table. Ouen wondered whether the words had sounded like something his father might say. He chose to be pleased by that possibility.

Lady Montrel answered before her husband did. She wrote that she had dismissed the players for the reason Guiran gave, that their conduct had been proper, and that she expected the fee for the three evenings to be paid. The reply contained a separate request that no further party be sent to question her household while the child was recovering.

Ouen had the steward return with the trunks.

He opened them in the hall. One buckle on the rebec’s case had been replaced with cord. Guiran checked the instrument before inspecting anything else, testing each string so softly that Ouen could hear only a little scrape and hum. The man named Colas counted the shoes and costumes into his own trunk. A woman had come with them; Guiran introduced her as his wife, Perrine. She knelt to look beneath the folded screen and took out a cloth parcel.

“Those are hers,” Guiran said.

Ouen had not asked. Perrine put the parcel aside and continued searching.

“There is a blue sleeve missing.”

“It may be with the curtains,” the steward said.

“Then bring it.”

“My lord’s curtains are not searched at the request of—”

“Is it listed?” Ouen asked.

Colas showed him the list. A costume with one removable sleeve was described, and the other sleeve lay across Perrine’s knee.

“Look with the curtains,” Ouen told the steward. “If it isn’t there, tell us where you have looked.”

The fee was settled with the repair money deducted. Guiran accepted the amount with a bowed head. Ouen required him to acknowledge that the agreed evenings had been paid and that he would make no further demand for the fourth.

“I didn’t ask to be paid for the fourth,” Guiran said.

“Then it will cost you little to say so.”

He said so. They signed. Perrine wrapped the damaged fastening of the instrument case with a dry strip she had brought for the purpose and asked where the cart could be loaded without obstructing the household.

Ouen wrote to Vaelrie that the matter had been resolved.

He described the competing claims, the lady’s intervention, and the release of the trunks. He was tempted to say that her friend had omitted part of the truth about the advance. Maelis had never claimed to know about it. Vaelrie had been careful to say what she knew. He contented himself with advising that Guiran have engagements written more clearly in future.

Then he read his own earlier complaint, which she had returned with her answer.

The words about the court’s entertainments were worse on a second reading. He folded the page and put it under the writing board, as though keeping it out of sight would improve what she had received.

I was impatient for news, he wrote. I should have asked for it without deciding why you had not sent it.

He disliked the sentence but could not find a shorter one that admitted as much. Beneath it he wrote about the two cups. Hew preferred the chipped one because he could find his own without being told. Iselde had said this was a convenient virtue to discover in damaged goods.

He asked when Vaelrie thought she might visit, adding that he would understand if she could not yet name a day. The qualification made him sound more reasonable than he felt.

Guiran’s missing sleeve arrived before the letter was sealed. The steward brought it himself and asked for an acknowledgement. Ouen had Halden give him one. From the yard he could hear Perrine laughing with the kitchen women while her husband tried to play a tune somebody had requested. Twice he stopped and began again at the same passage.

Ouen went to the window. The rebec sounded thinner than he expected in the open air, but Guiran’s face had changed while he played. His shoulders were down, his cap pushed back, and when a child called out he answered without losing the next note.

Ouen added that he had heard the man play and that Maelis might be right about his excessive pride in one tune. He asked Vaelrie what she had been listening to when she forgot the rest of Margery’s grievance.

Chapter 18 — The Hard Way

The chair would not go through the kitchen door.

Elyas had measured the table, the dresser, and the space between the hearth and the wall. He had assumed the chair would follow. Now Berent stood on the far side holding two legs while Elyas tried to turn the back through the opening without scraping away more of the paint.

“Lower your end,” he said.

Berent lowered it. The chair struck the sill.

“Only a little.”

“Yes, Highness.”

Mara came into the passage with a folded cloth over one arm. She looked at the chair, then at the marks on the doorpost.

“Take it round the yard.”

“The other door is narrower,” Elyas said.

“It opens all the way.”

He looked at the door beside him. A hook on the wall prevented it from lying flat. He had been measuring the opening and failed to notice what reduced it.

Berent kept his face carefully still. Elyas set his end down.

“We can move the hook.”

“We can,” Mara said. “After breakfast, when I have somewhere to sit.”

They took the chair round the yard. Outside, the pear tree had begun to show little pale points along its branches. The ground beneath it was wet and slippery. Berent carried the chair inside while Elyas held the door and tried to keep mud from the sill with a piece of old sacking.

Mara set the cloth on the table. There were several pieces beneath it, cut to different sizes, and he realized she had been working on dressings again.

“Are those for Gisla?”

“For a man who keeps pulling his off.”

“Will he keep these on?”

“He says the old ones catch his collar. We shall see whether that was the reason.”

She sat in the chair and rocked it experimentally. One leg moved against the uneven floor.

“It will do.”

“I’ll have it shortened.”

“Have breakfast first.”

Berent brought bread from the other room. He had learned which plates belonged to the household and which had come on loan from a woman who expected them returned, though he still placed the borrowed ones where guests would see them. Mara moved one without comment and gave him the plain bowl for himself.

They ate with the yard door open a little to let out the smoke. Elyas could see the pear tree from his seat. He had imagined mornings like this during the journey home, though in those imaginings the furniture had always been where it belonged and nobody had needed to cut cloth before eating.

Mara reached across to take a piece of crust from his plate. He caught her fingers lightly and held them until she looked up.

“Thief.”

“You weren’t eating it.”

“I was considering it.”

“Consider another.”

He let her take it. Berent concentrated on his bowl.

The royal messenger arrived while they were clearing the table.

Elyas knew it was an appointment about the south before he entered the room.

The messenger had been instructed to ask whether he could remain after the ordinary audience. Two of the maps laid out on the queen’s table showed Istovar. Tylan stood beside them with a captain from the road companies, and Veralyn was listening to an account of how long a body of foot would take to reach the western country if the roads remained passable.

Margery sat near the window with a cup. When Elyas was admitted she rose, said something quietly to the queen, and let Veralyn catch her hand for a moment before she went. The queen watched her as far as the door, then turned back to the maps.

“Elyas. How is the house?”

“Still standing.”

“Despite the furniture, I hear.”

“Mara has been speaking to someone.”

“She has been speaking to Jaennis. Sit down.”

He took the chair beside the table. The captain finished his account. Tylan asked him which part of the route he had inspected personally. The answer shortened the confident estimate by several days and lengthened the possible journey by as many. Veralyn thanked him and sent him to have the uncertainty entered on the working plan.

When the door had closed, she put her hand on the western edge of the map.

“Valdais must come out of this country eventually.”

“If the river closes to him.”

“Fayd is working on the houses. The middle cantons have taken losses they cannot keep replacing.”

“Some can.”

“Then we will give those more work.”

Tylan moved a marker a little away from the river. Elyas looked at where the roads divided.

“The Scar,” he said.

“Yes.”

“He knows it.”

“So do you.”

He looked up. The queen was watching him.

“I want you to go south through Istovar,” she said. “Take a force small enough to move without living off every holding it passes. Speak to the barons who will not answer Harald. Bring in the men they are keeping at home.”

“Under whose authority?”

“Yours, where they will give them to you. Mine for the troops I send.”

“And to do what?”

“To hold the western approaches. I intend to own the western Scar when he comes back through it.”
