Elyas rested both hands on the edge of the table. He had known a request was coming. He had expected service under Tylan, perhaps command of men already collected. The map lay between him and his own country.

“Have you asked my brother?”

“He has been asked to secure passage and deny aid to Valdais.”

“What did he say?”

Fayd’s written account lay beneath a folded corner of the map. Veralyn drew it out and gave it to him. Harald had acknowledged the danger, described disputes which prevented immediate action, and requested assurances about the disposition of royal forces after the campaign.

“These are reasonable questions,” Elyas said.

“They are,” Veralyn answered. “He has asked them again after receiving answers.”

Elyas read the dates. His brother had allowed weeks between the exchanges.

“He has lost our father. The houses which answered Oswyn will not all answer him.”

“I know.”

“You propose to make certain of that by having them answer me instead.”

“I propose that they answer someone before Valdais takes the road.”

Tylan remained silent. Elyas wished he would speak, then resented wishing for somebody else to enter an argument he ought to make himself.

“You are asking me to weaken my brother in his own duchy.”

“I am asking you to bring men he has not brought.”

“Those will be the same act to him.”

Veralyn nodded. The agreement gave him no assistance.

“My duties as a Ducal Prince of Vhasia take precedence over my duties as an Everblood,” he said. “I cannot surrender them because you and I are kin.”

“They do.”

He had prepared the next sentence. Her answer stopped him before he could say it.

For a moment he thought she was withdrawing the request. Tylan moved his hand away from the marker and folded his arms. Outside the room a servant called for another person to take a tray. The ordinary sound was very clear in the pause.

“Then you understand why I cannot promise you their submission,” Elyas said.

“Yes.”

Veralyn drew the map towards him so the western road lay in front of his chair.

“I can take it the hard way.”

He looked at her.

“I will not leave him a road home because Harald has difficulty with his barons. If you can make an arrangement they will keep, make it. If you cannot, I will bring the army.”

“And what am I to tell them that army will do?”

“Ask them what they intend to make necessary.”

Elyas pushed his chair back and stood. There was enough room to walk to the window without passing either of them. He went there because he needed a moment before answering, and looked down into the courtyard where a groom was trying to persuade a horse to pass a heap of whitewashed baskets.

He remembered travelling west with men who could tell which lord’s boundary they had crossed by the condition of the ditch. Each had spoken of protection as something owed to him by the next man. The households along the Scar would hear that a royal army was coming and begin moving things they could not afford to lose. The people who stayed would have reasons for staying. Few of those reasons would interest an officer looking for dry quarters.

He turned back.

“If I undertake this, the men remain under their own agreements. You cannot use their attendance as a claim to the duchy.”

“I have asked for the road.”

“Put the distinction into the commission.”

“I will.”

“Supplies purchased where they can be purchased. Requisitions entered against the officer who takes them. Complaints heard without requiring the complainant to follow us into battle.”

Tylan spoke at last.

“You will need money to buy, and somebody who can receive those complaints while you are riding elsewhere.”

“Yes.”

“We can furnish both, within limits.”

“Let me know the limits before I promise anything.”

Tylan nodded. Veralyn watched Elyas return to the table.

“How many?” he asked.

“Twenty-four horse and forty spears,” Tylan said. “You must obtain the rest.”

“They will see an escort.”

“That is what we can send now.”

Elyas looked at the road again. The barons would see him, his rank, and a force too small to compel them. They would also see what was likely to follow if they refused. He could not make the second part disappear merely by promising to conduct the first honourably.

“I will speak to them,” he said. “I will not tell them I can guarantee matters I cannot control.”

“Good,” Veralyn said. “They have heard enough of those.”

Tylan walked with him to the yard.

“The valley isn’t a gate,” Elyas said.

“No.”

“With that force I can’t close it.”

“Make him choose another way, or pay to use the one he wants.”

“He may pay.”

“I expect he will, if he thinks the other way costs more.”

They stopped beside the mounting block. The groom had got the horse past the baskets and was leading it back again, allowing it to inspect them.

“How long do you need?” Elyas asked.

Tylan looked at him directly.

“I cannot tell you until we know which houses have actually held to their terms. Bring your men away when staying ceases to buy us anything.”

“That will be easier to judge from behind us.”

“Yes.”

The answer was too honest to quarrel with. Elyas asked who would captain the royal foot. Tylan named a man he knew and trusted sufficiently to question. They agreed to meet him before the commission was sealed.

As Elyas put his foot in the stirrup, Tylan said, “Tell your wife before the servants begin packing.”

“I mean to.”

“She will hear how small the force is. Better from you.”

Elyas mounted and rode home.

Mara was scrubbing a basin in the yard.

She looked up as he came through the gate. He dismounted and gave the reins to Berent, then waited until the young man had led the horse away.

“I’ve been asked to go south.”

She set the basin on its edge.

“When?”

“As soon as the men can be assembled.”

“How many?”

He told her. She dried her hands on the cloth at her waist.

“That sounds small.”

“It is meant to become larger.”

“Whose men?”

He explained the commission. She listened without interrupting, then picked up the basin because it was about to fall and set it more securely against the wall.

“What does Harald say?”

“He hasn’t been told what I agreed.”

“What did you agree?”

“To speak to the barons. To bring men together under terms they can accept. To put them where Valdais will have to spend time and men getting past.”

“You make that last part sound very ordinary.”

“I don’t know how to make it sound.”

She looked at him for a little while, then took his hand and led him into the kitchen.

The chair was still uneven. He sat in it and felt it move beneath him. Mara remained standing, leaning against the edge of the table.

“If you refused?” she asked.

“The queen would take the country by force when she could.”

“And if you fail?”

“She may still do it.”

Mara nodded slowly.

“I wish you had been asked something else.”

“So do I.”

“You could have asked to think.”

“I thought.”

“For how long?”

He looked at the cloth laid ready for cutting. One edge had been marked with little notches to keep the pieces uniform.

“Long enough to know the people she would have to march through,” he said. “Not long enough to like any answer.”

She moved behind his chair and put her hands on his shoulders. He leaned back against her. For a while neither spoke.

“I will keep going to Gisla,” she said. “If you have wounded coming north, tell us where they will be sent. Don’t write that they have been cared for because someone has put them under a roof.”

“I won’t.”

“And Berent?”

“With me. In his proper duties.”

“Tell him what that means before someone else invents work for him.”

He caught one of her hands and kissed the back of it.

She went to put the kettle on. He stood, turned the chair over, and examined the leg that made it rock. There was a small wedge of wood in the yard which would hold it steady until the carpenter came. He found it and fitted it beneath the foot.

When Mara sat down, the chair remained still. She looked at him, then reached for the two cups.

Chapter 19 — Wyllmot’s Daughter

Sornay’s gate was open, but the baron had ordered the royal colours covered before the party entered.

The man carrying the request delivered it apologetically. Elyas listened, looked back at the small body of foot waiting on the road, and asked whether the request applied to his own banner as well.

“No, Highness.”

“Then the others remain as they are. We have announced ourselves.”

“The baron means no offence.”

“Tell him I have taken none. We will wait here for his answer.”

Captain Wace dismounted and had the foot stand easy. The men had been walking since dawn. One sat on a bank and took his boot off to remove a stone. Berent remained in the saddle beside Elyas, watching the messenger go back through the gate.

“Should I take your horse, Highness?”

“Not yet.”

A woman came down the lane carrying a basket under a cloth. She looked at the soldiers, calculated whether there was room to pass them without leaving the road, and chose the bank. Wace moved two men aside for her. She thanked him and went through the gate without being asked what colours she carried.

The messenger returned. The baron would receive the prince and his immediate attendants. The royal troops could use the lower field and would be furnished with water.

“Food?” Wace asked.

“That was not mentioned.”

“We have some,” Elyas said. “Use it. Enter the quantity.”

He took Berent and two men through the gate. The royal colours remained outside where everyone approaching the house could see them.

Sornay met him in the yard, bareheaded and correctly courteous.

He was older than Oswyn had been when Elyas first remembered him as a visitor, but the long nose and habit of looking past a speaker towards whatever else was happening remained familiar. He asked after Mara before speaking of the commission. Elyas thanked him for the enquiry.

“I had word of the marriage,” Sornay said. “Your father would have wished to see it.”

“He would.”

The baron let the answer stand. Elyas was grateful that he did not add what else his father might have wished.

Inside, a meal had been prepared for the prince’s party. Elyas ate enough to acknowledge the hospitality and asked whether his men in the field could buy bread. Sornay sent a servant to arrange it. Wace would later tell Elyas that the bread was good and the price entirely indifferent to rank.

“The duke has written to me,” Sornay said when they were alone.

“About my journey?”

“Before it. He requires the western roads protected and forbids separate agreements with foreign powers.”

“I wrote to him before leaving Valora. He knows what I have been asked to do.”

“Has he consented?”

“He has not answered.”

Sornay rested both hands on the table.

“Then you are asking me to violate an instruction from my duke while serving his brother.”

“I am asking what force you can provide to keep Valdais from using your country as his return road.”

“My country is precisely what I intend to protect.”

“Then we have something to discuss.”

The baron rose and went to a window. From it they could see the lower field, where the royal foot had arranged their packs in small groups and were eating. Their numbers looked smaller spread out on the grass.

“How many has the queen given you?”

Elyas told him.

“To stop an army?”

“To begin an arrangement.”

“Your father would have come with his own men.”

“My father would have expected yours to join them.”

Sornay turned back. For a moment Elyas thought he had pressed too hard. Then the baron pulled out the chair again.

“Sixty,” he said. “Twelve mounted. Men I can equip and feed until they reach an agreed muster. After that we must speak of supply.”

“Under whose command?”

“Yours.”

“Their own officers under me.”

“Yes. You.”

Elyas understood the emphasis.

“You will not be put under another baron merely because he brings a larger company.”

“Nor under the queen’s marshal without my consent.”

“He will direct the campaign.”

“Then let his instructions come through you.”

Elyas looked at the household men in the yard. He could place sixty on the road, or spend another day arguing for a more convenient authority and ride away with none.

“If I am elsewhere, you will obey the officer I leave responsible. His name will be known before your men march.”

“Provided it is not a man with whom I have an unsettled quarrel.”

“Whom do you mean?”

“Lormey.”

“I have not met him yet.”

“You will.”

The baron called for his captain. He was prepared to give the number before the legal wording was finished, which Elyas took as a useful sign. The captain, a lean man with a sun-darkened face, knew exactly which twelve horses could be sent. He had been waiting for an instruction his lord had not wished to give Harald.

The hard part came after the men had been named.

Sornay asked whether Seniro would follow the royal army into the western country. He used the name without a title. When Elyas replied that he could not give another commander’s movements, the baron looked towards the closed door.

“Will he have authority to take quarters here?”

“Under the same regulations as other officers.”

“I asked because I know the other officers less well.”

Elyas held the man’s gaze.

“What undertaking do you require?”

“That my people be furnished with a way of refusing him.”

“I cannot give you a blanket right to refuse an army.”

“No. Your queen would not permit it.”

“She has agreed that supplies be purchased where they are available, and that requisitions be entered under the name of the officer responsible. A place for complaints will be maintained behind the advance.”

“And while the complaint travels, my people will have him in their houses.”

Elyas had an answer about discipline. He had seen Seniro maintain it under conditions in which other commanders would have allowed men to take what they pleased. He could describe wounded enemies being brought safely across a bridge. None of that would alter what Sornay meant.

“I can name the officer assigned to your holding,” he said. “I can require that your steward receive the order before supplies are collected. I will put that in writing. If something is taken without authority, I will hear it myself when I am within reach.”

“When.”

“Yes.”

The baron looked at him for a while.

“Wyllmot’s daughter,” he said. “She sends his men to explain how carefully she will proceed.”

“She sent me.”

“For now.”

Elyas drew the draft towards him and wrote the proposed restriction beside the supply clause. He could hear the pen against the paper. Outside, somebody called a horse by name and received no apparent obedience.

Sornay read the addition. He requested a second copy for his steward and agreed to begin preparing the company.

Berent had found beds for the attendants and discovered that the royal foot were expected to sleep beyond the lower wall.

Their captain accepted the arrangement provided they could use the bakehouse after the ovens cooled. The household officer refused because bread was to be made before dawn. Berent proposed half the men inside and half under the eaves, then found himself with two objections instead of one.

Elyas came down to hear the last of them.

“Who is baking?” he asked.

“A woman and two helpers, Highness.”

“Ask them what room they need.”

The officer said he knew the room required. Elyas asked him to fetch the woman anyway.

She came with flour on her sleeve and no patience for being called away from the mixing. She wanted a path from the store to the oven, the bench kept clear, and the soldiers awake and out before the first loaves went in. She did not care where they slept if those things were done.

Wace agreed. Berent went to show the men where they could put their packs. Elyas heard him repeating the hour and the need to keep the path clear. He had learned to give the important part before allowing questions to multiply.

At the well, two of Sornay’s men were speaking to one of the prince’s grooms. Elyas stopped to let a servant carry water past and heard Seniro’s name.

“They called him the Red before that,” one man said.

“Charau,” the other answered. “That is where it stuck.”

“My father was in that campaign.”

“Your father was in Ethor.”

“He had family there.”

The first man began to answer, then saw Elyas. Both straightened. The groom looked away into the well as though he had been inspecting it throughout.

“See that the buckets remain here tonight,” Elyas said. “My men will use their own vessels for carrying.”

“Yes, Highness.”

He went on to the bakehouse. Berent was moving a pack out of the baker’s path.

Lormey would not receive the queen’s commission as the basis of the discussion.

He put it aside without unfolding the second page and asked Elyas whether he had come as a Vhasian prince or as a royal captain. The room was colder than Sornay’s hall. Lormey wore gloves while he spoke, one finger cut short so he could turn the pages of the document he had prepared himself.

“As both,” Elyas said.

“Then we must distinguish which asks and which promises.”

“Show me what you have.”

Lormey’s proposed undertaking concerned the protection of roads and holdings within Istovar. It named Elyas as commander, provided for a muster of eighty spears, and forbade the use of those men outside the duchy without a further agreement. There was no acknowledgement of Veralyn’s sovereignty, her jurisdiction over local disputes, or the permanence of any royal garrison.

Elyas read it through. The man had thought about what he wanted and employed someone capable of giving it form.

“You have prepared this before hearing my terms.”

“I heard enough of them from Sornay’s messenger.”

“He has not given you my commission.”

“No. He told me what he found objectionable.”

“Does he know you intend to join?”

“He knows I intend to preserve my holding. He may draw conclusions.”

Elyas laid the paper down.

“The queen will accept temporary service without treating it as submission. That has been agreed. I need the men available on the western approaches, including positions whose boundaries may be disputed. I cannot stop a movement while two stewards decide which field lies in whose jurisdiction.”

“Then name the limits now.”

They bent over a local plan. Lormey’s steward brought an older description when the boundary beside a coppice proved uncertain. Elyas asked what happened there in practice. The steward said that men from both holdings cut wood, and each lord accused the other of allowing too much.

“Include the approach and leave the ownership unresolved,” Elyas said. “The service decides nothing about the timber.”

Lormey considered this and agreed. The first useful line was entered.

The argument over supply took longer. Lormey would provision the men for the march and expected the royal purse to support them thereafter. He refused a clause which allowed the cost to be charged back to his holding if the general supply failed.

“Then if a wagon is late, you would have them hungry?” Elyas asked.

“If a wagon is late, I would have the officer responsible answer for it. You cannot ask me to pay for supplies and then pay again because somebody has sold them on the road.”

They agreed on an emergency issue from local stores, entered as an amount to be made good by the command. Elyas asked for someone from Lormey’s household to remain with the supplies and verify what had actually reached the men. The baron approved that readily.

Only when the main clauses were settled did he ask who would lead if Elyas were killed.

“Captain Wace will keep the common disposition until the surviving officers agree a withdrawal or receive further orders.”

“A Viterran.”

“An officer whose men will be beside yours.”

“And Sornay’s captain?”

“Will command Sornay’s company.”

“Keep him there.”

Elyas looked at the man. “You will both have to hold ground the other depends upon.”

“Then he should find me reliable. I will do what I sign.”

The steward added his lord’s mark to the draft. Elyas sat back and flexed the fingers he had kept still too long in the cold.

That evening Lormey’s first men arrived at the same lodging as Sornay’s advance party.

Berent came to Elyas before the quarrel reached the yard. He had been asked to choose which company got the stable and which got the outbuilding. The stable had space for mounted men, but Sornay’s party had already put their packs in the dry room above it. Lormey’s sergeant said his lord had been promised equal treatment.

“What did you tell him?” Elyas asked.

“That I could find their lodging, Highness. I couldn’t settle what their lords had promised.”

“Good. Fetch both captains.”

The difference was small enough to embarrass every man required to explain it and large enough that none would concede. Elyas gave the mounted men the space beside their animals and had the dry room shared. Wace put two of his own soldiers in it as well. The decision pleased nobody completely and had the advantage of being possible to enforce.

Afterwards Berent stood beside the stair with a bundle of blankets.

“Do they always dislike each other?” he asked.

“For longer than either captain has been alive.”

“Then how do we make them—”

“Tonight, we make them sleep. Tomorrow we find work which requires both.”

Berent nodded and went upstairs. Elyas remained by the door while the last men carried in their packs. A rider from Harald arrived before it was shut for the night.

The message required him to wait where he was. His brother was coming.

Chapter 20 — His Brother’s Authority

Harald arrived with eight men and asked why his brother had allowed a royal captain to receive him.

Elyas had been in the lower quarters when the riders entered. Wace had met them at the gate, provided space for the horses, and sent word at once. There had been no discourtesy in the reception. Harald waited in the yard while Elyas crossed it, keeping his gloves on.

“I came as soon as I was told,” Elyas said.

“I expected you to be ready.”

“The messenger said today.”

“And here I am.”

Elyas embraced him. Harald returned it briefly, with a hand between his brother’s shoulders, then stepped back. He had lost weight since they last met. His coat fitted badly beneath the arms, and there was a roughness at the corner of his mouth where he had bitten the skin.

“Have you eaten?” Elyas asked.

“I am not here to be fed.”

“You can be fed while we speak.”

Harald looked towards the men watching from the doorway.

“Somewhere private.”

They used the small chamber above the kitchens. Elyas sent for bread and wine and had Berent keep the passage clear. His brother took off his gloves only after the door was shut. The right glove had split at the thumb.

“My steward says you have Sornay’s men.”

“An undertaking for sixty.”

“And Lormey’s.”

“Eighty.”

“One hundred and forty men who were unavailable when I required them.”

“Yes.”

Harald turned towards the window, which overlooked the stable roof rather than the yard. He moved away from it again.

“You might at least have waited for my answer.”

“I wrote before leaving Valora. I have sent you the terms as they were made.”

“After they were made.”

“You knew I was coming.”

“I knew the queen wished it. I had not agreed to let her recruit my barons under my brother’s name.”

“They are joining to protect the western roads.”

“Then they could have joined when I summoned them.”

The bread arrived. Berent set it down and withdrew without attempting to find something else to do in the room. Harald watched the door close.

“Father would have had them here,” he said.

Elyas sat opposite him.

“Some of them would have delayed him too.”

“He would have known what to do about it.”

“Perhaps.”

Harald looked at him sharply. Elyas kept his hands on the table.

“I don’t know what he would have done with this,” he said. “I know what Valdais will do if no one is on the road.”

“He will pass through it.”

“With the stores he needs and the men he still commands. If the queen follows him, both armies will take their supplies here.”

“Then why not let him pass quickly?”

“Because she will pursue him whether we help or not. If we can impose terms now, there is a chance to keep her men to them. If she comes fighting through, we will be trying to obtain protection after they have taken what they need.”

“You have decided that her victory is certain.”

“No.”

“Certain enough to make an agreement against a man who may still win.”

Elyas looked at the wine between them. His brother had identified the part he had wanted to make sound inevitable. There were still choices. Every undertaking from a baron helped make one outcome more likely and attached that household to it.

“I have decided that I will oppose him,” he said. “I should have written that more plainly.”

Harald took up his cup and put it down again.

“And when he is past you?”

“Then the men I have recruited must still be brought home. The agreement does not place them at the queen’s disposal for every later campaign.”

“Does she know that?”

“She has accepted it in writing.”

“Show me.”

His brother read slowly and questioned almost every restriction.

Some questions were useful. He noticed that an officer might claim stores from a holding which had already furnished its full provision and insist the emergency clause permitted it. Elyas agreed that the first contribution must be counted. Other questions returned to matters already answered. Twice Harald asked whether the men could be sent outside the duchy, each time reading the same prohibition as though its words might have changed.

“Wace is to command if you fall.”

“To preserve the common position until the officers can act.”

“A Viterran.”

“Sornay and Lormey will not serve under one another.”

“They ought to serve under me.”

Elyas looked up.

“Will you come?”

Harald lowered the page.

“There is work here.”

“I know.”

“The western estates have not furnished their accounts. I have petitions which have waited since before Father’s death. Every man who has failed an obligation now claims I cannot know what my father meant.”

“Then come far enough to take the muster. Let them make the undertaking to you with me present.”

“And afterwards? When I go home, they obey you again.”

“The command must be in the field.”

Harald pushed the papers away. His face had reddened.

“You make it sound as though I object to your being useful. I object to being told after it is done that there is no honourable way for me to refuse it.”

Elyas said nothing. Below them a pan struck another pan, followed by a woman’s sharp rebuke.

“I sent the company,” Harald said. “You asked, and I sent it. I have received the names of men who will never work as they did before. I have to see their wives in my hall. You write that the service was good and move on to the next thing you need.”

“I have seen them too.”

“And then you went back to the queen.”

Elyas felt the answer rise and kept it back. His brother had received the wounded, arranged for families, and given men he could ill spare at a time when little else had been asked kindly of him. That did not make the western road safe. It did make the accusation harder to dismiss.

“I should have come to you before beginning with Sornay,” he said.

“Yes.”

“I cannot undo the undertakings now without putting those men in a worse position.”

“So we arrive where you intended.”

“We arrive where we are.”

Harald looked at the papers for a long time. Then he called for his secretary.

The consent was temporary and confined to the named service.

Harald insisted that the barons furnish copies to the ducal household, that disputes over land remain within his jurisdiction, and that no royal officer install a permanent garrison under the commission. Elyas accepted these requirements. The supply clause was amended to record prior contributions. The resulting paper carried more conditions than either man had wanted when they began.

It also allowed the men to march.

The secretary sanded the signatures and went to make copies. Harald remained seated. Elyas offered him more wine, and this time he accepted.

“How is Mara?”

“Well.”

“I received her letter.”

“She hoped you would.”

“She thanked me for the cloth. It was our mother’s.”

“I know.”

“Some of it. The housekeeper chose what could be used.”

Elyas had seen the cloth without asking how it had been selected. He imagined his brother standing in a room full of things their parents had left, being asked to give an answer about each of them.

“She will be glad you sent it.”

“She said so.”

Harald drank. His anger had tired him without quite leaving.

“When Father was near the end, he kept asking whether the lower room had been opened. Nobody knew for whom.”

Elyas looked down at his hands.

“We opened it,” Harald said. “He asked again the next day.”

“Was he comfortable?”

“Sometimes.”

His brother turned the cup slowly between his palms. Elyas let the silence remain. There was no account of the war which would answer this part of what Harald had kept while he was away.

When they went downstairs, the duke’s horse had been brought round. Elyas asked him to stay the night. Harald said he had promised to be at another holding before morning.

“Write before the next agreement,” he said.

“I will.”

Harald mounted and looked over the men in the yard. They stood correctly for him. He acknowledged the courtesy and rode out with his eight attendants, carrying his own copy of the consent.

Rousset offered forty men and asked for a favour before naming their captain. He laid his gloves side by side, aligning the fingers with the edge of the table, and invited Elyas to consider what was proper.

He wanted authority to resume possession of a holding administered by his brother-in-law. His niece was under age, her father dead, and the arrangements made during her mother’s illness had remained in place long after the illness ended. Harald’s court had not heard the case. The brother-in-law was now said to be considering an alliance with one of Valdais’s adherents.

“Which would put the holding on our road,” Rousset said. “You see why the matter has become urgent.”

Elyas read the description. The estate would provide useful forage and a place to quarter men before the western approaches. If he let Rousset take it under the commission, the local explanation would be that the prince had judged the wardship through his army.

“What does the child’s mother say?”

“She remains under her brother’s influence.”

“Does she wish the administration changed?”

“She will be given proper provision.”

“That does not answer.”

Rousset’s face grew still.

“Highness, I am asking to exercise a right. I do not propose to ill-use my own niece.”

“Then put the right before the duke’s hearing.”

“I have. It has sat there since before his father died.”

Elyas could believe it. He also had his brother’s fresh restriction on land disputes in the case beside him.

“I will ask for a date. I will not send your forty men to decide the matter by occupying the house.”

“They would secure it against the enemy.”

“Has its garrison declared for Valdais?”

“Not yet.”

“Then the military need does not give me the judgement you want.”

Rousset rose. He had welcomed the prince with a generosity which made the change in his manner noticeable to the servants by the door.

“My own security must be settled before I can spare men.”

“How many can you send without the forty?”

“None.”

Elyas closed the paper and gave it back. He had spent most of the day hearing a case he could not decide and lost the soldiers whose presence would have justified the halt.

Wace made no comment when told. He changed the expected number on his working list and asked whether they would move at first light. Elyas said yes.

Before leaving, Elyas sent for the men who kept the road under the duke’s authority.

Wace had proposed asking each baron for a guide. Elyas had watched Sornay describe Lormey’s ground and knew what such guides would provide: the other man’s bad roads, the other man’s broken bridges, reasons to lodge soldiers in the other man’s barns.

“Bring the men responsible for maintaining it,” he said. “Ask them together.”

“Under the queen’s commission?”

“Under the duke’s consent. Copy that part of Harald’s letter. They must know whose road they are answering for.”

Three came to the lodging, two reluctantly. The eldest had known Oswyn. He bowed to Elyas and began telling him how faithfully the roads had been maintained in his father’s time.

“Were they?” Elyas asked.

The man hesitated. One of the others laughed before stopping himself.

“Then tell me where they weren’t. I need a route for men coming away from the mill road, with wounded in a wagon.”

He put a rough plan before them. At first they spoke to him separately, each avoiding correction of the others. Elyas moved the plan into the middle and asked who would undertake to lead that wagon through.

Their answers changed. A lane which one had called sound ended at a gate too narrow for the heavier carts. The better route crossed a holding whose tenant had been forbidden to admit strangers while the boundary was disputed. Sornay’s men might pass if they kept their badge visible; Lormey’s would be stopped.

Wace looked at Elyas.

“I can put men at the gate.”

“And have the tenant call his neighbours to keep them out.”

“He would be obstructing the army.”

“He would believe he was defending his lord’s possession. His lord would agree, whichever of them he served.”

Elyas asked for the tenant’s name, then had the owner sent a request for temporary passage under his own undertaking. No boundary would be decided by its use. Both barons would receive the same words, with the duke’s consent attached. Elyas would answer any claim arising from damage by the troops he led.

The eldest road keeper tapped the narrow gate on the plan.

“They’ll want to know when it ends.”

“When the wounded and the last guard have passed. Have someone of yours there to witness it.”

“That leaves the gate broken if the enemy follows.”

“Yes. Put that in the claim too.”

Elyas signed the request before they left. He had wanted another forty men. Instead he had undertaken to repair a gate and hear whatever damage a retreating company might do to a field. He sent Berent with the keeper to learn the turn from the mill, then wrote to Harald before moving on.

The western valley opened before them under a pale sky.

Berent had ridden ahead with the guide, then stopped at the rise to wait. Elyas came up beside him and could see why. From here the Scar looked like country an army could cross almost anywhere. The slopes were long and moderate. Fields rose in shallow steps beyond the river, with orchards and a few strips of woodland between them. Smoke stood over a mill. A cart moved along the dry road on the far side, so distant that the horses appeared to pull it without taking any steps.

“That is the road he will want,” the guide said.

“Why that one?” Berent asked.

“Because someone has been paying to keep it a road.”

The man pointed to the other tracks. One climbed through the northern holdings and returned west after a long bend. Another crossed the water-meadows, passable for riders now but already marked by dark stretches where wheels would sink. A third went south through vineyards, with narrow turns and retaining walls which would have to be broken to take heavy wagons through in any quantity.

Elyas dismounted and walked down to the old mill road. The ground beside it was firm. A low bank followed the boundary of a field, interrupted by two wide gateways. Beyond the nearer gate lay a shallow ditch partly full of fallen leaves. The road itself curved enough to conceal men waiting beyond the mill’s outbuilding.

Wace joined him.

“He can go north,” the captain said.

“Yes.”

“With the wagons?”

“Given time.”

“He can bring men across this field and take us from the side.”

Elyas walked through the gate and looked back. From there the weakness was plain. The bank gave cover against the road and little against a force crossing higher ground. Holding the preferred route would require defending enough of the field to make that movement difficult, while keeping their own withdrawal clear.

“Sornay’s horse above us,” he said. “Foot along the bank. A reserve by the lower track, where it can cover either the field or our way out.”

“If they come.”

“When they come, we can decide which ground their numbers permit us to hold.”

The guide had begun speaking to the miller, who wanted to know whether the soldiers intended to occupy his wheelhouse. The mill was grinding for several households which would need the meal whether Valdais came or not. Elyas went to hear him.

They inspected the outbuilding, the yard, and the track carts used to turn. The miller showed him a raised strip where loaded wagons could stand without sinking after rain. The same strip would carry wounded men out if the bank could no longer be held.

“I need this kept clear,” Elyas said.

“Then where do I put the carts?”

They found another patch of firm ground beside the upper gate. It would make unloading slower. The miller said so and asked who would pay for the extra labour.

Wace called for the clerk travelling with the supply money. Elyas stayed until the amount was agreed. Berent went with the miller’s boy to see where the horses could drink without blocking the working yard.

At dusk Elyas rode back to the rise. Lamps had been lit below. The northern road remained open, climbing gently out of the valley and away. He could see a rider on it, moving at an easy pace between the last two fields.

He wrote to Tylan by the light in his lodging. He described the dry road, the alternatives, the force promised and the forty refused. He asked when he might expect news of the royal advance.

Then he began a second letter, to his brother, telling him exactly where he intended to put the men.

Chapter 21 — The Request Brought Twice

Guiran returned with his whole household and a paper which permitted him to travel in the wrong direction.

Ouen read the destination, turned the sheet over to see whether there was an amendment, and handed it to Halden. Outside the hall, Perrine was trying to prevent one of the children from sitting on the trunk that held the instruments. The other had already gone to look at the horses.

“You said you had passage,” Ouen told him.

“I thought I did.”

“This takes you north.”

“To the landing.”

“And then farther north.”

“The officer said we could cross there and turn.”

“Which officer?”

Guiran named him. Halden recognized the name and fetched a second account. The man had authority over the road to the landing. He could permit the players to reach it, but the ferry guard belonged to another command and would not let them cross into the queen’s country under a direction to continue north.

“What did the ferry guard tell you?” Ouen asked.

“To bring the proper paper.”

“He appears to be right.”

“Yes, my lord.”

Guiran looked tired. His cap had lost some of its shape, and a dark line marked the place where rain had soaked through one shoulder of his coat. Ouen could see that the man had hoped to arrive with a simple grievance against an obstructive guard and been presented with the guard’s reasonable explanation.

“Where are you engaged?”

“At Valora. Two evenings confirmed, and more if the company likes us.”

“Jaennis’s company?”

“Yes.”

Ouen looked at Halden.

“Has my sister written?”

“There is a letter. And another addressed to you from the lady Maelis.”

“Bring hers first.”

Maelis wrote a large, clear hand and used less paper than Vaelrie.

She acknowledged his help in recovering the trunks and confirmed that she had arranged the engagement. She gave the names of the four performers and the two children travelling with Guiran and Perrine. Colas and Mirelle were the other adults. The hired carrier was to take them only to the crossing and return, unless another driver could be obtained on the far side. Maelis had asked Jaennis’s household to obtain permission for the royal part of the journey; she was writing independently to ask whether Ouen could clear the Adrian part.

At the bottom she added that no one needed to be received under a false name, despite Colas’s conviction that changing his would improve his prospects.

Ouen smiled and gave the letter to Halden.

“Can we send them to the landing with that?”

“With your direction, yes. Beyond it, they need the royal instrument.”

“And where is that?”

“Expected with the next messenger.”

“How long?”

Halden gave a day, then the two reasons it might be later. Ouen asked whether the players could remain where they had lodged before. They could, if the lodging was paid. Guiran had spent the remainder of his fee getting to the first barrier and back.

Perrine came into the hall when Ouen sent for her. She stood beside her husband but answered the practical questions herself. There was bread for another day. They could sleep at the cheaper lodging, provided the keeper did not put them in the loft again; one of the children had been coughing. Colas had a little money but needed it to replace a reed before the engagement. None of this was presented as a demand until Ouen asked what they proposed.

“To stay until the paper comes,” she said. “We can work in the interval.”

“Here?”

“If you wish it.”

Hew had been asking to hear the music since the first visit. Ouen considered the prospect of a small evening at home, with no one explaining an account until afterwards.

“What do you charge?”

Perrine named an amount.

“For one evening?”

“For the four of us. And the instruments.”

He looked at Guiran, who was watching his wife with the relief of a man spared a familiar negotiation.

“Two evenings for that.”

“One, and we remain afterwards if your household wants to dance.”

“Until when?”

“Until the children must sleep. Or later, if someone can stay with them.”

Ouen agreed to the first arrangement. Halden wrote it down before anybody could improve upon it in memory.

The royal answer arrived after the first performance and before the second evening Ouen had hoped to obtain for free.

Hew had fallen asleep halfway through a tune and woken furious that the players had continued without him. Iselde had carried him out while Perrine quietly changed the next piece to something the child already knew. Hearing it from the passage, he had become willing to go to bed. Ouen found himself grateful enough to stop arguing about the additional food.

Now he read the royal reply at the same table where the musicians had set their cups.

It granted passage to the named party along a specified route. The ferry captain could admit them on the appointed day, after the men escorting witnesses from the Corbel drainage hearing had finished using the landing. An escort all the way to Valora was refused. A road company would take them as far as its own quarters; beyond that they must travel with the ordinary carriers.

“Witnesses,” Ouen said. “Do they need the whole ferry?”

“For the horses, apparently,” Halden answered.

“They have been arguing about that ground since my father was young.”

“It seems they have now been heard.”

“And the men must be escorted home afterwards.”

“Would you wish to give testimony against a powerful neighbour and find your own way back?”

Ouen set down the paper. He had meant only that the players’ journey was becoming absurdly complicated, but the question had an answer he could not improve by saying it.

A separate note corrected the passenger description. Someone in the royal office had compared the letters and noticed that the bearer’s household would not necessarily include Colas and Mirelle. All four adult names had been entered. The children were described by parentage. The carrier’s return was covered separately so that the permission to cross would not oblige him to follow the players south.

“Who has done this?” Ouen asked.

Halden examined the closing lines.

“Lyra, for Master Pellam.”

“Does she know these people?”

“Her note says she checked the engagement with the household.”

Ouen imagined the little cup in the upper room at Valora, his sister turning to listen to a voice behind her while clerks somewhere else determined who could cross a river to sing there. He had found the household comfortable. He had not considered how much work went into making it possible for people to arrive.

He called for Guiran.

The ferry captain’s objection came back before the players left.

He would honour a properly issued royal protection once the party had crossed. On his own side he required the authority of the commander responsible for the approach. Ouen’s first direction had permitted the journey to the landing, but had not said that the party could leave the duke’s territory from it.

Ouen read the answer twice.

“He knows what was intended.”

Halden did not dispute it.

“Then why ask?”

“He wants his own instructions covered.”

“With my signature.”

“Yes.”

The captain had not invented the caution. Men were leaving households without permission, carrying information with them or merely seeking quieter places to earn a living. Some returned with money whose origin they declined to explain. Ouen’s father wanted to know who passed out of his country. He had said so plainly in an instruction Ouen had accepted as reasonable before it concerned the six people waiting in his yard.

“Send for the officer who brought this,” Ouen said.

The man arrived with the captain’s copy and a description of the trunks. He had been told there were costumes. He wanted to know whether weapons could be hidden among them.

“Anything can be hidden in a trunk,” Ouen said.

“Then we must look.”

“Look here. In front of their owners. Enter what you find, seal it, and have your captain acknowledge the seal.”

“He may still require it opened.”

“Then he can open it with the same owners present. He is not to retain the instruments because he dislikes the shape of a case.”

The officer accepted that. He asked whether the passage was personal or under a general protection for people employed by the queen’s household.

“Personal,” Ouen said. “These names. This journey. Their employment gives them nothing else from me.”

He could give that answer with certainty. It was pleasant to have reached something he understood.

They inspected the trunks in the covered yard. The performers stood close while clothes and instruments were lifted out. Colas produced a short knife before anyone found it and said he used it for reeds. The officer examined the edge, tested the hinge of the case, and returned it. Mirelle grew angry when a painted mask was set face-down on a damp board. Ouen handed it back to her himself.

Perrine checked the folds as everything was replaced. The two children had been sent to Iselde and were being kept occupied with Hew’s wooden bird. Their absence made the inspection quicker and left their mother free to insist on the way the screen was packed.

The officer sealed the fastenings. Ouen signed the permission to leave.

Vaelrie’s letter had remained beside his cup all morning.

He opened it after the players had gone. She wrote that Maelis had received his answer about the trunks and was pleased, though she disputed the account of the tune. According to her, Guiran had three tunes about which he was excessively proud, and Ouen had been fortunate to hear only one.

There was a drawing for Hew, this time a bird with the correct feet and an absurdly large cup beside it. Beneath it Vaelrie had written that broken crockery did not excuse the failure to drink properly.

The man who spoke of Gelfrey has sent another request. I know this because I saw it carried in while I was waiting for Jaennis. I do not know what is in it or what the queen will answer. You may save yourself the trouble of asking me to discover it.

Ouen snorted. Iselde looked up.

“Your sister?”

“Who else.”

He continued reading.

Vaelrie had missed an evening she had wanted because she had promised to sit with Jaennis while a painful headache passed. Nobody had asked her to remain; she wrote that she would have been miserable enjoying herself and wondering whether Jaennis was worse. By the time she came downstairs the song she wanted to hear had been sung. Catrin had offered to sing it again another day. Maelis said they must let the first singer finish quarrelling with the musician before arranging a repetition.

It was an account full of people Ouen only partly knew, with details that mattered because his sister had wanted to be there. He put it beside his own copy of the players’ passage and began answering the part about the headache. Then he asked whether she would tell him how Guiran and the others were received when they arrived.

Before he finished, Hew came in carrying the wooden bird and asking where the little boys had gone. Ouen showed him the drawing. The child studied the cup, then went to fetch his own so they could be put beside one another on the table.

Chapter 22 — The Further Instruction

The woman in the cord shop had taken down the shutter facing the lane. Light fell through the opening onto her nephew’s hands. He was drawing a length of cord through a lump of wax, with an expression of such disgust that Cedric looked for a cut.

“He wants to be outside,” the woman said.

“I have finished.”

“You have reached the end. Do it again.”

The boy pulled the cord back through the wax. Cedric laid his gloves on the counter and asked for the woman’s usual measure. He had begun buying the same thing each visit, which had seemed prudent until she asked him what he could possibly be tying up.

Today she measured without comment. Her nephew watched the narrow loop of coins at Cedric’s belt.

“Your sister is here,” she said.

The boy dropped the wax. She caught his sleeve before he could run past her and wiped his hands with a cloth. When he had gone she left the cloth on the counter.

“There is someone waiting.”

“Your sister?”

“A man.”

Cedric looked towards the back door.

“He came yesterday. You’ll find him through there.”

The room behind the shop held a table with one leaf lowered, a stack of empty baskets, and a man removing the skin from an onion with his thumbnail. He was perhaps forty, with a large head and narrow shoulders. A travelling cloak lay across the other chair. He moved it when Cedric entered.

He asked the agreed question.

Cedric gave the answer. The man set down the onion and took a folded letter from inside his coat.

“Read that first.”

The outer fold bore Cedric’s name. Beneath it was Fayd’s seal, intact. He had seen the impression on the paper settling his travelling expenses, and the same small break crossed its edge. Inside, the hand was the one which had altered the allowance for a groom.

Fayd acknowledged the report concerning Verren. He repeated the question Cedric had heard the lord ask, including the word horse. The next sentence named the new work Ysabeau had offered Cedric and observed that it would give him access beyond the public hours of her command.

Cedric read to the foot of the page. Then he went back to the sentence after the acknowledgement.

You are to kill Dame Ysabeau de Corvaunt before the spring dispositions place her beyond your ordinary access.

The man across the table was putting the onion skins into an empty bowl.

“Who are you?” Cedric asked.

“Perrat. I carry Master Fayd’s instructions.”

“Is that your name?”

“It’s the name you may use.”

Cedric laid the paper flat. His fingers remained on it, covering the words about her offer.

“I was sent to report.”

“You have reported well.”

“And now?”

“The instruction is there.”

He read the rest. It was short. The carter, the ferry, and the lodging beyond were to be available on the arrangements already made. Perrat was authorised to settle particulars and receive Cedric’s answer. If the work were completed, Cedric would join Fayd’s office with responsibility for men operating on the river approaches. Payment would begin from the day he left Ysabeau’s service. The claim against his former allowance remained untouched.

He had expected, absurdly, that the last lines would alter the first. They concerned a horse.

“Does the queen know?”

“I carry Master Fayd’s instruction.”

“I can read who signed it. Does she know what he is asking?”

“I have no instruction from her to show you.”

Perrat looked at the paper until Cedric moved his hand.

“Don’t give yourself an answer I haven’t brought.”

Outside, something wooden struck the shop counter. The woman spoke to a customer in the loud, agreeable voice Cedric knew from his first visit. She was explaining why the length he wanted would cost more than the one in his hand.

Cedric stood. His chair caught beneath the table leaf and lifted it. Perrat steadied his bowl while Cedric pulled the chair free.

“I need air.”

“There’s a yard.”

“I know there is a yard.”

Perrat inclined his head towards the door. He made no move to follow.

A hen was standing on the woodpile with one foot raised. Cedric shut the door behind him. The hen lowered its foot and watched him.

He had sat in this yard before, on the first journey south, eating bread the shopkeeper had brought because he had arrived too late for the ordinary meal. He remembered resenting the wait. He had wanted a horse held ready for him and someone who knew how far he had ridden.

Now he could hear his own horse moving in the shelter beside the gate.

He could go to it. The woman would perhaps call after him about the cord. Perrat could write that he had refused. There would be no need to invent an answer until he reached Ysabeau’s quarters, where he could tell her he had been buying what he had in fact bought. She would ask why it had taken so long only if she needed him sooner.

He put a hand inside his coat. Fayd’s letter lay against the lining.

If he showed her, she would read the whole thing. She would reach the acknowledgement of Verren and ask what report he had sent. He imagined her asking it twice, because his first answer would concern something else.

The hen jumped down. Cedric moved his foot to give it room.

He had written to his father about the post she offered. The letter was already gone. He had described the men he would be responsible for, the pay, the possibility of remaining when the armies dispersed. He had omitted the reprimand after the affair with Gauter. It had seemed proper to send good news without supplying Edran with a reason to qualify it.

Lorran might have read it by now. He might have had it read to him while his hands rested on the arms of his chair. Cedric could see him turning his head at the part about continuing employment. He had used Ysabeau’s full name and title.

Behind the door Perrat coughed. A chair scraped, then grew still.

Cedric returned before the man could come looking for him.

“Taken alive,” he said.

Perrat had bread now. He put the piece he held back on his plate.

“By whom?”

“Men brought in under a reason she would accept. She rides to inspect quarters. There are places a small party could be separated from the rest.”

“You could arrange that?”

He could think of places. Ysabeau had shown him two herself, stopping him from sending wagons ahead of their escort. He began describing one, then heard how much he would have to explain: the turning hidden by the mill wall, the farther track, the houses from which someone might see.

“Perhaps,” he said.

“And keep her?”

“Until the houses have made their agreements.”

Perrat drew the bowl of skins aside.

“What happens when Valdais offers to exchange someone for her?”

“The offer can be refused.”

“By the queen? On what charge would she hold a captured commander after accepting terms with the houses concerned?”

“She needn’t accept an exchange during a war.”

“No. She could be asked to refuse it. Master Fayd has chosen another course.”

“Then this isn’t about whether it could be done.”

“I haven’t said it couldn’t.”

Perrat broke his bread. Cedric wanted to knock the plate out of his hands. Instead he sat down, more carefully than he needed to.

“She has captains. Killing her won’t remove them.”

“No.”

“Rovel knows the roads. Derrit can bring a company across bad ground. She doesn’t sit over every order they give.”

“Which of them can demand horse from Verren and expect him to send it?”

Cedric said nothing.

“You were there.”

“He sent six.”

“And kept explaining the others. You wrote that too.”

Perrat waited. Cedric remembered Verren talking across the table while Ysabeau listened. She had known the name of a steward, the amount held in a store, the excuse used on an earlier occasion. The men she could send mattered. So did the certainty that she would remember what had been promised.

“Valdais can appoint someone else.”

“He can. The houses can answer us before that man has established himself.”

“Us.”

“Master Fayd’s office.”

“And if they don’t answer?”

Perrat wiped his fingers on the edge of the cloth.

“They will have seen that he can reach someone they thought he couldn’t.”

The customer in the shop laughed. The woman laughed back, shorter, and Cedric heard the sound of something being dragged across the counter.

He bent towards Perrat.

“You expect them to know who did it?”

“I expect them to consider who can protect them.”

“From you.”

“From the men still requiring their service. From their neighbours. They have several worries.”

“And I am to be one of them.”

Perrat looked at him for a moment.

“If you undertake this, you will have work afterwards.”

They spoke until the light no longer reached the floor between their chairs.

Cedric proposed delaying her with a summons. Perrat asked whose summons she would obey, and Cedric had to say Valdais’s. Neither of them could supply it. He suggested taking the dispatches which fixed the spring movements. Perrat said they would be useful and that their loss would buy a few days. The instruction remained.

At last Cedric asked whether Fayd had considered approaching her.

“You have served her. Would she change sides?”

“No.”

The answer came too quickly to soften. Cedric resented having been made to give it.

Perrat leaned back. One boot was split where the upper bent above the toes. A traveller’s repair had drawn the leather tight with coarse thread. Cedric looked at it while the man told him what the promised appointment would require.

There would be two men at first, perhaps a third when the agreements justified another crossing. Cedric would direct their journeys and judge what information should go forward. He would answer to Fayd. He would have money for the work and have to explain its use. No patent of nobility was waiting in the other room; Perrat said that without being asked, and Cedric flushed.

“I didn’t ask for one.”

“No.”

“Would I be received openly?”

“At the office, yes.”

“Under my name?”

“Under your name.”

He imagined Edran arriving there with a question about the roads. He disliked himself for it and continued imagining the man outside the door asking whether Master Aeryl could see him.

Ysabeau would have let him remain. She had offered quarters, work, the prospect of knowing what he would do when he woke. In her household he could become good at something and be paid to do it. He had wanted that so badly that he had written home before he knew whether he ought to wait.

Fayd was offering him men whose work he would direct. A place in the queen’s government, however far from the rooms where she sat. He would be helping end the war. He tried the thought again, with Verren’s frightened question in it. Houses would cease sending men. Valdais would have to draw back.

There would still be the woman in the room with him when he did it.

“What if I refuse?”

Perrat folded the cloth around the remaining bread.

“I take your answer back.”

“And the money already paid?”

“You earned it by reporting.”

“I keep reporting?”

“Master Fayd doesn’t propose to maintain the present arrangement after you give your answer. I am to close it.”

“Either answer.”

“Yes.”

Cedric could remain with Ysabeau. He could stop sending her movements away, do the work she gave him, and let Fayd find another man. He did not have to produce the letter to her. The knowledge of what had been asked would remain, and he would have to decide what to do with it each time a stranger entered the house.

“Will you send someone else?”

“I have no instruction about anyone else.”

“You have an answer for everything until I ask something I need to know.”

“I can tell you what happens if you agree. I can’t tell you what Master Fayd will decide when he hears you haven’t.”

There was enough anger in Cedric now to make speaking easier. He stood and paced the short distance to the baskets. One had a broken handle. He turned it so the wicker would stop catching his coat when he passed.

“How long?”

“For your answer?”

“For the work.”

“Before the dispositions are made. You know when she’s moving better than I do. Give me a day we can use for the carter.”

“I won’t have another man sent into the house.”

Perrat watched him.

“I can get in,” Cedric said. “There is no reason to put someone else there.”

“Then you accept?”

He stopped by the table.

“Yes.”

Perrat drew the letter towards him. Cedric let it go.

The arrangements took less time than their argument.

Cedric gave the earliest day he could be certain Ysabeau would be back at her quarters. There was work to finish first. She would be away with the men and return by the ordinary road; he would have duties in the house that evening. Perrat repeated the day and asked whether a delay was likely. Cedric said it was always likely.

“The carter can wait another day. After that you’ll have to get word here.”

“And the ferry?”

“The evening bell still closes it. If you’re late, you stay in the yard.”

Perrat indicated the door towards the woman in the shop. She had already told Cedric how to reach the carter. Cedric wanted to ask whether she knew what had changed, but he could not see what use the answer would be. She would sell cord when he came through, as she had before.

He would need to leave his own horse. Perrat asked about that and then the clothes he would be wearing. They discussed the ordinary things which would be on Cedric’s body when he came out of Ysabeau’s room. He answered until Perrat had what he wanted.

At the door Cedric turned.

“She gave me a place.”

Perrat had begun gathering the papers. He stopped.

“I understand.”

“No, you don’t.”

The man let the correction stand.

Cedric went through the shop, paid for the cord, and took his gloves from the counter. The nephew had returned. He was eating an apple, holding it by the unbitten skin so that his newly cleaned fingers would remain clean. Cedric almost told him to wash them afterwards. Instead he put the cord inside his coat and left.

Ysabeau was in the stable when he returned. A groom held Bastian while she ran a hand along the horse’s neck beneath the mane. There was a small rub where the collar lay.

“Did you find Rovel?” she asked.

“Before I went into the town.”

“And?”

“He wants the men who can walk sent first. The wagons can follow when the loading is finished.”

“He would. What did you tell him?”

“That I’d ask you.”

She glanced at him.

“Come here.”

He came close enough to smell the horse’s warm coat. She placed his hand against the leather where it lay over the rubbed place.

“Feel that edge.”

He did. A little hard ridge ran beneath his fingertips.

“Someone has mended it folded. It looks neat until you put the weight on. Show him, will you?”

The groom leaned in. Cedric moved his hand aside so he could see. Ysabeau waited while they took the collar off, then returned to Rovel’s request.

“Who goes with the wagons?”

“He has kept four men.”

“Can they lift Hannet without hurting him?”

“One helped last time. I’ll make sure he’s there.”

“And the others?”

“I’ll find out.”

She nodded. The groom carried the collar towards the light at the door. Bastian followed a step and was brought back with a quiet word.

Ysabeau reached into a pocket for a folded sheet.

“This came for you.”

His brother’s hand was on the outside. Cedric took it.

“Good news, I hope.”

“I haven’t read it.”

“No. I can see that.”

He looked up. She was smiling a little, waiting for him to catch up with himself. He managed to answer the smile.

“Thank you.”

“There’s food in the house. Aude has sent enough for six people, on the understanding that I eat all of it myself. You’ll have to help.”

She went to see the repair. Cedric remained beside Bastian, with the letter unopened in his hand. The horse lowered his head and nosed the pocket where Cedric had put the cord.

He moved it to the other side before the horse could pull it out.

Part IV — Before the houses answer

Chapter 23 — The Work Finished

Hannet wanted his boots.

They had been put beneath the seat while the men were loaded, and now a chest stood in front of them. Cedric offered to have them brought out when they stopped. Hannet looked at the chest, then at the two men waiting to lift him.

“I can get out myself if I have them.”

“You can’t walk that far.”

“I can get out.”

One of the men reached over the chest and pulled up a boot by the heel. He shook it, expecting the other to follow. Nothing did. Hannet watched him with the injured arm held close against his body.

Cedric climbed onto the cart. The second boot had slipped beneath a folded blanket. He put both beside Hannet, who worked his feet into them before he allowed anyone to help him up.

Rovel’s party was already on the road. Those fit enough for service were going to their new quarters; the men released for recovery were travelling with the wagons as far as the turn towards their own canton. Hannet’s sister was to meet them there. He had spoken of her six times that morning, each time supplying some new reason why she might have found it difficult to come.

“She has a boy to help,” he said now.

Cedric took the chest down. It belonged to the house they were leaving and should never have been loaded.

“Their man said take it,” the carter objected.

“Then their man can come and claim it.”

“He’s gone.”

A woman appeared in the doorway behind them, carrying a pot with a lid held on by string. She set it beside the chest.

“That’s his mother’s,” she said. “He wants it brought home when you pass.”

Cedric looked along the road. The house was three fields out of their way.

“You can leave it with us,” the carter said. “I’ll take it after.”

“You’ll have a second load after.”

“I know where she lives.”

“So do I. She can send someone.”

The woman tucked the pot beneath her arm again. Her expression made him feel ungenerous, but she took the chest by one handle and called for help. Cedric went round to the other handle before the carter could offer to carry it after all. They put it inside the door.

By the time he came back, Hannet had settled himself and was telling the other wounded man that the road was better beyond the rise. Cedric had ridden it the previous afternoon. It was worse. He let Hannet finish.

Edran’s letter was in his saddlebag.

He had read it after supper, then once more when the house was quiet. Their father had been pleased by Ysabeau’s offer. Edran hoped Cedric would serve her well. Two men from Harwick had returned from winter service, one with a cough that frightened his wife. There was trouble with a tenant whose elder son wished to take the holding before his father died. Edran had described the quarrel because Cedric knew the family and would understand why the younger son had taken the father’s side.

At the bottom, in a narrower space, he had written:

I have not shown Father the account of our quarrel you sent with your last letter to me. He has enough to consider. I was there, Cedric. You need not explain to me what I intended.

There was no invitation home. He had read the first paragraph again, looking for something in pleased that would make it warmer than the rest. The word had not changed.

Now he watched a man walking beside the second wagon, one hand on its side to steady himself. Ysabeau had told him to ride, but the jolting hurt more than walking. Each time a wheel entered a rut he lifted his hand until the wagon had steadied. Cedric could hear the man behind him breathing through his mouth.

The loads began to bunch on the rising ground. He rode forward.

At the top, Ysabeau had stopped beside a carter who was arguing with Derrit. The carter’s hired team was due back at noon. He would take his two horses from the wagon then whether the wagon had arrived or not.

“You agreed the far storehouse,” Derrit said.

“And the man who agreed said we’d reach it by noon.”

Ysabeau turned to Cedric.

“What have you arranged at the other end?”

“Men to unload. The empty wagons go back with the drivers.”

“Whose men?”

“The steward is to have them there.”

“Has he answered?”

“He acknowledged the order.”

She held out her hand. Cedric found the folded copy in his bag.

While she read it, the carter rubbed the nose of the nearer horse with his knuckles. The animal leaned towards him. Its shoulders were dark beneath the harness.

“Your wife needs them?” Ysabeau asked him.

“My brother. There’s seed to take up before the rain.”

“Would he accept our team this afternoon?”

The man considered the horses behind her with evident distrust.

“If they can pull.”

“They can. Derrit will go with them.”

Derrit looked at her. She handed Cedric back the order.

“And you will see that he gets them home. Both horses. Send your men back here and remain if the work takes longer.”

Derrit agreed without enthusiasm. The carter wanted to know whether the harness would come too. When that was settled, Ysabeau walked her horse down the line with Cedric beside her.

“You’ve ordered the steward to have it done,” she said. “He can obey by telling someone else.”

“He’s responsible for the storehouse.”

“Yes. Who takes the last man off the wagon?”

“I haven’t named anyone.”

“Find someone who will be there. Ask him what he needs. Then tell the steward what you’ve agreed.”

Ahead of them the first wagon had cleared the rise. The next carter called to his horses, walking beside the wheel with a hand lifted as if the load might follow it.

“I can go ahead,” Cedric said.

“You should have gone yesterday. Go now.”

The steward had provided six labourers. Four were boys young enough to find the arrival of soldiers entertaining, and two were old men who had been promised work lasting only until the midday meal. There were sacks piled across the storehouse floor, exactly where Cedric had expected to put the bedding.

He found the steward supervising the weighing of a delivery in the adjacent yard.

“They will have gone before you need the space,” the man said.

“The wagons are behind me.”

“Then they can wait outside. We can’t put these sacks in the rain.”

There was no rain yet. Cedric looked at the sky and refused the argument before it could begin.

“The men will need the floor whether it rains or not.”

“The upper room is empty.”

“Can you carry a man up those stairs?”

“Your men can.”

He went into the storehouse. A woman in a brown wool dress was stacking bowls beside the door. The youngest of the boys was helping her, handing them up two at a time. She stopped when she saw Cedric looking at the sacks.

“His brother won’t take delivery until he’s counted them himself,” she said.

“Whose brother?”

“The steward’s. His store is on the other side of the yard.”

“Is it locked?”

“No.”

Cedric went back. He asked the steward to come and count with him. The man protested that this had nothing to do with the soldiers. Cedric stood beside him until he put down the weight in his hand.

They counted the sacks into the brother’s store. Cedric carried the first two himself; the boys took that as permission and began bringing them in pairs. One split at the seam and spilled grain across the threshold. The steward made such a noise that both boys dropped their ends. Cedric knelt, held the torn mouth together, and told the smallest boy to fetch a bowl.

The woman came with a shallow basket instead. She gathered the loose grain while Cedric lifted the sack out of the doorway.

“Your name?” he asked her.

“Alis. I keep the place.”

“Will you be here when they come?”

“If you want them fed.”

“Have you anyone strong enough to help lift?”

“My son. He’s gone for wood.”

“Send a boy to bring him. We’ll carry the wood later.”

She wiped her hands on her apron and considered him.

“He gets paid for the wood.”

“Then he gets paid for carrying men. Ask him.”

By the time the wagons arrived the sacks were counted, the floor was swept, and Alis had sent one of the older boys for straw. Her son came across the yard carrying a bundle of wood in each hand. He set them down and listened while Cedric showed him where they would put the men. He wanted a second person of his own choosing at the heavy end. Cedric let him choose.

Hannet watched this preparation from the wagon. His sister had not arrived.

“We’ll get you inside first,” Cedric said.

“She’ll think I’ve gone on.”

“I’ll leave someone at the turn.”

“She doesn’t know the house.”

“I’ll tell him your name.”

Alis’s son climbed up beside Hannet. He crouched, listened to how the arm had to be held, and asked Hannet to show him with the sound hand. Cedric waited on the ground. When the man was passed down, his weight came suddenly into Cedric’s arms, heavier than he had expected. Hannet made a small sound against his shoulder and gripped the back of his coat.

They got him onto the bedding. Alis brought water. Cedric stayed on his knees until Hannet let go.

Ysabeau arrived with the last wagon. The men who could walk had reached their quarters, and Rovel had sent back two who knew Hannet. They found Cedric in the storehouse, explaining to Alis which men could feed themselves and which would need help cutting food.

“Ask them,” she said. “They have tongues.”

One of the men on the floor raised his head.

“He’s told her I can’t eat.”

“I said you can’t cut it.”

“Put the knife in my other hand.”

Cedric did. The man began sawing through his bread with great care. Alis left him to it.

At the door Ysabeau stopped Cedric and looked at his sleeve. Hannet had left a long mark of dried sweat and dirt where he had gripped it.

“Where are the drivers?”

“Eating in the yard. The wagons are unloaded.”

“And their teams?”

“Watered. Derrit’s gone with the replacement pair. I’ve sent a man to the turn for Hannet’s sister.”

She looked through the room, then went to speak to Alis. Cedric could hear her asking what had been promised for the use of the place. Alis told her, adding the hire of her son without hesitation. Ysabeau called for the steward.

Cedric went outside while they settled it. A driver was asleep against the wheel of his empty wagon with a piece of bread still in his hand. The youngest boy stood a little way off, waiting to see whether he would drop it.

“Leave him,” Cedric said.

“I wasn’t doing anything.”

“Go and help your mother.”

“She sent me out.”

“Then sit down.”

The boy sat on the step beside him.

Hannet’s sister came late in the afternoon. She was taller than he was, with the same narrow face. She had brought a small cart, bedding, and a son who clearly disliked being called a boy by the sentry at the turn. Cedric met her in the yard and started explaining where Hannet lay. She was already past him.

Inside, she stopped a pace short of the bedding.

“You’ve got thin.”

Hannet looked at her cart through the open door.

“Is that all you’ve brought?”

“What else did you expect?”

“Something to eat.”

She put her hands against her mouth, pressing them there. Cedric found a reason to go out again. A little later he heard her scolding Hannet for trying to sit without help.

They would travel in the morning. Alis had room for the sister and her son near the kitchen. Cedric told the sentry he could return to his company, then went to find Ysabeau.

She was standing with the steward, waiting for him to finish a copy of the agreed payment. Her horse cropped a little grass beside the wall. Bastian’s new collar lay smoothly against his neck.

“Done?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“Then come and eat before someone gives it away.”

They stayed the night because Ysabeau had to hear a dispute at the next village in the morning. Cedric slept in the loft above the stable. He woke once to a horse stamping and lay listening until another man told it to be still.

He had arranged to return the following evening. Perrat’s carter would be ready. He could still send a message postponing it, though there was no reason in the work he had been given. He thought of Edran’s letter in the bag beside his head and turned over.

The dispute kept them until after noon. Two officers had claimed the same billets, each with a paper issued by someone entitled to issue it. Ysabeau divided the rooms and sent one officer’s personal baggage back to his own house. The officer said his clothes would be ruined if they travelled in rain. She told him to lend a cover.

On the road home she asked Cedric whether his father was well.

“As well as before.”

“Did he answer you?”

“My brother wrote.”

She waited, giving him a chance to say more. He asked her instead about the men released for recovery. Could they be called back before the agreed term?

“If they can serve and their lord requires it. Why?”

“Hannet thinks going home means he’s finished.”

“Hannet would like it to. So would I. His arm may oblige us both.”

She rode ahead to speak to Rovel. Cedric followed at the distance the road allowed.

Aude was at the house when they reached it. Her own horse stood in the yard, and two servants were bringing a covered basket indoors. She came to the door with her sleeves rolled up.

“You said before dinner.”

“I was wrong,” Ysabeau said.

“You generally are about meals.”

“Is there any left?”

“There will be, if you stop talking in the doorway.”

Aude had to return to her sister that evening. She was coming back the following week, and she wanted the little room above the kitchen cleared of the saddles someone had stored there. Her sister’s daughter would be with her. The girl had never stayed in a house full of soldiers and had been promised a door that shut.

Cedric took the saddles down with the groom. Beneath them he found a stool missing one leg. Aude told him to leave it; she had a man at home who would mend it properly. He wrote a note to tie to it so nobody would burn it before she returned.

When the room was clear, she brought up a small coverlet from her basket and spread it on the bed. She stood back, moved it a little towards the window, then asked Cedric whether he thought the chimney smoked.

“I’ve never slept here.”

“Ask someone who has, would you? Before I promise her a fire.”

He said he would. Downstairs, Ysabeau was calling for the account of the movement. He went to fetch it.

Chapter 24 — His Father’s Horses

His father arrived while Hew was being persuaded to surrender a dead mouse.

The child had found it under the covered walk and wanted to show it to the hawk. Iselde had explained that the hawk’s food was chosen by someone who knew what might have killed it. Hew accepted the explanation and continued to hold the mouse by the tail.

“Your mother said put it down,” Ouen told him.

“I am putting it down.”

He lowered it an inch.

Valdais came through the arch into the yard, saw the small hand extended before him, and stopped his horse.

“What have you caught?”

Hew looked up. Recognition changed his whole face. He ran towards the horse, the mouse swinging from his hand, and Ouen caught him by the back of his coat.

“Wait until he’s down.”
