Valdais was already dismounting. He handed the reins to a man who had to step quickly to take them, bent, and held out both hands.

“Show me.”

“Father says it will make her ill.”

“Does he? Then he knows more about it than I do.”

Hew looked at Ouen as though this were an unexpected qualification.

“Leave it here,” Valdais said, “and show me the bird.”

He indicated the little wall beside the walk. Hew put the mouse on it, arranged its tail, and took his grandfather’s hand. Iselde stepped aside to let them pass. Valdais kissed her cheek and asked if the boy had been well, listening to the answer while Hew pulled at his fingers.

Ouen had received notice only that morning. He had ordered the upper rooms opened, sent a man to see what could be bought for supper, and found Halden already arranging both things. His father had come with fewer attendants than expected and twice as many horses.

He looked at them now. One grey was being led. A second carried a rolled cloak and nothing else.

“Are those staying?” he asked the officer beside him.

“His Grace will tell you.”

Of course he would. Ouen followed his family towards the mews.

The hawk disliked the company. Ouen sent Hew back to Iselde and took her onto the glove himself. His father stood just outside the door, watching her feet settle.

“She looks well.”

“She is.”

“Still going beyond the ditch?”

“When there’s something worth taking.”

Valdais smiled. He remembered the ground. Ouen had not expected that to please him so much.

“Show me tomorrow.”

“If the wind holds.”

“It will have to. I leave after dinner.”

Ouen turned the bird away from the light. Her wings lifted a little, then closed. He ran a finger beneath the jess to make sure it lay properly.

“I thought you would be here longer.”

“I thought I would have won the war by now. We are both disappointed.”

His father said it lightly. Ouen glanced at him to see whether he was allowed to laugh. Valdais had already turned towards the stable.

“I’d like to see Joss’s mare.”

“She cannot be ridden.”

“I was told.”

“Then why—”

“To see her, Ouen.”

They found the chestnut standing loose with her head over the half-door. The sore beneath the girth had closed, leaving a patch of new skin and sparse hair. Valdais asked the groom to bring her out, watched her walk, and ran his hand down the foreleg she had been favouring.

“Keep her here.”

“That was the intention.”

“Good. Where’s Joss?”

“Back with your men. On the fresh horse.”

Valdais nodded. He asked whether a particular bay had ever come back from the same company. Ouen did not know. The groom did; it had arrived thin and was eating better. They went to look at it.

His father could remember a horse he had seen once at a muster six months before. He knew the small white mark above its knee and that it had thrown its rider while a strap was being tightened. Ouen watched him feed it from his hand. The animal nosed his coat for more.

“That one can work again,” Valdais said.

“Light work.”

“Light work is work.”

Ouen felt the visit changing shape.

“How many do you want?”

His father looked at him.

“Let us eat first.”

At supper Valdais asked Iselde about the roof over the nursery, which had leaked during his last visit. It had been mended. He asked who had done it, remembering a man whose repairs he distrusted, and was satisfied when she supplied another name. Hew was allowed to sit with them long enough to show his wooden hawk, then refused to go because his grandfather had not yet seen it fly.

“Tomorrow,” Iselde said.

“He is going tomorrow.”

“After dinner.”

“What if it rains?”

“Then it flies indoors,” Valdais said.

Ouen looked at his wife. Her mouth tightened with the effort of remaining courteous.

“His bedchamber, perhaps,” she said.

“My bedchamber,” Valdais agreed. “There is less in it that belongs to anyone sensible.”

Hew accepted the promise. He held the bird against his chest as he was carried away, calling back an instruction about the window.

For a while they spoke of Vaelrie. Valdais had received a letter describing a supper and very little about the people who had attended it. Ouen knew the supper from another letter, with twice as many people and a quarrel about the music. He began explaining it, then stopped before he found himself giving his father something his sister had chosen not to give.

“She seems happy,” Iselde said.

“Yes,” Valdais answered. “She does.”

He drank and looked into the cup. Ouen wondered if he would say something about Jaennis. Instead he asked whether Maegwen had sent the cloth she had promised. Iselde said it had come with a note about the children.

The meal had nearly ended when a servant brought a little packet to Valdais. He put a hand over it before the servant could explain who had sent it.

“Later.”

Ouen saw the ribbon, a narrow green one with a gold edge. He had seen it in a woman’s hair that afternoon, at the house opposite the lower stable. She had been leaning from a window to speak to one of his father’s men.

Valdais put the packet inside his coat and resumed asking about Maegwen’s household. Iselde answered him with the same civility. Ouen could feel how carefully she kept her gaze from the place where the packet had disappeared.

When she rose, his father stood and held her chair. He thanked her for the meal, warmly and with particulars. She inclined her head, asked whether anything more was needed, and left them.

Ouen waited until the door had shut.

“Taliya sent cloth for the wounded men.”

“I know. I wrote to thank her.”

“She asked if it had arrived.”

“And it has.”

His father watched him. Ouen had meant to go further. The packet had been received in Iselde’s house, before her servants, and he disliked being made one of the people who pretended not to understand. Yet he could not find a sentence that did not sound like a child repeating a lesson on marital fidelity to a man with an army.

Valdais set down his cup.

“You may say it.”

Ouen looked at the table.

“Not here.”

For a moment his father seemed ready to take offence. Then he pulled the packet from his coat and put it beside his cup.

“I should have had it sent elsewhere.”

It was so little of what Ouen had meant that he nearly laughed. Valdais saw that too.

“Your grandfather could sit an entire evening beside a woman everyone knew adored him and ask only after her husband’s health,” he said. “I used to wonder whether he enjoyed disappointing her.”

“Which grandfather?”

Valdais’s face closed.

“Wymund.”

Ouen had not intended the question to sound as it did. He reached for the wine, missed the neck of the jug, and took it properly on the second attempt.

“I’m sorry.”

His father accepted the apology with a movement of his hand. When Halden entered, both men greeted him with more attention than the occasion required.

The horses were wanted for a service Ouen could understand. Valdais had wounded men to move and wagons which could not be left on roads about to carry troops. A team sent south now could return with men who would otherwise remain in quarters his army might have to abandon.

“Six teams,” his father said.

“From here?”

“From the district. Yours among them.”

Ouen looked at Halden. The older man laid out the list they had prepared. Three teams were already engaged carrying seed and food between the lower holdings. Two more belonged to carriers whose work had been repeatedly postponed through winter. Another was promised to the next household consignment.

Valdais read without speaking. He drew a finger down the page, stopping at the names of two owners.

“These have furnished very little.”

“They furnished in the autumn,” Halden said.

“To whom?”

He supplied the officer’s name. Valdais remembered him and made a small, dissatisfied sound.

“Then he has not entered it.”

“We have their acknowledgement here.”

“Show it to my secretary before he leaves.”

Ouen sat back. For once the difficulty appeared to be going where it belonged. Then his father turned the paper towards him.

“The teams are still needed.”

“The seed must go out.”

“Some of it must.”

“All of it, if they are to sow it.”

“Do you suppose I want it left in a store?”

Ouen could hear his own voice becoming high. Halden stood beside the table with a second paper in his hand, waiting for a question neither of them was asking.

Valdais took that paper too.

There followed an interval in which Ouen’s father and Ouen’s officer discussed his holdings over the space where he sat. He understood enough to resent it. Two of the seed journeys were to places beyond the next ridge. They could be combined if the loads were taken first to the same yard. It would require unloading and loading again, which someone would have to pay for. The carriers could do it with one fewer team over three days, provided they began at once.

“Then you have one,” Ouen said.

“I have one,” Valdais agreed.

“The household consignment can wait.”

“That makes two.”

“Those two owners will give a team each if their earlier service is acknowledged.”

Halden looked at him.

“They may,” he said.

“They have said they would.”

“They said they would consider another call when the first was entered.”

Valdais’s patience broke.

“Have them here in the morning. They can consider it in my presence.”

Ouen folded his hands beneath the table. He wanted to say that this was exactly why nobody told his father the truth until a thing had already gone wrong. The owners would agree. Afterwards Halden would have to discover which horse had been borrowed and what work had been abandoned.

He saw the grey being led through the yard in his mind, the one he had given Renaud after making two promises about the same five animals.

“I can give you four certainly,” he said. “The last two need hiring. They won’t be here tomorrow.”

“How long?”

“Halden?”

“Four days, if the posting houses have what we need.”

His father considered it. Then he altered the required departure, separating the first four teams from the other two.

“You will send them to the receiving officer. They are to bring men back. If someone asks to keep them with a baggage train, he is to show my instruction for it.”

Ouen wanted that written. He asked, expecting another quarrel. Valdais called for his secretary.

By the time Ouen went upstairs, Iselde had dismissed her woman and was trying to undo a fastening at the back of her neck. She turned when he came in.

“Do this before you start.”

“I haven’t said anything.”

“You were drawing breath.”

He put down the candle and worked the little hook free. There was a red place beneath it. He rubbed it with his thumb, and she let her head fall forward.

“Better?”

“Yes. Keep doing it.”

He had prepared an account of the horses in which he had conducted himself rather well. With her hair against his wrist, it seemed an awkward thing to begin. He kissed the place instead.

She turned under his hand and kissed him properly.

“What was that for?” he asked.

“I wanted to.”

He smiled. She touched the corner of his mouth.

“There you are.”

He drew her towards him. The loosened collar slipped from one shoulder, and she caught it before it fell farther.

“Your father has promised Hew an indoor flight.”

“In his own room.”

“Which was a generous offer of someone else’s window.”

Ouen laughed against her hair. She was laughing too, quietly, with a hand over his mouth when he tried to imitate Hew explaining how small a horse the hawk might take. He caught her wrist and kissed her palm.

“Come out with us tomorrow.”

“I thought you wanted nobody else.”

“I want you.”

She studied him for a moment, then took her hand away from his mouth.

“Then you can ask for my horse when you ask for yours. Otherwise I’ll still be standing in the yard when you remember.”

“I will.”

He reached for the bell. She caught his sleeve.

“Later. They’re still awake downstairs.”

She drew him back to her. He left the candle where it was.

The morning wind held. Iselde came down to find her horse waiting beside his. She glanced at Ouen and smiled.

They took the hawk beyond the ditch, with Hew riding before a groom and Iselde following at her own pace. His father had wanted more company. Ouen refused it on the bird’s behalf, and Valdais yielded so readily that he wished he had thought to use the argument the night before.

The hawk went wide. For a moment Ouen lost her against the pale ground beyond the hedge. Then she rose again and turned. He felt his father’s attention beside him, exact and unhurried, and forgot the wagons while he watched her come down.

They went across on foot. Valdais held Hew back until Ouen had the bird settled. The child whispered a series of questions which grew louder whenever nobody answered them.

“Will she take a horse?”

“No.”

“A small horse?”

“No.”

“An ill one?”

Valdais laughed. Ouen looked up, smiling despite himself.

On the way back his father let the others go ahead. They walked their horses along the field edge. Ouen could hear Hew explaining the flight to Iselde as if she had been elsewhere.

“Ysabeau will have the cantons ready before the main movement,” Valdais said. “The river houses complain because complaint remains cheaper than refusal. She knows which ones mean it.”

“And if they do refuse?”

“Then I shall know before I depend on them.”

“Will you come back through the Scar?”

“If I must. I would rather be going the other way.”

Ouen had been thinking about the wagons since supper. He knew the western road where carriers tried to save half a day’s journey. One wet week could leave the verge so soft that a cart passing another had to be hauled back onto the stones.

“The mill road is narrow beyond the bend.”

“I’ve used it.”

“With an army?”

“With enough men to know it doesn’t grow wider out of courtesy.”

“There are other roads.”

“Yes.”

His father waited. Ouen knew the longer northern road cost time and the vineyard tracks cost repairs. He had meant to offer information. What emerged sounded like a demand that his father admit he had overlooked a road he had ridden himself.

“If someone held the bend,” Ouen said.

“With what?”

“Men.”

Valdais looked at him until he flushed.

“Harald will write a protest if his secretary finds a form strong enough. The barons will wait to see which neighbour is asked first. A few men can make a road unpleasant. They cannot keep an army in that valley.”

“Elyas is home.”

“So I hear.”

“He might persuade them.”

“He might. If he does, I will have to deal with what he brings. I shall not turn every wagon onto a worse road because your cousin may find followers.”

It was a reasonable answer. Ouen hated how much he wanted it to be foolish.

His father put a hand briefly against his shoulder.

“Keep the teams coming. Let me worry about the road when I reach it.”

Before he left, Valdais spent half an hour in the upper room letting Hew launch the wooden hawk from a chest. It struck the bedpost twice and lost some blue paint. Iselde came to fetch the child before a third attempt could reach the window.

Ouen stood beside his father’s horse in the yard. The secretary had given Halden the signed instruction. Four teams were to leave first; two hired teams would follow within four days. Their service ended with the return of the wounded unless a further order named them. Valdais had said nothing about the hiring cost. Ouen had ordered it entered against the household and would argue that later.

“Write to your sister,” his father said.

“I do.”

“Tell her I was pleased by her letter.”

“You could tell her yourself.”

“I will. You can tell her I said it too.”

He mounted, spoke to Iselde, and reached down to touch Hew’s upturned hand. The party moved out beneath the arch. Ouen watched until the last horse had gone.

Hew asked when his grandfather would return.

“I don’t know.”

“Tomorrow?”

“No.”

“After dinner?”

Ouen bent to pick up the wooden bird. A little blue flake came away on his finger. He rubbed it off before handing the toy back.

“We’ll ask him in our letter.”

Chapter 25 — The Door Left Open

The account was where Cedric had left it, beneath a book in his room. He lifted the book, checked that the sheets were in order, and discovered a line he had forgotten to correct. The payment to Alis’s son was entered with the hire of the wagons. He crossed it out and put it where it belonged.

There was a little daylight at the window. In the yard below, Aude’s horse was being brought round. He heard her ask for the basket, then heard the groom answer that it was already fastened behind her saddle.

His sword stood beside the bed. He left it there. The short blade he ordinarily wore was at his belt. He touched the fastening, then picked up the account.

Downstairs, Aude was waiting for Ysabeau to finish speaking to a servant. She saw Cedric on the stair.

“The chimney?”

“I haven’t asked.”

“Don’t forget. She’ll promise me anything and remember none of it when there’s a horse to move.”

Ysabeau turned.

“I heard that.”

“I meant you to.”

They went out together. Cedric waited in the passage while Aude mounted. She leaned down to say something close to Ysabeau’s ear; Ysabeau laughed and put a hand against her knee. Then the horse turned towards the gate.

Cedric could see the whole departure from where he stood. Aude would be on the ordinary road until dark, then at her sister’s house. Someone might be sent to fetch her before morning. He pressed the papers against his side until their edge hurt through the coat.

Ysabeau came back in.

“Have you got it?”

“Yes.”

“Bring it through.”

She went ahead of him into the little room where she received reports. Its window faced the wall of the adjoining house. A lamp had been set out but not lit. She moved it aside to make room on the table.

A servant followed with a jug. Ysabeau asked for another cup. Cedric heard himself say he did not need one, and she answered that she intended to make him read the thing to her, so he might as well wet his throat.

The servant went away. The door remained open.

Ysabeau sat, holding out her hand for the account. Cedric gave it to her and stood at the corner of the table. She read the first page with a finger following the names.

“Hannet went?”

“In the morning. With his sister.”

“And the boy?”

“Her son drove.”

“Good.”

She turned the page. The correction caught her attention.

“Was there a dispute about this?”

“No. I put it in the wrong place.”

She nodded and read on.

Cedric looked towards the passage. The servant was in the next room, setting something down. Beyond that lay the kitchen, where two women were arguing about the evening meal. Through the open door he could see the edge of Ysabeau’s cloak hanging from a peg.

He could take the cup when it came. He could sit down and read the account, ask her to approve it, go upstairs. His bag was still there. Edran’s letter was in it. He had packed nothing which would make a servant stop and wonder where he was going.

“Here,” Ysabeau said.

She pointed to the last line on the page. He moved closer.

“Derrit hasn’t come back?”

“He was to stay until the work was finished.”

“Yes. Put down who will receive the team when he does. Otherwise he’ll wake the entire house asking.”

Cedric took the pen. His hand was steady enough to write the groom’s name.

The servant returned with the cup, set it down, and withdrew. Ysabeau reached for the jug.

Cedric drew the knife and struck before she had set it down.

She turned against him. The jug hit the table and broke. Her hand caught his wrist with a force that drove him sideways, and the knife dragged loose. She was rising. He put his weight against her, stumbling over the chair, and struck again at the place beneath her ribs.

Her breath went out against his face. She gripped his coat. He held her against the table until her knees failed, then went down with her because she still had hold of him.

There was a sound in the passage. He could not tell whether it was a footstep or the door moving.

Ysabeau’s hand opened. He pulled the knife free and stayed crouched beside her. Wine was dripping from the edge of the table onto her sleeve. He could hear each drop.

He put his hand against her neck. He had to do it twice because his fingers were shaking now and he could not distinguish their movement from anything beneath them.

Then he stood.

Someone had called from the kitchen. He answered that he was coming, too loudly.

His cuff was wet. He pulled the cloak from the peg and wrapped it over his arm, hiding the sleeve. At the door he stopped long enough to look back. Ysabeau lay between the table and the chair, one foot caught beneath the rung. Her eyes were open.

He went out.

The woman at the kitchen door had a dishcloth over her shoulder.

“Does she want the meal brought through?”

“Wait a little.”

“It’s ready now.”

“I’ll tell her.”

He walked past her towards the rear door. She called something about the groom. He did not answer. A moment later he heard her call the same thing to someone else.

Outside, the yard held the ordinary confusion of horses settling for the evening. He passed the stable without going in. His own bay was there, with the saddle already taken off. The groom looked up from a bucket.

“Going out again?”

“Only into the town.”

The man accepted it. Cedric had gone on foot before.

He crossed the yard and used the smaller gate. The cloak caught against the latch. He pulled too hard and heard a stitch tear. Then he was in the lane with the gate shut behind him.

For a few paces he walked as if he were still being watched from the stable. At the turning he began to hurry. His wrist hurt where she had held it. He looked down and saw that he was carrying the folded account in his other hand.

He had brought it out with him.

The carter had almost finished loading when Cedric reached the yard. Two covered hampers stood on the wagon beside a box of pottery. A woman in a green coat waited on the step with a boy asleep against her shoulder. She watched Cedric come in but said nothing.

“You’re late,” the carter said.

“I am here.”

“Get in, then.”

Cedric stood beside the wheel. The step seemed unusually high. He put his foot on it and pulled himself up, catching the torn cloak beneath him. The carter gave the reins to the woman and came round to fasten the back.

“That belongs forward,” he said, pointing at the box by Cedric’s knee.

Cedric moved it. Something inside rattled.

“Carefully.”

The man checked the box, then climbed up. They left while there was still light enough to see the road without a lantern.

Cedric put the account inside the cloak. A spot had reached the paper, darkening a name at the edge. He folded that part inward. His other hand remained closed beneath the cloth.

The woman turned to him.

“Would you take his feet?”

“What?”

“The boy. He’s sliding.”

She had to lift the child with both arms while the wagon jolted. Cedric took the small boots and moved them across his knee. The boy did not wake. His mother settled back with a hand beneath his head.

“Thank you.”

Cedric looked at the boots resting against him. One had been repaired with a piece of lighter leather. He kept his hand where she had put it.

The town fell behind. At the first rise the carter got down to walk beside the team. The woman called to ask whether they would reach the ferry before it shut.

“If the road is clear.”

“It was clear yesterday.”

“Then we should have gone yesterday.”

She turned away. Cedric had a question about who she was and why she was here, but asking it would require explaining why he did not know. He watched the road through the space beside the carter’s shoulder.

At a narrow place two wagons were waiting nose to nose. A wheel had gone beyond the firm surface, and the driver would not move until men came to help. Their carter climbed down and went forward, swearing under his breath. Cedric looked back. The road behind them was empty.

After a while the woman woke the boy and told him to sit up. She climbed down too. Cedric heard her offering a length of rope, then arguing when a man said it would break. The boy watched her from the wagon.

“Are you ill?” he asked Cedric.

“No.”

“You look ill.”

Cedric turned his face away.

The rope held. He heard the wheel come free and the shout which followed it. Their carter returned carrying the borrowed length, muddy and stiff. The woman took it from him without comment.

They had lost the remaining daylight.

At the ferry the gate was shut.

A lantern hung beside the little shelter where men waited when rain stopped the crossing. Beyond it Cedric could hear the water striking something wooden. The ferry itself was on the other side.

The carter got down and shouted. After a time a man came from the house behind the gate with his coat pulled over a shirt.

“Morning,” he said.

“We were expected.”

“You were expected before the bell.”

“The road was blocked.”

The man looked at the wagon, at Cedric beneath the borrowed cloak, then at the child leaning against his mother.

“It’s closed.”

Cedric reached for his purse. The carter put out a hand before he could bring it clear.

“Leave it.”

“I can pay.”

“It won’t bring the boat back without the men.”

The ferryman had heard. He pointed across the dark water.

“Two have gone home. One is drinking, and I’m not fetching him to put that wagon on the river. Come in the morning.”

“Open the yard, at least,” the woman said. “We’ve paid to wait there.”

He studied her, then went to get the key.

Cedric sat motionless while the gate was opened. The woman took the boy down, and the carter led the team through. There was a low building against the far wall with light at one window. Beyond the gate the road continued along the bank, disappearing between trees.

He could leave the wagon and follow it. He had no horse. He did not know the next crossing. Somewhere behind him the kitchen woman would have gone to ask why the meal could not be brought through.

The carter looked back.

“Are you coming?”

Cedric put the account more securely inside the cloak. He climbed down and went into the yard.

Chapter 26 — The Cheap Exit

Lormey’s men came up the mill road in pairs, carrying their bundles above the mud. Elyas counted them as they passed the gate. He counted again when they had formed beside the bank, giving the late arrivals time to come out from behind the wagon.

Sixty-two.

Captain Aubert, Lormey’s thin officer with the square-cut beard, presented the letter as though it might supply the missing eighteen.

Lormey had kept them to guard his quarries. A neighbour had been seen riding near the boundary with men whose purpose remained uncertain. He regretted the necessity and trusted that the circumstances would be understood.

Elyas looked towards the captain.

“Did he name the neighbour?”

“No, my lord.”

“Did anyone enter his ground?”

“Not while I was there.”

Behind the captain, one of the men set his bundle down and began rubbing his shoulder. Another sat on the bank without waiting for permission. They had been walking since before daylight.

“Feed them,” Elyas told Wace. “Then show the captain where they are to stand.”

Wace glanced towards the higher field. Elyas nodded. They both knew what had been lost. Eighteen men could hold the second gate or strengthen the withdrawal party; they could not do both even if Lormey sent them now.

Berent had brought the writing board from the mill. Elyas dictated an answer asking for the men at once. He kept the language civil until he heard trusted that the circumstances would be understood coming back in his own voice. He stopped, struck out the last sentence, and signed.

“Take a horse that can come back,” he told the messenger. “You are not to wait overnight for an answer.”

When the man had gone, Wace returned from the bank.

“They have brought three days’ food.”

“He promised six.”

“The captain says the other half is coming.”

“Did he see it loaded?”

“He says he saw the sacks.”

Elyas looked at the men eating by the gate. One had taken off his boot. His companion was tearing a strip from the edge of a cloth to wrap the heel.

“Have our cook speak to theirs. They eat tonight.”

“And tomorrow?”

“I’ll speak to the miller.”

Sornay rode in before the messenger returned, with all twelve of his mounted men. His foot had arrived the previous day. They were in a better temper now that the dry loft had been divided as agreed, though one man continued to insist that Lormey’s people had taken his place beside the wall.

Sornay dismounted in the yard and handed his reins to Berent.

“I hear Lormey has discovered an enemy at home.”

“You hear quickly.”

“He has made sure of it.”

The baron looked at the little line of men beside the bank. He could count as well as Elyas.

“If he wants me to leave my own ground uncovered while he sits his men in a quarry, he can come and ask.”

“You gave the undertaking to me.”

“I remember to whom I gave it.”

His voice had sharpened enough for Berent to move away with the horse. Elyas waited until they were beyond hearing.

“I need your mounted men above the field. Wace will show you the route back. If they turn the bank, you will be covering men from both houses.”

Sornay looked towards the slope. It rose gradually enough for a man to walk up carrying a load. From the top, horse could come down on the foot behind the bank if no one opposed them. Nothing on either side prevented an enemy going farther round. The field only offered a shorter approach.

“And who covers us?”

“Wace’s mounted reserve.”

“How many?”

Elyas told him how many royal horse were away watching the roads and how many he meant to keep ready. Sornay listened, turning his gloves over in his hands.

“You’ll want the watchers back when you know where he is.”

“Yes.”

“Don’t leave them admiring him.”

The baron put on one glove, then the other. He asked to see the higher field.

They walked it together. At the far edge stood a low boundary wall with two gaps in it. Sornay disliked the lower gap because a horse passing through had to turn sharply around a fallen stone. He wanted the stone moved. The miller, who had followed them up, said the wall belonged to his cousin.

“Then tell your cousin I will pay for it to be put back,” Sornay said.

“He’ll want to hear that from the prince.”

The baron’s face changed. Elyas gave the promise before he could answer.

On the way down, Sornay stopped beside Lormey’s captain. He asked how many men were ill. The captain said two had sore feet and one a flux, but none needed to be sent away.

“My man has salve for the feet,” Sornay said. “Send them up.”

Then he walked past before gratitude could turn the offer into conversation.

Tylan’s messenger arrived on a horse which had lost a shoe. The rider had kept going at a walk until he found a smith, then lost more time waiting while the smith finished work for another party. He expected to be reprimanded. Elyas sent him to eat and asked Berent to see the horse rubbed down.

The dispatch brought two accounts of the fighting in the cantons. Seniro’s men had taken a road junction after a morning’s contest around the houses bordering it. The defenders withdrew in order, leaving wounded behind. Farther along the river approach, Tylan had made the men holding a causeway abandon it by getting a company across below their position. It had cost him boats and men. The crossing could not yet carry his wagons.

Elyas read the casualty names attached to the second account. He knew one of the officers slightly. The man had danced badly at the wedding and apologised to Mara for standing on her hem. Tylan had entered him as wounded and sent back, with no detail of the wound.

The canton troops had lost the ground which let them support one another easily. Valdais was drawing his separate bodies closer. His baggage had begun moving before the latest fighting. Scouts had seen wagons take the road towards the Scar, and a captured driver confirmed where he had been ordered to deliver his load.

Tylan asked Elyas to hold the preferred western passage as long as he reasonably could. He required an account of the force actually present, the ground chosen, and a route by which his own men might reach Elyas if Valdais delayed.

Wace read it after him.

“He won’t reach us before they do.”

“Perhaps not.”

“We should decide on that basis.”

Elyas agreed. He sent for the captains.

The miller objected to the wagons being put by the upper gate.

He had agreed to the arrangement when it concerned grain carts. Now Wace wanted the same firm ground for the baggage Elyas could not afford to lose, and the miller’s men were still trying to unload the morning’s arrivals.

“You said they’d be brought round after,” the miller told Elyas.

“After unloading.”

“And that one hasn’t unloaded.”

He pointed to a wagon with a wheel sunk nearly to the hub beyond the hard surface. It belonged to a woman who had hired it to bring two households’ grain. She stood beside it, holding the end of a rope which no one had yet taken from her.

“If you leave it there, the next has to go wide,” she said. “That’s how mine went in.”

Wace wanted the wagons moved behind the mill, beyond sight from the road. He could put men with them there. Getting them out again would require bringing them singly through a narrow opening while foot withdrew around them.

“If they’re loaded here, I can cover them,” he said. “If they’re out there, they can move. I haven’t enough men to do both.”

The woman looked from him to Elyas.

“Will there be fighting here?”

“Yes,” Elyas said. “There may.”

“Then I want my grain.”

The miller began explaining whose load was to be ground first. She did not wait for him to finish.

“I’ll take it whole.”

Elyas walked to the wagon. It had only a partial load now. The sacks already taken down were inside the mill; the woman named how many and looked sharply at the boy who had been carrying them.

“We’ll get you back on the road,” Elyas said. “You leave today.”

“And the hire? I owe another day if it stays until tomorrow.”

“It leaves today.”

He called over men from his own company. They took the remaining sacks down, used boards beneath the wheel, and pulled while the team strained forward. Elyas put his shoulder beside a man whose name he had forgotten and pushed until the wagon lurched onto firm ground. His boot came off in the mud. He went back for it while the others laughed.

The woman had already begun counting her sacks out of the mill. She thanked him without stopping. Her immediate concern was whether the boy had mixed the two households’ grain.

Wace came to stand beside him.

“That clears one.”

“The others leave too. Give them men to load. We keep one sound wagon at the raised strip for wounded. The baggage goes ahead by the northern road.”

“With an escort.”

“Four men. Enough to keep it moving, not to defend it against an army.”

“And if the road isn’t clear?”

“Then they leave the loads and bring the teams away.”

Wace repeated the instruction, looking at the wagon which held spare clothes, bedding, and the things men had sent for from home. Elyas knew what he was being asked to authorise.

“Yes,” he said. “Tell them before they go.”

The extra sheet in Tylan’s dispatch had almost been overlooked. Elyas found it folded inside the cover when he sat to write his reply. It concerned a question Veralyn had put to her marshal before the latest advance.

She wanted an estimate for a national army, keeping companies in the crown’s service through peace as well as war. The pay was to be better than most household captains could offer, enough to hold good men when the harvest or another lord called them away. She wanted instruction for officers in Valora, in an academy maintained for that purpose, rather than relying on what a young man might learn in whichever household received him.

Tylan had underlined one phrase in his own addition: Paid on the day promised.

Elyas smiled despite the mud drying inside his boot.

The queen had asked for the number of men a first establishment might support and what would bring suitable young officers to Valora. Tylan wanted Elyas’s observations when he could spare the time. This was work for years. In ten they might have a force whose habits no longer depended on a particular captain; in twenty, something a successor could inherit without assembling it again from promises.

Wace came in while Elyas was reading. He had given the baggage order. Two men had asked to put letters on the departing wagon, and he had told them they had until it was loaded.

Elyas passed him the sheet.

“Would you have gone?”

“To Valora?”

“When you were young.”

“For instruction, or for pay?”

“Both.”

Wace read it again.

“My father would have wanted to know who was receiving me. He wouldn’t have sent me somewhere to be taught by men whose names he didn’t know.”

“Even for better pay?”

“He would have asked why they had to pay so much.”

Elyas could hear Oswyn asking the same question. He wrote it down. Beneath it he added that a young officer needed to learn when an undertaking would cease to be kept and how to feed the men left waiting when it did.

“That would be a cheerful first lesson,” Wace said.

“Perhaps they begin with riding.”

“They usually do.”

The captain gave the sheet back. Outside, someone shouted for a hand with the wagon. He went to help.

Elyas finished the account Tylan needed now: the actual men, the open roads, the bank, and the high field. He described the northern route clearly enough that no one could mistake the position for a closed valley. Valdais could avoid him. Doing so would cost time and require moving the baggage again. If he chose the mill road, Elyas could make him fight for it.

He did not know how long his men could remain once that began. He wrote what would determine his withdrawal instead of guessing a number of hours.

Mara’s letter had come in the same packet.

She had spent two days at Gisla’s station helping a woman whose husband had returned with a wound he refused to let anyone touch. Mara had finally got him to agree by asking him to hold the basin while she washed the skin around it. He complained throughout and came back the next morning of his own accord.

There was a sentence about the pear tree. One branch looked dead. She had been told to wait before cutting it and disliked being given advice which consisted entirely of waiting.

Elyas read that part to Berent, who had arrived with hot water for his boot. The boy smiled and said the branch had looked dead when they left.

“Then we were both wrong to leave it.”

“Perhaps it isn’t dead.”

“Write and tell her that.”

Berent realised too late that he had been caught. Elyas let him off by asking for a clean sheet.

He wrote to Mara about Harald’s visit and the cloth, though she would probably have received both news and gift before his letter. He told her where he was in terms she would understand from the roads they had travelled. He stopped before describing the bank. She could do nothing with that except picture him behind it.

Instead he asked after the man with the basin and whether she had found anyone to mend the chair properly.

When he sealed the letter, Berent was sorting the spare straps. He had put the sound ones on a stool and the doubtful ones on the floor.

“You’ll remain by the mill unless I send for you,” Elyas said.

The boy’s hands stopped.

“I can ride a message.”

“I know. If I send you, you ride it to the named officer and come back by the way you were told. You don’t follow a man because he’s going towards something you want to see.”

Berent looked down at the strap in his hands.

“Yes, my lord.”

“If the wounded wagon goes, you go with it unless I have given you another task. Wace knows that.”

This time the answer took longer. Elyas waited for it.

Outside, Sornay’s men were moving the fallen stone. It scraped against the ground, stopped, then moved again to a shout of encouragement. They were still working when Elyas went out to walk the route by which he intended to bring them back.

Chapter 27 — The Paid Road

The boy woke before dawn and wanted to go home.

His mother told him they were going to a different house. He asked whether his bed was there. She said there would be a bed. He began to cry, softly at first, with his face pressed into her coat so the others would not hear.

Cedric lay against the wall with his eyes open.

The room had three sleeping places. The carter occupied one, boots on, an arm folded across his face. The woman and child had the second. Cedric had taken the place nearest the door. He had put the cloak beneath him when they came in, and now he could feel the folded account through it whenever he moved.

The woman got up and carried the boy outside. Through the thin boards Cedric heard her pointing out the lantern by the gate. She was trying to find something he could see which would make being awake in a strange place tolerable.

He sat up. His wrist was swollen. Four dark marks lay beneath the cuff.

The carter lowered his arm.

“You’ll want that looked at.”

“It will mend.”

“They mostly do.”

He rose and went to see the horses. Cedric waited until his steps had passed the window, then drew the cloak out from beneath him. The account was creased and stained. He read the groom’s name which he had entered at Ysabeau’s request. There was a little stroke of ink beyond it where the pen had rested before he set it down.

He folded the sheet again. He could not leave it for the woman who swept the room. He put it inside his own coat and rolled the cloak with the wet part inward.

When the mother came back she had obtained a cup of warm milk. The boy held it in both hands. A skin formed on top, and he pushed it away with one finger until she told him to drink around it.

“I’m Berthe,” she said to Cedric. “This is Colin.”

He gave his name.

She seemed to recognise it, though she made no comment. He wondered what she had been told about him. She sat with the child between her knees, combing his hair back with damp fingers.

The carter returned before she had finished.

“Soldiers have taken the first crossings.”

“How many?” Cedric asked.

“Enough.”

“Are they looking for someone?”

“They’re trying to get their own horses over. We wait.”

Berthe shut her eyes. The boy leaned back against her.

By midmorning the carter had lost patience. He was hired for a delivery across the water and a return journey that afternoon. The men at the ferry would take the passengers when a space opened, but they would not promise to take the wagon until the mounted party was across. Its last horses had not yet appeared.

“The pottery can stay,” he said. “The receiver can fetch it from the shed. I’m going back.”

Berthe pointed to the box. “He hasn’t accepted it.”

“I’ll leave word.”

“And when he says two pieces were broken before they came?”

“He can say what he likes.”

“You’ll have to answer it.”

They were standing beside the unloaded hampers. The boy had found a stick and was drawing in the mud near the wall. Cedric watched the gate. Men passed it at intervals, some leading horses, one carrying a saddle on his shoulder. None stopped to look at him.

The carter wanted his hire. The paid lodging was across the river. Cedric’s onward horse was waiting there too; he had been told to ask for it by name. The arrangement had assumed he would arrive the previous evening and leave early. Every extra hour made some other part less certain.

Berthe had no horse waiting. She was to travel with a carrier from the far landing, whose departure had been fixed for that morning. The ferry delay had already made her late for it.

“Someone can be sent after him,” Cedric said.

“Who?”

“The keeper.”

“Will he leave his house?”

Cedric opened his mouth and stopped.

Berthe looked tired enough to be angry at anything he offered. She lifted a hamper to the edge of the shed, failed to get it over the lip, and set it down again. He saw her rub the wrist she had strained helping with the rope on the road.

He went to the other handle.

“What’s in it?”

“Clothes. Things I was allowed to bring.”

They lifted together. The boy came to watch, trailing the stick.

“Who allowed you?” Cedric asked.

“My father.”

“Does he know where you’re going?”

“Yes.”

He waited. She adjusted the cover over the hamper.

“He thinks I should have stayed. I wrote the account they asked for. He says if I meant to sign it, I should be willing to stand beside it.”

“What account?”

“The dues collected at our landing. What the collector calls repair money, and which repairs were done. I kept the books for him. I know.”

“For your father?”

“For the collector.”

She looked towards Colin. The child had begun pushing the end of his stick into a hole in the shed wall.

“They said I could bring him.”

Cedric took the second hamper by its handle.

“We’ll get you to the keeper.”

“And then?”

He set it beside the first before answering.

“I’ll speak to him.”

They crossed without the wagon after noon. The ferryman took the pottery into his shed and signed for the unopened box in front of the carter, who remained dissatisfied but departed. Berthe’s hampers went with the passengers. Cedric carried one; a man who had come across to collect his brother took the other as far as the landing.

The keeper was serving food when they reached his house. He remembered Cedric from the journey south and asked whether he wanted eggs again.

“Later.”

“You said that last time.”

He had a round face and a little burn on one hand. His wife was bringing bowls from the kitchen. She looked at Colin, put down what she carried, and found him a place beside the hearth.

The horse was ready. The onward carrier had gone. The keeper had been paid for a night’s lodging for Berthe and the boy, not for an indefinite stay. He had room that evening but expected a party the next day. He could send them to another house if they could pay.

“She’ll stay here tonight,” Cedric said.

“As I said.”

“And tomorrow she’ll take the horse.”

The keeper looked at him.

“Your horse?”

“The one paid for. Can she take the boy with her?”

“If she can ride.”

Berthe, who had been listening from the door, said she could.

Cedric asked where the horse was to be delivered at the end of its stage. The keeper gave him the place. Berthe knew the road as far as the first village, then no farther. The man who would collect the horse could meet her there if he were sent for now.

“Who can take word?” Cedric asked.

The keeper called his wife. They argued briefly about whether her younger brother could be spared. She said he had already been spared for an errand which had apparently required most of the morning and two cups of ale. She went to find him.

“And you?” the keeper asked Cedric.

“I’ll wait for another carrier.”

“You can walk to the next house. It’s no great distance.”

Cedric looked at the road through the open door. A man could cover it before dark. He would be alone, carrying a cloak taken from the room where Ysabeau lay, and would have to explain himself to anyone who chose to ask.

“When is the next carrier?”

“Tomorrow. If he comes.”

“Then I’ll wait until tomorrow.”

The keeper studied him, then gave him a bowl.

“Eat while it’s hot.”

News reached the house that evening with a horse trader who had been delayed on the other bank.

Dame Ysabeau de Corvaunt had been killed in her quarters. No one agreed who had done it. The trader said a servant had been taken up for questioning and that men were being stopped on the roads. He had been searched twice. He complained about that until the keeper’s wife asked whether anyone knew if Aude had been told.

The trader did not know who Aude was.

“She stays there sometimes,” the woman said. “Her sister lives near my cousin.”

The trader returned to the searches. Cedric kept his hands beneath the table.

Berthe was sitting by the hearth with Colin asleep against her. She looked over once, when Ysabeau’s name was repeated. Cedric could not tell whether she had seen something in his face. He wanted to leave the room and knew that leaving would make her watch him.

“Her captains won’t take kindly to it,” the keeper said.

“No. Rovel has men out already.”

Cedric pictured the old captain trying to get his boot over the bad foot in a hurry. Someone would have to saddle for him. He would be angry at the delay and at anyone who offered to go in his place.

Berthe drew Colin more closely against her. The keeper’s wife asked if she wanted to put him to bed. She said she would wait a little longer.

Nobody spoke to Cedric about the horse again.

He saw Berthe leave in the morning. The keeper’s brother had returned with an answer from the man who would meet her. She had repeated the name twice and made the keeper describe him. Her hampers would follow with the carrier, who agreed to take Cedric too.

Colin sat before his mother, holding a little piece of bread. She thanked Cedric. He said the keeper had arranged it, which was true and spared him having to answer anything more.

The horse went out onto the road. Cedric watched until it turned from view, then helped load the hampers.

The next stages took days. He travelled with men who spoke about Ysabeau as they spoke about rain ahead, wanting to know how it would affect the road they meant to use. Some thought her death would end the war. One said Valdais would hang the next lord who refused him horses, and that would settle the matter. Another had heard that she was still alive, though wounded. Cedric listened to all of them.

He left the cloak with the keeper, asking him to burn it. The man looked at the stain before folding it again. He asked no question. Cedric kept the account in his coat. At a later lodging he sat by the fire after everyone else had gone to bed and fed the sheets into it one at a time. The page with the groom’s name curled away from the flame before catching.

By the time he reached Valora his clothes had dried into creases he could not brush out. He hired a boy to show him the office because he could not remember which turning led to it. The boy took him to the wrong door first, then argued that both buildings belonged to the queen and he should therefore be paid.

Cedric paid him and found the entrance himself.

Fayd was dictating when he arrived. Cedric waited in an outer room with a man who wanted a protection renewed and another who had brought information he would not discuss in front of the first. A clerk gave Cedric water and asked his name. When he supplied it, the clerk went through the inner door.

Fayd came out before the letter was finished.

“Come in.”

The room was warmer than Cedric remembered. A different plan lay on the table. Fayd dismissed the clerk, waited for the door to shut, and asked when he had left.

Cedric told him.

“And the delay?”

He described the ferry, the missed carrier, and Berthe’s horse. Fayd asked whether she had taken her account with her.

“I don’t know.”

“Did you see papers?”

“No.”

Fayd wrote a line on a separate slip and put it aside.

“You reached the house when expected?”

“Yes.”

“She was alone?”

Cedric looked at the edge of the table. Someone had scorched it with a lamp.

“In the room.”

“Who saw you leave?”

He answered. Fayd asked a few more questions, all about people still alive. Cedric found that easier until he realised he had got through the whole account without being asked what Ysabeau had said to him.

At last Fayd folded the paper he had been using.

“Her captains have kept the companies together. Rovel has taken the immediate command. Verren has requested a further meeting.”

“Will you tell him?”

“He has been informed of her death.”

“How?”

“Our notice says she was killed in her quarters and that the command has passed to men still serving Valdais. Both things are true.”

“Does it say who killed her?”

“No.”

Cedric sat with his sore wrist resting on his knee. Somewhere outside the room the petitioner was arguing about the date on his protection.

Fayd opened a drawer and took out another paper.

“This is the household you will accompany me to meet. Read it today. Mark anything that contradicts what you saw or heard while you were with her.”

Cedric took it. Verren’s name stood at the head.

“He asked who would protect him.”

“He still asks it.”

“What will you say?”

“You will hear.”

The smaller room assigned to Cedric had a desk, two stools, and no fire. A clerk brought a brazier after he complained. The same clerk returned with the terms of his pay and the names of the two men he would be directing.

One was already out. The other appeared before the brazier had warmed the room. He was older than Cedric and had a damaged ear. He wanted to know which papers he should carry to the river and who would receive them. Cedric read the instruction twice, then sent him to ask the clerk who had prepared it to come back.

The clerk had named the destination without the person who would be there. He seemed irritated when Cedric asked for the name. They found it on another sheet and entered it. The man with the damaged ear waited without comment.

When he had gone, Fayd came to the doorway.

“You’ll need to understand where Verren’s money comes from.”

“The ferry. His market. He has stores along the road.”

“Which does he own, and which does he borrow against?”

Cedric looked at the papers. The distinction was not in them.

“Find out,” Fayd said. “We have men who can ask the lenders. You can tell them which properties matter to his command.”

“To judge whether he’ll keep the agreement?”

“That too.”

Fayd came in and rested a hand on the empty stool.

“His tenants pay to pass through his market. His neighbours borrow to equip their sons. He lends, takes their service in return, and has men when he needs them. Other houses do the same. An earl with good rents and his own lenders can disappoint the crown for years.”

“And you want the rents?”

“I want him unable to maintain that independence.”

Cedric looked up.

“If the queen protects another market, his loses trade. If money can be borrowed elsewhere on better terms, his neighbours need him less. There are ways to bring a house to the point where it must seek the crown’s assistance. First we need to know which would work.”

“He’ll see what you’re doing.”

“Of course. He will try to stop it.”

Fayd moved the stool out of the draught from the door.

“This may take ten years. Twenty, for some houses. The queen is considering soldiers paid well enough to remain in her service and officers taught together in Valora. They will need support which doesn’t disappear when a lord takes offence. We should know where that support can be found.”

“Has she ordered this?”

“She has asked for estimates for the army. I am asking you about Verren’s lenders.”

Fayd waited while Cedric turned to a clean sheet. Then he set a second letter beside the inquiry.

“This goes to a correspondent of the western Vigil. He has offered men. Ask which men he can actually answer for.”

“The queen’s office will employ them?”

“If they accept its instructions. He has so far offered to assist while retaining the right to decide which work suits his own friends.”

“Then why use him?”

“Because he can do things we need, and because leaving him outside does not make him idle.”

The other letter bore an Ecktorian address. Cedric recognised the city but not the name.

“Another of yours?”

“One who may become so. The Unburnt serve the old patrician houses in Ecktoria. This circle has begun to doubt that keeping the Amber Palace undisturbed will restore an empire.”

“And they will serve a Viterran queen instead?”

“They have agreed to send a man to hear why they should.”

Fayd took back that letter before Cedric could reach for it.

“The northern correspondents and the western houses here have been acting separately. Some have encouraged rebellion; some have preserved a lord’s private power and called that fidelity to Utara. They cannot keep doing both in her service. I mean to have one answer from them.”

“And from Ecktoria?”

“From the men who come. The Unburnt are not all of one mind, and they are not the whole Ecktorian Vigil.”

Cedric wrote the western inquiry. For the first time he had a name to put to the people beyond the river whose letters Fayd kept apart.

After he had gone, Cedric wrote the names of the storehouses he knew. At one of them Ysabeau had stood beside him while Verren explained why the promised horses were late. He could remember where she had tied Bastian and the way she had checked that the knot would not tighten if the horse pulled.

He entered the storehouse on the list and left a space for the owner’s name.

Chapter 28 — The Escort Refused

The first account had Ysabeau killed on the road. The second placed her in her own house, surrounded by servants who had heard nothing until she was dead. Ouen read them in that order and believed the second less readily, though Halden said it had come by the shorter route.

“She would have had men with her.”

“She had men in the house.”

“Then where were they?”

Halden could not tell him. Neither writer had been there. One supplied the names of three people who might have done it, with a different reason for each; the other said only that her captain Rovel had taken command and the roads were being watched.

The wagons stood loaded in the lower yard. Ouen could see their covers from the window. They held food for the receiving station his father had named, with clean cloth packed above it for the men returning on the empty wagons. Four teams had already gone. The two hired teams, delayed by a lame horse at the posting house, were ready at last.

He was still holding the second letter when the representative from the house providing their escort arrived.

The man introduced himself as Alain, spoke of the weather, and asked after the duke’s health. His gloves were damp. He kept rubbing one thumb along the seam of the other hand.

“The horses cannot be furnished,” he said.

Ouen waited for the explanation. Alain looked at Halden.

“His lordship requires them at home.”

“They were required here.”

“Yes, my lord.”

“By an undertaking he made three days ago.”

“The circumstances have altered.”

“Whose circumstances?”

Alain drew a folded paper from his coat. There was no letter from his lord in it. It was a copy of a protection offered to the house by the queen’s office, with spaces left for the undertakings which would bring it into effect.

Ouen read the first lines, then held it away from him.

“He sends me this?”

“No, my lord. I brought it so you would understand.”

“Has he signed?”

“He has requested clarification.”

“Of which part?”

Alain’s mouth moved. Ouen thought he was going to answer, but he only moistened his lips.

“Has he signed?” Ouen asked again.

“Not when I left.”

Halden moved the chair nearer the fire. Alain did not sit until Ouen told him to. He held his wet gloves above his knees as though they might stain the cloth.

“You have heard about Dame Ysabeau,” he said.

“We have heard several things.”

“They say the doors were open.”

Ouen looked at him. Alain had not come to discuss whether a convoy could be guarded. He had come to explain why his lord no longer wished his horse recognised on the road beside Valdais’s stores.

“Your house is well away from her quarters.”

“Yes.”

“And my father’s men are nearer.”

Alain lowered his head. Ouen heard the threat only after he had spoken it. He had wanted the man to feel the absurdity of refusing six mounted escorts while an army depended on the road. Instead Alain looked as though he had been asked to choose which of two doors he wished to be dragged through.

“I cannot give you men I haven’t brought,” he said.

Ouen went down to the wagons because remaining in the room would have obliged him to say something further.

Ernet, who had waited through his wife’s illness for Ouen to release his team, was tightening a fastening on the nearer cover. He looked up and waited. The replacement horse had been harnessed beside an animal it did not like; every few moments it turned its head and bared its teeth, provoking a weary correction from the boy holding the team.

“How long can you wait?” Ouen asked.

“Here?”

“Where else?”

Ernet tucked the loose end of the fastening beneath the knot.

“Until you send us. I pay the other driver whether he drives or stands.”

“Your wife?”

“Better.”

Ouen was glad enough to ask another question. Ernet answered it, then looked at the loaded wagon.

“If we miss the receiving men, my lord, we’ll have to find someone else to take it. They said they were moving on.”

That had been in his father’s letter. Ouen had read it as a reason to have the wagons ready. They were ready; the difficulty had moved to something which had seemed settled when he read it.

Halden followed him with a fresh note.

“From His Grace. The rider came through the north gate.”

Valdais had written the whole of it himself. The letters were larger than his secretary’s, with the last line forced upward to avoid the fold.

Send food before bedding. The men can sleep on the cloth they have; they cannot eat it. Have the returning teams stopped at the receiving house and the wounded brought under cover before they come farther. I will not have them shaken halfway across Adria to satisfy a driver who wants his horse home.

Below it he had added: Do not hold sound teams waiting for a perfect escort. Ask where the road is clear today.

Ouen read the note to Halden.

“Where is it clear?”

“Our last man came through yesterday.”

“Send him here.”

The rider described an empty road as far as the first receiving house. Beyond it he had passed men from a company he could not name. They had asked whose horse he rode and let him pass when he answered. He could not say whether they were still there.

“Then we send to the first house,” Ouen said.

“The order names the farther station.”

“I know what it names.”

Ernet looked away. Ouen made himself read the note again. His father had asked for food first and had also told him to find out where the road was clear. Both instructions were now in his hands, and the rider could not make them fit better by repeating himself.

“The first house receives the food,” Ouen said. “A man goes ahead from there to find the officer. If he will come for it, he can. We don’t send the wagons beyond the road we have news of.”

“And the cloth?” Halden asked.

“Keep half here. The returning wounded will need it.”

The teams would have to be unloaded again. Ernet untied the knot he had just finished and called for help.

Ouen provided four men from his own household. It left the lower store guarded by two old servants and a boy who knew how to shout. Halden proposed moving the most easily carried goods into the house; Ouen objected to filling the passage, then saw how Halden had already arranged the larger room to receive them.

Dame Barenne’s four bales remained apart. He had been tempted, looking at the cloth for the wounded, to ask whether hers could be used instead. He did not ask. The marked bales had become an inconvenience whose shape he knew too well to pretend it belonged to someone else.

Alain came into the yard before the wagons left. His horse was saddled for the return journey.

“What am I to tell his lordship?”

“That I have sent four of my own men.”

“Yes, my lord.”

“And that I expect his answer in writing. If he is withdrawing the undertaking, he can put his name to the withdrawal.”

Alain looked miserable. Ouen added that the man himself could have food before leaving. It did little to improve the expression.

When the wagons were gone, Ouen found Iselde in the room being prepared for wounded men. She was shifting a bench which had been placed across the shortest way to the door. He took the other end without asking.

“How many?” she said.

“I don’t know yet.”

“We must leave space for them to be carried.”

They set the bench against the wall. The room smelled of old rushes and the vinegar being used to clean the floor. His hunting gloves lay on a stool where he had left them that morning. Iselde handed them to him.

“I was going out,” he said.

“I know.”

He put them in his pocket.

The answer from his father arrived after the first wounded men.

Valdais accepted the shortened delivery because the receiving officer had found it in time. He wanted the missing cloth sent with the next guarded party. He asked for the name of the house which had refused its escort.

Ouen had supplied it in his first account. His father had either overlooked it or wanted the name written again without the surrounding explanation. Ouen wrote it again.

Then he opened Vaelrie’s letter.

She had been angry with him for failing to say whether Hew liked the new drawing. By the bottom of the second page she had forgiven him enough to describe Guiran’s first evening in Valora. The performance had gone well until Maelis’s cricket began answering an instrument from inside its box. Guiran had taken the interruption personally. Vaelrie had laughed at the wrong moment and spent the rest of the evening pretending she had been coughing.

He wanted to write an answer in which the greatest difficulty was a musician quarrelling with an insect. He began twice. Downstairs someone cried out while a dressing was changed, then apologised so loudly that he heard that too.

The drawing pleased him, he wrote. I have been late because Father’s men are coming here wounded. Iselde has made room. I am sorry I let you wait.

He added that Ysabeau was dead, though she would surely know by now, and stopped before giving an account he could not trust.

The servant collecting letters came while he was still holding the pen. Ouen folded the single page and gave it to him.

Chapter 29 — The Answer Collected

Verren had brought his own chair.

It stood beside the table in the hired house, taller than the others and fitted with a cushion which his servant straightened before he sat. Cedric remembered the chair in the storehouse, plain wood with a split beneath one arm. He had never considered whether the lord had chosen it to make a visitor feel at ease.

Fayd accepted the lower seat without comment. They had ridden for three days to reach the meeting, and his boots were stained as high as the knee. Verren’s were clean. The lord had arrived from a house nearby and would be sleeping there whatever the meeting decided.

He looked at Cedric for a long moment.

“I thought you were with Dame Ysabeau.”

“I was.”

“When did you leave?”

“Recently.”

Verren waited. Fayd laid the offered terms on the table. Cedric felt the lord’s attention shift away from his face and could breathe properly again.

“The same commercial passage,” Fayd said. “The same right to bring the disputed dues to a hearing. The period for acceptance has changed.”

“So I see.”

Verren did not pick up the paper. He asked after the queen’s advance, and Fayd gave him the news already circulating on the roads. Two canton positions had been lost. Valdais had drawn back part of his baggage. The forces still with him remained substantial.

“And who commands the mounted companies?” Verren asked.

“Captain Rovel has taken the immediate charge.”

“You are certain?”

“Yes.”

“My last letter named three men who might.”

“Rovel is giving the orders.”

Verren turned to Cedric.

“He has a bad foot.”

“He can command from a chair.”

The answer came more sharply than Cedric intended. Verren’s eyes narrowed. Fayd watched the servant pour the wine.

When they were alone, the lord unfolded the terms.

“You require an undertaking against military passage.”

“Further military passage in aid of Valdais, through the holdings you control.”

“I cannot prevent an army walking where it pleases.”

“You can refuse fresh horse, guides, and advance storage. You can stop commanding your people to assist.”

“And when an officer takes the horse anyway?”

“You report the taking. You do not call it a gift afterwards.”

Verren rubbed the side of his nose. Outside, a servant was leading the riding horses towards a shed. Cedric could hear one shod hoof strike the paving unevenly at each step.

“You ask me to lose what I cannot keep and then accuse me of giving it away.”

“The clause concerns your willing assistance. We can make that more exact.”

Fayd turned the page towards Cedric, indicating the place for the correction. Cedric took out his pen.

Verren held the paper down before he could write.

“I have men with her companies.”

“With Rovel now,” Fayd said.

“They went at my order. You require me to withdraw my assistance. Do I send them a letter telling them to leave?”

“That would announce your choice.”

“And if I don’t send it, you can say I have broken the undertaking before your ink is dry.”

Fayd looked at the military clause again. Cedric knew the men involved: the eleven already serving when Ysabeau came, the six he had brought away, and the remainder whose arrival he could no longer confirm. One had sat in his room while he wrote the statement which protected his pay.

“How many are there now?” Fayd asked.

“Seventeen I can name. Others may have reached them.”

“Give us the names and the dates sent. Existing service can be distinguished from fresh assistance.”

“Can it?” Verren asked. “Or will you require it ended on a day you choose?”

Cedric stopped writing. The first version had done exactly that. The date of acceptance applied to everything beneath it.

Fayd drew a line beside the clause.

“You issue no fresh order in aid of the duke. The men already in service are recalled through an acknowledged request to their commander. We record when it is delivered.”

“Rovel may refuse.”

“Then the refusal is recorded.”

“And their families?”

Fayd waited.

“Two live in quarters belonging to the command. One has a wife who cannot ride. Does she become the price of his lord changing his mind?”

Cedric could see the woman he meant. She had brought a basket to the stable one morning and waited outside because the man she wanted was still on duty. He had taken food from it while speaking to Cedric. Cedric had forgotten her until now.

“We can include their passage,” Fayd said.

“From a house you don’t command.”

“Once released. I cannot promise that Rovel will release them because we write it here.”

“Then you cannot promise what I need.”

Verren withdrew his hand from the paper. He sat back, leaving the pen between them.

Fayd offered protection for the dependants who could reach an agreed place, without requiring the soldiers to have arrived first. Verren rejected the first place: it lay across a road used by Valdais’s horse. Cedric named another receiving house, then had to explain that he had seen its covered yard and that a cart could reach it from the quarters without taking that road.

“Who owns it?” Fayd asked.

Cedric told him. The owner was no party to the agreement.

“Then we must ask before naming it.”

Verren rang for his servant. A steward came, listened, and said the owner was a cousin who would probably oblige him. Fayd required an answer before calling the arrangement settled. The steward was sent with a short request and enough time allowed for a refusal.

While they waited, Verren asked what would happen if one of the men chose to remain with Rovel.

“His choice would have to be distinguished from your instruction,” Fayd said.

“By whose witness?”

“The officer receiving your recall and the man himself, if he can be heard.”

Verren looked at Cedric.

“You served there. Would you come home because someone showed you this paper?”

Cedric felt Fayd’s attention without looking towards him.

“I would want to know who had signed it.”

“And if I had?”

“They know your hand.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

“No,” Cedric said. “It wasn’t.”

The lord let the answer remain. He took back the pen and required the distinction between old service and new assistance to be written in the clause itself, where a later reader would have to encounter it before making an accusation.

They spent an hour on the mills.

Verren claimed the right to charge carts passing between two holdings because the road had been maintained by his father. The queen’s proposed protection would exempt certain carriers whose loads supplied her forces. Verren wanted payment for every passage. Fayd offered a fixed contribution towards repairs, provided the work was inspected.

“By whom?”

“Someone agreed between your officer and the crown’s.”

“Your man deciding whether my man has done his work.”

“Two men, my lord. That is why I said agreed.”

Verren rose and went to the window. He could have seen very little through it except the horses. He remained there until Cedric’s pen stopped moving.

“My father built the nearer culvert himself. He stood in the water with the masons.”

Fayd waited.

“He was a younger son. We did not always have money to hire people to stand in water for us.”

“Then his work deserves maintenance.”

“It deserves respect.”

Cedric had heard the same voice in his own father’s room, though Lorran would not have spoken about money in front of a visitor. He looked at Verren’s clean boots and the chair brought from home. The nearer culvert was still there. Cedric had crossed it with Ysabeau.

“The upstream stones are moving,” he said.

Verren turned.

“The last flood took soil from the end. Your steward put hurdles there. They will catch branches if the water rises.”

“I know what my steward did.”

“Would you have the inspection before the repairs?” Fayd asked. “It would establish what is already damaged.”

The lord came back to the table. They wrote that the first inspection would occur before military carriers used the exemption. Fayd accepted a higher repair contribution and refused the claim on each wagon. Verren agreed at last, but had the amount written in both copies before he moved on.

Cedric blotted the page. The lord watched his hands.

“You learned something while you were there.”

“Yes,” Cedric said.

The steward returned during the meal. His cousin would receive the dependants for three nights, provided their food came with them or was paid for. He would not promise to house an entire company. Verren undertook the food from his own stores. Fayd accepted the cost of onward protected travel once the families reached the house. Cedric entered both promises and the limit of the stay.

When the servant had cleared the meal, Verren dismissed him a second time and closed the door himself.

“They found her beneath the table.”

Cedric kept looking at the papers.

“Is that in your account too?” the lord asked Fayd.

“I have heard several particulars.”

“I had word before the household sent to me. A man came to tell me she was dead and that your terms remained open. He knew which room.”

“He was told to ensure you received the offer.”

Verren drew his chair nearer. Its legs made a heavy noise against the floor.

“I asked who would put horse at my house if she came. You have answered a different question.”

“The danger has changed. The protection has not been withdrawn.”

“Do you think I cannot hear you?”

Fayd folded his hands on the table.

“I think you have heard me from the beginning.”

Cedric could feel the room growing warm. A fire had been kept alight because Verren disliked draughts, and the afternoon sun now lay across the table. He wanted someone to open the window. He did not move towards it.

The lord looked down at the acceptance date.

“And if I wait?”

“After that day I cannot promise these terms. The queen will be farther on. Her officers will make arrangements according to what they find.”

“Will you send another messenger to explain them?”

“If you ask for one.”

Verren held Fayd’s gaze. Then he asked for the pen.

He read every clause before signing. At the one concerning protection of his household he stopped and demanded that the dependants living outside the principal house be named by their holdings, rather than left to the phrase those under his roof. Fayd agreed. Cedric copied the correction with a hand that had begun to ache.

The lord inspected both versions again. He signed only when satisfied they matched.

On the ride back Cedric asked whether every house had answered.

“One has refused,” Fayd said. “Dervain has asked to meet us on the return road. His steward wants terms I am not authorised to offer.”

“What will you do?”

“Hear him. He may accept what I can offer.”

“And if he doesn’t?”

“Continue with the houses that will deal with us.”

They rode for a while without speaking. A cart of cut wood came towards them, and they moved onto the verge to let it pass. Cedric’s horse tossed its head at a loose end trailing beside the wheel.

“Verren is frightened,” he said.

“Yes.”

“He will be looking for a way out.”

“Then we should perform what we promised him.”

Fayd looked ahead to the next rise. Cedric rode beside him, carrying the signed terms in the case he had been given at the office.

Dervain’s steward met them at their next lodging with three instruments and no request for wine.

His lord had advanced money and materials to merchants before the roads closed. Some goods had been taken by armies; some remained in warehouses whose owners could no longer pay. Dervain wanted the crown to guarantee the advances in return for his agreement on passage.

“Some supplied the duke,” Fayd said.

“Some supplied your queen before she was queen. Their custom did not tell us who would win a war.”

Cedric examined the list. Fairport appeared beside two entries. One concerned timber and fittings used in rebuilding a quay store. The advance had been made to the owner of the store, secured on the materials until payment. Dervain’s men had furnished the roof they now proposed to claim.

“The crown will hear proved claims,” Fayd said. “It will not make every debtor solvent.”

“Then there is very little in this for my lord.”

“Protected passage for his carriers. An agreed hearing before goods are taken to satisfy a disputed claim.”

“The goods are his security.”

“And the taking may be disputed.”

The steward opened the instrument concerning the roof. The owner had signed. A builder had witnessed the quantity delivered. Cedric could see no convenient mistake in it.

“What would you have him do?” the steward asked. “Leave his property where another man can earn from it, and wait for the earnings to be spent on claims brought by everyone else?”

Fayd offered to arrange a short suspension while the merchants proposed payment. The steward asked whether it would bind the other creditors too. Fayd said each would have to be heard.

“Then ours waits while theirs is paid.”

“No one has proposed that.”

“No one has prevented it.”

The room grew quiet. Cedric looked again at the store. It had been described as covered and ready for a limited number of loads. Without the roof, that description would cease to mean anything.

“Give us a fortnight,” Fayd said.

“What do I take home for the fortnight?”

“An undertaking that the claim will be placed for hearing.”

“I can obtain that without signing away the rights on the road.”

Fayd closed his copy of the terms. The steward gathered the instruments without hurrying. At the door he said Dervain would act through the local officers and expected the queen to respect an ordinary claim even if his house had refused her new agreement.

“She will hear it,” Fayd said.

After the man left, Cedric asked whether they should warn the warehouse owners.

“Yes. Send the particulars ahead. They must know what may be taken and when.”

“And Dervain?”

“He has made his choice.”

Fayd put the refused terms with the other papers. Cedric wrote the warning before they resumed the journey.

Pellam refused to register the travel charge beneath the description Fayd had supplied.
