The charge concerned several journeys, grouped as expenses of the new river agreements. One payment had been made before Verren requested the meeting. Another named the ferry through which Cedric had passed. The clerk who received the papers put a mark beside it, consulted someone in the adjoining room, and told Cedric to wait.

Lyra came out with the earlier authority in her hand.

“This payment belongs to the office’s existing information work.”

“It concerns the river houses.”

“So does the later negotiation. They have different authorities.”

Cedric recognised her, though they had scarcely spoken. She had been with Elyas’s party. He remembered Berent standing near her with a face which offered nothing to the household he had left.

“Master Fayd sent it under this description.”

“Then Master Fayd must correct it.”

“He is waiting for the instruments.”

“The instruments can be registered. This charge cannot be transferred to their account.”

She showed him the date of the original payment. He knew it. The carter and the lodging had been arranged before the further instruction, and now the date lay beneath her finger where anyone could read it.

Pellam came to the doorway. He asked Lyra which authority had been used, listened, and told Cedric exactly what was required. The original expense would remain against the office which had ordered it. No future negotiation would be allowed to conceal or replenish expenditure already made. Fayd would supply a separate return. Until it was accepted, the disputed sum could not be drawn again from the negotiation allowance.

Cedric took the correction back. Fayd read it, crossed out the grouped description, and signed a fresh division of the charges.

“Bring me the instruments when they are sealed,” he said.

Lyra was arranging another packet when Cedric returned. He could see a place-name from the west and, on the sheet beside it, a letter addressed to Amru. She covered that sheet before receiving Fayd’s correction.

“Is the western hearing finished?” he asked.

She looked at him.

“No.”

“I heard there was to be one.”

“There is a day fixed for taking my evidence. The written account goes ahead. The arrangements for my journey must be confirmed before I leave.”

“Will Master Pellam spare you?”

“He has agreed the absence.”

Cedric heard the limit in her voice. He had no right to the rest. A clerk approached with a question about who was to keep the original testimony. Lyra gave him the name of Amru’s receiving officer from the letter, then checked that the covering instruction required acknowledgement on delivery. She wrote her own name beneath the witness arrangements.

Pellam signed the office correction when he returned. Lyra kept the earlier authority with it. Cedric watched her fasten the two papers together.

“Do you need that one still?”

“Yes.”

He could think of no harmless reason to ask why. She handed him the registered river instruments and retained the payment papers.

Outside, the man with the damaged ear waited with their horses. Cedric gave him the packet for the first house and said who would receive it. The man repeated the name, tucked the packet away, and rode off.

Cedric took the rest to Fayd. On the first page the queen’s undertakings were written cleanly, with enough space beneath them for each lord’s name.

Part V — The house that remains

Chapter 30 — What the Passage Costs

The first riders stopped beyond bowshot and looked at the bank.

Elyas could see them through the bare branches beside the mill. Four men, with another farther back holding a spare horse. One rode a little way up the slope, turned, and went back to the others. They spoke for several minutes before two continued along the northern road.

“They’ve seen the higher field,” Sornay said.

“They would have to be blind.”

The baron watched the men disappear behind the rise.

“Lormey would have sent eighteen men to tell us that.”

The missing spears had not come. Lormey’s answer had enlarged the danger to his quarries and wished Elyas success. Captain Aubert, who commanded the sixty-two actually sent, had listened to the letter in silence, then asked whether his men were to remain by the lower gate.

They remained there now. Elyas could see the tops of their spears where they stood behind the bank. Some had pulled earth up against the lowest places. Others were still trimming the branches which caught their shafts when they brought them forward. Wace had told them to stop improving the ground and rest; each man seemed to know one last thing which ought to be done before resting was possible.

The scouts came back before noon. A larger party followed them, bringing men on foot. Elyas sent his own watchers in by the higher route and put their horses behind the mill. Two riders remained out to carry word if Valdais chose the longer road.

A herald approached under a small white cloth.

Sornay wanted to receive him. Elyas went himself.

The man gave Valdais’s titles accurately and required the road opened. Men obstructing the duke’s lawful passage would be held answerable for the blood spilled in clearing it.

“The road is held under my command,” Elyas said. “You may tell His Grace who answers for it.”

“He knows who commands.”

“Then he has his answer.”

The herald looked past him at the bank. Elyas could see him estimating how few men stood there.

“Is there any other message?”

“No.”

The man rode back. Elyas waited until he was clear, then returned to the mill.

They tried the lower gate first.

Men with shields came up the road at a walk, keeping close to the ditch. A few bowmen behind them shot into the bank and the bushes above it. Most arrows struck earth or wood. One found a man who had stood too high to see what was coming. He sat down suddenly and kept looking at the shaft in his shoulder until someone pulled him below the bank.

Aubert smoothed his square-cut beard with two fingers and held his men quiet. When the shields reached the gate, the front ranks came up together. The first pressure shook the timber braced behind it. Someone shouted that it was giving. Aubert struck the speaker across the back with the flat of his hand and ordered him to push.

Elyas stood far enough back to see both the gate and the bend. He wanted to go forward. Every part of his body proposed a reason: he could make them hold, he could see what they faced, he could spare the man taking the first blows. Wace stayed beside him without speaking. Above them, Sornay’s horse waited in the higher field.

The men at the gate struggled in a knot of shields and spear shafts. One attacker tried to climb the bank beside it and was pulled down by his own companion before a spear found his throat. The gate held. After a time the attackers withdrew, dragging two men between them.

The cheering began at once.

“Quiet,” Aubert shouted. “They’ve only gone to fetch the others.”

Elyas sent water forward. The man with the arrow in his shoulder was brought to the mill. He had become angry with anyone who touched him and apologised each time he cried out.

Berent held the basin while a woman from the mill cut the cloth away. Elyas had met her the evening before, when she insisted that her husband would not leave the place and therefore neither would she. She looked up at him now.

“I can keep it clean. I can’t take that out.”

“Someone is coming.”

“Then send him here when he does.”

Elyas went back outside. Wace was looking towards the high field.

The next movement took hours.

Men appeared along the wall beyond Sornay’s position, carrying tools. Others went farther round, beyond sight. Elyas could see enough to understand the intention and too little to know when it would be ready. If he took men from the bank to meet it, the road lay open. If he waited, the force coming round might reach the route by which he meant to withdraw.

He sent a rider to the far end of the field. The man returned with news of mounted troops gathering behind the low rise. They were having the wall opened for them.

Sornay came down to ask for the reserve, his long nose turned past Elyas towards the waiting horses even while he spoke.

“Not yet,” Elyas said.

“They’ll come through with room to form. I can reach them before that.”

“And be held there when the road comes on.”

The baron looked at Wace, seeking agreement. Wace kept his eyes on the men moving beyond the wall.

“I gave you the horse to use,” Sornay said.

“I am using it.”

“Standing here.”

“For now.”

Elyas expected anger. Instead Sornay looked towards the bank and slowly put his glove back on.

“Send early enough that I can still turn,” he said, and rode up again.

A messenger from the road watchers came with news of wagons halted farther back. Valdais was holding his baggage short of the fighting. Men had been sent to examine the northern road, but the wagons had not turned towards it. His commanders were preparing to clear the mill road before bringing them forward.

Elyas asked about Tylan. The messenger had no news.

He ate a piece of bread while Wace spoke to the captains. It was hard to swallow. Berent brought water and waited until Elyas had drunk before taking the cup back.

“Have you eaten?”

“Yes.”

“What?”

“Bread.”

“How much?”

The boy looked annoyed. Elyas sent him to eat more.

The main attack came in the afternoon, with enough men behind it that Elyas could no longer see the road between their bodies.

They advanced past the places where the earlier wounded had lain. Bowmen spread out to either side, shooting into the higher ground while men pressed towards both gates. At the same time horse began coming through the opened wall.

Sornay moved before Elyas could send. His small company crossed the field at an angle, forcing the first enemy riders to turn while those behind them were still coming through the gap. The collision vanished behind a scatter of horses and raised weapons. A loose animal ran downhill with one stirrup striking its side.

“Now,” Elyas told Wace.

The reserve went up the route they had walked. Elyas watched them until they passed the mill’s corner, then turned back to the bank.

Aubert’s men were giving ground at the lower gate. The timber had broken near one end, leaving a space wide enough for a shield. A man forced himself through it and fell beneath the press of those behind him. The defenders tried to close around the opening. Someone shouted Aubert’s name.

Elyas ran down with the foot he had kept beside the mill. They reached the gate as a second shield came through. He struck at the edge, felt his blade glance, and drove his shoulder against the man carrying it. The bank prevented him taking a proper step. For several breaths there was nothing to see except wood close to his face and the wet red mouth of a man trying to bite through pain.

Then the man fell back. Someone beside Elyas thrust into the opening and the pressure eased.

Aubert lay behind the gate. His beard was dark with blood. One of his men was trying to lift him by the shoulders, calling him captain as though the title were an instruction he could still obey.

“Leave him until we have room,” Elyas said.

The man stared at him.

“Take his place.”

He had to point. At last the soldier let go and stood in the gap beside the broken timber.

Elyas moved along the bank, giving the same order twice to men who could not hear the first time. At the upper gate the defenders still held. Farther uphill he could see Sornay’s horse coming back in fragments, some turning to face pursuers, others trying to form on Wace.

He sent a man for the wounded wagon.

A man in Lormey’s colours was kneeling behind the bank, trying to straighten a bent spearhead against a stone. Every blow made it turn farther out of line. Elyas told him to take another.

“There isn’t one.”

“From the ground.”

The man looked towards the broken gate. The nearest spare shaft lay beyond it, beneath the leg of an attacker who had fallen across the opening. Elyas had given an order without looking where it sent him.

“Stay here.”

He took a spear from a wounded soldier being carried back. The man protested until Elyas promised it would be returned if he could find him. He handed it to the kneeling soldier and pushed his shoulder towards the place another defender had just left.

The attack pressed again. A shield came over the lowest part of the bank, followed by a face with its eyes squeezed shut against the earth falling from it. The defender thrust too early. His point caught the shield and slid; before he could draw it back the man beneath it had got a foot on the bank.

Elyas struck the raised arm. He felt the blade meet resistance and wrench in his hand. The attacker fell sideways, pulling the spear with him. For a moment the defender stared at his empty hands.

“Back,” Elyas said.

Another man took his place. The attacker below the bank was still moving, trying to push himself away with his sound arm. Someone began to lean over to finish him.

“Face the road.”

Elyas had to drag the man round. The next shields were already close. An arrow struck the earth between them, throwing dirt against his cheek. He felt for blood and found only grit.

The first defender had retrieved his bent spear. He held it low now, waiting until the shield in front of him lifted. When he thrust, the point went beneath it. The man carrying the shield cried out and fell against the one beside him. The opening filled at once.

Above the mill a horse screamed. Elyas turned far enough to see Wace’s men pressed against the corner of the field. A rider had come off near the fallen stone. Two of Sornay’s men were trying to get him onto a saddle while their companions faced the approaching horse.

One could not lift him alone. The other had to turn his back to help.

Elyas called to the few foot still waiting beside the mill and sent them up to cover the men at the stone. He had meant to keep them for the withdrawal. They went carrying spears at different angles, two running faster than the rest until their sergeant called them back together.

They reached the riders in time. Elyas saw the fallen man dragged across a saddle, saw the horse turn under the awkward weight. Then the men he had sent had to come away themselves. An enemy rider crossed behind the last two. One dropped into the ditch. The other was struck before he reached it.

There was no reserve left beside Elyas. The next request would have to be answered with men already holding somewhere else.

He looked towards the mill. The wounded wagon had begun moving onto the raised strip. He made himself watch the gate again.

Berent found him near the bend.

“Captain Wace says the far side is going. He can hold the corner while the men come through.”

Elyas caught his sleeve before he could run back.

“Where is the wagon?”

“Loaded. There are two men still waiting.”

“Who is with them?”

“The miller’s wife.”

Elyas looked up the slope. More horse had come through the wall. The company facing Wace was growing wider. If he held the bank much longer, his men would have to cross that open ground while mounted troops came down on them.

“Tell Wace I am bringing the foot out. Sornay covers the near side. Then go with the wagon.”

Berent repeated it. Elyas let him go.

A man at the bank heard enough to cry that they were leaving. The cry travelled faster than an order. Several men began turning before their neighbours knew why. Elyas seized the nearest by his shoulder strap and put him back facing the road.

“Together. You come out when the man beside you comes out.”

Aubert’s company had no captain now. Elyas pointed to the soldier who had tried to carry him.

“You. Take the men by the lower gate first. Keep them on this side of the mill.”

“I can’t—”

“Show them where to go. That’s all.”

The man looked at the route, then began calling names.

They came away badly at first. Men tripped over the bank they had built higher that morning; others tried to carry too much. One dropped his spear to lift a wounded companion and was pushed aside by a man who still held his own. Elyas caught the wounded man under the arm and helped him as far as the mill wall. There was no room on the wagon.

The miller’s wife had brought a door out on two short poles. Her husband stood at one end, furious with her and unable to make her go without him. They put the man on it beside the other who was waiting.

“You can’t carry two,” Elyas said.

“Then give us men.”

He gave her four from his own foot. They lifted and moved, swaying beneath the weight.

At the upper gate the remaining defenders began to come back. The enemy followed through before the last men were clear. Elyas turned with those nearest him and held the narrow space beside the building while the others passed. A spear caught his coat and drove him against the wall. He cut at the shaft, failed to sever it, and had to wrench himself free, leaving cloth pinned behind him.

Someone pulled him by the belt.

“Come away, my lord.”

He stumbled into the lane beyond the mill. The wagon was already moving.

Sornay kept the undertaking.

His men came down on the riders trying to follow the foot into the lane, met them long enough to make them check, then turned towards Wace’s remaining horse. A man fell between the two parties and was left. Sornay looked back once. Elyas saw him make the choice to keep riding.

The foot reached the broken wall beyond the mill. Here the lane turned away from the road, giving them cover for a short distance before open ground. Wace had put men on both sides of the turn. His left arm hung close against him. Blood ran down the outside of his glove.

“The bank?” he asked.

“Gone.”

“Everyone out?”

“No.”

Wace nodded as if the answer were one more fact he had to use. He sent the men across the open ground in small groups while the horse held the turn. When the pursuers came close enough to shoot into it, he ordered the horse away too.

Elyas found his own mount with a boy holding the reins. He mounted badly, one foot missing the stirrup at the first attempt. The boy slapped the horse’s neck and ran after the foot.

At the disputed holding the gate stood open. The eldest road keeper waited beside it with the tenant, holding the copy of Elyas’s undertaking. Sornay’s riders came through first. When Lormey’s foot followed, the tenant stepped forward as though to challenge their badges. The keeper put a hand against his chest and showed him the paper again.

“Wagon first,” he called to the men bunching at the opening. “Let the wagon turn.”

Berent was already off the seat, showing the driver how widely to take it. He had walked this turn with the keeper before the fighting. A wheel struck the post, splintering its edge, but cleared. The foot followed without having to break into the field.

Elyas came through with the last mounted guard. The tenant looked at the damaged post.

“I’ll have your name to that,” he said.

“You already have it.”

Elyas stayed long enough to see the keeper count the last men through, then rode on. Behind them the mill disappeared among men entering the yard. A little smoke rose from somewhere beyond it. Elyas could not see whether a fire had been set or a cooking hearth disturbed. He kept looking until the road turned.

They halted only when the wounded wagon could go no farther without a wheel being tightened.

Berent was sitting beside the driver, holding a man’s head against his thigh. The man had bled through the cloth wrapped round his scalp. When Elyas approached, the boy looked up without moving.

“He’s breathing.”

“I see.”

“I thought he had stopped.”

Elyas laid a hand on the boy’s shoulder. It was trembling beneath the coat.

“Stay with him while they mend the wheel.”

The miller and his wife arrived with the improvised stretcher. One of the men on it had died on the road. She covered his face before asking where the other could be put.

Elyas sent riders to gather stragglers. He had the captains count their men, then count again when the last parties came in. Lormey’s soldiers stood apart, waiting for an officer who would not return. Sornay went to them himself and told them where they could find water.

Wace submitted to having his arm bound. The wound was deep but had missed the bone. He asked for the account before it was complete.

“Thirty-one we cannot find,” Elyas said. “Some are prisoners. Some may still come.”

“And hurt?”

He told him. Wace closed his eyes while the binding was tightened.

A rider came after dark. Valdais’s men held the mill road. Their first wagons were moving through, slowly, past wounded being gathered from the bank. The army would continue north when the passage was clear. The road which Elyas had held since morning now belonged to them.

Near midnight another rider brought word from Tylan. The delay had allowed the royal advance to close on Valdais’s rearward movements. He wanted Elyas’s survivors brought towards a place where they could be supplied, and he wanted the account of the fighting as soon as it could be written.

Elyas sat beneath a lantern while Berent held the board. He gave the place lost, the road taken, and the time the enemy had needed to force it. Then he began the names.

At Aubert’s he stopped. He did not know the captain’s first name. One of Lormey’s men came over and supplied it, spelling it slowly until Berent had written it correctly.

Chapter 31 — You Will Remain

Ouen recognised his grey before he recognised the man riding it.

The horse came through the gate with its head low, one rein knotted near the bit. Renaud had lost his hat. The officer had been stiff with offence when Ouen could not furnish the five horses he had promised; now he scarcely looked up. There was a strip of cloth tied round his neck where something had cut him, and the coat Ouen remembered was hidden beneath mud.

“Where is my father?”

“Coming north.”

“To us?”

“To Bethencourt.”

Ouen reached for the rein. The horse turned towards him with a familiarity that made the rest of the yard seem briefly strange. He had given it away expecting never to see it again. Now he could see a rubbed place beneath the saddle and a little swelling above one knee.

“Get down,” he told Renaud.

The man tried. Ouen had to help him take his leg across.

Renaud slept for six hours. During that time other men arrived, some with instructions and some because they knew there would be food. Their accounts placed the fight at different hours and gave Elyas anything from a company to half the queen’s army. They agreed that the road had been held and cleared, and that men who should have reached the next stage before night had been brought there after dark.

The returning teams had to be stopped where they stood while fresh loads went forward to Bethencourt. Ouen’s father’s instructions named the city plainly. He would not repeat the long retreat towards Ethor. The northern fortifications were provisioned, their garrison ready, and the roads inside Adria still obeyed him.

Ouen sent for Halden.

“I am going.”

“To the city?”

“To my father.”

Halden read the order, then looked up.

“He requires the teams here.”

“You can send them.”

“I can send the teams. The men will ask whose authority releases what they carry.”

“Mine.”

“Then give me the limits before you go.”

Ouen had expected refusal. This practical acceptance annoyed him more. He named what could be sent and what must remain for the wounded already in the house. Halden wrote it down, stopping him twice to distinguish a quantity from everything in a store.

Iselde came in while he was speaking.

“How long?” she asked.

“I don’t know.”

“Will you come back for more clothes, or shall I send them?”

He looked at her. She was holding one of Hew’s stockings, turned inside out to find a thing caught in the toe.

“I may stay with him.”

“I know what you mean.”

He wanted an argument which would let him defend going. She gave him none. When he had finished dictating, she asked him to tell his father that the first wounded men were recovering and that she needed the promised cloth before the next party arrived.

He said he would. At the door she caught his sleeve.

“Come back if he sends you.”

Ouen pulled his arm free more sharply than he intended.

“Why would he?”

She looked at him until he could no longer mistake the question for ignorance.

He met his father outside the city, at a house being used to receive officers before they entered the gates.

The yard was crowded. A horse with blood dried down its foreleg stood beside a wagon of rolled bedding. Men waited against the wall, holding papers which had been folded until the edges grew soft. Ouen gave his name and was admitted past them. Their faces followed him.

Valdais stood at a table with his secretary and two officers. He had a clean coat on, though he had not shaved. A platter of food sat untouched beside the papers. When he saw Ouen he dismissed the officers but kept the secretary.

“What have you brought?”

“News from home.”

“Good. Sit.”

Ouen gave Iselde’s message first. His father listened, asked how many men had arrived, and directed the secretary to send cloth from a nearer store instead of waiting for the next convoy. The change was made before Ouen could feel useful for bringing it.

“I want to remain with you.”

Valdais looked at him across the table.

“No.”

“I have men.”

“You have work.”

“There are officers at home.”

“And here. I do not need another man to ask whether the gate is shut.”

Ouen felt the secretary become very still.

“You let others come.”

“Others command companies which must come inside. Yours remain outside.”

“Then I will bring them.”

His father put down the pen he had taken up.

“You will remain where I have placed you.”

The words were quiet. Ouen had heard that quiet before, usually addressed to someone who had mistaken the first refusal for an opening to bargain.

“I am your son.”

“Yes.”

“Then let me be seen beside you.”

Valdais leaned back. For a moment Ouen thought he might soften. Instead he asked the secretary to leave.

When they were alone, his father pushed the platter towards him.

“Eat something.”

“I am not hungry.”

“Neither am I. We should both eat.”

Valdais took a piece of meat and chewed without pleasure. Ouen remained standing until his father indicated the chair again.

“Bethencourt can hold through the season. Longer, with care. There is grain within the walls and water we need not fetch past an enemy. If you come in, I must either employ you where another officer knows the work better or keep you near me so every man can watch me protect you.”

“I don’t ask to be protected.”

“I know. Sons rarely do.”

Ouen sat. Through the wall he could hear a man coughing in the yard.

“Elyas held against you.”

“He did.”

“You said Harald would not.”

“Harald did not.”

His father met his eyes. The distinction was accurate enough to be infuriating.

“Elyas found more men than I expected. They chose their ground well. I have paid for the mistake and am not proposing to pay for it twice.”

Ouen looked at the untouched side of the platter. His father had admitted the thing he had wanted admitted, and it gave him none of the satisfaction he had imagined.

“Could you have taken the other road?”

“Yes. With time I no longer wished to give her.”

“And now?”

“Now she can come here.”

Valdais rose and crossed to the narrow window. A line of carts was moving towards the city. He watched them for a while.

“You are not to empty the outer households to feed this one. The stores within are sufficient. Keep people on their holdings where they can remain safely. Receive the men who cannot. If officers take supplies, require them to give their names. I may need to know who has been making enemies for me.”

“And when they refuse?”

“Do what you can. Send the refusal.”

Ouen almost laughed. His father was asking him to do work in which an order could be answered with another man’s refusal, then ride on to somewhere the order might be obeyed. He had been doing it all winter.

“Halden knows,” Valdais said.

“Yes. Halden generally does.”

His father turned from the window.

“Then use him. I kept men wiser than I was at your age. Your grandmother insisted on it.”

“Did you like them?”

“Some of them. Eventually.”

For the first time that afternoon Ouen smiled.

His father came back and put a hand on his shoulder. The weight remained there long enough for him to stop preparing another objection.

“Go home.”

He returned before the next day’s meal. Iselde looked up from the table where she was cutting cloth and asked whether his father was well. He gave the message about the nearer store. She set the shears down.

“Did he send you?”

“Yes.”

She nodded and went back to the cloth. He was grateful that she did not say she had expected it. He was also angry that she had.

Halden had three matters waiting. Ouen heard the first badly and had to have it repeated. A man wanted to move his family into the household because soldiers had begun marking barns farther down the road. They carried the queen’s authority. They had not yet taken anything from him, but they had counted what he possessed.

“Then why has he come here?”

“He would rather move before they return.”

“Everyone would.”

The man waited outside the door with a girl holding his sleeve. Ouen could see her through the opening. She was old enough to understand that the men inside were deciding whether she would sleep where she had slept the previous night.

“Bring them in.”

The father described the officers, the mark made on his barn, and the cattle taken from a neighbour. He wanted a cart and permission to bring what food he could carry. If he left the rest, he asked whether Ouen would send men to guard it.

Ouen had no men to send. The four who had escorted the shortened delivery were back, exhausted and needed at the store. He told the man so.

“Then I’ll lose it.”

“You may.”

The answer sounded cruel. Any better one would have promised something Ouen could not provide.

“You can have a cart until tomorrow,” he said. “Bring the food with the children. We will find room.”

The man asked where. Ouen called Iselde, and together they went to look at the room which had been emptied for the last wounded party.

Chapter 32 — To Prove a Point

The steward gave Seniro the chair required by his rank and sent for no wine.

There was wine on the table. An officer who had arrived earlier held a cup of it, and two more cups stood beside the jug. When Tylan entered, the steward poured for him, set the jug down, and asked whether the marshal wished to inspect the buildings before his men were billeted.

“After we’ve heard the complaint,” Tylan said.

The steward inclined his head. He kept his gaze on Tylan while Seniro unfolded the complaint between them.

It concerned grain taken from a barn which had already supplied the army three days before. The second officer had found more and treated its presence as proof that the first collection had been evaded. The owner said it was seed brought from another holding after the first taking. He had the carrier with him to swear it.

“Where is the grain now?” Seniro asked.

“In your camp, my lord.”

“Which part?”

“The officer would not say.”

Tylan asked the same question and received the man’s best guess, including the colour of a company badge. Seniro let the omission stand. He sent his own clerk to find the officer.

Outside the window the first royal wagons were being brought into the lower yard. Their wheels had worn the lane down to stones which had lain beneath it for years. Seniro could hear men arguing over which load should be taken first.

The owner of the grain came in without removing his hat. The steward said his name sharply. He took the hat off and held it by the brim, leaving his eyes on Seniro.

“My lord the Red.”

Seniro returned the greeting with the man’s proper title, a courtesy he had been careful to learn before entering the house.

“They tell me you have brought the queen’s justice.”

“I have come to hear what was taken.”

“It is written there.”

“Then tell me what the officer said.”

The man did. Seniro listened until he had finished, asked the carrier when he had brought the seed, and had the answer entered beside the first account. Neither man thanked him. The steward supplied a stool for the carrier and moved the unused cup farther down the table.

They found half the grain.

The rest had already been issued to companies farther along the siege line. Some would have been ground before the complaint reached the house. The officer maintained that he had acted within the quantity assigned to his party. He had a sealed instruction to prove it.

“That names what you may take,” Seniro said. “It doesn’t tell you to take it twice from the same man.”

“The first taking was not entered on the barn.”

“It was entered on the paper he showed you.”

“I had no way of knowing whether that paper belonged to the grain before me.”

“Did you ask the carrier?”

“I was sent to bring grain.”

The officer seemed genuinely bewildered that this answer had failed. Men had been hungry at the end of his road. He had found food and brought it, with the owner’s name written down. In a month’s time some clerk might determine what compensation was owed. By then the troops would either have eaten or dispersed to find their own meals.

Seniro returned the unused sacks and required a witnessed acknowledgement for the rest. The owner wanted replacement seed, not a promise of money. Tylan gave him a date by which a decision would be made on the nearest available supply. He would not promise its arrival before knowing it existed.

The man heard him, folded the paper, and left.

The steward held the door for Tylan. Seniro followed without being offered the remaining wine.

Two people waited outside the queen’s tent, a man in a travelling coat and a woman whose sword belt had been mended close to the buckle. Neither wore a household badge. Tylan’s officer knew their names and had admitted them as far as the outer guard.

Seniro recognised the man from a description sent from Valora.

“The Mound?”

The man inclined his head.

“You have followed the army.”

“We have asked to serve her person. There would be little use waiting in Valora while she is here.”

“And the woman?”

“She has stood watch for people less able to reward it.”

The woman looked directly at Seniro.

“I can answer for myself.”

He asked where she had served. She named a northern holding and the captain who would know her. Seniro knew the name. It was a useful answer, containing nothing he needed to take on faith.

“Who has sent you?”

“Our hearing agreed it,” she said. “We each consented to come.”

“The Shadow-Court takes names to its hearings.”

“Yes.”

“Do you propose to bring those names to the queen?”

“That is among the things she must decide.”

Seniro looked towards the tent. He would not have called the pair an army, and could not yet decide what danger came with letting them stand nearer to her than one.

The officer lifted the entrance cloth for him. They remained outside.

Constano’s account lay open in the queen’s tent.

It had passed through enough hands to acquire three different notes about delays along the road. The quantities named were useful. The distances remained the same whatever number stood beside them. Teams could carry more from the nearer stores only by being taken from other work, and the farther supplies would consume part of their own loads reaching Bethencourt.

Veralyn read the last note twice.

“He says these can be brought by water.”

“As far as the landing,” Tylan said. “Then we need teams again.”

“We have teams.”

“We have them on the nearer road. Put them at the landing and those loads wait.”

She put a finger beside the date.

“How long can we remain?”

“At the present issue, with what we can obtain nearby, a few weeks before the difficulty grows worse. Less if the roads fail. We can stretch it. We cannot feed the army here for the months they have within the walls.”

Seniro watched her read. Shadowsteel rested beside the chair, its hilt within reach of her hand. She had laid a glove over it while taking the papers. Now she moved the glove away, although it was not obstructing anything.

“Then they believe we must go.”

“They have reason to,” he said.

She looked up at him. There was no anger in it.

“What have we left outside?”

Tylan named the stores already taken, those under protection, and the districts from which fresh forage might come. Veralyn asked about a road beyond the western houses. He had not yet received a complete report on it.

“It will carry horse,” she said.

“Some of it. I would want a man over the farther part before sending the wagons.”

“Send him.”

She spoke as if the answer had arrived. Then she asked for the actual report when it came, and Seniro felt foolish for having heard more than an impatient queen deciding what to do next.

A servant brought food. Veralyn ate standing while Tylan explained the difficulties at the landing. She gave him the larger piece of bread without looking, having noticed that he had taken only the heel. When the servant asked whether more was needed, she said enough for everyone and pulled a stool towards Seniro with her foot.

The city’s representative came out the next morning under truce.

Sire Belan was old enough to have known the earlier war and proud enough to name the place of each meeting as if correcting a younger man about his own childhood. He had brought a clerk and an officer responsible for the western gate. His immediate business concerned families who had come into the city with no right to its stores and now wished to leave.

“Wish?” Veralyn asked.

“They cannot all be maintained within.”

“Then say which are being sent out.”

Belan gave the number. His clerk supplied the households. The gate officer explained that they would carry food for the road but could not be permitted to take carts belonging to the garrison.

“Where are they going?”

“To their own places where possible. To kin otherwise.”

“And where my companies stand between them and those places?”

“We ask passage.”

Veralyn turned to Tylan. He pointed to the crossings at which parties could be received and moved beyond the active lines. Each required men diverted from other duties. He proposed separate departures rather than bringing everyone out at once.

Belan objected to the delay. The queen asked whether the city had so little food that it could not maintain the families for another two days.

“No, Your Grace.”

“Then we have time to move them properly.”

He inclined his head. Seniro saw the effort it cost him.

They agreed the first departure. Names would be given before the gate opened; people arriving would be checked against them without separating children from the adults who claimed them. Men leaving under this passage could not return carrying intelligence or supplies. Belan wanted a general protection for their property outside the walls. Veralyn asked how much of it had already supplied the garrison.

“It remains their property.”

“It remains outside your walls.”

The officer beside Belan shifted. Seniro heard leather creak beneath the table.

“My lady,” Belan said, “we have provision for a long defence. Your father also believed determination could bring a fortress down.”

Veralyn looked at him for the length of a breath.

“No, I do not storm fortresses to prove a point.”

Seniro had been looking at her hand. It lay flat beside the list of households, the fingers relaxed. He remembered the same hand gripping a rein until the glove creased white across the knuckles, and her father’s horse going where no order would bring it back. He put the memory away before it grew any larger.

Belan glanced towards him, then back to the queen.

“What protection, then, may the people outside expect?”

“Those who observe the agreed passage have it. The rest depends on the terms your lord will make.”

The clerk’s pen had stopped. Belan told him to finish writing the departures.

After the envoys left, Seniro walked the road from the parley ground towards the nearer camp.

A girl was trying to lead two cows past a wagon whose driver refused to move until a broken trace was repaired. One cow would pass; the other would not follow. Seniro dismounted and held the first animal while the girl went back for the second. She thanked him before looking properly at his face.

Then she saw the men behind him, the badge on his coat, and stopped speaking.

He handed her the rope. She took it without touching his fingers and hurried both animals off the road.

At the tent, Veralyn was listening to the report from the western route. It would carry horse. Wagons would need work at two wet places. She asked how quickly men could be put on the farther side.

Tylan answered. Seniro remained at the entrance until she noticed him.

“I want to send you into the city,” she said.

He came forward. The account of the families lay beneath her hand, beside the map of the country outside the walls.

Chapter 33 — Four Places

Veralyn waited until the last officer had left.

The entrance cloth moved in the wind. Seniro stood beside the table while she looked once more at the country around Bethencourt. Someone had marked the nearest stores with small crosses. Beyond them the map grew less exact, roads narrowing into lines whose condition no one in the tent yet knew.

She lifted her eyes to his.

“Coldmorrow, Lence, Charau, Bethencourt.”

He had expected an instruction to seek terms, perhaps a demand concerning the gates. For a moment he heard the words without allowing them to become his message.

Her hand remained beside the map.

He could have asked her to say more. She would have had to make some part of it explicit, and he could then have answered that part as an officer advising his queen. He knew the refuge in that. He had used it before.

He bowed and went to arrange the embassy.

The safe-conduct came out before noon.

At the gate an officer checked Seniro’s name against it, counted the men permitted to accompany him, and required the others to remain beyond the approach. He performed each courtesy without omission. When Seniro dismounted, a groom took the horse and led it away before he could ask whether it would be watered.

The officer saw him look.

“It will be cared for according to the undertaking, my lord.”

“Thank you.”

They passed beneath the gate. Men stood on either side, close enough that Seniro could smell the damp wool of their coats. One had a bandage across his cheek. Another looked at the badge on Seniro’s breast and made a small sound which could have been a laugh.

The officer halted.

“A herald’s company is under protection.”

No one answered. They continued.

Inside, the city was busy. Grain carts stood by a storehouse with men measuring the loads as they came off. Women carried baskets through the lane beside it. A boy was sweeping spilled grain towards the wall, trying to keep it out of the path of the wheels. There was enough food here to make the threat of a long siege credible. Seniro had seen stores assembled in panic; these were being managed by people who had expected to use them.

A shutter closed as he passed.

At the house where Valdais received him, Belan waited at the foot of the stair. He greeted Seniro by title, asked whether the journey had been untroubled, and led him up without offering further conversation.

Valdais stood at the window overlooking the inner yard.

He turned when they entered and indicated the chair opposite his own. Belan remained by the door. The clerk who had attended the parley sat at a smaller table with a clean sheet prepared.

“My niece sends you.”

“Yes.”

“She might have come herself.”

“She has sent me.”

Valdais looked at him for a moment, then sat.

“Give me her message.”

Seniro had carried demands longer than a page through hostile country and delivered them with less difficulty.

“Coldmorrow.”

The clerk’s pen touched the paper.

“Lence.”

Valdais ceased moving the ring on his hand.

“Charau.”

He looked up then. Seniro met his eyes. There was no question in them about which man had been sent or why.

“Bethencourt.”

The clerk waited, pen raised. Seniro said nothing more.

Belan moved away from the door.

“Those are the terms?”

“The message.”

Valdais held up a hand before Belan could speak again.

For a while the only sound was the work in the yard. Someone called for a cart to be moved. The driver answered that there was nowhere to move it to.

At last Valdais asked, “How much country does she believe she needs?”

Seniro kept his hands still on his knees.

“She knows what is within reach.”

“And you have told her how it is done.”

“I have told her what our army can obtain.”

Valdais looked towards Belan.

“The reports from the outer holdings. Bring them.”

They laid the reports across the table between the two chairs.

A steward had brought cattle inside the city before the northern road closed. Another had sent the cattle away, towards kin beyond the likely passage of the army. A third requested men to stop soldiers taking seed. Each report concerned a place with people in it. Seniro recognised two names from the complaint heard the previous day.

Valdais asked which roads remained open. Belan answered from the latest scouts. The royal companies could move around the city more readily than the baggage could follow them. There were farms beyond the immediate line from which food could still be brought, provided men were sent to protect the carriers.

“Against how many?” Valdais asked.

Belan gave no number. He named the commanders reported in the country and the last places their troops had been seen.

Valdais read the request about seed again.

“You have enough within,” Seniro said.

“I know what I have within.”

He set the paper down with care.

“My people could come inside.”

“Some have. Others could.”

“And those who cannot leave?”

Seniro had no answer which would improve the question. He could name the protections already agreed for particular parties, but that was not what Valdais had asked.

Belan put a finger on one road.

“We could strike their collecting parties. Make them carry guards with every wagon.”

“Yes,” Valdais said.

“We have men enough.”

“Yes.”

Belan waited for the order. It did not come.

Valdais rose and went back to the window. Seniro could see his reflection in the uneven glass, broken at the shoulder where one pane met another. A man in the yard was trying to turn a team without touching the cart behind it. Valdais watched until the horses had made the turn.

“When you leave here,” he said, “does she expect you to carry an answer?”

“Yes.”

“Or permission to begin?”

Seniro said nothing.

Valdais turned from the window.

“Then she shall have an answer.”

He required the clerk to write the conditions as he gave them.

The city would admit an agreed royal party after acceptance, sufficient to receive the gates and supervise the stores. Its people would keep their property, subject to the ordinary claims proved against it. No soldier would be allowed to call the surrender licence for a taking. Men serving Valdais would give their names, surrender the military obligations covered by the settlement, and receive safe passage home unless accused of a separately stated offence.

Belan objected at that clause.

“Accused by whom?”

“An accusation must name the act and be heard,” Valdais said. “Service under me is not the act.”

The clerk amended it.

The royal army would cease punitive collections in the lands covered by the agreement. Necessary purchases and existing claims would be settled through named officers. Valdais wanted the protection to begin with acceptance, before he personally left the city. Seniro told him that the proposed timing must be carried back as part of the offer.

“Carry it.”

He required his household to be maintained, his children protected, and the government of his holdings entrusted to Ouen under the laws already binding them, with the specific military restrictions of the settlement written separately. The crown could appoint men to witness performance. It would not replace every local officer by calling him a rebel.

Seniro listened, saying where a phrase might claim more than could be granted. Valdais changed some. Others he ordered left for the queen to answer.

At last Belan asked about the duke himself.

Valdais looked at him, then at Seniro.

“I will remain in custody for the rest of my life. In a house, with a household fit to maintain it. No command. No return to these lands.”

Belan drew breath.

Valdais stopped him with a look.

“I will write to my children.”

“The letters will be read,” Seniro said.

“I assumed you would discover that precaution.”

“By clerks empowered to withhold instructions.”

“Then let them distinguish an instruction from a father asking after his grandson.”

The clerk wrote quickly. Seniro waited until he caught up.

“Have you a house in mind?”

Valdais’s mouth moved, almost into a smile.

“My brother found one adequate for my mother.”

Seniro looked at the paper in the clerk’s hand.

“Would you offer that?”

“It has the advantage of having satisfied a king already. If your queen requires another, she may name it. I require the house and correspondence. I am past requiring pleasant associations.”

Belan went to the window. He stood where Valdais had stood, looking down into the same yard.

They argued over the collections until the afternoon light changed.

Valdais insisted that grain seized after acceptance be returned if still available, paid for if consumed, and entered in a form the owner could use without travelling to Valora. Seniro could hear Constano’s objections in advance: different measures, claims submitted twice, papers destroyed or never given. He named the need for witnesses. Valdais named the people who would have none because an armed officer had not waited while they fetched neighbours.

“Then permit testimony,” he said. “You need not believe every man to hear him.”

The clerk added it.

A separate provision allowed a supervised meeting with his family during transfer. Future visits would require permission; correspondence would be regular, with originals examined and copies retained. The household required for his confinement would be chosen subject to the queen’s approval. No armed retinue. No messengers sent privately.

Valdais made them read the whole offer aloud.

At the provision concerning Ouen he corrected one word. His son would exercise delegated government while he lived. He was not being declared dead for the convenience of a seal. At the promise to cease military claims, he listened without interruption. When the clerk finished, he took the pen.

Seniro watched him sign.

Belan escorted him down.

At the foot of the stair the older man stopped. Men were passing through the entrance carrying bundles, and for a moment neither could speak without being overheard.

When the way cleared, Belan said, “There are people here who remember Charau.”

“Yes.”

“They will remember this too.”

Seniro received the safe-conduct from him. It bore the same exact titles as before.

His horse had been watered. A groom brought it to the mounting block, checked the girth, and stepped back without waiting for a coin. Seniro mounted and rode towards the gate.

The boy sweeping grain was still in the lane. He had gathered it into a cloth and was tying the corners together. An empty cart rattled past him. He held the bundle against his chest until the wheels had cleared, then ran across to the storehouse.

Outside the walls Seniro’s waiting men fell in behind him. He carried the offer inside his coat, where the stiff folded paper pressed against his ribs each time the horse moved.

Chapter 34 — What Was Undertaken

The acceptance reached Elyas while he was trying to persuade a wounded man to give his name.

The man had been brought from the Scar with no badge on his coat and a horse which belonged to someone else. He expected that naming himself would lead to a demand he could not meet. Elyas had offered to send word to his household. The offer made him more suspicious.

“Let him think,” Wace said.

“He’s been thinking for two days.”

“Then another hour won’t ruin it.”

A rider came into the yard with the sealed order. Elyas left the man on the bench and received it at the door.

Veralyn had accepted Valdais’s offer. The gates would be received on the appointed day. The military restrictions were entered separately from the civil protections, and the protections took effect upon acceptance. Officers were to halt collections forbidden by the instrument and preserve records of those already made. The queen required acknowledgement from each command.

Elyas read the order twice before passing it to Wace.

“And him?” Wace asked.

“Confinement. For life.”

The captain read farther. His bound arm rested against his chest. When he reached the provision for the household, he looked towards the men sitting in the yard.

“We’ll need copies.”

“We’ll need men carrying them.”

Rousset’s officer arrived before Elyas had dispatched the last copy.

He had come with an authority to collect supplies from a holding along the road. The name was familiar: the property belonging to Rousset’s underage niece. Her uncle now claimed that it had furnished grain to Valdais and could therefore be required to support the victorious army.

“His lordship offers transport,” the officer said. “He wishes only what is proper.”

Elyas remembered Rousset straightening his gloves before asking for the same holding.

“For what quantity?”

“All that can be spared.”

“And who decides what can be spared?”

“His steward knows the place.”

Elyas called for the copy of Rousset’s earlier request. The officer watched it being brought with an expression of increasing discomfort.

“Is the wardship decided?”

“Not yet, my lord.”

“Then the same people remain entitled to speak for the holding.”

“They supported the duke.”

“Read this.”

He put the acceptance before him. The officer bent over it. His lips moved at the longer clauses.

“This taking was ordered before the agreement.”

“Has it occurred?”

“Men have been sent.”

“Then we go after them.”

The niece’s mother met them outside the barn.

She was a small woman with her hair coming loose beneath a cap. Two carts stood loaded behind her. Rousset’s men had taken the key from the servant holding it and broken a second fastening when no one could produce the other key quickly enough.

Elyas dismounted. The officer he had brought went ahead to explain the new order. The men by the carts looked first at their own officer, then at the royal badge on Wace’s coat.

The woman came directly to Elyas.

“They have taken the seed.”

“How much?”

She named the sacks. He asked which cart held them. She showed him.

“Take those down first.”

A man beside the wheel objected that he had been ordered to load them. Wace told him who was now ordering the reverse. The man began untying the cover.

Inside, the girl whose property was being disputed sat on a chest with a woman holding her hand. She was perhaps eleven. Elyas had pictured an infant when Rousset spoke of a ward. He found it harder to speak about the holding now that she was watching.

“Will my uncle come?” she asked.

“I don’t know.”

“He says Mother can’t keep it.”

“That question must be heard where it belongs. An army does not decide it by taking your grain.”

The mother looked at him sharply, as if testing whether he had offered more than the words contained.

“Will you give us that in writing?”

“Yes.”

He wrote at the table while the sacks were brought back. The girl watched the pen. When he finished, she asked if his name would keep the men away.

“It should make them ask before they return.”

“Will it?”

He called Rousset’s officer into the room and made him hear the order. The mother kept her hand on the paper until the man had acknowledged it.

The claim for the retreat gate arrived with a piece of the broken post.

The road keeper had brought it because the tenant feared the damage would be called old. Elyas recognised the fresh split. There were other claims: a strip of trampled crop, two sheep missing after the troops passed. He accepted the gate and ordered the others heard with witnesses, refusing to call them false because they were inconvenient.

Wace watched him sign the repair.

“We could have broken through.”

“With men coming behind us and a wagon stuck between the posts.”

The captain nodded towards the keeper, waiting outside.

“Would he have opened it for me?”

“Not until he had asked his lord. You would have had to wait, threaten him, or break it. He could take my undertaking because my father had heard claims from that road, and my brother must hear the next ones.”

“And now they expect you to.”

“Yes.”

Elyas sent Harald the account with a proposal: the road keepers should be heard together before either baron summoned men over the disputed land again. He offered to attend the first hearing as the man whose passage had damaged it. The request gave his brother work he could own, with the parties named and already obliged to answer. Elyas dispatched it before the barons could each supply a version in which the other had caused everything.

Rousset complained that Elyas had humiliated him before his tenants.

The letter arrived two days later, with an account of service rendered by his house in earlier wars. The forty men withheld from the Scar did not appear in it. He demanded assurance that his proper rights would not be diminished by an officer’s desire to conciliate people who had supplied Valdais.

Elyas answered that those rights would be heard under the existing jurisdiction, as they would have been before the campaign. He enclosed the accepted terms and the account of the taking stopped at the barn.

Wace read the answer.

“He won’t furnish men for you again.”

“He didn’t furnish them this time.”

“He may remember that differently.”

Elyas signed. He had promised Harald to leave such disputes where the law placed them. Rousset would tell everyone who listened that the prince had favoured a woman against the man able to keep her property safe. Some would believe him because they thought safety should belong to the stronger claim.

Berent carried the letter to the messenger. When he returned, he asked whether the girl would keep her land.

“I don’t know,” Elyas said. “I have stopped one taking. The case remains.”

The boy seemed disappointed. Elyas understood the feeling too well to rebuke it.

The accounts to the households took longer than the orders to stop collecting.

Men thought missing at the Scar began to return. A few had been taken prisoner and were released under the settlement. Others had walked to relatives instead of seeking their companies. Two came together, one supporting the other, and found that letters announcing them unaccounted for had already gone ahead.

Elyas had fresh letters written. He refused to let a clerk describe the earlier reports as mistakes. At the time they had not known where the men were. Their families deserved to know why they had been frightened.

Sornay came to collect his surviving men. His gaze kept leaving Elyas to follow the men packing in the yard. He had a list of property lost while serving, including a horse whose owner had borrowed it. Elyas recognised the baron’s writing but another hand had supplied the values.

“These will be disputed,” Sornay said.

“Some of them.”

“They are real losses.”

“Then bring the owners when they are heard.”

The baron looked towards Lormey’s men, who were waiting for an officer from home.

“Aubert’s widow will need more than the amount allowed for his coat.”

Elyas turned back to him.

“Have you written to her?”

“My wife knows her sister.”

He said it impatiently, as if Elyas had required an explanation for an ordinary decency. Then he asked whether the body could be brought home.

They had recovered it under the truce. Elyas told him where it lay. Sornay sent two of his own men with the cart. When Lormey’s officer finally came, the work was already arranged.

Before leaving, the baron took Elyas aside.

“You held the promise about command.”

“As far as I could.”

“I know what you held.”

He looked at the empty places in the line of men gathering for the road.

“Don’t ask me to be grateful for the rest.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

Sornay mounted and went to take his company home.

Harald required an account in person.

Elyas found him in a room full of papers, with an older officer waiting outside and a servant trying to discover when dinner should be served. Harald dismissed the servant twice without answering. Elyas told him they could eat while speaking. His brother looked offended, then hungry, and ordered the meal brought.

“You have made them accustomed to coming to you,” Harald said.

“They came because I brought a force.”

“They will come again.”

“Yes.”

The frank answer left Harald with the next accusation unspoken. He cut a piece of bread and did not eat it.

“You were to preserve my authority.”

“I preserved the terms we agreed. Land disputes remain yours. The barons’ service ends as specified. The royal men do not stay as garrisons.”

“And everyone knows you made the arrangements.”

Elyas could not repair that by describing the terms again.

“They will need you to hear the claims. Rousset will come. Aubert’s family may. Men who lost horses will ask who is paying.”

“So you leave me the quarrels.”

“They were always going to be yours.”

Harald put down his knife. For a moment Elyas thought he would order him out.

“Did you think I would let Valdais take whatever he pleased?”

“I did not know what you would do in time.”

It hurt him. Elyas saw that clearly enough to wish he could withdraw the sentence, but his brother had asked for the truth.

Harald sent for the waiting officer. He required the claims brought in by district, with the first hearings named before anyone left. He was clumsy about it, interrupting an answer to repeat the question differently. The officer waited him out and gave the names needed.

Elyas stayed until the work had begun. He left the account of the dead on his brother’s table.

Mara was at the station when he returned to Valora.

He found her holding a man’s arm while Gisla examined the healing wound beneath it. A bowl stood on the floor at her feet. She saw Elyas in the doorway and smiled, then looked back when the man flinched.

“You’ll have to wait,” she said.

“I can.”

“Outside the light.”

He moved. Berent stood beside him, carrying the small bundle Mara had asked for from their house. The boy watched the work with an attention he had not possessed before the Scar.

When the dressing was finished, Mara washed her hands. Elyas wanted to take them while they were still wet. He waited until she had dried them on the cloth.

She looked him over, touching the tear repaired beneath his arm.

“Here?”

“The coat.”

“And you?”

“Bruised.”

She kept her hand there for a moment. Behind her another patient called for Gisla.

“The branch has leaves,” she said.

“The dead one?”

“The one we thought was dead.”

He laughed, unexpectedly, and found he could not quite stop. Mara put her forehead against his shoulder until he had finished. Then she asked him to carry a basin into the next room before they went home.

Chapter 35 — The Children Allowed

Ouen objected to the word absence.

His father’s secretary had used it in the instrument assigning the government of the household and lands. It occupied a comfortable space in the sentence, between the authority being delegated and the duties which followed. A man might be absent because he had gone hunting or because the river had risen while he visited a friend.

“He is being imprisoned,” Ouen said.

“The circumstances are named in the settlement.”

“Then name them here.”

The secretary looked at Halden. Halden kept his eyes on the paper.

“Would that alter the authority, my lord?”

“No.”

“Or its duration?”

“No.”

Ouen read the sentence again. He had thought exactness would make him feel less as though his father were being quietly put away while clerks distributed his belongings. Instead it obliged him to imagine the sentence with for the remainder of his life written beneath his own name.

“Leave it,” he said.

The secretary continued.

Ouen would receive petitions, confirm ordinary appointments, collect the established revenues, and maintain the household obligations. He could not raise forces against the queen or dispose of specified ducal property to fund another claimant. The existing courts retained their work. Men appointed to witness the settlement could inspect its performance but were not thereby entitled to decide every local quarrel.

There were provisions for his father’s maintenance, Taliya’s household, and the younger children. Maegwen’s existing arrangements were preserved. Vaelrie’s residence at court conferred no authority over her correspondence with the family beyond the restrictions on letters to their father.

“Why is that there?” Ouen asked.

“Because someone asked whether it should be,” Halden said.

“Who?”

“The answer is useful regardless.”

Ouen wanted to argue and found nothing in the clause to argue against. He signed beneath the undertaking to exercise the government, then had the secretary read aloud the part about the lands which could not be sold.

Vaelrie arrived with a small escort and more luggage than the length of her stay appeared to require.

Ouen met her in the yard. She got down before he reached the horse and stood looking at him. He had prepared a greeting which would make the occasion easier. She put her arms round him before he could use it.

He held her awkwardly at first, then properly.

“You’ve become thin,” she said against his coat.

“I have not.”

“You have in the face.”

“It is the light.”

She drew back and examined him, smiling a little. Then Iselde came out with Hew and the child required all her attention. He had brought the blue cup to show that it still existed. Vaelrie received it with the gravity he expected and asked whether his father had taken proper care of it.

“He doesn’t use it.”

“That is probably best.”

They went indoors. Ouen followed with the smaller bag because no servant had taken it quickly enough. It was heavier than it looked.

“What have you brought?”

“Clothes.”

“For how many women?”

“One, who knows how to change them.”

He was glad enough to hear her sounding like herself that he carried the bag upstairs without another complaint.

They spoke after supper, when Hew had been taken away and Iselde had gone to hear a matter in the room where the wounded still slept.

Vaelrie sat with her feet tucked beneath the edge of her gown. She had taken out a letter and folded it again several times without opening it.

“From Jaennis?” Ouen asked.

“Yes.”

“Does she require an account?”

“No.”

He looked at the letter. Vaelrie put it in her lap.

“I told her I was coming. She asked whether I wanted someone to travel with me. I said I had the escort.”

“And she let you.”

“She is not my gaoler.”

Ouen looked away. He had made the visit sound like something borrowed from the court’s convenience.

“I’m glad you came.”

“I know.”

“Does she know you will see him?”

“Of course. She helped make certain the permission had arrived before I left. She asked nothing in return.”

The firmness in her voice discouraged the next question. Ouen found another.

“Will you stay here afterwards?”

“For a little. Then I want to go back.”

“To her.”

“Yes.”

She waited, then added, “And to the others.”

Her face changed as she spoke of them. Catrin had promised to teach her a song and then discovered Vaelrie knew a different ending, which she maintained was better. Margery had borrowed a clasp and returned it with a ribbon chosen to go with a dress Vaelrie had not yet admitted she wanted. Maelis was staying through the next entertainments, though Guiran was threatening to leave unless someone persuaded the cricket to respect musicians.

“You speak of that room as if nothing else is happening,” Ouen said.

“Things still happen in it.”

He saw the hurt before she hid it. She put the letter away.

“I know why I’m here.”

He moved to the chair beside her.

“I didn’t mean you had forgotten.”

“What did you mean?”

He had meant that he resented her having a place to return to where she was wanted for more than the next answer. He could not say that without making her responsible for it.

“I don’t know.”

Vaelrie looked at him for a while, then rested her head against his shoulder.

“You are a dreadful man to argue with.”

“I have been told.”

“By everyone?”

“Most people with the opportunity.”

She laughed, and remained where she was.

They were allowed to see their father at a house on the transfer road.

The permission named them. Other family arrangements had been made separately; Taliya’s letters and the messages from the younger household were already with the officer in charge. Ouen had brought Hew’s drawing and a small packet from Maegwen. He had been told that both would be examined.

The guard looked through the packet in front of him. It contained linen, a little book, and a note asking after Valdais’s health. The man unfolded the linen too. Ouen began to object, then felt Vaelrie’s fingers touch his sleeve.

“Let him finish.”

“I can see what he’s doing.”

“So can I.”

The guard refolded it carefully. He returned nothing except the outer wrapping and led them into the room.

Valdais rose. He had been sitting near the window with a cup beside him. The room was comfortable enough to make Ouen briefly ashamed of imagining a cell, then angry at the comfort for allowing the thought.

Vaelrie went to her father first. He held her with both hands against her back and closed his eyes. When she drew away he touched her cheek, studying her face as if she had been absent much longer than she had.

“Are they feeding you?”

“Constantly.”

“Then I approve of that part.”

He embraced Ouen next. The guard remained at the door, far enough away to grant the appearance of privacy and near enough to hear.

They spoke first of the household because none of them could begin elsewhere.

Ouen gave the account of the wounded men. His father asked about Joss’s mare and the grey which had returned with Renaud. He objected when told the grey would need a longer rest than the groom first expected.

“You always rested him too much.”

“He is lame.”

“He was sound when he left.”

“He has been ridden since.”

Vaelrie looked between them.

“You have half an hour and are spending it on a horse neither of you can ride.”

Valdais laughed. Ouen heard how easy it still was for his sister to make him do that.

He produced Hew’s drawing. The bird was recognisable chiefly because it had claws. Beside it stood a man much larger than the house.

“Me?” Valdais asked.

“He says so.”

“An excellent likeness.”

“The horse is on the back.”

Valdais turned the paper over. He smiled for longer than the drawing required, then laid it beside the cup.

Maegwen’s note was read aloud. He asked what Taliya had written to them and whether Braymond and Cullan understood the arrangement. Ouen had no answer for the understanding; he could only repeat what the last letter said. Valdais listened without correcting him.

At length Vaelrie asked where he would be taken.

“A house you have not seen.”

“May we come?”

“Not whenever we please. We must ask.”

“Will they allow it?”

He looked towards the guard, then back to her.

“They have allowed this.”

She lowered her eyes. Ouen wanted to promise that he would obtain another visit. He caught the promise before it left his mouth.

“I will ask,” he said.

His father nodded.

Valdais had instructions about the household which he had not been permitted to put in his private letters without having them entered as part of the transfer of government.

He delivered them now, with the guard listening. Ouen was to retain the officers who knew their work unless he had a reason to replace them. He was to hear tenants without letting every complaint become a quarrel between servants. The accounts left unfinished by the war were to be heard in the order agreed, not in the order in which the claimants flattered him.

“I don’t require this explained,” Ouen said.

“Then remember it.”

Vaelrie looked at the cup. Ouen could see she was afraid they would waste the visit arguing. He swallowed the next answer.

His father reached for her hand.

“And you?”

“I am going back to Valora.”

“To Jaennis.”

“Yes.”

He studied her. She met his gaze.

“She has been kind to you.”

“She loves me.”

The certainty made Ouen uncomfortable. Valdais squeezed her hand.

“Then let her continue.”

The guard stepped forward to tell them the time. Ouen stood too quickly, knocking his chair against the wall. He set it straight while Vaelrie embraced their father again.

When his turn came, he could think of nothing to say which would survive the hearing of a stranger. Valdais held his shoulder as he had at Bethencourt.

“Write about the boy.”

“I will.”

“And the bird.”

“Yes.”

They went out together. Vaelrie stopped in the passage and put one hand flat against the wall. Ouen waited until she could go on.

On the road home she rode beside him instead of in the small carriage provided for the longer journey.

Neither spoke for some time. At a turning, the escort went ahead and left them a little space. Ouen looked at her profile, so like their father’s when she was trying not to show annoyance or grief.

“How often will you write?”

“As often as I can.”

“That is what everyone says.”

“Then answer when I do.”

He nodded.

She reached across between the horses. He took her hand carefully, keeping the reins in the other, and they rode that way until the track narrowed.

Chapter 36 — The Next Instruction

The first request which reached Cedric under his new authority concerned a chest he had helped carry upstairs.

Aude wanted it returned from Ysabeau’s quarters. It contained her sister’s daughter’s clothes and the coverlet she had laid on the bed. The officer making the household inventory had included it with the dead commander’s effects because no one present could say which things belonged to whom.

There was also a claim for the groom’s unpaid wages. Rovel had acknowledged the service but could not release money from a household whose property was being held pending the settlement of claims. The groom had written his name beneath the request with letters so heavily pressed that one stroke had torn the paper.

Cedric put the two requests side by side.

His new charge covered the office’s river correspondence and the men sent to collect or verify it. He could commission journeys within the allowed sum, require an account from agents returning, and send routine answers without bringing every word to Fayd. He could not settle title to a dead woman’s property merely because the request had reached his desk.

The younger clerk suggested forwarding both matters to the same officer who had held them.

“He’ll answer that he lacks authority,” Cedric said.

“Then he should ask for it.”

“He has. Here.”

He pointed to the attached note. The clerk had read the petition and missed the officer’s request beneath it.

They found the household authority named in the accepted settlement. Cedric required the inventory keeper to separate property claimed by living visitors from Ysabeau’s own effects and take a witnessed description before releasing it. The wage claim went to the officer receiving Valdais’s household obligations, with Rovel’s acknowledgement attached and a date for an answer.

A reply concerning Dervain’s claim had arrived with the household papers. The Fairport store owner had obtained a brief delay, sufficient to have the secured materials inspected. No general protection against recovery had been granted. A note from the receiving office said the merchants were trying to open one quay before the waiting carriers went elsewhere.

Cedric had the warning copied to the men arranging commercial passage. He could name where the roof timber had come from and who claimed it. He could not make the debt vanish by putting the papers in Fayd’s room.

The clerk asked whether Aude’s description of the chest would be sufficient.

“It has a broken fastening on the left. The coverlet is folded with the dark edge out.”

The clerk looked up.

“I saw it there,” Cedric said.

He entered the details as his own testimony, signed, and gave the papers back.

Fayd sent him to hear a returning agent before the man was paid.

The agent claimed that a river house had withheld its promised escort. His report recommended suspending the protection of its carriers until it complied. Cedric asked what the house had said when challenged.

“The captain was away.”

“Who answered for him?”

“His steward.”

“And what did he say?”

“That the men were already on the road.”

“Were they?”

“I didn’t see them.”

Cedric looked at the route. The escort had been directed to a different landing in a correction issued after the agent departed. A copy of that correction lay among papers awaiting delivery. No one had marked whether the house had received it.

He sent a rider to the original landing before sending the suspension. The agent objected that his own word was being doubted.

“Your report says you saw no men,” Cedric replied. “I believe you.”

“Then why wait?”

“Because we may have sent them somewhere else.”

The answer came the next day. The escort had waited at the first landing, then returned home after the expected party failed to appear. The house wanted payment for the wasted days.

Cedric rejected the proposed suspension and authorised the payment for the agent’s completed journey. He struck out the additional allowance for a second visit made necessary by the office’s own failure, requiring it to be claimed separately. The agent left angry. Fayd read the decision and told Cedric to have the delivery of corrections entered with the original instructions from now on.

No one congratulated him. By evening three more reports required answers.

Edran found him there two days later.

He stood in the doorway until Cedric rose. They had not seen each other since Harwick. Edran looked older in a way Cedric disliked, as though time had been permitted to make changes at home without asking him.

“I was told this was your room.”

“It is.”

“And these men?”

“They bring their accounts to me.”

Edran looked at the stools, the brazier, the papers. Cedric had imagined this visit often enough to recognise how little of it resembled the imagined one.

“Sit down.”

“I can’t stay long.”

“Father?”

“Much the same. He asked me to find you.”

Cedric reached for the jug. His hand no longer hurt, but the movement made him remember the marks which had faded from his wrist.

“He received my letter.”

“About Dame Ysabeau’s offer. Yes.”

“I have written since.”

“He received that too.”

Edran sat without taking the cup.

“He wants to know whether your present employment is secure.”

“As secure as any office.”

“And the restitution?”

“Continues. I have not asked it to be changed.”

“Good.”

Cedric set the jug down. He had hoped to give that answer as evidence of responsibility. His brother received it as the least that could be expected.

Edran looked at the papers waiting beneath a stone.

“You left her service when she died.”

“Yes.”

“Quickly.”

“I had work here.”

There was a long silence.

“Father had begun a letter thanking her,” Edran said. “For finding you a place. He hadn’t sent it when the news came.”

Cedric stared at the cup.

“He should not send it now.”

“No, Cedric.”

His brother stood. Cedric rose with him, irritated and relieved at once.

“Is that all you came to say?”
