“I came to see whether you were well and whether what you had written was true.”

“And?”

“You have a room. Men bring you papers. I will tell him that.”

At the door he stopped.

“Don’t make him thank Master Fayd for what he doesn’t understand.”

Cedric could have asked what Edran believed he understood himself. The question would have kept him in the room, and Cedric suddenly wanted him gone. He said he would write to their father.

Edran nodded and left.

Veralyn received the river-house proposals after returning to Valora.

Cedric attended with Fayd because he knew the agreements and would have to arrange the journeys which followed. He stood near the end of the table while Fayd named the first petitioners. Tylan was present, with a packet of military estimates he had not yet put down. Jaennis sat by the window, listening while a servant pinned a loose fastening on her sleeve.

The queen asked which matters required her own judgement and which could be heard by an officer. Fayd wanted her to receive all three houses in person.

“They have agreed to bring their disputes here because they expect to be heard by you.”

“What have you promised?”

“An audience to present them. No favourable judgement.”

She took the nearest petition and read the opening.

“This one concerns a boundary.”

“And passage through it.”

“Then the people using the passage must be heard as well. Send for their representatives before fixing the day.”

Cedric noted the direction. Fayd agreed. The second house would be received as proposed. The third was to furnish a copy of an older grant before anyone travelled.

Jaennis asked whether the second household intended to bring its entire following into the palace. Fayd supplied the number. She reduced the guest arrangements to the principals and their necessary attendants, saying the rest could be lodged in the town without insulting anybody whose principal purpose was business.

Veralyn smiled at her.

“Whom do you mean to make room for instead?”

“People who will enjoy the room.”

The queen laughed. For a moment Cedric saw the household he had heard of from outside, warm with intimacies no office could supply. Then Tylan laid the military estimates before her and she turned to them.

Before the military estimates were opened, the queen had the two petitioners from the Mound brought in.

Cedric knew the name from a note concerning rooms and access. He had expected masked people. The man entered carrying his hat; the woman wore an ordinary sword. Tylan confirmed that her former captain had answered for her service.

Veralyn addressed the man.

“You called me Gelfrey’s heir. What do you expect to inherit with me?”

“A hearing, Your Grace. He was a face men could come to when they could not safely bring a charge before their lord. That work remains.”

“And you would judge for me.”

“We would bring you what the ordinary courts cannot reach. Men of the Mound can hear a name without proclaiming it in the market. Some can go where a royal badge would shut every door.”

Fayd stood motionless beside Cedric. This was a proposal for work he might otherwise have expected to receive himself.

“Your courts also sentence,” Veralyn said.

“They do.”

“And send someone else to carry it out.”

The woman answered before her companion could.

“Everyone who assents carries the stone afterwards.”

“What stone?”

“From the field where the dead lie. We put them beside the hearing place. A hand raised in a room is part of the killing. Myrmis does not release it because another hand held the knife.”

Cedric moved his sore wrist against his sleeve, though it had ceased to hurt weeks before. The woman had not looked at him.

“The Bloody Table has used necessity to excuse a great deal,” Tylan said.

“They are an offshoot with their own assemblies,” the man replied. “They do not send us. Nor does the wreck of the uprising give any survivor the right to command every hearing.”

Veralyn sat back.

“You may stand in my guard. Four to begin, with names given to the captain and duties learned before you take a watch. Those who work outside the household report to me through a person I name. You will not give a sentence in my service and let me hear of it only after the death.”

“And when you give one?” the woman asked.

“I will have given it.”

There was an interval in which Cedric heard a chair move in the room beyond. The woman’s gaze rested on the queen’s face. At last she bowed.

The man asked whether their petitioners would have a way to reach her without passing through the local lord accused. Veralyn directed Pellam to arrange it and required the Mound to distinguish a charge brought for hearing from an immediate threat against her household.

Fayd asked to receive accounts of threats affecting his work.

“Where they concern it,” she said. “These people are in my service.”

He acknowledged the distinction. The woman gave her name to the captain waiting at the door and went with him to learn the watches. Her companion remained long enough to receive the first direction: find a petitioner turned away from his own lord’s hearing, establish who else had witnessed the taking he alleged, and bring the matter back quietly. No sentence had been given.

When he left, the queen asked Tylan for his estimates.

The first proposal for the academy was three pages long.

Veralyn read the names of possible instructors and asked which had actually taught anyone. Tylan removed one name with his finger.

“He can do the work. I am less certain he can bear watching someone learn it.”

“Then he can inspect the work after they have learned.”

The pay proposed for the standing companies exceeded what most lords offered their ordinary household men. Tylan had put the cost of prompt payment beside the nominal rate, including the reserve needed when collections were delayed. Veralyn spent longer over that figure than the rate itself.

“If I promise it, they must receive it.”

“Yes.”

“What do we begin with?”

“A small establishment. We must find the money which can be promised twice, not only once after a victory.”

She looked towards Fayd. He spoke of revenues already owed and of the information still needed about the river houses. He did not describe to her the conversation Cedric remembered about making those houses unable to maintain their independence.

“Bring me the estimates separately,” she said. “I want to know which income is mine to use before someone offers it to me.”

Fayd bowed his head.

Jaennis had finished with the fastening. She came to the table, looked at the proposed room requirements, and asked who had decided an academy could fit into a house already occupied. Tylan said it was a first inquiry. She said she would make another before they displaced anyone.

Veralyn listened, one hand resting beside the hilt at her chair. When Tylan described how long it might take to build a force with common training and dependable service, she nodded at the years as if accepting the length of a journey already chosen.

“We begin with what we can keep,” she said.

Pellam had brought Lyra to the audience to take the directions concerning the river petitions. When the queen had gone, he asked her to remain with Fayd and Cedric while the first journeys were assigned.

She placed the three petitions beside the notes she had made. The first house, whose boundary petition awaited the carriers’ representatives, had named its lord and his officers. It had not named anyone speaking for the carriers affected by its demand.

“They can be added when the household arrives,” Fayd said.

“The queen asked that they be sent for before the day is fixed.”

“The lord can identify them.”

“He can identify the ones he wants heard.”

Fayd glanced towards Pellam. The chancellor was reading the older grant requested from the third house. He looked up when Lyra set down her pen.

“I would like to keep these returns,” she said. “The promise, the person who must perform it, and the answer when it comes back. We know what was offered. We don’t always know who has been left waiting.”

“Cedric has the river correspondence,” Fayd said.

“Yes. I would require his answers as well.”

Cedric shifted beside the table. She had spoken without looking at him, as though he were an ordinary officer who could be asked for something and expected to supply it.

Pellam considered the petitions.

“Here, in the chancery?”

“Yes.”

“You would need access to the instruments and their corrections. You would refer disputes to me. You could require missing particulars; you could not alter an undertaking because you thought it a poor one.”

“I understand.”

Fayd drew the first petition towards himself.

“It would be quicker to have someone in my office do this. She would receive the reports as they arrived.”

“And the petitions from people who say your reports are wrong?” Lyra asked.

“They could be forwarded.”

“I would rather they came here.”

The words were quiet. Cedric watched Fayd’s hand rest on the paper. He had seen men grow angry at smaller refusals and knew better than to expect it of him.

“You will make us write more letters,” Fayd said.

“If the first ones have the answers, I won’t need to.”

Pellam took up his pen. He assigned the work within the chancery, subject to his review, and named the officers from whom she could require returns. Fayd’s office was among them. Lyra asked who would receive those answers during her absence for the western hearing. Pellam named the clerk who already held the copies of her testimony and called him in to hear the arrangement.

Then she returned to the first petition.

“The carriers use this road,” Lyra said, putting her finger on the disputed boundary in the petition. “They can be reached through the receiving house used for Verren’s dependants. Its keeper knows which people were promised passage and which actually came. May I ask the carriers to name representatives themselves?”

Pellam agreed. Fayd supplied the current receiving officer’s name, correcting the one Cedric had written in his notes. Lyra entered the correction and asked when a reply could reasonably arrive.

They settled a date after allowing for the journey. Until then, the audience remained provisionally accepted rather than booked. Cedric would send the inquiry; the reply would come directly to the chancery, with a copy to Fayd.

When they left, Fayd asked Cedric to have it dispatched that afternoon.

“You might have told her we already intended to ask,” Cedric said in the passage.

“Did we?”

Cedric did not answer. Behind them Lyra had called the copying clerk back to show him where the return was to be kept. Cedric went to find the man who would carry her first inquiry.

The Ecktorian visitor arrived while the first river inquiries were being dispatched.

Fayd received him in the office after the clerks had gone. Cedric remained to record the agreement. There were two other visitors: a woman authorised by western Viterran correspondents, and a northern man who objected to the word authorised because his people preferred to answer each request separately.

“Then they have sent you to listen,” Fayd said. “Listen.”

The Ecktorian set a letter from his sponsors on the table. He served a circle within the Unburnt, he said, and could commit the men whose names were enclosed. He could not commit every household in the Amber Palace, still less the Censer-Knights or the Red Vigil.

The western woman asked what his sponsors expected from a queen who could not protect their houses across the sea.

“A future which does not end at the palace gates,” he said. “We have been protecting the same rooms while the country outside them is sold in pieces.”

“You would prefer ours?”

“I would prefer someone able to govern more than a room.”

He drew a smaller sheet from the letter and put it beside his sponsors’ names.

“These men are owed eleven weeks’ pay.”

The western woman looked at Fayd.

“You have brought a demand for money?” he asked.

“I have brought the men you asked whether we could furnish. That is what furnishing them requires.”

Cedric reached for the sheet. Six names, with the service beside each. Two guarded a gate through which provisions entered the Amber Palace. Another accompanied the carts from the warehouses.

“They protect flour?” Cedric asked.

“They protect the people permitted to eat it. At the gate you can stand beneath an emperor carved in marble and listen to a cook refusing a sack with damp in it. Outside, there are people who would scrape the mould off and eat it. We keep them outside.”

The northern man shifted his chair nearer the brazier.

“And the households cannot pay?”

“They paid for the wine delivered through the same gate. My men saw it come.”

“Who has kept them there?” Fayd asked.

“I have. From rents which my brother expected for repairs to our house. He has sent me the builder’s demand.”

“You could have let the guards leave.”

“And replaced them with whose men? The next house would have been pleased to lend us guards. It would want to know who entered by the gate.”

Fayd read the smaller sheet.

“I can pay for work from the day we employ them. Their earlier service belongs to your sponsors.”

The Ecktorian tapped the two gatekeepers’ names.

“If I offer them another master while leaving that money unpaid, they will ask what my word was worth the first time.”

“Then settle it before you offer mine.”

For a while the visitor looked at the sheet. He asked Cedric for a pen, struck out the gatekeepers and the man with the carts, and put his initials beside the alteration.

“Three can begin. The others when I have an answer from home.”

Cedric changed the number on his own copy. The envoy put the amended list beside his sponsors’ letter.

Fayd laid out the immediate undertaking. Shared reports where their work crossed, no competing recruitment of the same man, and no incitement against Veralyn by people claiming to act for their common restoration. The northern correspondent objected that some of his associates had taken oaths to houses which had fought her.

“Then decide which undertaking you mean to keep,” Fayd said. “You cannot give me information to protect the queen and pay another man to unseat her.”

“You once found resistance useful.”

“I did. It broke loyalties I wanted broken. Now we have someone who can gather what comes after. Continuing to break it would defeat the work.”

The Ecktorian asked whether Fayd meant a greater Viterran kingdom.

“I mean an empire,” Fayd said. “Utara restored under a living sovereign. Veralyn has the force of will. She must acquire the institutions and the allegiance. I intend to help her do it.”

“Does she intend it?”

“She intends an army she can pay and officers she can trust. She is hearing petitions from houses which once would not have brought them to her. Ask what those things may become in twenty years.”

The northern man had stopped turning the cord of his cloak around his finger.

“An empress.”

“That is the future I am working towards.”

They argued over who could direct their people. Fayd did not obtain a general surrender of the foreign circle’s affairs. He obtained one named channel for work touching his office, and its agreement to refuse competing instructions in that work. The western representative accepted the same terms. The northern man struck out the names of two correspondents he could not bind and signed for the others.

Cedric copied the exclusions as carefully as the promises. The undertaking joined less than the whole Vigil, but the three parties would now have to answer one another when their work conflicted. He made three copies for them and one for Fayd.

At the door the Ecktorian asked when the queen would receive his sponsors’ first petition.

“When it is ready to be put before her,” Fayd said. “She has not signed this.”

The visitor accepted the answer with less satisfaction than he had shown at the word empire. Cedric put the office copy away from the instruments bearing the queen’s seal.

The answer about Aude’s chest reached Cedric the following morning. Its release had been authorised. The groom’s wages were accepted as a claim and would be paid at the next household disbursement, with the place and receiving officer named.

Cedric read the answer and sent it on without adding a personal line.

Then he opened the first account of Verren’s borrowing. A lender had provided money against three years of market income. Another held a claim on rents from two holdings used to maintain the lord’s mounted men. Neither would welcome a new protected market nearby.

Cedric drew the plan towards him and marked the road by which carriers could reach the alternative place. He knew the ground. At one turning a wagon could pass only if the fence were moved, and he wrote down whose permission would be required.

Chapter 37 — Before Going Out

Dame Barenne put her thumb through the damaged cloth and held it up to the light.

Ouen had expected her to be pleased that the bales existed. He had kept them separate through the winter, resisted using them when his own stores ran low, and had them moved twice to make room for wounded men. Four bales with the proper mark stood before her. The mark was visible. The thread beneath her thumb was not.

“That was on the bottom,” Halden said.

“I can see where it was.”

She drew the cloth farther out. The damp had reached only a narrow strip, but it ran across several folds. Ouen looked towards the roof. The store was dry now.

“It came to us damaged.”

“How much?”

He could not answer. Halden fetched the first description. It recorded torn wrapping and a stained edge, with the mark copied beside it. Dame Barenne read it and inspected the cloth again.

“Some of this may have happened before,” she said.

Ouen waited for the rest.

“Some may not.”

Her nephew had brought a measuring rod. They laid the cloth over a clean board and established how far the damage reached. Ouen stood with them instead of returning to the house. He had signed the guarantee and now there was a length of spoiled cloth where the words about safe keeping had seemed sufficient.

At the end Dame Barenne named the loss she would claim. It was less than he had feared and more than he wished to pay.

“I will have it heard with the others,” he said.

“You can. Or you can agree the damaged length now and leave the question of where it happened to the men who moved it.”

Halden looked at him. Ouen thought of the separate claims running back through the seizure at Fairport, the river crossing, and the officers who had sent the goods north. He would not reach the first wet floor by refusing to acknowledge the one before him.

“Enter the length,” he said. “We will pay that amount and keep the claim against whoever is found responsible.”

Dame Barenne nodded. She had the damaged portion cut away before the bale was wrapped again.

The two disputed packages remained.

One contained cloth whose mark had been partly cut off. The other had been wrapped in someone else’s sacking. Dame Barenne thought the first might belong to a neighbouring warehouse and refused to say the second was hers merely because no one could prove otherwise.

“I would have to sell what is inside,” she said. “Then the owner would hear of it before I did.”

Ouen almost smiled. She was as suspicious of a gift as he had been of her claim.

The four identified bales went onto her hired wagon. The nephew checked the numbers against Halden’s list and climbed up beside the driver. They were bound for a quay newly opened to limited commercial loading, under an arrangement which had taken more letters than Ouen had thought a quay could require.

Constano’s name appeared on two of those letters. Matteo Tireno had obtained men and a store which could be used while the larger premises remained damaged. A roof had been repaired; another still needed timber. Carriers would be admitted on named days, with loads declared before they arrived. The first departure would carry very little compared with the traffic which had once passed there.

Dame Barenne had come because very little was enough to begin selling again.

“Will you return to Fairport?” Ouen asked.

“For part of the year.”

“And the warehouse?”

“The wall stands. A wall is useful if one can persuade someone to roof it.”

She put on her gloves. One had been repaired across the palm with leather of a different colour.

“Your guarantee has been performed, my lord.”

“Except the damage.”

“That is agreed. It need not be invisible to be agreed.”

She offered her hand. Ouen took it, then stood aside while she mounted the step beside her nephew.

They had not passed the gate when a rider brought the warning from Fairport.

The store’s roof was under a claim. Loading beneath it had been suspended while officers determined what could be removed without damaging the building. The first departure might be lost.

Dame Barenne read the warning from her seat on the wagon.

“The buyers won’t wait.”

“They might,” Ouen said.

“They have a choice of sellers.”

Halden asked for the instrument named in the warning. A copy was folded behind it, bearing a description of the timbers and fittings supplied on credit. Ouen looked from it to the loaded bales. He had spent the morning making certain they could leave. Now they could leave and arrive nowhere useful.

“Can the goods be kept elsewhere?”

“Until the carrier departs without them,” she said. “I have already paid to send them.”

Ouen gave the warning back. He wanted to tell her his guarantee ended here. She had said so herself. The words would be correct, and the wagon would still be standing in his gate.

“Wait until morning,” he said. “I will go with you.”

Fairport smelled of wet timber.

Ouen had expected more of the damage to have disappeared. From the river he saw walls which stood without anything behind them and a stair ending in open air. Men worked beside these absences as if the missing rooms were merely occupied by tenants they did not wish to disturb.

At the quay, a builder had set a ladder against the disputed store. Two men waited beside it with tools. The local officer had forbidden them to touch the roof until its fastenings had been inspected. He had also forbidden goods to be put under it, since he could not then allow men to work above them.

Matteo Tireno met Ouen at the landing. He had helped hear the claim to Barenne’s cloth when it was still possible for Ouen to hope the bales belonged to someone else. His coat had white dust on one sleeve.

“I heard you were bringing the cloth.”

“I had intended to finish bringing it.”

“So had the carrier.”

They walked to the store. Dame Barenne’s nephew followed, carrying the papers which established what the wagon held. The officer looked through them and said there was no dispute about the cloth.

“Then let it be loaded,” Ouen said.

“From where, my lord?”

He pointed out the cleared space required beneath the ladder, the only covered passage from the yard to the water. Rain had begun while they crossed. Men were already pulling covers over loads which had been brought out in expectation of sailing.

Dervain’s agent stood inside with the store owner. The owner had spent the morning explaining that the first loading would produce money. The agent had heard the explanation before. He held a copy of the obligation with the materials named in it.

“The roof stands as security until payment,” he said. “The time has passed.”

“You could wait for this departure,” Matteo said.

“I could. Then another claimant could seize the earnings before they reached me.”

“Has no one put this to the queen’s people?” Ouen asked.

The agent opened the leather cover protecting his papers and took out another sheet.

“My lord’s steward put it to Master Fayd. We asked for the advances to be secured when we agreed to passage through our lands. He would undertake nothing on the queen’s behalf.”

He held the reply for Ouen to read. The refusal was brief. The signatures below it occupied nearly as much space as the answer.

“Then you refused him.”

“We could not agree.”

“And now you take down a roof.”

“The day for payment was fixed before either man came to the meeting.”

Ouen looked at Matteo, who nodded reluctantly.

“Could you send to him again?” Ouen asked.

“To Master Fayd? Certainly. Shall I tell these men to wait for the answer?”

Through the doorway Ouen could see one of the carpenters holding his coat over the tool basket. Water ran from its hem. The agent waited with the refusal still held out. Ouen gave it back.

“Show me what Constano proposed.”

Constano’s letter had proposed that the first freight money be held for the rebuilding obligations. The arrangement lacked the agreement of two claimants, one of whom had paid the workmen when the owner could not. Dervain would not take a place behind him without knowing how much that man would demand.

Ouen listened while the same proposed payments went round the room. He had arrived expecting to give an order which would make a man ashamed of obstructing the peace. No one here appeared to think peace altered what he was owed.

Outside, a carpenter tested the lower end of a beam with the point of his knife.

“Can they take it down?” Ouen asked.

“They can take anything down. Putting it up again is the trouble.”

“If it stays until the ship leaves?”

“Then we’ll lose the two men hired for the work. They’ve another roof after this.”

“Let them go.”

The carpenter looked at him.

“They’ll expect paying for coming.”

Ouen glanced towards Matteo. He saw the answer before the man spoke. Every delay required someone who had already worked without assurance of payment to do so again.

Dame Barenne came out of the store.

“I can move the cloth to a loft away from the quay. It will cost, and I’ll lose the sailing.”

“How long until another?”

“No one will promise while this is happening.”

Her nephew stood behind her with the list getting wet in his hand. She took it from him and folded it beneath her coat.

Ouen asked Matteo how much would keep the roof in place through the loading and satisfy the workmen who had come to remove it. The sum was smaller than the whole claim. It was large enough that Ouen knew immediately where he would have to find it.

Halden had sent the household’s available funds with him in a careful account. Some were already spoken for. He had read those promises on the journey and been irritated to find so little money without someone’s name beside it.

The largest unspent amount would buy the long timbers for the roofs at Martin’s holdings. Two families were still sleeping in the lower room of a neighbour’s house. Martin had been told that their work would begin when the winter traffic cleared.

“It is an advance,” Ouen said.

“If it is repaid,” Dame Barenne answered.

She did not offer to contribute her compensation for the damaged cloth. He had almost asked and now disliked himself for having considered it.

He required Dervain’s agent to name the sum which would suspend recovery through the first departure and the next ten days. Matteo required a corresponding direction to the officer: loading could resume, and no worker would begin removing anything while the suspension held. The remaining claim stayed open. The store owner would have to make his settlement with both creditors; Ouen was not guaranteeing whatever he might owe after this.

The agent agreed to the payment after counting it twice.

Ouen signed the advance. He had it charged to the household money waiting for the roof timbers. When Matteo asked where repayment should be sent, he gave Halden’s name and said which work was waiting on it.

The officer read the suspension aloud to the builder. The builder wanted his men paid before they put their tools away. Ouen waited while that was done.

Only then did the ladder come down.

They loaded in the rain.

Dame Barenne stood inside the store counting her bales as they passed. Other loads followed. A man whose boat had waited all morning argued about the order until Matteo brought him the amended departure list and showed him his place. There was no cheer when the first boat moved away. Men simply carried the next load into the space it had left.

Ouen watched it from beneath the newly secure roof. He had not bought the quay or freed its trade from future argument. Dervain’s claim remained, and another payment would be needed before the suspension ended. But the cloth was aboard, the workmen had their money, and Dame Barenne could send word to buyers who had expected her to fail again.

She thanked him before he left. He found he did not enjoy it as much as he had expected.

Martin came to the house the morning after Ouen returned.

Halden had already told him about the timbers. Ouen had written from Fairport rather than let the men discover the delay when their order failed to arrive. Martin brought the promise Ouen had signed earlier and laid it beside the new message.

“Which one do I show them?”

“Both.”

“They can’t sleep under either.”

“I know.”

“The smaller child has been coughing. They keep the fire up for him and the rest of the room can’t sleep. His mother won’t put it out.”

Ouen looked towards the window. He had returned to a house with every roof sound. The thought made the room less comfortable without suggesting how he could replace the money.

“Is there another room on the holding?”

“If there were, we’d be using it.”

“We can take one family here.”

“And the other stays. Which?”

Martin waited. He had not raised his voice. Ouen almost wished he would.

“The child who is ill comes here. His family with him. Iselde must be told before they arrive. We’ll keep them apart from the wounded.”

“That leaves their animals.”

“I will pay a man to see to them.”

“From the timber money?”

Ouen looked up sharply. Martin met his eyes, then lowered his head just enough to acknowledge the rank without withdrawing the question.

“From what remains for this month’s household,” Ouen said. “Halden will show you the sum. I cannot promise the roof until the advance comes back or we find another way to pay for it.”

“So there is no day.”

“No.”

Martin took both papers. Ouen let him leave without asking him to say he understood.

His father’s first letter was waiting when he returned from Fairport.

It came in an outer cover bearing the mark of the officer responsible for the house. The original inside had been examined. A separate note stated that a copy had been retained. Ouen resented the note before opening the letter, though it told him nothing the settlement had not already required.

Valdais complained about the position of a window and asked whether Hew still believed the wooden bird would fly if thrown from a greater height. He had kept the drawing. He thought the horse on the reverse better observed than the man on the front, which suggested that the child might yet have judgement.

Ouen read that part aloud at supper. Hew wanted to know whether his grandfather could send the drawing back so he could improve the man.

“He wants to keep it.”

“I can do another.”

“Then do another.”

“Now?”

“After you eat.”

Iselde held out the bread before the child could begin bargaining. Ouen continued reading the parts about the house: the room in which his father ate, the walk permitted within the boundary, the cook who understood that a piece of meat did not improve merely because it had been cut small.

He did not read the line beneath them.

I had expected to see what sort of man Hew becomes. I must depend on what you remember to tell me.

He had put a finger over it when the boy leaned towards the paper. Now he folded the letter and asked Hew which part of the day should be sent to his grandfather.

The child considered. He had seen a dog carrying a piece of bread so large it had to walk with its head sideways. Ouen said that would do.

Vaelrie’s letter came two days later, with a drawing of the dog added in response to the message Iselde had sent ahead.

She was back in Valora. Maelis had agreed to remain with the company, though she had insisted on time for work of her own and on payment for entertainments arranged as duties. Joan had obtained a quieter corner for the cricket’s box and then discovered she disliked the silence when it was moved away.

The first evening after Vaelrie’s return had ended late. Jaennis had asked whether she wanted to speak about the visit. Vaelrie had said no, then begun speaking while Jaennis was taking the pins from her hair. She did not give Ouen the conversation. She told him only that she had been allowed to change her mind.

There was more about Catrin’s song. Vaelrie had learned the other ending and still preferred hers, though she now admitted the tune sat more comfortably beneath Catrin’s version. Margery had heard her practising and accused her of choosing the singer over the song. Vaelrie wrote that Margery was insufferable and should be made to mind her own affairs.

Ouen could hear the pleasure in the complaint.

Later in the letter she described Maelis laying out the knotted silk beside the lamp, recounting an afternoon they had all experienced and remembering the part each listener wished she would omit. They had argued over whether Guiran had bowed before or after discovering that the wrong strings were tuned. Maelis had made the knot again while they argued. Nothing in the account suggested that the disagreement would ever be settled.

I meant to answer you before supper, Vaelrie concluded. Then there was supper. You may laugh, but answer me anyway.

He did laugh. Iselde asked what had pleased him, and he read the last lines to her.

The household had begun to make demands unrelated to the army.

A tenant wanted timber for a roof which had been failing before the war. A servant asked permission to marry a man from another holding. Two brothers disputed which of them had been promised a field by their father. Ouen heard the first, referred the third to the proper officer, and asked the servant whether the man knew she had already begun making arrangements.

“He asked me first, my lord.”

“That was prudent of him.”

She smiled. He gave the permission and remembered to ask when they intended to marry. She told him at length. He listened until Halden shifted a paper on the table, signalling that another claimant waited.

Afterwards Ouen went upstairs to answer his father.

He wrote about the grey’s recovery and the four bales returned. He began to explain the disputed damage, decided his father would tell him what he should have done, and left the explanation in anyway. Then he wrote about the dog, the new drawing, and the way Hew now insisted on examining the hawk’s food before anyone brought it into the mews.

At the line about the drawing he stopped. He could picture his father looking at a thing made in this room and keeping it because he could not come to the room himself.

Ouen turned the sheet and wrote more about the boy.

Halden found him putting on his glove.

“The lower holding, my lord. The bank by the water has gone.”

“Again?”

“The temporary repair held through the winter. They need timber now.”

Ouen had been planning a short flight before the wind changed. Hew was waiting in the yard with the groom, wearing a cap which would not stay straight. From the window he could see the child trying to balance the wooden hawk on one finger.

“How much timber?”

Halden gave the amount. The short piles already in the store would serve; the roofs needed long, sound beams they had yet to buy. Ouen thought the number of piles excessive and said so. The old man explained which part was needed to keep the remaining bank from following the breach. Water would reach the lower field if they waited for the next ordinary collection of repairs.

“Who has seen it?”

“The carpenter and Martin.”

“Do they agree?”

“On what must be done. Not on whose men should carry it.”

“Give them the timber. Use the household wagon for the first load. Tell Martin his men must bring the second.”

“The wagon is wanted for the kitchen.”

“The kitchen can have it afterwards.”

Halden began to turn away.

“Wait. How long afterwards?”

They settled that too. Ouen removed the glove to sign, then put it on again. Below, Hew had dropped the bird and was examining its blue wing with great concern.

Ouen left the letter open for the child’s drawing and went down to him.

Chapter 38 — The King’s Displeasure

The first letter they returned to him had no marks on it.

Valdais had expected a line through the offending words, perhaps a note in the margin explaining what a man of his experience was not permitted to ask his son. Instead Master Talbot laid the sheet on the table and placed a second, smaller paper beside it.

“The third paragraph, Your Grace.”

“Read it.”

The clerk did. It concerned the horses still held by two households which had not completed their service before the settlement. Valdais had asked Ouen to establish their condition and keep them from being sold until he had reported back.

“They are mine.”

“The direction concerns property under your son’s government.”

“It concerns horses.”

“Which could be retained for service.”

“So could the animal carrying your next letter.”

Talbot inclined his head. He was a man of about fifty, with a narrow face and hands kept clean despite the ink he used. Valdais had found him neither stupid nor easily provoked, two qualities which made the mornings longer.

“You may ask after the animals,” the clerk said. “You may not direct that they be held awaiting your order.”

“I have given no order.”

“You have asked your son to do a thing he would understand as one.”

Valdais looked towards the window. Beyond it lay a strip of garden, a wall, and trees whose upper branches moved in the wind. He could see enough sky to know it would have been a fine riding day.

“Strike out the sentence, then.”

“We ask you to write the passage again. There will be no uncertainty about which words you intended to send.”

“And a copy of the first?”

“Retained with the reason it was returned.”

“Of course.”

Talbot waited. Valdais took a clean sheet.

There were two clerks.

The younger received the completed letters, copied them, and entered the day of dispatch. Talbot read the originals against the copies. A passage they considered doubtful was put before the officer responsible for the house, and the letter waited until the question had been settled. The same care attended incoming correspondence. Broken seals were enclosed in the outer cover so nobody needed to pretend the letter had arrived untouched.

Valdais found the courtesy more irritating than carelessness would have been. Carelessness offered a man work. He could discover its shape, ask a question through it, find someone who disliked a colleague enough to make an exception. Here the work had been arranged by people who expected him to look for exceptions.

He could ask for review. He did, once, over a letter to Maegwen in which he had described an old quarrel with a neighbour. The passage was eventually allowed after he explained that the neighbour had been dead for thirteen years and the quarrel concerned a dog.

The permission gave him less pleasure than he had anticipated. Maegwen would read a recollection he had made tedious by having to defend its harmlessness before strangers.

He sent it anyway. She had loved the dog.

The house was smaller than he remembered from the account of his mother’s confinement.

He had pictured it over the years as he pictured other places spoken of in anger: enlarged by the person forced to endure it. The rooms were comfortable when the fires were kept properly. The stairs were narrow. A window in his bedchamber caught the afternoon light at an angle which made the opposite wall seem warm long after the air had cooled.

He complained about the draught beneath the door. The housekeeper had a roll of cloth fitted there the next morning. He thanked her. She seemed surprised, which annoyed him until he remembered that he had addressed her sharply the day before.

He was allowed to walk within the marked grounds, accompanied by an officer or one of the men assigned to him. The boundary passed along a wall at the far end of an orchard. Beyond it a track ran towards fields he could not see.

On his first walk he went all the way to the wall. The guard came with him and stopped at the same distance from it. Valdais looked over the stones for a while, then asked who owned the field beyond.

The man told him.

“Does he ride?”

“I don’t know, Your Grace.”

“Everyone here has perfected that answer.”

The guard said nothing. Valdais turned back before he could begin a quarrel with a man who had no reason to know the habits of a farmer.

Ouen’s letter arrived with the child’s drawing folded inside.

The dog had four legs on one side and none visible on the other. Hew had explained, through his father, that the missing legs were behind it. Valdais examined the drawing and decided the explanation could stand.

He read the rest at the window. Four bales returned. Damage agreed. The grey recovering. Men who had slept on borrowed bedding beginning to go home. Ouen had described an argument with a carrier and left in enough of the other man’s answer to make his own part less impressive than he probably intended.

At the end he had written three paragraphs about Hew. Valdais read those again.

His son had always been exact about things which pleased him. A bird’s flight could be retold in detail while a request made before the flight disappeared from memory. Now the same attention followed a child across a room. Valdais could see the movement because Ouen had bothered to tell him where the stool stood and why Hew believed he could reach the shelf without help.

He put the letter beside the first drawing, then took it up once more to check whether the child had fallen. He had not. Iselde had caught him before the stool tipped.

Valdais wrote an answer beginning with the height of shelves. He stopped after two lines. Ouen knew how high his own shelf stood. He turned the sheet and began with the dog.

The days acquired habits before he consented to them.

He woke early because he always had. Breakfast came later than he wanted until the housekeeper altered it. Letters were brought after the clerks had read them. When there were none, Talbot came only if some other matter required an answer, and Valdais found himself listening for footsteps he resented hearing.

The books supplied for him were mostly respectable and several were dull. He asked for a history he remembered owning and received a different volume with almost the same title. He sent it back. The right one would have to be found and inspected before it came.

In the meantime the housekeeper offered to open a chest of books already in the house.

“Whose?”

“Some were the queen’s. Others have been left since.”

“My mother’s?”

“Yes, Your Grace.”

He looked at her.

“Why are they still here?”

“They were kept with the things which weren’t called for when she left. I have an old list. No one has asked for them while I’ve had the house.”

He had expected that every object belonging to Elianor would have travelled after her, summoned by a letter which made its return inevitable. The thought of something left behind through simple neglect disturbed him more than a deliberately preserved relic would have done.

“Bring the chest.”

“It is heavy.”

“Then have the books brought.”

She sent two servants. They laid the books on the table in uneven piles, leather bindings beside a volume whose cover had been repaired with cloth. Dust came away on one man’s sleeve. The housekeeper checked the titles against the old list before leaving them with him.

Among them was a small psalter with a damaged board.

Valdais knew it as soon as he picked it up. He had seen his mother turn its pages with a care she did not give the finer book presented to her later. Once, when he asked why she kept a thing so poorly bound, she had told him she learned Utaran from it at nine, with Brother Tomas sitting beside her and believing the psalms were the important part.

He had laughed then. She had not corrected him.

He opened it carefully. A child’s cramped work stood in the margins of the early pages, amended by an older hand. Farther on the writing changed. There were words he could almost hear her pronouncing, then crossing out because the exact word pleased her better.

He read until the housekeeper came to ask about dinner.

Vaelrie’s next letter described a song he did not know and an argument about whether its ending had been improved.

He could picture her making the argument. She would turn her head slightly when she was pleased with a point, then look offended if someone smiled before she had finished. He had seen Elianor do it. He had seen Ouen do it badly, at an age when every disagreement seemed a trial before an unfair judge.

Vaelrie wrote that Jaennis had been well and that she intended to remain in Valora. She asked whether he had enough books.

He answered that he had found one of her grandmother’s. He did not describe the room in which he read it. She could imagine that for herself if she wished, and he did not want to spend another page deciding whether to make it sound comfortable.

Talbot collected the letter. This one was allowed without amendment.

When the clerk had gone, Valdais took the psalter to the chair nearer the fire. The light was better there in the late afternoon. He rested the book on his knee and turned back to a passage with writing crowded beneath it.

The damaged board shifted under his hand.

He caught it before it fell away. Something thin moved between the loosened covering and the wood. At first he thought it part of the binding. He put a finger beneath the edge and drew out a folded sheet.

The paper had yellowed where it touched the board. The outer writing remained clear.

To whomever else earned the king’s displeasure.

He sat with it unopened for a moment. Then he looked towards the door. No one was there. The clerks had taken the day’s letters; the housekeeper would not come again until the meal.

He opened it.

Ask for a fire before you agree that the room is comfortable. If you agree first, you will be told all winter that you said so yourself.

The window catch in the smaller chamber was defective when I came. Have it mended if it still is. A draught is no part of a lawful sentence, whatever the men responsible for the house may believe.

Be civil to the people who bring the food. They did not choose the king. You may find that difficult to remember on a morning when everybody else has somewhere to go.

If you have brought papers, obtain a room in which to keep them, with a fire. Put the fire in the request. I have learned that a room without one can be granted with perfect honesty by a man who knows exactly what you meant.

The pear tree by the farther wall bears badly. I was assured it would improve with care. So far this has meant that it receives care and I receive assurances. You will recognise the arrangement.

I have left this here because I was told the house would never again be needed for this purpose. If you are reading it, ask whoever told you the same to put his assurance in writing. There is room behind the other board.

Elianor.

He read it again, beginning with the fire. Then Valdais laughed.
